# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 35

## Page 16448

Volume Thirty-Five
From the Book on Fighting Rebels to the End of the Book on Acknowledgment

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger.

Chapter: The Caliphate, Kingship, and Fighting Rebels

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, with no partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah send blessings and abundant peace upon him.

To proceed:

This is “A Brief Statement on the Obligation to Obey Allah and His Messenger” in every circumstance, incumbent upon everyone, and on the fact that what Allah and His Messenger have commanded, including obedience to Allah and to rulers

---

## Page 16449

and offering them sincere counsel, is obligatory, as are the other prescribed duties. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to render trusts to their rightful owners and, when you judge between people, to judge with justice. How excellent is the counsel Allah gives you! Indeed, Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing.”

Allah Most High also says:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is best and leads to the finest outcome.”

Allah thus commanded the believers to obey Him, His Messenger, and those in authority among them, just as He commanded them to render trusts to their rightful owners and to judge with justice when judging between people. He also commanded them, whenever they disagree over anything, to refer it to Allah and the Messenger.

The scholars said: Referring it to Allah means referring it to His Book, and referring it to the Messenger after his death means referring it to his Sunnah. Allah Most High says:

“Mankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning their differences. None differed over it except those who had been given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of mutual envy. Then Allah, by His permission, guided those who believed to the truth concerning which they had differed. And Allah guides whom He wills to a straight path.”

Thus Allah made the Book He revealed the judge between people concerning their differences.

In Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, it is reported from ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, that whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, rose to pray at night, he would say:

“O Allah, Lord of Gabriel and Michael

---

## Page 16450

and Isrāfīl, Creator of the heavens and the earth, Knower of the unseen and the seen, You judge between Your servants concerning their differences. By Your permission, guide me to the truth concerning which people have differed. Indeed, You guide whom You will to a straight path.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Tamīm al-Dārī, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Religion is sincerity. Religion is sincerity. Religion is sincerity.” They asked, “To whom, O Messenger of Allah?” He replied, “To Allah, His Book, His Messenger, the leaders of the Muslims, and their common people.”

Also in Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah is pleased with three things for you: that you worship Him without associating anything with Him; that you hold firmly, all together, to the rope of Allah and do not become divided; and that you offer sincere counsel to those whom Allah has placed in authority over you.”

In the Sunan collections, Ibn Mas‘ūd and Zayd ibn Thābit, may Allah be pleased with them both, reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah brighten the face of someone who hears a hadith from us and conveys it to someone who has not heard it. One who conveys religious knowledge may convey it to someone with greater understanding than himself, and one who conveys religious knowledge may himself lack understanding. There are three things toward which a Muslim’s heart bears no malice: devoting one’s deeds sincerely to Allah, offering sincere counsel to those in authority, and adhering to the Muslim community, for their call encompasses them from behind.”

The well-known reading is yaghallu, with the a-vowel. The expression ghallā sadrahu fa-ghalla is used of someone whose breast is filled with deceit, rancour, and hatred. That is, a Muslim’s heart bears no malice toward these three qualities. They are the same three mentioned earlier in his words:

“Allah is pleased with three things for you: that you worship Him without associating anything with Him; that you hold firmly, all together, to the rope of Allah and do not become divided; and that you offer sincere counsel to those whom Allah has placed in authority over you.”

For if Allah

---

## Page 16451

is pleased with these things for us, the heart of a believer who loves what Allah loves cannot resent them, hating and disliking them and thus harbouring rancour toward them. Rather, the believer’s heart loves them and is pleased with them.

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī and Sahīh Muslim, and elsewhere, ‘Ubādah ibn al-Sāmit, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, accepted our pledge to hear and obey in hardship and ease, whether willing or unwilling, even when others are favoured over us; not to contest authority with those who hold it; and to speak or stand up for the truth wherever we may be, fearing no one’s reproach for Allah’s sake.”

Also in both Sahīhs, ‘Abd Allah ibn ‘Umar reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A Muslim must hear and obey, whether he likes it or dislikes it, unless he is commanded to commit a sin. If he is commanded to commit a sin, there is neither hearing nor obedience.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You must hear and obey in hardship and ease, whether willing or unwilling, even when others are favoured over you.”

His words, “even when others are favoured over you,” and “even when others are favoured over us,” mean: even if those in authority favour themselves over you, treating you unfairly and withholding your rights. Thus, in both Sahīhs, Usayd ibn Hudayr, may Allah be pleased with him, reported:

“A man of the Ansār spoke privately with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘Will you not appoint me to office as you appointed so-and-so?’ He replied, ‘After me, you will encounter favouritism, so remain patient until you meet me at the Cistern.’”

Likewise, in both Sahīhs, ‘Abd Allah ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘After me, there will be favouritism and things you will disapprove of.’ They said:

---

## Page 16452

‘O Messenger of Allah, what do you instruct those of us who live to see that to do?’ He replied, ‘Fulfil the duties you owe, and ask Allah for the rights that are yours.’”

In Sahīh Muslim, Wā’il ibn Hujr, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“Salamah ibn Yazīd al-Ju‘fī asked the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saying, ‘O Messenger of Allah, if rulers rise over us who demand their rights from us but withhold our rights, what do you command us to do?’ He turned away from him. Then he asked him again, and he turned away. Then he asked him a second or third time, whereupon al-Ash‘ath ibn Qays told him: ‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Hear and obey, for they are responsible for the duties entrusted to them, and you are responsible for the duties entrusted to you.”’”

Thus, what Allah and His Messenger have commanded—obeying those in authority and giving them sincere counsel—is obligatory upon a Muslim, even if they favour themselves over him. What Allah and His Messenger have forbidden—disobeying them—is forbidden to him, even if he is coerced into it.

Section

What Allah and His Messenger have commanded of obeying those in authority and giving them sincere counsel is obligatory upon a person, even if he has made no pledge to them concerning it and has sworn no solemn oaths to them. This is just as the five daily prayers, zakat, fasting, pilgrimage to the House, and the other acts of obedience commanded by Allah and His Messenger are obligatory upon him. If he swears an oath to do so, this reinforces and confirms what Allah and His Messenger have commanded of obeying those in authority and giving them sincere counsel. Thus, anyone who swears an oath concerning these

---

## Page 16453

matters may not act contrary to what he has sworn to do, whether he swears by Allah or takes any of the other oaths sworn by Muslims. For obeying those in authority and giving them sincere counsel, as Allah has required, is obligatory even without an oath; how much more so when one has sworn to do so! Likewise, disobeying them and deceiving them, which Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, is prohibited even without an oath concerning it.

This is like someone swearing that he will perform the five daily prayers, fast the month of Ramadan, discharge what he owes, and testify truthfully. These things are obligatory upon him even without an oath; how much more so when he has sworn to do them! Whatever Allah and His Messenger have forbidden—associating partners with Allah, lying, drinking wine, wrongdoing, acts of gross indecency, deceiving those in authority, and departing from the obedience to them that Allah has commanded—is prohibited even without an oath to refrain from it; how much more so when one has sworn such an oath!

Therefore, if someone has sworn to fulfil what Allah and His Messenger have commanded—whether obeying those in authority and giving them sincere counsel, performing prayer, paying zakat, fasting Ramadan, fulfilling trusts, acting justly, or similar duties—no one may give him a fatwa permitting him to act contrary to what he has sworn and break his oath. Nor may he seek a fatwa to that effect.

Whoever gives such people a fatwa permitting them to act contrary to what they have sworn and break their oaths is fabricating lies against Allah and issuing fatwas contrary to the religion of Islam. Indeed, even if an ordinary person were given a fatwa permitting him to break his sworn commitment to honour a contract of sale, marriage, lease, or any other binding

---

## Page 16454

contract—which must be honoured even without an oath, and all the more so when an oath has been sworn—the person who gives such a fatwa permitting the breach of these contracts and the breaking of his oath is fabricating lies against Allah and issuing fatwas contrary to the religion of Islam. How much more serious is this when it concerns covenants with those in authority, which are the most solemn contracts that Allah has commanded people to honour!

Similarly, the majority of scholars hold that an oath taken under wrongful coercion is not binding, whether sworn by Allah or involving a vow, divorce, or manumission. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad.

Yet if the ruler compels people to fulfil their duty to obey him and give him sincere counsel, and to swear to do so, no one may permit them to abandon what Allah and His Messenger have commanded in this regard or allow them to break these oaths. Whatever is obligatory without an oath is reinforced, not weakened, by an oath—even supposing that the person was coerced into taking it.

If someone wishes to argue that the terms of certain kinds of oaths are binding without qualification because those in authority sometimes require people to swear them, he should be told: “This objection applies equally to your own position on an oath taken under coercion, for you maintain that it is not binding even when those in authority take such oaths. It also applies to many of your fatwas permitting legal stratagems, despite the disobedience to Allah Most High, His Messenger, and those in authority that they entail.”

---

## Page 16455

As for people of knowledge, religion, and virtue, they do not permit anyone, under any circumstances, to commit what Allah has forbidden: disobeying those in authority, deceiving them, or rebelling against them. This is well known from the longstanding practice of the people of the Sunnah and religion, past and present, and from the conduct of others.

It is established in the Sahīh, on the authority of Ibn Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A banner will be erected at the rear of every traitor on the Day of Resurrection, commensurate with his treachery.”

He said: “This is among the gravest forms of treachery”—meaning treachery against the leader of the Muslims. Abdullah ibn Umar related this when some people of Medina rose to renounce obedience to their ruler and break their pledge of allegiance to him.

In Sahīh Muslim, Nāfiʿ said:

“Abdullah ibn Umar came to Abdullah ibn Mutīʿ when the events of al-Harrah took place, during the reign of Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah. Ibn Mutīʿ said, ‘Put down a cushion for Abū Abd al-Rahmān.’ He replied, ‘I have not come to sit down. I have come to relate a hadith to you. I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: “Whoever withdraws his obedience will meet Allah on the Day of Resurrection with no argument in his defence. Whoever dies without being bound by a pledge of allegiance dies a death like that of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance.”’”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Abbās, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sees something in his ruler that he dislikes should bear it patiently, for anyone who withdraws from the ruler’s authority by even a handspan and dies in that state dies a death like that of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever renounces obedience and separates from the community, then dies, dies a death like that of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance. Whoever fights under a banner of blind allegiance, becoming angry out of partisan loyalty,

---

## Page 16456

or calling for partisan loyalty, or championing it, and is killed, meets a death like that of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance.” In another wording:

“He is not of my community who attacks my community, striking its righteous and wicked alike, neither sparing its believers nor honouring the covenant of those under its protection. He is not of me, and I am not of him.”

“The first” is the one who withdraws obedience from the ruler and separates from the community.

“The second” is the one who fights for partisan loyalty and leadership, not in Allah’s cause, like those who follow their own desires, such as Qays and Yumn.

“The third” is like the highway robber who kills whomever he encounters, whether a Muslim or a non-Muslim under Muslim protection, to seize his wealth. It also includes the renegade Harūriyyah whom Alī ibn Abī Tālib fought, concerning whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Any one of you would consider his prayer insignificant beside theirs, his fasting beside theirs, and his recitation beside theirs. They recite the Quran, but it does not go beyond their throats. They pass out of Islam as an arrow passes through its quarry. Wherever you encounter them, kill them, for whoever kills them will have a reward with Allah on the Day of Resurrection.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded obedience to the ruler, even if he were an Abyssinian slave. As recorded in Sahīh Muslim, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Listen and obey, even if an Abyssinian slave whose head is like a raisin is appointed over you.”

Abū Dharr said: “My beloved friend enjoined me to listen and obey, even if he were an Abyssinian with mutilated

---

## Page 16457

limbs.” Al-Bukhārī reports:

“Even if it is an Abyssinian whose head is like a raisin.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Umm al-Husayn, may Allah be pleased with her, reported that she heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saying during the Farewell Pilgrimage:

“Even if a slave is appointed to lead you according to the Book of Allah, listen and obey.”

Another wording reads: “A mutilated Abyssinian slave.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Awf ibn Mālik, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best of your rulers are those whom you love and who love you, for whom you pray and who pray for you. The worst of your rulers are those whom you hate and who hate you, whom you curse and who curse you.”

We asked, “O Messenger of Allah, should we not then oppose them with the sword?”

He replied: “No, so long as they establish prayer among you. No, so long as they establish prayer among you. Indeed, whoever has a ruler appointed over him and sees him committing an act of disobedience to Allah should detest that act of disobedience, but must not withdraw his obedience.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Abd Allāh ibn Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Those who act justly will be with Allah on pulpits of light, to the right of the Most Merciful—and both His hands are right hands. They are those who are just in their judgments, with their families, and in whatever is entrusted to them.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, reported that she heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saying:

“O Allah, whoever assumes responsibility for any of my community’s affairs and makes things difficult for them, make things difficult for him. Whoever assumes responsibility for any of my community’s affairs and treats them gently, treat him gently.”

In the two Sahīh collections, al-Hasan al-Basrī said: “Ubayd Allāh ibn Ziyād visited Maqil ibn Yasār during the illness from which he died

---

## Page 16458

, and Maqil said to him: ‘I will relate to you a hadith that I heard from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: “Whenever Allah entrusts a servant with a people’s care, and he dies while betraying their trust, Allah forbids him Paradise.”’”

Another narration in Muslim reads:

“No ruler who assumes responsibility for any of the Muslims’ affairs, then fails to strive for their welfare and act sincerely toward them, will enter Paradise with them.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, each of you is a shepherd, and each of you is accountable for his flock. A man is a shepherd over his household and is accountable for them. A woman is a shepherd over her husband’s home and is accountable for it. A slave is a shepherd over his master’s property and is accountable for it. Indeed, each of you is a shepherd, and each of you is accountable for his flock.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, reported:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, dispatched an army and appointed a man to command them. The man lit a fire and said, ‘Enter it.’ Some wanted to enter it, while others said, ‘It is from the Fire that we have fled!’ This was mentioned to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said to those who had wanted to enter it, ‘Had you entered it, you would have remained in it until the Day of Resurrection.’ He spoke kindly to the others and said, ‘There is no obedience in disobedience to Allah; obedience is only in what is right.’”

---

## Page 16459

Section

Allah Most High said:

“I did not create jinn and mankind except to worship Me.”

Allah Most High also said:

“We sent no messenger except to be obeyed by Allah’s permission.”

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

And He said:

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge in their disputes, then find within themselves no resistance to your judgment and submit fully.”

And He said:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive you your sins.’”

And He said:

“On the Day their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say, ‘If only we had obeyed Allah and obeyed the Messenger!’”

“And they will say, ‘Our Lord, we obeyed our leaders and our great men, and they led us astray from the path.’”

“‘Our Lord, give them double the punishment and curse them with a mighty curse.’”

And He said:

“Whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger will be with those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs and the righteous. What excellent companions they are!”

Obedience to Allah and His Messenger is obligatory upon everyone. Obedience to those in authority is obligatory because Allah has commanded that they be obeyed. Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger by obeying those in authority for Allah’s sake will receive his reward from Allah. But whoever obeys them only for the office and wealth he receives—if they give to him, he obeys them; but if

---

## Page 16460

they withhold from him, he disobeys them—will have no share in the Hereafter.

Al-Bukhārī and Muslim narrated from Abū Hurayra, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are three whom Allah will neither speak to nor look at on the Day of Resurrection, nor will He purify them, and theirs will be a painful punishment: a man who has surplus water in the wilderness but withholds it from a wayfarer; a man who sells another man an item after the afternoon prayer and swears to him by Allah that he bought it for such-and-such a price, so the other believes him, although that is not true; and a man who pledges allegiance to a ruler solely for worldly gain—if the ruler gives him some of it, he keeps his pledge, but if he does not, he fails to keep it.”

---

## Page 16461

He also said—may Allah sanctify his soul:

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of the worlds. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant them abundant peace.

A Principle

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The prophetic caliphate will last thirty years; then Allah will bestow His kingship—or kingship—upon whomever He wills.”

Abū Dāwūd’s wording, as transmitted by ‘Abd al-Wārith and al-‘Awwām:

“The caliphate will last thirty years, then there will be kingship.”

“The caliphate will last thirty years, then it will become kingship.”

This is a well-known hadith transmitted by Hammād ibn Salama, ‘Abd al-Wārith ibn Sa‘īd, al-‘Awwām ibn Hawshab and others, from Sa‘īd ibn Jumhān, from Safīna, the freedman of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It was narrated by the compilers of the Sunan, such as Abū Dāwūd and others. Imam Ahmad and others relied upon it to establish the caliphate of the four Rightly Guided Caliphs. Ahmad affirmed its authenticity and cited it against those who withheld judgment concerning ‘Alī’s caliphate because of

---

## Page 16462

the people’s division over him. Ahmad even said:

“Whoever does not recognize ‘Alī as the fourth caliph is more astray than his family’s donkey.”

He also forbade intermarrying with such a person. This recognition is agreed upon among the jurists, scholars of the Sunnah, and people of spiritual knowledge and Sufism; it is also the position of ordinary Muslims.

Only certain followers of desires among the speculative theologians and others disagree with them: the Rāfida, who impugn the caliphate of the first three; the Kharijites, who impugn the caliphate of the two sons-in-law from Banū ‘Abd Manāf, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī; certain Nāsiba—those hostile to ‘Alī—who deny his caliphate; and some ignorant, self-professed Sunnis who withhold judgment concerning it.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, died in Rabī‘ al-Awwal, in the eleventh year after his migration. Thirty years later, the Messenger of Allah’s grandson, the noble leader al-Hasan ibn ‘Alī, reconciled two groups of believers by relinquishing authority in Jumādā al-Ūlā of the year 41 AH. That year was called the “Year of Unity,” because the people united under Mu‘āwiya, who was the first king.

In the hadith narrated by Muslim:

“There will be a prophetic and merciful caliphate, then kingship with mercy, then kingship with coercion, then oppressive kingship.”

And he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the well-known, authentic hadith recorded in the Sunan:

“Whoever among you lives after me will see much disagreement. Adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs who come after me. Hold firmly to it and cling to it with your molar teeth. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every religious innovation is misguidance.”

---

## Page 16463

Those who came after the rightly guided caliphs may also be called “caliphs,” even though they were kings and were not successors to the prophets. This is supported by the report recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their Sahīh collections, from Abū Hurayra, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The Children of Israel were governed by prophets. Whenever a prophet died, another prophet succeeded him. There will be no prophet after me, but there will be caliphs, and they will be many.” They asked, “What, then, do you command us to do?” He replied, “Honor the pledge of allegiance to the first, then the next. Give them their due, for Allah will question them about those He entrusted to their care.”

His words, “They will be many,” indicate that this refers to others besides the rightly guided caliphs, for they were not many. Likewise, his words, “Honor the pledge of allegiance to the first, then the next,” indicate that they would differ among themselves, whereas the rightly guided caliphs did not differ. His words, “Give them their due, for Allah will question them about those He entrusted to their care,” support the position of Ahl al-Sunnah concerning giving rulers their due from wealth and spoils of war.

I have explained elsewhere that authority passing to kings and their deputies—governors, judges, and commanders—is not due solely to shortcomings in them, but to shortcomings in both rulers and subjects. For, “As you are, so are those appointed to rule over you.” Allah Most High has said:

“Thus We set some wrongdoers over others.”

It has been amply reported and established elsewhere that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded obedience to rulers in matters that do not involve disobedience to Allah, sincere counsel to them, patience with their judgments and distributions, going on military campaigns with them, praying behind them, and similar matters

---

## Page 16464

such as following them in good deeds that only they can carry out, for this falls under “cooperating in righteousness and piety.” He also forbade believing their lies, helping them in their oppression, obeying them in disobedience to Allah, and similar conduct that falls under “cooperating in sin and transgression.”

He likewise commanded enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong, with respect to rulers and others alike, in the manner prescribed by Islamic law. This includes conveying Allah’s messages to them: one must not neglect this out of cowardice, miserliness, fear of them, or a desire to exchange Allah’s revelations for a paltry price. Nor should one do it to gain authority over them or over the common people, out of envy or arrogance, or to make a show before them or the common people.

An evil must not be removed by something still more evil, such as taking up arms against them and stirring up turmoil. This is a well-known principle of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamāʿah, as indicated by the prophetic texts, because the harm this causes exceeds the harm arising from their oppression. Rather, Allah must be obeyed in dealing with them and with others: what He commands must be done, and what He forbids must be avoided.

This is a summary whose detailed explanation would require extensive discussion. The purpose here is to explain “the full range of good and evil deeds” arising after the prophetic caliphate, both in holding authority and in refraining from it, for this is a perilous matter. His report that the “prophetic caliphate” would come to an end contains censure and criticism of kingship, especially since, in the hadith of {Abū

---

## Page 16465

Bakrah: he was distressed by the dream and said, ‘A prophetic caliphate; then Allah grants kingship to whomever He wills.’”

Then there are the texts requiring the appointment of imams and rulers, and the reward attached to the righteous deeds they undertake: these commend such authority and encourage it. Thus, its praiseworthy aspects must be distinguished from its blameworthy aspects, and the ruling when both coexist must be considered. It is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah gave me the choice between being a servant and Messenger or a prophet and king, and I chose to be a servant and Messenger.”

If, then, the underlying issue here is the mixed character of authority, whether in governance or adjudication, is kingship permissible in principle, with the caliphate merely recommended? Or is it permissible only when needed because of insufficient knowledge or an inability to govern without it? We argue that it is not permissible in principle; rather, the prophetic caliphate is obligatory, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs after me. Hold firmly to it and cling to it with your molar teeth. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every innovation is misguidance.”

This followed his statement:

“Those of you who live after me will see much disagreement.”

This is a command and an exhortation to adhere to the Sunnah of the caliphs, a command to hold firmly to it, and a warning against newly introduced matters that contradict it. This command and prohibition from him clearly establish that such adherence is obligatory.

---

## Page 16466

More specifically, there is his statement:

“Follow the example of the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and Umar.”

Thus, he commanded that these two be followed as examples, while commanding adherence to the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs. This distinguishes Abū Bakr and Umar in two respects. First, “Sunnah” refers to what they established as a practice for the people, whereas following their “example” also includes following them in actions they did not establish as a Sunnah. Second, he attributed the Sunnah to the caliphs, not to each of them individually. It might therefore be said that this concerns what they agreed upon, rather than what some of them adopted individually. Following their example, however, specifically means following each of these two. This second argument is open to question.

It may be understood from this that there is no command to follow Uthmān and Alī in those exercises of independent judgment in which Abū Bakr and Umar had previously adopted a better course—whose superiority is supported by the scriptural texts and the agreement of the majority of the Muslim community—and which were prompted by the community’s division. Such actions do not belong to the Sunnah of the caliphs.

Abū Bakr and Umar governed the community through incentives and deterrence, and remained free of interpretive judgments concerning bloodshed and wealth. Uthmān, may Allah be pleased with him, placed greater emphasis on incentives and acted on interpretive judgments concerning wealth. Alī placed greater emphasis on deterrence and acted on interpretive judgments concerning bloodshed. Abū Bakr and Umar were wholly detached from wealth and leadership. Uthmān was wholly detached from leadership, and Alī was wholly detached from wealth.

---

## Page 16467

Moreover, the fact that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was distressed at kingship following the prophetic caliphate indicates that it entails neglecting some obligatory part of the religion. Those who permit kingship may invoke texts such as his statement to Mu‘āwiyah, “If you become a king, rule well,” and similar reports. This argument, however, is open to question.

It is also argued that, when Umar visited Syria, he acquiesced in the royal pomp he observed around Mu‘āwiyah after the latter explained its benefit to him. Umar said, “I neither command you nor forbid you.” It may be said in response that Umar did not forbid him, but neither did he authorize him to do this: Mu‘āwiyah explained why it was needed, but Umar was not convinced of that need. The matter was therefore, broadly speaking, open to independent judgment.

There are thus two moderate positions: either that the caliphate is obligatory and departure from it is permissible only to the extent required by need; or that one may accept from kingship what facilitates, rather than hinders, the fulfilment of the purpose of governance. Whatever is required to bring that purpose within reach must be permitted. As for kingship, whether it should be obligatory or recommended is a matter for independent judgment.

There are two extremes here. The first consists of those who insist upon this in every circumstance and age, and for everyone, condemning anyone who departs from it, whether without qualification or because of a need. Such is the position of the people of innovation among the Khawārij and Mu‘tazilah, and certain groups who profess adherence to the Sunnah and asceticism.

The second:

---

## Page 16468

those who permit kingship without qualification, without adhering to the practice of the caliphs, as do tyrants, antinomians, and certain members of the Murji’ah. This is a sound distinction, whose full explanation will follow.

The matter may be precisely stated as follows: the transition from the prophetic caliphate to kingship may result from people’s inability to maintain the prophetic caliphate, from legitimate independent judgment, or occur despite their having the requisite knowledge and practical ability. If it results from a lack of knowledge or practical ability, the king is excused, even though the prophetic caliphate is obligatory when one has the ability to maintain it. Other obligations likewise cease to apply when one is unable to fulfil them. Such was the situation of al-Najāshī when he embraced Islam but could not openly profess it among his people. Indeed, the situation of Joseph the truthful resembles this in certain respects. Kingship, however, was permissible for some prophets, such as David, Solomon, and Joseph.

If the requisite knowledge and practical ability are present, and it is supposed that the prophetic caliphate is recommended rather than obligatory, and that choosing kingship is permissible under our revealed law just as it was under other revealed laws, then—assuming this supposition is correct—the just king likewise bears no sin.

Judge Abū Ya‘lā mentioned this argument in al-Mu‘tamad when discussing the legitimacy of Mu‘āwiyah’s caliphate. He based it on the clear evidence of his Islam, uprightness, and good conduct, and on the fact that his leadership was established after Ali’s death, when al-Hasan conferred it upon him. That year was called “the Year of Unity.” He also cited the hadith of ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd: “The millstone of Islam turns at the end of five

---

## Page 16469

and thirty.” He said:

Ahmad said, in the report transmitted by Ibn al-Hakam, relating from al-Zuhrī that for five years nothing in Mu‘āwiyah’s rule was objected to: “This accorded with the Prophet’s statement—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—‘thirty-five years.’”

Ibn al-Hakam said: I asked Ahmad, “Who said that the years in Ibn Mas‘ūd’s hadith, ‘The millstone of Islam turns for thirty-five years,’ are counted from the Prophet’s migration—may Allah bless him and grant him peace?”

He replied: “He has indeed reported this. What objection is there to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—describing Islam’s course in relation to wrongdoing? He is merely describing the years that would follow him.”

He said: The apparent meaning of Ahmad’s words is that he understood the hadith according to its apparent meaning, and that Mu‘āwiyah’s caliphate was included within those thirty-five years. He also mentioned that a man asked Ahmad about the caliphate, and he replied: “Every pledge of allegiance given in Medina establishes, in our view, a prophetic caliphate.”

The judge said: “The apparent implication is that whatever was established outside Medina was not a prophetic caliphate.”

I say: Ahmad’s explicit statements that the caliphate reached its completion with Ali are very numerous.

The judge then raised against this the Prophet’s statement:

“The caliphate will last thirty years, then become kingship.”

The questioner said: “Since he limited the caliphate after him to thirty years, ending with Ali’s final days, and stated that thereafter there would be kingship, this indicates that what followed was not a caliphate.”

The judge replied that what might be meant was that the “caliphate” after him, untainted by kingship, would last “thirty

---

## Page 16470

years.” Such was the caliphate of the four caliphs.

As for Mu‘āwiyah, his caliphate was mingled with kingship. This does not detract from his caliphate, just as Solomon’s kingship did not detract from his prophethood, even though other prophets were poor.

I say: This implies that mingling the caliphate with kingship is permissible under our religious law and does not compromise moral uprightness, although an unalloyed caliphate is better.

Anyone who defends Mu‘āwiyah, regards him as exercising independent judgment in his affairs, and does not attribute sin to him must adopt one of two positions: either that mingling the caliphate with kingship is permissible, or that no blame attaches to him for doing so. This is therefore tenable if (1) he says that the prophetic caliphate is obligatory. If that were supposed—then, if he committed a misdeed, it was a major sin, even if it was a matter of religion; or because a transgressor is someone whose bad deeds outweigh his good deeds, which is not the case here. This is his mercy toward just kings, for among the Companions they are people whose example is followed.

As for the adherents of innovation, such as the Mu‘tazilites, they declare Mu‘āwiyah a transgressor for fighting Ali and for other matters, on the grounds that he committed a major sin and that this entails declaring him a transgressor. One of these two premises must therefore be rejected.

If this is admissible for kings, it is also admissible for judges, governors, and others like them. But if the prophetic caliphate is obligatory and attainable, yet has been abandoned, then abandoning an obligation is grounds for blame and punishment. Is abandoning it, then, a major sin or a minor one?

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A lacuna of approximately one line in the original.

---

## Page 16471

If it is a minor sin, it does not compromise moral uprightness; if it is a major sin, the two views apply.

However, it may be said here: If someone exercising kingship and authority performs the good deeds commanded and refrains from the evil deeds prohibited to such an extent that his reward exceeds the punishment for the obligations he neglects or the prohibitions he violates, then his good deeds outweigh his bad deeds. If someone else falls short in the acts of obedience that this ruler performs, while remaining free of his sins, there are “three possible cases”:

The surplus of the ruler’s good deeds may be greater or less than the total good deeds of the other person. If that surplus is greater, the ruler is superior; if it is less, he is inferior; and if the two are equal, they are equal in merit. This is what justice requires and what the texts of the Quran and Sunnah concerning reward and punishment entail. It rests upon the view that good and bad deeds are weighed against and offset one another in determining recompense, and likewise in assessing moral uprightness.

As for those who say that a person deserves the threatened punishment for a single major sin, even if he has many great good deeds, this reasoning does not apply according to their view. A group of scholars holds this position concerning moral uprightness. The first view, however, is more correct, as indicated by the texts.

From this arises a “question”: What if someone can perform a good deed of outweighing merit only by committing a sin whose punishment is less than the reward for that good deed? This has two cases:

---

## Page 16472

The first: When this is the only possible way, the act no longer counts as a sin, for whatever is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation or performing a recommended act is itself obligatory or recommended. If its harm is less than that benefit, it is not prohibited. Examples include eating carrion out of dire necessity and other prohibited acts that become permissible because of need, such as wearing silk in cold weather and similar cases.

This is a matter of great importance. Many people sense the wrongness of an act but overlook the countervailing need, addressing which earns a reward for good that outweighs that harm. Thus, the prohibited act becomes included among acts beloved to Allah, or becomes permissible when need alone is what weighs against its prohibition. Conversely, some permissible acts—indeed, even acts commanded as obligatory or recommended—may involve an outweighing harm that renders them forbidden or less preferable. Examples include fasting for a sick person and purification with water for someone whose life would thereby be endangered. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“They killed him—may Allah kill them! Why did they not ask when they did not know? The cure for ignorance is to ask.”

This principle underlies the permissibility of sometimes departing from certain practices of the caliphs, just as necessity permits leaving some obligations of the Sharia unfulfilled and committing some of its prohibitions. This applies when one is unable to follow certain practices of theirs, or when necessity requires doing something they prohibited: namely, when the duties that governance is intended to fulfil cannot be discharged except through something whose harm is lesser.

---

## Page 16473

The same applies to “the question of refraining,” as we stated at the outset, explaining that only the people of innovation and others like them who are ignorant and unjust disagree with this.

“The second case” is when good deeds can be performed without committing a sin, but only with hardship that a person cannot bring himself to endure, or with an aversion arising from his disposition. He cannot bring himself to perform those great good deeds, commanded as obligatory or recommended, unless he allows himself some prohibited things that he desires, whose sinfulness is outweighed by the benefit of the good deed. This situation occurs frequently among those engaged in authority, governance, and jihad; scholarship, adjudication, and theological disputation; worship and Sufism; and among ordinary people.

An example is someone who cannot bring himself to discharge the duties of governance—enjoining right, forbidding wrong, enforcing the prescribed punishments, securing the roads, fighting the enemy, and distributing funds—unless he enjoys certain prohibited advantages, such as appropriating some funds for himself, exercising dominion over people, showing favoritism in distribution, and satisfying other desires.

Likewise, in jihad, a person may be unable to bring himself to fight without a measure of recklessness. In scholarship, he may be unable to bring himself to master jurisprudence and the foundations of religion without engaging in some prohibited forms of speculative opinion and theological disputation. Nor can he bring himself to master the knowledge of prescribed worship and acquire the knowledge he is commanded to seek except through a measure of monasticism.

This situation has become widespread under the rule of kings, occurring among the kings themselves and many of their commanders, judges, scholars, and worshippers—by whom I mean the people of their time. And because of this

---

## Page 16474

strife arose within the Muslim community. Some people considered the prohibited acts they had committed and condemned and hated them. Others considered the commanded deeds they had performed and loved them. The former would sometimes count their good deeds as evil, while the latter would sometimes count their evil deeds as good.

The underlying principle of this question has already been discussed: if fulfilling the duties of governance is difficult without some form of kingship, does kingship become permissible, just as it becomes permissible when fulfilling those duties would otherwise be impossible? We have mentioned the two views on this. If difficulty is treated as equivalent to impossibility, this is not sinful; if it is not, it is sinful. But where there is neither impossibility nor difficulty, departing from the practice of the caliphs amounts to following one’s desires.

“The correct assessment” is that good deeds remain good deeds, and evil deeds remain evil deeds: these people have mixed righteous deeds with evil ones. The ruling of the Sharia is that they are neither permitted nor commanded to commit the evil deeds they have committed. Nor is their personal gratification to be accepted as an excuse for their actions when the Sharia has not excused them. Rather, they are to be commanded, urged, and encouraged to perform the good deeds they have performed, even if it is known that they will only perform them while committing misdeeds whose harm is outweighed by that good. Thus, rulers are commanded to wage jihad even if it is known that they will only do so while committing some injustice whose harm is slight compared with the benefit of jihad.

Then, if it is known that forbidding them those misdeeds would lead them to abandon obligatory good deeds whose benefit outweighs that harm, they are not to be forbidden those misdeeds, because doing so would cause the harm of abandoning obligatory good deeds. The exception is when both requirements can be met; in that case, the duty is fulfilled in its entirety, just as

---

## Page 16475

Umar ibn al-Khattāb would appoint someone guilty of misconduct because the benefit of his work outweighed that misconduct, then put an end to his misconduct through his strength and justice.

Refraining from forbidding such misdeeds in those circumstances is like refraining from stopping wrongdoing by physical force or arms when doing so would cause greater harm than the wrongdoing itself.

If, in a particular case, forbidding wrongdoing necessarily leads to the abandonment of a greater good, this is equivalent to its necessarily leading to the commission of a greater evil. An example is someone who embraces Islam on condition that he perform only two prayers, as is reported of some who embraced Islam during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Another is a powerful king who embraces Islam while drinking wine or committing other prohibited acts, but who would renounce Islam if forbidden to do so. There is thus a difference between a scholar or ruler refraining from forbidding certain people to do something because forbidding it would cause greater harm, and his granting permission to do it. This varies according to the circumstances.

In other circumstances, the prohibition must be openly declared: to make clear that the act is forbidden, foster conviction in its prohibition and fear of committing it; in the hope that it will be abandoned; or to establish the proof against those concerned—as circumstances require. This is why the Prophet’s conduct, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, varied in his commands and prohibitions, his fighting and pardoning, his enforcement of prescribed punishments, his sternness and his mercy.

---

## Page 16476

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Section

I have previously discussed kingship: Is it permissible under our religious law, while the caliphate on the prophetic model is recommended and superior to it? Or is the caliphate on the prophetic model obligatory, such that abandoning it in favour of kingship is permitted only for a valid excuse, as with other obligations? I have addressed this question.

Under the religious law of those before us, kingship was permissible. Like wealth, it was sometimes granted to prophets and sometimes to righteous people. Allah Most High said concerning David:

“Allah granted him kingship and wisdom and taught him whatever He willed.”

He related that Solomon said:

“My Lord, forgive me and grant me a kingdom such as no one after me will have. Indeed, You are the Bestower.”

He related that Joseph said:

“My Lord, You have granted me a share of kingship and taught me the interpretation of dreams.”

These are three prophets whom Allah tells us He granted kingship. He also said:

“Or do they envy people for what Allah has bestowed upon them of His bounty? We certainly gave the family of Abraham the Scripture and wisdom, and We granted them a mighty kingdom.”

“Some of them believed in it, while others turned away from it. And sufficient is Hell as a blazing fire.”

Thus, there was kingship among the family of Abraham and kingship among the family of David. Regarding His words, “You grant kingship to whom You will,” Mujāhid said: “Prophethood.” He thus regarded prophethood itself as kingship.

---

## Page 16477

The precise position, however, is that some forms of prophethood involve kingship. A prophet may be in one of three situations. He may be disbelieved, neither followed nor obeyed; such a prophet has not been granted kingship. Alternatively, he may be obeyed, and the very fact that he is obeyed constitutes kingship. Yet if he commands only what he has been commanded to convey, he is a servant-messenger without kingship. If, however, he commands whatever he wishes, being permitted to do so as a king is, then he is a prophet-king, as Solomon was told:

“This is Our gift; so give or withhold, without being called to account.”

Here, the king stands in contrast to the servant-messenger, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was told:

“Choose to be either a servant-messenger or a prophet-king.”

Under the first interpretation, however—being obeyed and followed—kingship is a category within prophethood and messengership, and those in this category are more perfect. This was the condition of our Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—for he was a servant-messenger, supported, obeyed and followed. He was granted the benefit of being obeyed and followed so that he would receive a reward like that of those who followed him, and so that people would benefit through him and receive mercy through him, while he would receive mercy through them. He did not choose to be a king, lest the enjoyment of authority and wealth that this entails diminish his share in the Hereafter. For the servant-messenger is superior in Allah’s sight to the prophet-king.

Thus, the standing of Noah, Abraham, Moses and Jesus son of Mary was higher than that of David, Solomon and Joseph. Some of the People of the Book have even impugned the prophethood of David and Solomon, just as many people question whether certain people of authority and wealth are friends of Allah. Yet this is not so.

---

## Page 16478

As for “righteous kings,” consider His words, glorified is He:

“Allah has appointed Saul as your king.” They said, “How can he have kingship over us when we have a greater right to it than he does, and he has not been given abundant wealth?” He said, “Allah has chosen him over you and endowed him abundantly with knowledge and stature. Allah grants His kingship to whom He wills. Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing.”

“Their prophet said to them, ‘The sign of his kingship is that the Ark will come to you.’”

And His words, glorified is He:

“They ask you about Dhū al-Qarnayn. Say, ‘I will relate to you an account of him.’”

“We established him firmly in the land and gave him the means to accomplish everything”—and so forth.

Mujāhid said: “Two believers and two disbelievers ruled the earth. The two believers were Solomon and Dhū al-Qarnayn, and the two disbelievers were Nebuchadnezzar and Nimrod. A fifth, from this community, will rule it.”

And His words, exalted is He:

“O my people, remember Allah’s favor upon you when He raised prophets among you and made you kings.”

As for “kings in general,” references to them are numerous, such as His words:

“Beyond them was a king who seized every ship by force.”

And His words:

“The king said, ‘I see seven fat cows being eaten by seven lean ones.’”

---

## Page 16479

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Know that Allah Most High sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with guidance and the religion of truth, to make it prevail over all religion. He perfected the religion for his community and completed His favor upon them. He set him upon a prescribed path in religious affairs and commanded him to follow it, not the way of those who do not know. He made his Book a guardian over the scriptures that preceded it and a confirmation of them. He appointed for him a law and a way, and prescribed for his community the paths of guidance. Religion can only be upheld through the Book, the balance, and iron: a Book by which He guides, and iron that supports it. As Allah Most High says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance so that people may uphold justice. And We sent down iron, in which there is great might and benefits for people.”

Through the Book, knowledge and religion are upheld. Through the balance, rights are upheld in financial contracts and the receipt of property. Through iron, prescribed punishments are enforced against disbelievers and hypocrites. Thus, in later times, the Book fell to scholars and worshippers; the balance to ministers, scribes, and administrative officials; and iron to rulers and soldiers.

Prayer pertains to the Book, and jihad to iron. Hence, most Quranic verses and prophetic hadiths concern prayer and jihad. When visiting the sick, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would say:

“O Allah, heal Your servant so that he may attend prayer for Your sake and inflict harm upon an enemy for Your sake.”

And he, blessings and peace be upon him, said:

“The foundation of the matter is Islam, its pillar is prayer, and its pinnacle is jihad in the cause of Allah.”

---

## Page 16480

For this reason, He brings the two together in several places in the Quran, as in His words, Most High:

“The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger, then never doubt, and strive with their wealth and their lives in the cause of Allah.”

Prayer is the first of the deeds of Islam and the foundation of the deeds of faith. Hence, He called it faith when He said:

“Allah would never let your faith go to waste.”

That is, your prayers facing Jerusalem. This is the explanation transmitted from the early generations. Allah Most High also says:

“Do you consider providing water to pilgrims and maintaining the Sacred Mosque equal to believing in Allah and the Last Day and striving in the cause of Allah? They are not equal in Allah’s sight.”

And He says:

“Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble toward the believers, mighty against the disbelievers, striving in the cause of Allah and fearing no one’s reproach.”

He thus describes them as possessing love, which is the very essence of prayer. Likewise, He says:

“Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. Those with him are stern toward the disbelievers and merciful among themselves. You see them bowing and prostrating, seeking Allah’s bounty and pleasure.”

He thus describes them as stern toward disbelievers and misguidance.

It is reported in al-Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked, “Which deed is best?” He replied, “Faith in Allah and jihad in His cause.” He was then asked, “What comes next?” He replied, “Then an accepted Hajj.”

Alongside this is his statement in the authentic hadith: When Ibn Masʿūd asked him, “Which deed is best?” he replied, “Prayer at its proper time.” He asked, “Then what?” He replied, “Dutifulness to parents.” He asked, “Then what?” He replied, “Jihad in the cause of Allah.”

His words “faith in Allah” include prayer. He did not mention dutifulness to parents in the first report because not everyone has parents. The first statement is therefore unrestricted, while the second is restricted to those who have parents.

---

## Page 16481

For this reason, the practice of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him), his Rightly Guided Caliphs, and the rulers who followed their path under the Umayyad and Abbasid dynasties was that the ruler led in both these fundamental matters: prayer and jihad. Whoever led the people in prayer also led them in jihad; responsibility for jihad and prayer was united, whether at home or travelling.

When the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) appointed a man to govern a town—such as ‘Attāb ibn Asīd over Mecca, ‘Uthmān ibn Abī al-‘Ās over Tā’if, and others—that man would lead its people in prayer and carry out the prescribed punishments. Likewise, when he appointed someone to command a military expedition—as he appointed Zayd ibn Hārithah, his son Usāmah, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and others—the military commander also led the people in prayer. For this reason, the Muslims took his appointment of Abū Bakr to lead the prayer as evidence that he had given him precedence for leadership of the community as a whole.

The same applied to the commanders appointed by “al-Siddīq”—such as Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān, Khālid ibn al-Walīd, Shurahbīl ibn Hasanah, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and others: the military commander was also the prayer leader. So too with the deputies of “‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb”: in Kufa, for example, he appointed ‘Ammār ibn Yāsir to oversee military affairs and prayer, Ibn Mas‘ūd to oversee the judiciary and the public treasury, and ‘Uthmān ibn Hunayf to oversee revenue collection.

This was the origin of the offices of military command, revenue administration, and judicial authority. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb was the Commander of the Believers. When the believers spread out, defeated the disbelievers and conquered their lands, and needed more extensive organization, he established

---

## Page 16482

the “register system” for them: a revenue register for money collected, and a register of stipends and expenditures for money disbursed. He also founded garrison towns for them, establishing Kufa, Basra, and Fustāt. He did not wish a great river, such as the Tigris, Euphrates, or Nile, to separate him from the Muslim army, so he situated these towns on the side nearest him.

Section

The mosques were “the seats of the rulers and the gathering places of the community.” The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) founded his blessed mosque upon piety. In it, prayer, Quran recitation, remembrance of Allah, teaching, and sermons took place. It was also the place for governing, conferring military standards and banners, and appointing commanders and headmen. There the Muslims gathered around him to address their concerns regarding their religious and worldly affairs.

The same applied to his governors in places such as Mecca, Tā’if, the regions of Yemen, and other towns and villages, as well as his officials among the desert communities. They had a gathering place where they prayed and where their affairs were governed, as the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“The Children of Israel were governed by the prophets. Whenever a prophet passed away, another prophet succeeded him. There will be no prophet after me, but there will be caliphs whose conduct you will approve of and disapprove of.” They asked, “What, then, do you command us to do?” He replied, “Honour the pledge of allegiance given to the first, then the next, and ask Allah on your own behalf, for Allah will question them about those He entrusted to their care.”

---

## Page 16483

The “caliphs and commanders” lived in their own homes, just as other Muslims did, but the ruler’s public assembly was held in the congregational mosque.

Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqās had built himself a palace in Kufa and said, “I shall keep the people away from me.” So ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb sent Muhammad ibn Maslamah to him and ordered him to burn it down. He bought a bundle of firewood from a Nabataean, stipulating that the seller carry it to Saʿd’s palace, and burned it down. ʿUmar disapproved of a governor secluding himself from his subjects. Nevertheless, palaces were built for the commanders.

When Muʿāwiyah came to power, he secluded himself for fear of being assassinated as ʿAlī had been. Screened enclosures were installed in mosques so that the ruler and his entourage could pray within them, and he acquired mounts. The caliphs who ruled as kings followed his example in these practices. Thus, although they commanded armies, led the people in prayer, personally conducted Friday and congregational prayers, waged jihad, and enforced the prescribed punishments, they also had palaces where they lived and received leading figures. Such was “al-Khadrā’” (the Green Palace), which belonged to the Umayyads and stood on the Mecca-facing side of the congregational mosque. The mosques remained gathering places for worship, learning, and similar activities.

Section

As time passed and the Muslim community became divided, each group clung to one branch of religion, introducing additions of its own while turning away from another branch. Kings and commanders established “citadels and fortresses,” whereas previously fortifications and strongholds had been built only along the frontiers, for fear of

---

## Page 16484

a sudden enemy attack when there was no one to repel it. They called the Syrian frontier districts “al-ʿAwāsim” (the protective districts), namely Qinnasrīn and Aleppo.

“Colleges” were established for scholars, and “lodges and Sufi hospices” for those devoted to worship. I believe these began to spread under “Seljuk rule.” The construction of colleges and lodges for the poor, with endowments assigned to support their residents, first began during the vizierate of “Nizām al-Mulk.” Before that, colleges and lodges are indeed mentioned, but I do not think endowments had been assigned to maintain their residents; they were simply residences set aside for them.

Imam Maʿmar ibn Ziyād, one of al-Wāhidī’s associates, mentioned in “Accounts of the Sufis” that the first small lodge built for them was in Basra. As for “colleges,” I have seen references to them before Seljuk rule, during the fourth century, whereas their rule began only in the fifth century.

Likewise, most of these “citadels and fortresses” in Syria are of later construction, such as the citadels of Damascus, Bosra, and Harrān built by al-Malik al-ʿĀdil. This was because the Christians frequently raided their lands. After the third century, the people had become too weak to defend the coasts against the Christians, until the latter gained control over many of Syria’s coastal frontier strongholds.

---

## Page 16485

Section: The Caliphate and the Ruler, and in What Sense He Is Allah’s Shadow on Earth

Allah Most High says:

“And when your Lord said to the angels, ‘Indeed, I will place a successor on earth.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“O David, We have made you a successor on earth, so judge between people with truth, and do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from Allah’s path.”

His words, “Indeed, I will place a successor on earth,” encompass Adam and his descendants, though the term directly designates Adam himself. This is like His words:

“We have certainly created man in the best form”; and:

“He created man from clay like pottery,”
“And He created the jinn from a smokeless flame of fire”; and:

“He began the creation of man from clay,”
“Then He made his descendants from an extract of a lowly fluid,”
“Then We placed him as a sperm-drop in a secure resting place”—

and other similar passages.

Thus, there was such an affinity between David and Adam that Adam loved David when Allah showed him his descendants. Adam asked about David’s lifespan and was told, “Forty years.” He then gave him sixty years from his own lifespan of a thousand years. This hadith is authentic; al-Tirmidhī and others narrated it, and al-Tirmidhī graded it authentic. For this reason, each of them was tried with the sin through which Allah tested him, just as each of the two

---

## Page 16486

bore a resemblance to the other, for the two desires were of the same kind. Each was raised in rank through the profound repentance by which he attained such love from Allah and such rejoicing on His part. The accounts of their weeping, remorse, and sorrow likewise resemble one another.

A khalīfa (successor) is someone who takes another’s place. The word follows the pattern fa‘īla with the meaning of fā‘ila, an active participle. When travelling, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would say:

“O Allah, You are the Companion on the journey and the One who takes our place in caring for our families.”

He also said:

“Whoever equips a warrior has himself participated in the campaign, and whoever takes good care of his family in his absence has himself participated in the campaign.”

He also said:

“Is it that whenever we go out on a military expedition, one of them stays behind, bleating like a male goat, giving one of the women a portion of milk? If Allah gives me power over any of them, I will surely make an example of him.”

In the Quran:

“Those who were left behind among the Bedouins will say to you.”

And His words:

“Those who were left behind rejoiced in staying behind Allah’s Messenger.”

What is meant by “successor” is that he succeeded the created beings who came before him. Succession entails a correspondence, just as Abū Bakr al-Siddīq was the successor of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, because he succeeded him in leading his community after his death. Similarly, whenever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, travelled for Hajj, ‘umrah, or a military expedition, he would appoint someone to take his place in Medina for a specified period. Sometimes he appointed Ibn Umm Maktūm, and sometimes someone else. During the expedition to Tabūk, he appointed ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib to take his place.

The territories over which the ruler appoints deputies are called makhālīf (administrative districts), such as the districts of Yemen and those of the Hijaz. This usage also occurs in the hadith:

“When he went from one district to another.”

Another instance is Allah Most High’s saying:

“And it is He who has made you successors upon the earth and has raised some of you

---

## Page 16487

above others in rank, that He may test you in what He has given you.”

And His words, Most High:

“We certainly destroyed the generations before you when they did wrong”—through to His words—“Then We made you successors upon the earth.”

Another example is His saying, Most High:

“Allah has promised those among you who believe and do righteous deeds that He will surely grant them succession upon the earth, just as He granted it to those before them, and that He will surely establish for them their religion which He has chosen for them”—to the end of the verse.

Some who have spoken on this matter in error—such as Ibn ‘Arabī—have supposed that the “successor” is someone who acts in Allah’s place, like a deputy of Allah. They claimed that this is what it means for man to be appointed a successor. They sometimes interpret “teaching Adam all the names” in terms of man encompassing the meanings of those names. They also interpret “creating Adam in His image” in this sense.

They adopted the philosophers’ statement that man is the microcosm. This is plausible. But they added to it that Allah is the macrocosm, basing this on their unbelieving doctrine of the unity of existence: that Allah is the very existence of created beings. Thus, among the manifestations, man is the successor who encompasses the names and attributes. From this stem their eventual claims to lordship and divinity, which lead them into Pharaonic, Qarmatian, and esoteric doctrines.

They sometimes treat “messengership” as one rank among others and claim that they are above it. Thus they acknowledge lordship, oneness, divinity, and messengership, yet lapse into Pharaonic doctrine. Such is their faith. Or, in their conduct, they go so far as to become (unrestrained, subject to neither command nor prohibition, neither obligation nor interdiction).

---

## Page 16488

And Allah can have no deputy. Thus, when people said to Abū Bakr, “O deputy of Allah,” he replied, “I am not Allah’s deputy; rather, I am the successor to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. That is enough for me.” Rather, He—glorified be He—may Himself take the place of others. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, You are the Companion on the journey and the Guardian of the family in our absence. O Allah, accompany us on our journey and look after our families in our absence.”

This is because Allah is Ever-Living, Witness over all, Guardian, the Self-Sustaining Sustainer, Watchful, Preserver, and independent of all creation. He has neither partner nor helper, and no one intercedes with Him except by His permission. A deputy serves only when the one he represents is no longer present, through death or absence, and because that person needs someone to take his place. He is called a “successor” because he is left behind when the other departs on a military campaign, standing in his place. All these meanings are inapplicable to Allah Most High, and He is exalted above them.

For He is Ever-Living, the Self-Sustaining Sustainer, Witness over all: He neither dies nor becomes absent. He is Self-Sufficient; He provides and is not provided for. He provides for His servants, grants them victory, guides them, and keeps them safe through the means He has created. These means are themselves His creations and depend on Him just as effects depend on their causes. Allah is the Self-Sufficient, the Praiseworthy. To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens, whatever is on earth, and whatever is between them:

“Everyone in the heavens and the earth asks of Him; every day He is attending to a matter.”

“And it is He who is God in heaven and God on earth.”

No one can succeed Him or take His place, for He has no namesake and no equal. Whoever assigns Him a deputy thus associates a partner with Him. As for the prophetic hadith:

“The ruler is Allah’s shade on earth, in whom every weak and distressed person seeks refuge.”

This is correct, for shade needs someone to seek refuge in it, and it is a companion to him

---

## Page 16489

and corresponds to him in a certain respect. Whoever takes refuge in the shade is sheltered by the one who casts it—the owner of the shade. Thus, the ruler is Allah’s servant, a created being dependent upon Him, unable to do without Him even for the blink of an eye. He possesses a measure of power, authority, protection, support, and other qualities of leadership and being a recourse for others upon which the welfare of creation depends, such that he may be likened to Allah’s shade on earth. He is the most powerful means by which Allah sets right the affairs of His creatures and servants.

When the ruler is upright, people’s affairs are set right; when he is corrupt, they deteriorate in proportion to his corruption. Yet they do not deteriorate in every respect; some benefits necessarily remain, for he is Allah’s shade. Sometimes, however, the shade is complete, shielding against all harm, while at other times it shields against only some harm. But when the shade is absent, affairs fall into ruin, as they would in the absence of the secret of lordship by which human society is sustained. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 16490

He also said—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

Section

Our scholars—such as Judge Abū Ya‘lā and others—reported Imam Ahmad’s views on whether Abū Bakr’s caliphate was established through the Muslims’ selection of him, through an implicit designation by the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), or through an explicit one.

“The first view” is that it was established through selection. This is the position of the majority of scholars, jurists, hadith scholars, and theologians, including the Mu‘tazilah, the Ash‘ariyyah, and others.

“The second view” is that it was established through an implicit designation. This is the position of some groups among hadith scholars and theologians, and it is also reported from al-Hasan al-Basrī. Some adherents of this view maintain that the designation was explicit.

As for the claim of the “Imāmiyyah” that the caliphate was established through an explicit designation of ‘Alī, the claim of the “Jārūdī Zaydiyyah” that it was established through an implicit designation of him, and the claim of the “Rāwandiyyah” that it was established through a designation of al-‘Abbās—these views are manifestly false in the judgment of people of knowledge and religious commitment. Only someone ignorant or unjust would profess them, and many of those who profess them are heretics.

---

## Page 16491

The correct conclusion concerning Abū Bakr’s caliphate—and this is what Ahmad’s statements indicate—is that it was established through the Companions’ selection of him and their pledge of allegiance to him; that the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) foretold it with praise and approval; that he commanded obedience to him and the entrusting of authority to him; and that he directed and guided the Muslim community to pledge allegiance to him.

These three forms—foretelling, commanding, and guiding—are established from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace).

The first is exemplified by his saying:

“I saw myself as though I were at a well, drawing water from it. Then Ibn Abī Quhāfah came and drew a bucketful or two.” The hadith continues.

And his saying:

“It was as though a balance had been lowered from heaven to earth. I was weighed against the Muslim community and outweighed them; then ‘Umar was weighed.” The hadith continues.

And his saying:

“Call your father and your brother for me, so that I may write a document for Abū Bakr over which people will not disagree after me.” Then he said: “Allah and the believers will accept none but Abū Bakr.”

This was his declaration that Allah and the believers would confer the caliphate on none but Abū Bakr, whom he had intended to designate explicitly.

And his saying:

“Tonight a righteous man was shown a vision in which Abū Bakr appeared linked to the Messenger of Allah.” The hadith continues.

And his saying:

“The prophetic caliphate will last thirty years, then become kingship.”

As for commanding, examples include his saying:

“Follow the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

And his saying:

“Adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the upright, rightly guided caliphs after me.”

When the woman asked him, “What if I do not find you?” he replied:

“Then go to Abū Bakr.”

He also said to those handling the alms:

“If you do not find him, give them to Abū Bakr.”

And there are other examples of this kind.

---

## Page 16492

And the third consists in his appointing Abū Bakr to lead the prayer, his saying:

“Close every small doorway into the mosque except Abū Bakr’s,”

and the other distinctions and merits particular to him. These three forms, established by the Sunnah, are also indicated by the Quran.

The first is indicated by His words:

“Allah has promised those of you who believe and do righteous deeds that He will surely make them successors…”

The verse continues. And by His words:

“Allah will bring forth a people whom He loves and who love Him.”

And:

“Allah will reward the grateful.”

The second is indicated by His words:

“You will be called to face a people of great military might; you will fight them, or they will submit…”

The verse continues.

The third is indicated by such passages as His words:

“But the most God-fearing will be spared it.”

And:

“The prophets and the utterly truthful.”

And:

“The first forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers.”

And other passages of this kind.

Thus, the legitimacy of his caliphate and the obligation to obey him are established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and consensus, even though his caliphate came into effect only through consensus and selection. Similarly, if Allah commands that a person be appointed to office, married, or involved in some other arrangement, that matter comes into effect only through the act of appointment or the marriage contract. The scriptural texts indicate that Allah commands that contract and loves it. Thus, the texts indicate that they were commanded to select him and invest him with authority, and that Allah approves of this and loves it.

As for bringing about what is commanded and beloved to Allah, this occurs only through compliance. When they therefore complied with what they had been commanded, they invested him with authority by their own choice. This was better for them and raised them higher in rank.

---

## Page 16493

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Those who follow their own desires hold various views concerning the fighting between Alī and those who fought him.

The Khārijites declare both warring parties to be unbelievers. (*)
