The Rāfidah declare those who fought Alī to be unbelievers, despite the mass-transmitted reports that he treated them as Muslims and forbade declaring them unbelievers. Concerning the fighting involving Talhah, al-Zubayr, and Ā’ishah, they hold three views:

The first is to declare one of the two parties to be transgressors, without specifying which. This is the view of Amr ibn Ubayd and his followers.

The second is to declare those who fought him to be transgressors, except those who repented. They say that Talhah, al-Zubayr, and Ā’ishah repented. This is what follows from the views reported from the majority of them, such as Abū al-Hudhayl and his followers, Abū al-Husayn, and others.

Some people held that he was mistaken in fighting Talhah and al-Zubayr, but not in fighting the people of Syria.

In short, the adherents of religious innovation—the Khārijites, the Rāfidah, the Muʿtazilah, and others like them—regard participation in the fighting as grounds for a judgment of unbelief or transgression.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 245) said:
> The compiler—may Allah have mercy on him—commented in the margin on the wording “fa-tukaffiru al-tā’ifatān al-muqtatilān” (they declare both warring parties unbelievers), saying: “A variant usage of the dual.” He means retaining the alif in the nominative, accusative, and genitive alike. Here, the expression occupies an accusative position: the dual normally takes yā’ in the accusative, whereas it appears here with alif.
> I find it unlikely that this wording originated with the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—because, in this very section, he did not follow that usage. Rather, in three places after this expression, he used the regular genitive form, al-tā’ifatayn, with yā’. Nor is this usage known from him elsewhere. Moreover, even according to this usage, the correct wording would be “al-tā’ifatayn al-muqtatilatān” (the two warring parties), not “al-muqtatilān.” The more likely conclusion, therefore, is that this wording resulted from an alteration by a copyist. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16494

As for the people of the Sunnah, they agree on the uprightness of those involved. However, scholars of our school and others hold differing views as to who was right and who was wrong.

The first view is that Alī alone was right. The second is that all were right. The third is that only one side was right, without specifying which. The fourth is to refrain altogether from discussing the disputes that arose between them, while knowing that Alī and his companions were the closer of the two parties to the truth, as stated in the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd, in which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“A faction will break away at a time of division among the Muslims, and the closer of the two parties to the truth will kill them.”

This concerns the war against the people of Syria. The hadiths indicate that the Battle of the Camel was an episode of civil strife and that refraining from fighting in it was preferable. This is the position expressed in the explicit statements of Ahmad and most of the people of the Sunnah. That verbal and physical conflict lay at the root of what subsequently occurred within the Muslim community in religious and worldly affairs. Let the discerning take a lesson from this. This is the position of the people of the Sunnah and the united community.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Two groups of farmers fought one another, and one defeated the other. The defeated group fled, and several of them were killed after their defeat. Are those killed among the defeated to be judged as destined for the Fire, and do they fall within the Prophet’s saying, peace and blessings be upon him:

“Both the killer and the one killed are in the Fire”?

Or do they not? Does the same ruling apply to someone who flees as to one of them who kills on the battlefield, or not?

---

## Page 16495

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the fleeing man fled with the intention of repenting of unlawful fighting, he is not to be judged as destined for the Fire, for Allah accepts His servants’ repentance and pardons their sins. But if he fled merely because he was unable to fight, and would have killed his opponent had he been able to overcome him, then he is in the Fire, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When two Muslims confront one another with their swords, both the killer and the one killed are in the Fire.” He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, we understand why the killer is, but why the one killed?” He replied, “He intended to kill his companion.”

If the one killed is in the Fire because he intended to kill his companion, then this applies all the more to the one who fled. Both shared the same intention and engaged in the same action, but the one killed suffered harm that the defeated man did not. If that calamity did not expiate the sin of fighting, then the calamity of defeat is even less likely to expiate it. Indeed, the sin of the fleeing man who remains intent on fighting is greater than that of the man killed in battle, and he is more deserving of the Fire: the latter’s evil deeds ceased with his death, whereas the former persists in grave wickedness.

For this reason, a group of jurists held that a fleeing rebel is to be killed if he has a group to which he can retreat and there is reason to fear his return to battle. By contrast, a rebel incapacitated by his wounds is not to be killed. The reason is that the wounded man’s capacity to cause harm has ceased, whereas the fleeing man’s has not.

Furthermore, it may be said of the one killed that the calamity of being killed may lessen his punishment, even though he is among the people of the Fire; and the calamity of defeat is less severe than that of being killed. It is thus clear that the defeated man is worse off than the one killed if he remains intent on killing his brother. As for whoever repents, Allah is indeed All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.

---

## Page 16496

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

Regarding “rebels and Kharijites”: are these synonymous terms with the same meaning, or is there a distinction between them? Does the Sharia distinguish between them in the rulings applicable to them? If someone claims that the leading scholars unanimously agreed that there is no difference between them except in name, while another disputes this, citing the fact that the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī—may Allah be pleased with him—distinguished between the people of Syria and the people of Nahrawān, which of them is correct: the claimant or his opponent?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The claim that the leading scholars unanimously agreed that there is no difference between the two except in name is false, and whoever makes it speaks recklessly. Denying any distinction is merely the position of a group of scholars among the followers of Abū Hanīfa, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others, including many who have written on “fighting rebels.” They may classify Abū Bakr’s fighting against those who withheld zakat, ‘Alī’s fighting against the Kharijites, his fighting against those at the battles of the Camel and Siffīn, and other instances of fighting against those who profess Islam, under the heading of “fighting rebels.”

---

## Page 16497

Nevertheless, they agree that Companions such as Talha, al-Zubayr and others like them are upright, and that it is impermissible to judge them to be unbelievers or morally corrupt. Rather, they exercised independent judgment, whether correctly or mistakenly, and their sins are forgiven. They also state without qualification that rebels are not morally corrupt. If these two groups are treated alike, it follows that the Kharijites and all those who fight them must be equally regarded as people exercising independent judgment who retain their uprightness. For this reason, some have held that rebels are morally corrupt. Ahl al-Sunnah, however, agree on the uprightness of the Companions.

The majority of scholars distinguish between the “Kharijites who break away” and “those who fought at the battles of the Camel and Siffīn,” as well as others counted among rebels acting on an interpretive judgment. This is the position known from the Companions and held by the generality of hadith scholars, jurists and theologians. It is also explicitly stated by most leading scholars and their followers, including the followers of Mālik, Ahmad, al-Shāfi‘ī and others.

This is because it is authentically established that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A group will break away at a time of division among the Muslims. They will be killed by whichever of the two parties is closer to the truth.”

This hadith mentions the three groups and makes clear that those who break away constitute a third group who are not

---

## Page 16498

of the same kind as those two parties; for ‘Alī’s party was closer to the truth than Mu‘āwiya’s party.

Concerning the Kharijites who break away, he said:

“One of you would consider his prayer insignificant compared with theirs, his fasting compared with theirs, and his recitation compared with theirs. They recite the Quran, but it does not pass beyond their throats. They pass out of Islam as an arrow passes through its quarry. Wherever you encounter them, kill them, for whoever kills them will have a reward with Allah on the Day of Resurrection.”

In another wording:

“If those who fight them knew what reward had been promised them through their Prophet, they would desist from good works.”

Muslim narrated the hadiths concerning them in his Sahīh through ten chains of transmission. Al-Bukhārī also narrated this through several chains, as did the compilers of the Sunan and Musnad collections. These reports are widely transmitted from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and have been received with acceptance. The scholars of the Muslim community, from among the Companions and those who followed them, unanimously accepted them, and the Companions agreed on fighting these Kharijites.

As for “those who fought at the battles of the Camel and Siffīn,” a group of the Companions fought on one side, whereas most of the leading Companions fought on neither side. Those who refrained from fighting cited the numerous reports from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—concerning abstaining from fighting during civil strife, and explained that this fighting was indeed civil strife.

‘Alī—may Allah be pleased with him—was glad to fight the Kharijites and would relate the Prophet’s hadith commanding that they be fought. As for the fighting at “Siffīn,” however, he stated that he had no explicit revealed text to support it; rather, it was a judgment he had reached. At times, he would praise those who did not consider fighting appropriate.

---

## Page 16499

It is also established in the Sahīh that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said of al-Hasan:

“This son of mine is a leader, and through him Allah will reconcile two great groups of Muslims.”

He thus praised and commended al-Hasan for the reconciliation Allah would bring about through him between the two parties: the followers of ‘Alī and the followers of Mu‘āwiya. This shows that refraining from fighting was better, and that fighting was neither obligatory nor recommended.

As for “fighting the Kharijites,” it is established that he commanded and urged it. How, then, can one equate what he commanded and urged with something for which he praised and commended those who refrained from it?

Whoever equates the fighting among the Companions at the battles of the Camel and Siffīn with fighting against Dhū al-Khuwaysira al-Tamīmī and others like him—the Kharijites who pass out of Islam and the transgressing Harūriyya—speaks like those guilty of ignorance and manifest injustice. This position necessarily places its advocate alongside the Rāfida and Mu‘tazila, who declare those who fought at the Camel and Siffīn to be disbelievers or flagrant sinners, just as similar judgments are made concerning the Kharijites who pass out of Islam. The early generations and the leading scholars held two well-known positions on whether these Kharijites were disbelievers, while agreeing that the Companions who fought at the Camel and Siffīn should be praised and that one should refrain from discussing the disputes that arose among them. How, then, can the two be compared?

Moreover, “the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded that the Kharijites be fought before they engaged in fighting.” As for “the rebels,” Allah Most High said concerning them:

“And if two groups of believers

---

## Page 16500

fight, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, reconcile them with justice and act equitably. Surely Allah loves those who act equitably.”

Thus, He did not command fighting the transgressing group at the outset. Initiating fighting is not commanded. Rather, if they fight, He commands reconciliation between them; then, if one group transgresses, it is to be fought. Hence some jurists have said that fighting must not be initiated against rebels until they themselves fight.

As for the Kharijites, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said concerning them:

“Wherever you encounter them, kill them, for whoever kills them will have a reward with Allah on the Day of Resurrection.”

He also said:

“If I live to encounter them, I will surely kill them as ‘Ād were killed.”

The same applies to those who withhold zakat: al-Siddīq and the Companions initiated fighting against them. Al-Siddīq said:

“By Allah, if they withheld from me even a young female goat that they used to give to the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—I would fight them over it.”

They are to be fought if they refuse to fulfil their obligations, even if they acknowledge that these are obligatory. The jurists then differed over whether someone who withholds both, and whom the ruler fights on that account, is a disbeliever despite acknowledging their obligatory nature. They held two positions, both reported from Ahmad, just as two positions are reported from him concerning whether the Kharijites are disbelievers.

As for those whose offence is rebellion alone, they do not become disbelievers, according to the consensus of the leading religious scholars. The Quran explicitly affirms their faith and brotherhood despite the fighting and transgression. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16501

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What punishment is someone who curses Mu‘āwiyah liable to? Did the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—say these hadiths: “If two caliphs fight one another, one of them is accursed,” and “‘Ammār will be killed by the transgressing group”—and it was Mu‘āwiyah’s army that killed him? Did they revile the Prophet’s household? Or did al-Hajjāj kill a man of noble lineage?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Whoever curses any of the Companions of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—such as Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and others like them; or those superior to them, such as Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī, Abū Hurayrah, and others like them; or those superior to these, such as Talhah, al-Zubayr, ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, ‘Umar, or ‘Ā’ishah, the Mother of the Believers, and the other Companions of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—deserves severe punishment, according to the consensus of the leading religious scholars.

The scholars differed over whether such a person should be punished by death or by a lesser punishment, as we have explained at length elsewhere.

It is established in the two Sahīh collections, on the authority of Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose hand is my soul, if one of you were to spend the equivalent of

---

## Page 16502

Mount Uhud in gold, it would not equal a mudd given by one of them, nor even half of it.” Cursing is more grievous than reviling.

It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Cursing a believer is like killing him.”

Thus, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—likened cursing a believer to killing him. The Companions of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—are the best of the believers, as is established in his saying:

“The best generation is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

Everyone who saw Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—while believing in him has a share of companionship commensurate with that. It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“An army will go on campaign, and it will be asked, ‘Is there anyone among you who accompanied Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ They will say, ‘Yes,’ and victory will be granted to them. Then an army will go on campaign, and it will be asked, ‘Is there anyone among you who saw Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace?’ They will say, ‘Yes,’ and victory will be granted to them.” He also mentioned the third generation.

Thus, he linked this outcome to seeing Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—just as he linked it to accompanying him. Since the term “companionship” has both a general and a particular sense, a Companion distinguished from others by a particular kind of companionship may be described as possessing that companionship, to the exclusion of those who did not share it.

In the previously mentioned hadith of Abū Sa‘īd, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said to Khālid ibn al-Walīd, when he and ‘Abd al-Rahmān had a dispute:

“O Khālid, do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose hand is my soul, if one of you were to spend the equivalent of Mount Uhud in gold, it would not equal a mudd given by one of them, nor even half of it.”

For ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf and others like him were among the earliest pioneers who spent before the victory—the victory of al-Hudaybiyah. Khālid ibn al-Walīd and others who embraced Islam after al-Hudaybiyah, and who spent and fought, were lower in rank than those earlier Companions. Allah Most High says:

“Not equal are those among you who spent before the victory

---

## Page 16503

and fought. They are greater in rank than those who spent and fought afterwards. Yet Allah has promised the finest reward to all.”

The “victory” here means the victory of al-Hudaybiyah, when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—received his Companions’ pledge beneath the tree. Those who pledged allegiance to him numbered more than 1,400, and they were the ones who conquered Khaybar. It is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“No one who pledged allegiance beneath the tree will enter the Fire.”

Allah revealed Sūrat al-Fath, which mentions this, before Mecca was conquered—indeed, before the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed ‘umrah. He had received his Companions’ pledge beneath the tree in the year of al-Hudaybiyah, in 6 AH, and concluded the well-known Treaty of al-Hudaybiyah with the polytheists. Through that treaty, victories were achieved whose extent Allah alone knows, although many Muslims had disliked it and had not understood its favourable outcome. Sahl ibn Hunayf even said:

“O people, distrust your own judgment! I remember how I was on the day of Abū Jandal: had I been able to reject the decision of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—I would have done so.”

This was narrated by al-Bukhārī and others. The following year, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—performed ‘umrah. He and those accompanying him entered Mecca to perform it, while the people of Mecca were still on the side of the polytheists. Then, in 8 AH, he conquered Mecca during the month of Ramadan.

Allah had revealed in Sūrat al-Fath:

“You will surely enter the Sacred Mosque, if Allah wills, in safety, with your heads shaved or your hair shortened, without fear. He knew what you did not know, and granted you a near victory before that.”

Thus, in Sūrat al-Fath, He promised them that they would enter Mecca in safety, and He fulfilled His promise in

---

## Page 16504

the following year. Concerning this, He revealed:

“A sacred month for a sacred month, and violations of sanctity are subject to retribution.”

All of this preceded the conquest of Mecca. Whoever supposes that Sūrat al-Fath was revealed after the conquest of Mecca is plainly mistaken.

The point is that those who accompanied him before the victory enjoyed a distinction in companionship that entitled them to precedence over those who came afterwards. He even said to Khālid:

“Do not revile my Companions.”

For they had accompanied him before Khālid and others like him did. Abū Bakr al-Siddīq—may Allah be pleased with him—enjoyed a distinction in companionship that set him apart from all the other Companions. Accordingly, the Prophet singled him out in the authentic hadith narrated by al-Bukhārī from Abū al-Dardā’:

“Abū Bakr and ‘Umar had an exchange of words. Abū Bakr asked ‘Umar to seek forgiveness for him, but ‘Umar refused. Abū Bakr came to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and told him what had happened. Then ‘Umar regretted it and went looking for Abū Bakr at his house. He was told that Abū Bakr was with the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When ‘Umar arrived, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—became angry on Abū Bakr’s behalf and said: ‘O people, I came to you and said, “I am Allah’s Messenger to you.” You said, “You are lying,” whereas Abū Bakr said, “You speak the truth.” Will you then leave my companion alone for my sake? Will you then leave my companion alone for my sake?’”

No one mistreated him after that. Here, he singled him out by calling him his companion, just as the Quran singled him out in the words of Allah Most High:

---

## Page 16505

“One of two, when they were in the cave, when he said to his companion, ‘Do not grieve; Allah is with us.’”

In the two Sahīhs, Abū Sa‘īd reported that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah gave a servant the choice between this world and the Hereafter, and that servant chose what is with Allah.”

Abū Bakr wept and said, “Rather, we would ransom you with our lives and our wealth!”

He continued: “The people were astonished: the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had spoken of a servant whom Allah had given the choice between this world and the Hereafter. Yet it was Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who had been given that choice, and Abū Bakr understood this better than any of us. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

‘No one has been more generous to us in companionship and wealth than Abū Bakr. Were I to take anyone on earth as an intimate friend, I would take Abū Bakr as an intimate friend. But he is my brother and companion. Seal every small doorway into the mosque except Abū Bakr’s.’”

This is among the most authentic hadiths, by the agreement of scholars versed in the Prophet’s sayings, deeds, and circumstances—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The point is that companionship has both a specific and a general sense. Its general sense includes everyone who saw him while believing in him. Thus, one may say, “I accompanied him for a year,” “for a month,” “for an hour,” and so forth.

Mu‘āwiyah, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and others like them were believers; none of the early generations accused them of hypocrisy. Indeed, it is established in an authentic hadith that when ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās pledged allegiance to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—he said:

“On condition that my past sins be forgiven.”

He replied:

---

## Page 16506

“O ‘Amr, do you not know that Islam wipes away what came before it?”

It is well known that the Islam which wipes away past sins is that of believers, not that of hypocrites. Moreover, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās and others like him who emigrated to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—after al-Hudaybiyah left their homelands voluntarily, not under compulsion. There were no hypocrites among the Emigrants. Hypocrisy was found only among some of those from the Ansār who entered Islam. The Ansār were the people of Medina; when their leading men and the majority of their people embraced Islam, the rest found it necessary to profess Islam hypocritically, because Islam had gained strength and ascendancy among their people.

As for the people of Mecca, their leading men and the majority of their people were unbelievers. Thus, no one openly professed faith unless he was a believer both outwardly and inwardly, for anyone who openly professed Islam was mistreated and shunned. A hypocrite professes Islam only to further his worldly interests, whereas anyone who openly professed Islam in Mecca suffered in his worldly affairs.

Then, when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—emigrated to Medina, most believers emigrated with him. Some, however, were prevented from emigrating to join him. Among those prevented were men of Banū Makhzūm, such as al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, Khālid’s brother and Abū Jahl’s maternal half-brother. For this reason:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would supplicate for these men in prayer, saying: ‘O Allah, deliver al-Walīd ibn al-Walīd, Salamah ibn Hishām, and the oppressed believers. O Allah, punish Mudar severely, and afflict them with years of famine like those in Joseph’s time.’”

Among the Emigrants, from the first to the last, there was no one whom anyone accused of hypocrisy. Rather, they were all believers whose faith was attested.

“Cursing a believer is like killing him.”

---

## Page 16507

As for “Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān and others like him” among those granted amnesty who embraced Islam after the conquest of Mecca—such as ‘Ikrimah ibn Abī Jahl, al-Hārith ibn Hishām, Suhayl ibn ‘Amr, Safwān ibn Umayyah, and Abū Sufyān ibn al-Hārith ibn ‘Abd al-Muttalib—these and others became sincere and upright Muslims, by the agreement of the Muslims. None of them was subsequently accused of hypocrisy.

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—employed Mu‘āwiyah as a scribe and said: ‘O Allah, teach him writing and arithmetic, and protect him from punishment.’”

His brother Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān was better and more virtuous than he was. He was one of the commanders whom Abū Bakr al-Siddīq—may Allah be pleased with him—sent to conquer Syria. Abū Bakr gave him well-known instructions while walking alongside him as Yazīd rode. Yazīd said to him, “O successor to the Messenger of Allah, either mount or let me dismount.” He replied, “I shall not mount, nor shall you dismount. I seek Allah’s reward for the steps I take in His cause.”

Another commander was ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās; the third was Shurahbīl ibn Hasanah, and the fourth was Khālid ibn al-Walīd, who was their commander-in-chief. Then ‘Umar dismissed him and appointed Abū ‘Ubaydah ‘Āmir ibn al-Jarrāh, whom the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—attested to be the trustworthy man of this community, as established in an authentic hadith. Thus, Syria was conquered under Abū ‘Ubaydah, and Iraq under Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās.

Then, when Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān died during ‘Umar’s caliphate, ‘Umar appointed his brother Mu‘āwiyah in his place. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb was among the most discerning of men, the most knowledgeable about people’s character, and the most steadfast

---

## Page 16508

in upholding the truth, and the most knowledgeable of it. Indeed, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with him—said, “We used to say that tranquillity spoke through ‘Umar’s tongue.” The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah has firmly established the truth upon ‘Umar’s tongue and in his heart.”

He also said:

“Had I not been sent among you, ‘Umar would have been sent among you.”

Ibn ‘Umar said, “I never heard ‘Umar say concerning something, ‘I think it is such and such,’ without it turning out as he thought.” The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—also said to him:

“Satan has never seen you taking a path without taking a path other than yours.”

Neither ‘Umar nor, indeed, Abū Bakr ever appointed a hypocrite to authority over the Muslims. Neither appointed any of his own relatives, nor did either fear anyone’s reproach in Allah’s cause. Rather, after fighting the apostates and bringing them back to Islam, they prohibited them from riding horses and bearing arms until the sincerity of their repentance became apparent.

‘Umar would say to Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās, while he was governor of Iraq, “Do not appoint any of them to authority, nor consult them about warfare.” For they had been prominent leaders, such as Tulayhah al-Asadī, al-Aqra‘ ibn Hābis, ‘Uyaynah ibn Hisn, al-Ash‘ath ibn Qays al-Kindī, and others like them. When Abū Bakr and ‘Umar feared some degree of hypocrisy in these men, they did not appoint them to authority over the Muslims.

Thus, had ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, Mu‘āwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān, and others like them been among those in whom hypocrisy was feared, they would not have been appointed to authority over the Muslims. Indeed, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—appointed ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās as commander on the expedition of Dhāt al-Salāsil, and the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—never appointed a hypocrite to authority over the Muslims.

“He also appointed Abū Sufyān ibn Harb, Mu‘āwiyah’s father, as governor of Najrān, and the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—died while Abū Sufyān

---

## Page 16509

was his deputy in Najrān.” The Muslims have agreed that Mu‘āwiyah’s Islam was better than that of his father, Abū Sufyān. How, then, could these men be hypocrites when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—entrusted them with the Muslims’ affairs in matters of knowledge and practice?

It is well known that conflicts occurred between Mu‘āwiyah, ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās, and others. Yet none of their allies, those who fought them, or those who did not fight them accused them of lying about the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, all the scholars among the Companions and the succeeding generation agreed that these men were truthful in reporting from the Messenger of Allah and trustworthy in transmitting his words. A hypocrite, by contrast, cannot be trusted in reporting from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, he both lies about him and disbelieves him.

If they were believers who loved Allah and His Messenger, then whoever curses them has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger. It is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī, in substance, that:

“A man nicknamed Himār (‘Donkey’) used to drink wine. Whenever he drank, he was brought before the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who would flog him. On one occasion, when he was brought before him, a man said, ‘May Allah curse him! How often he is brought before the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace!’ The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Do not curse him, for he loves Allah and His Messenger.’”

Every believer loves Allah and His Messenger, and whoever does not love Allah and His Messenger is not a believer, although believers differ in their degree of faith and in the love and other qualities it encompasses.

This is so even though he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“cursed wine, those who press it, those who have it pressed, those who drink it, those who serve it, those who carry it, those to whom it is carried, and those who consume its proceeds.” Yet he forbade cursing this particular individual, because cursing falls under the category of “threats of punishment”; such a judgment is therefore applied

---

## Page 16510

in general. As for a particular individual, the threatened punishment may be lifted because of sincere repentance, good deeds that erase sins, afflictions that expiate them, accepted intercession, or other causes whose harm lifts punishment from the sinner. This concerns someone whose sin is established.

Likewise, Hātib ibn Abī Balta‘ah did what he did, and he used to mistreat his slaves. Indeed, it is established in the Sahīh that his slave said, “O Messenger of Allah, by Allah, Hātib ibn Abī Balta‘ah will certainly enter the Fire!” He replied, “You are wrong. He was present at Badr and al-Hudaybiyah.”

It is also reported in the Sahīh, from ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—sent him and al-Zubayr ibn al-‘Awwām, telling them, “Go to Rawdat Khākh. There you will find a woman carrying a letter.”

‘Alī said, “We set off, our horses racing along with us, until we encountered the woman. We asked, ‘Where is the letter?’ She replied, ‘I have no letter.’ We told her, ‘You will either produce the letter, or we will strip off your clothes.’” He said, “She then took it out of her braids. We brought it to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and found that it was a letter from Hātib to some of the polytheists in Mecca, informing them of some of the Prophet’s affairs—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—asked, ‘What is this, Hātib?’ He replied, ‘By Allah, O Messenger of Allah, I did not do this because I had renounced my religion or accepted disbelief after embracing Islam. Rather, I was affiliated with Quraysh but was not one of them by descent. The Muslims with you had relatives through whom they could protect their families in Mecca. Since I lacked such ties among them, I wanted to earn their favour so that they would protect my relatives.’”

In another wording: “And I knew that this would not harm you”—meaning that Allah grants victory to His Messenger and those who believe.

‘Umar said, “Let me

---

## Page 16511

strike off this hypocrite’s head.” The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, “He was present at Badr. And how do you know? Allah may well have looked upon the people of Badr and said to them, ‘Do whatever you wish, for I have forgiven you.’”

Allah thus forgave him this grave misdeed because he had participated in Badr. This shows that Allah may forgive a grave misdeed on account of a great good deed. The believers believe in both the promise of reward and the threat of punishment, accepting the Prophet’s saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

“Whoever’s last words are ‘There is no god but Allah’ will enter Paradise,”

and similar statements, alongside Allah’s words:

“Indeed, those who unjustly consume the property of orphans are only consuming fire into their bellies, and they will burn in a blazing Fire.”

For this reason, no particular individual is declared to be destined for Paradise without specific evidence, nor is any particular individual declared to be destined for the Fire without specific evidence. Neither declaration may be made merely on the assumption that someone falls within the general statements, for a person may fall within both categories and thus deserve both reward and punishment. Allah Most High says:

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it,
and whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.”

When a servant has both bad deeds and good deeds, although he deserves punishment for his bad deeds, Allah rewards him for his good deeds. He does not nullify a believer’s good deeds because of the sins he has committed. Only the Khawārij and the Muʿtazilah maintain that a major sin nullifies all good deeds. They hold that those who commit major sins will remain in the Fire forever, that they will not leave it through intercession or any other means, and that no faith whatsoever remains in someone who commits a major sin. These are false views that contradict the Book, the mass-transmitted Sunnah, and the consensus of the Companions.

---

## Page 16512

All the people of the Sunnah and the community, together with the leading scholars of the religion, hold that none of the Companions, the Prophet’s relatives, the earliest believers, or anyone else is infallible. Rather, they acknowledge that these people may commit sins. Allah Most High forgives them through repentance and raises their ranks thereby. He also forgives them through good deeds that erase sins, or through other means. The Most High says:

“Those who bring the truth and believe in it—they are the righteous.
They will have whatever they wish with their Lord. That is the reward of those who do good,
so that Allah may absolve them of the worst of what they did and reward them according to the best of their deeds.”

The Most High also says:

“Until, when he reaches full maturity and reaches forty years, he says, ‘My Lord, inspire me to be grateful for Your favour which You have bestowed upon me and upon my parents, and to do righteous deeds that please You. Make my offspring righteous for me. Indeed, I have repented to You, and I am one of those who submit to You.’
Those are the ones from whom We accept the best of what they did and whose misdeeds We overlook, among the people of Paradise.”

It is the prophets, however—may Allah Most High be pleased with them all—whom the scholars describe as protected from persisting in sin. As for the foremost in truthfulness, the martyrs, and the righteous, they are not infallible. This concerns actual sins.

As for matters in which they exercise independent scholarly judgment, sometimes they are right and sometimes they are mistaken. When they exercise such judgment and reach the correct conclusion, they receive two rewards. When they exercise it and are mistaken, they receive one reward for their effort, and their error is forgiven.

Those who are astray regard error and sin as inseparable. At times they go to excess in venerating these people, saying that they are infallible; at other times they treat them harshly, saying that their errors make them transgressors. People of knowledge and faith are not infallible, nor are they deemed sinful for their errors.

---

## Page 16513

From this arise many sects of innovation and misguidance. One group reviled and cursed the early generations because they believed that they had committed sins and that whoever committed such sins deserved to be cursed. They might even declare them sinful or unbelievers, as did the Khawārij, who declared ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, and their supporters unbelievers, cursed and reviled them, and considered it lawful to fight them. These are the people of whom the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Any one of you would consider his prayer insignificant compared with theirs, his fasting compared with theirs, and his recitation compared with theirs. They recite the Quran, but it does not go beyond their throats. They pass out of Islam as an arrow passes through a hunted animal.”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A group will break away at a time of division among the Muslims, and the more deserving of the two parties will fight them for the sake of the truth.”

These were the renegades who broke away from the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, and declared everyone who supported him an unbeliever. The believers had divided into two parties: one with ʿAlī and the other with Muʿāwiyah. These renegades fought ʿAlī and his companions, and events unfolded just as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had foretold. Likewise, it is authentically reported from him in the Sahīh that he said of his son al-Hasan:

“This son of mine is a leader, and through him Allah will reconcile two great parties of Muslims.”

Allah thus reconciled the supporters of ʿAlī and the supporters of Muʿāwiyah through him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praised al-Hasan for this reconciliation, which he brought about, and called him a leader on that account, because what al-Hasan did was loved and approved by Allah and His Messenger. Had the fighting that took place among the Muslims been what Allah and His Messenger commanded, this would not have been the case. Rather, al-Hasan would have abandoned what was obligatory, or what was more beloved to

---

## Page 16514

Allah. This authentic, explicit text makes clear that what al-Hasan did was praiseworthy and pleasing to Allah and His Messenger. It is also established in the Sahīh that:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would seat him on his thigh and also seat Usāmah ibn Zayd, saying: ‘O Allah, I love them both, and I love those who love them.’”

The effects of his love and supplication, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were also evident in this: both were most eager to achieve that for which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praised al-Hasan, and most averse to anything contrary to it. This also makes clear that, in the Prophet’s estimation, those killed at Siffīn did not have the same standing as the renegade Khawārij whom he commanded people to fight. Rather, he praised reconciliation between these parties and did not command that they be fought.

For this reason, the Companions and the leading scholars agreed on fighting the renegade Khawārij. ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, openly expressed his joy at fighting them, and his transmission of the Prophet’s command to fight them is well known. As for the fighting among the Companions, no report concerning it was transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. ʿAlī showed no joy in it; rather, he showed grief, wished that it had not happened, and expressed gratitude to some of the Companions. He also declared both parties free of unbelief and hypocrisy and permitted prayers for mercy upon those killed on either side. These and similar matters establish the agreement of ʿAlī and the other Companions that each of the two parties consisted of believers.

The Quran itself testifies that fighting among believers does not take them outside the faith. Allah Most High says:

“If two groups of believers fight, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against

---

## Page 16515

the other, then fight the group that transgresses until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, make peace between them with justice and act equitably. Surely Allah loves those who act equitably.”

“The believers are but brothers, so make peace between your brothers, and fear Allah so that you may receive mercy.”

Thus He called them “believers” and made them “brothers,” despite their fighting and transgression. As for the aforementioned hadith, “If two caliphs fight, one of them is cursed,” it is a fabricated lie. No scholar of hadith has transmitted it, nor does it appear in any of the authoritative Islamic collections.

Mu‘āwiyah did not claim the caliphate, nor was allegiance pledged to him as caliph when he fought Alī. He did not fight on the grounds that he was caliph, or that he was entitled to the caliphate and should be acknowledged as such. Mu‘āwiyah himself acknowledged this to anyone who asked him about it. Neither Mu‘āwiyah nor his companions believed that they should initiate fighting against Alī and his companions or seek ascendancy.

Rather, Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, and his companions held that the others were obliged to obey him and pledge allegiance to him, since the Muslims could have only one caliph. They considered them to be withholding obedience to him and refusing to fulfil this obligation, while possessing armed strength. Alī therefore considered that he should fight them until they fulfilled this obligation, thereby bringing about obedience and unity.

They, however, said that this was not obligatory upon them and that, if they were fought over it, they would be wronged. They said: “For Uthmān was killed unjustly, as all Muslims agree, and his killers are in Alī’s army, where they hold the upper hand

---

## Page 16516

and wield armed strength. If we refuse, they will wrong us and attack us. Alī cannot restrain them, just as he could not defend Uthmān. We are only obliged to pledge allegiance to a caliph who can secure justice for us and treat us fairly.”

Among the ignorant on both sides were people who harboured false suspicions about Alī and Uthmān—accusations of which Allah has exonerated them both. Alī was suspected of having ordered Uthmān’s killing. Alī would swear—though he was righteous and truthful even without an oath—that he had neither killed him, nor approved of his killing, nor colluded in it. This is known with certainty concerning Alī, may Allah be pleased with him.

Some who loved Alī, and some who hated him, spread this claim about him. Those who loved him intended thereby to disparage Uthmān, claiming that he had deserved to be killed and that Alī had ordered his killing. Those who hated him intended thereby to disparage Alī, claiming that he had helped kill the wronged and martyred caliph, who bore his ordeal patiently, did not defend himself, and shed no Muslim’s blood in his own defence—let alone in seeking obedience to himself. These and similar matters are used by those who deviate against the partisans of Uthmān and Alī.

Nevertheless, every group of these partisans acknowledges that Mu‘āwiyah is not Alī’s equal in entitlement to the caliphate, and that he may not be caliph when it is possible to appoint Alī, may Allah be pleased with him. For Alī’s excellence, his precedence in Islam, his knowledge, his religious devotion, his courage and all his other virtues were clear and well known to them, as were the virtues of his brothers Abū Bakr, Umar, Uthmān and others, may Allah be pleased with them.

---

## Page 16517

And none of the members of the consultative council remained except him and Sa‘d. Sa‘d had withdrawn from the matter, and the choice had been narrowed down to Uthmān and Alī. When Uthmān died, no candidate for the caliphate remained except Alī, may Allah be pleased with him. The evil arose only because of Uthmān’s killing, which strengthened the people of injustice and aggression and weakened the people of knowledge and faith. This brought about such division and discord that people were obeyed when others were more entitled to obedience. This is why Allah commanded unity and concord and prohibited division and discord.

Hence the saying:
“What they dislike in unity is better than what they gain through division.”

As for the hadith which states:
“Ammār will be killed by the transgressing group,”

some scholars have challenged this hadith. Nevertheless, Muslim narrated it in his Sahīh, and it appears in some copies of al-Bukhārī’s collection. Some have interpreted “the transgressing group” as meaning the group seeking vengeance for Uthmān’s blood, citing the words:
“We seek vengeance for Ibn Affān with the points of our spears.”

This interpretation has no merit. Rather, we affirm that whatever the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said is true, just as he said it. The fact that Ammār would be killed by the transgressing group does not contradict what we have stated, for Allah Most High said:

“If two groups of believers fight, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the one that transgresses until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, make peace between them with justice and be fair. Indeed, Allah loves those who are fair.”

“The believers are but brothers, so make peace between your two brothers.”

Thus, despite their fighting and transgression, He described them as believers and brothers. Indeed, even while commanding that the transgressing group be fought, He described them as believers. Nor does everything that amounts to

---

## Page 16518

transgression, wrongdoing, or aggression take people in general outside the fold of faith, nor does it warrant their being cursed. How, then, could it take those who belonged to the best generations outside the faith? Anyone who transgresses, commits injustice or aggression, or perpetrates a sin falls into one of two categories: those acting on an interpretation and those who are not.

Those who exercise scholarly judgment and act on an interpretation include people of knowledge and piety who examined matters and differed over their permissibility: some considered certain things lawful, while others considered them forbidden. Thus, some deemed certain kinds of drink permissible; others, certain transactions involving usury; and others, certain contracts for marriages intended to make a woman lawful again for her former husband, or for temporary marriages, and similar matters. Such things occurred among the finest of the early generations. The most that can be said of those who exercise scholarly judgment and act on such interpretations is that they are mistaken.

Allah Most High said:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake.”

It is established in the Sahīh that Allah answered this supplication. He, glorified be He, has told us that David and Solomon, peace be upon them, judged concerning the field. He singled out one of them for knowledge and judgment, while praising both for their knowledge and judgment.

Scholars are the heirs of the prophets. If one of them understands something about an issue that another does not, this is no cause for blame, nor does it detract from his recognized knowledge and piety. Yet committing the act in question with knowledge of its ruling would constitute sin and injustice, and persisting in it would constitute grave disobedience. Indeed, when its prohibition is necessarily known, declaring it lawful constitutes disbelief. Transgression belongs to this same category.

---

## Page 16519

But if the transgressor exercises scholarly judgment and acts on an interpretation, without realizing that he is transgressing, but instead believes himself to be right—even though that belief is mistaken—calling him a “transgressor” does not establish that he has sinned, much less that he is defiantly disobedient. Those who hold that transgressors acting on an interpretation should be fought say: “Although we are commanded to fight them, we fight them to ward off the harm of their transgression, not to punish them, but to prevent aggression.”

They maintain that such people retain their moral uprightness and are not deemed defiantly disobedient. They say that they are like those who are not legally accountable: children, the insane, the forgetful, the unconscious, and the sleeping are restrained from aggression so that they do not commit it. Indeed, even animals are restrained from aggression.

The explicit text of the Quran requires anyone who kills a believer by mistake to pay blood money, although he incurs no sin thereby. The same applies to someone liable to a prescribed punishment who is brought before the ruler and repents after being apprehended: the ruler still carries out the prescribed punishment upon him, although one who repents of a sin is like one who has no sin. A transgressor acting on an interpretation is subject to flogging according to Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. There are numerous comparable cases.

Even assuming that the “transgression” is not based on an interpretation, it is a sin, and the punishment for sins can be removed through various means: good deeds that erase sins, afflictions that expiate them, and other means.

Furthermore, the statement, “Indeed, ‘Ammār will be killed by the transgressing faction,” does not explicitly state that this wording refers to Mu‘āwiyah and his companions. Rather, it may refer to the band that charged at him and killed him, which was one group within the army. Whoever approved of ‘Ammār’s killing would come under the same ruling as that group. It is well known that

---

## Page 16520

there were men in the army who did not approve of ‘Ammār’s killing, such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās and others. Indeed, everyone condemned ‘Ammār’s killing, even Mu‘āwiyah and ‘Amr. It is related that Mu‘āwiyah interpreted the matter as meaning that the one who killed him was the one who brought him, not those who fought him; and that ‘Alī refuted this interpretation by saying, “Then we must have killed Hamzah.”

There is no doubt that what ‘Alī said was correct. Yet anyone who examines the statements of scholars engaged in debate, with neither warfare nor a struggle for power between them, will find that some of their interpretations of the texts are far weaker than Mu‘āwiyah’s. Whoever adopted this interpretation did not consider himself to have killed ‘Ammār and therefore did not believe himself to be a transgressor. Someone who does not believe himself to be a transgressor, though he actually is, is mistaken in his interpretation.

None of the jurists holds that one should fight alongside those who killed ‘Ammār. Rather, they hold two well-known views, both of which were held by leading Companions: some maintain that one should fight alongside ‘Ammār and his faction, while others maintain that one should refrain from fighting altogether. Both groups included a number of the earliest and foremost Companions. Those who held the first view included ‘Ammār, Sahl ibn Hunayf, and Abū Ayyūb. Those who held the second included Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās, Muhammad ibn Maslamah, Usāmah ibn Zayd, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, and others like them. Perhaps most of the leading Companions held this latter view. Apart from ‘Alī, no one in either army was superior to Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās, yet he was among those who abstained from fighting.

---

## Page 16521

The “hadith of ‘Ammār” may be cited by those who consider fighting warranted, since, if those who killed him were transgressors, Allah says:

“Then fight against the group that transgresses.”

Those who hold the other position cite authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to the effect that “abstaining from civil strife is better than fighting in it.” They maintain that this fighting and others like it constitute fighting amid civil strife, as authentic hadiths make clear. They also maintain that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, neither commanded this fighting nor approved of it; rather, he approved of reconciliation. Allah commanded fighting the transgressor, but He did not command fighting him at the outset. Rather, He said:

“If two groups of believers fight one another, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight against the group that transgresses until it returns to the command of Allah. If it returns, make peace between them with justice and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably.”

They say: Allah did not command the initial fighting, nor did He command everyone subjected to transgression to fight whoever transgressed against him. For if someone killed every transgressor, he would fall into disbelief. Indeed, most believers—indeed, most people—are not free of wrongdoing and transgression. But when two groups of believers fight one another, reconciliation between them is obligatory, even if neither was commanded to fight. If one group then transgresses, it is to be fought because it has neither ceased fighting nor agreed to reconciliation, so its harm cannot be repelled except by fighting. Fighting it thus becomes like fighting an assailant whose wrongdoing against others cannot be repelled except by fighting, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever is killed defending his property is a martyr; whoever is killed defending his life is a martyr; whoever is killed defending his religion is a martyr; and whoever is killed defending his honour is a martyr.”

They say: Even assuming that the entire army consisted of transgressors, we were not commanded to fight them at the outset; rather, we were commanded to bring about reconciliation

---

## Page 16522

between them. Moreover, it is not permissible to fight them if those with them are unwilling to fight, for they frequently opposed him and showed little obedience to him.

The point is that this hadith does not permit cursing any of the Companions, nor does it establish that any of them is an evildoer. As for the Prophet’s household, they were never reviled, praise be to Allah. Al-Hajjāj did not kill anyone from Banū Hāshim; rather, he killed men from the Arab nobility. He had married a daughter of ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ja‘far, but Banū ‘Abd Manāf, Banū Hāshim and Banū Umayyah disapproved of this and brought about their separation, since they did not consider him a suitable match for her. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is Allah’s ruling concerning outbreaks of civil strife among righteous people and similar cases, in which they kill one another and violate one another’s sanctity?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Such outbreaks of civil strife and the like are among the gravest prohibitions and the greatest evils. Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, fear Allah as He should be feared, and do not die except as Muslims.”

“And hold firmly to the rope of Allah all together and do not become divided. And remember the favour of Allah

---

## Page 16523

upon you: when you were enemies, He brought your hearts together, and by His favour you became brothers. You were on the brink of a pit of Fire, and He saved you from it. Thus Allah makes His signs clear to you, so that you may be guided.”

“Let there be among you a group who call to goodness, enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong. It is they who will prosper.”

“And do not be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them. They will suffer a tremendous punishment.”

“On the Day when some faces will turn white and others will turn black. As for those whose faces turn black: ‘Did you disbelieve after having believed? Then taste the punishment for your disbelief.’”

These people became divided and differed until they committed the disbelief they did. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Do not revert after me to being disbelievers, striking one another’s necks.”

This is a form of disbelief, although a Muslim does not become a disbeliever by committing a sin. Allah Most High says:

“If two groups of believers fight one another, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, reconcile them with justice and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably. The believers are but brothers, so make peace between your brothers, and fear Allah so that you may receive mercy.”

This is Allah’s ruling concerning believers who fight one another. He declares that they are brothers and first commands that peace be made between them when they fight. Then, “if one of them transgresses against the other,” and they refuse reconciliation, “fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, reconcile them with justice.”

He thus commands that they be reconciled with justice after the transgressing group “returns to Allah’s command”—that is, comes back to obeying Allah’s command. Whoever returns to Allah’s command must be treated justly in relation to his adversary, and equity must be observed between them. Thus, after the two groups have fought, but before we fight the transgressing group, He commands us to reconcile them without qualification, because neither group has been subdued through fighting.

---

## Page 16524

Since this is so, efforts must be made to reconcile these two groups in the manner commanded by Allah and His Messenger. Each group should be asked, “What grievance do you have against the other?” If it is established that one group has wronged the other by causing loss of life or destruction of property, it must compensate for the losses it has caused. If each has inflicted losses upon the other, their respective claims should be offset against one another, as Allah Most High says:

“Prescribed for you is retribution for those killed: the free for the free, the slave for the slave, and the female for the female.”

A number of the early generations mentioned that this verse was revealed concerning a similar situation involving two groups that had fought one another, whom Allah commanded to offset their respective claims. He says:

“If anything remains due to someone from his brother…”

The word ‘afw means a surplus. Thus, if a balance remains due to one group from the other,

“then let it be pursued fairly.”

The party that owes it must pay it graciously. If one group is unable to compensate the other, a man may assume a financial liability and pay it to restore peaceful relations. He may subsequently obtain that amount from the Muslims’ zakat and ask people to help him in this situation, even if he is wealthy.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to Qabīsa ibn Mukhāriq al-Hilālī:

“O Qabīsa, asking for financial assistance is permissible only for three: a man whose wealth has been swept away by a calamity—he may ask until he has enough to meet his basic needs, then he must refrain; a man stricken by poverty, when three people of sound judgment from among his people stand and testify, ‘So-and-so has been stricken by poverty’—he may ask until he has enough to sustain himself and meet his basic needs, then he must refrain; and a man who assumes such a financial liability—he may ask until he obtains the amount needed to discharge it, then he must refrain.”

Every Muslim who is able must strive to reconcile them and, as far as possible, urge them to do what Allah has commanded.

---

## Page 16525

Whoever in either group believes that he has been wronged and subjected to aggression—if he is patient and pardons, Allah will honor him and grant him victory. As recorded in the Sahīh, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah only increases a servant in honor for pardoning others. No one humbles himself for Allah except that Allah raises him, and charity does not diminish wealth.”

Allah Most High says:

“The recompense for an evil deed is its equivalent, but whoever pardons and makes reconciliation—his reward is with Allah.”

He also says:

“Blame falls only on those who wrong people and commit aggression in the land without right. Those will have a painful punishment.”

“But whoever is patient and forgives—surely this is a matter requiring firm resolve.”

Allah exacts retribution from the unjust aggressor in this world and the Hereafter, for aggression brings about his downfall. Ibn Mas'ūd said:

“Even if one mountain were to commit aggression against another, Allah would level the aggressor to the ground.”

Among the wise sayings expressed in poetry are these lines:

Allah has decreed that aggression brings its people down,
And that calamities come round upon the aggressor.

This is borne out by His words, Most High:

“Your aggression is only against yourselves—an enjoyment of life,”

to the end of the verse. A hadith states:

“No sin is more deserving of having its perpetrator’s punishment hastened in this world than aggression, and no good deed is more deserving of having its doer’s reward hastened than maintaining ties of kinship.”

Whoever in either group is an unjust aggressor should therefore fear Allah and repent. Whoever has been wronged and subjected to aggression, yet remains patient, will receive glad tidings from Allah. Allah Most High says:

“And give glad tidings to the patient.”

Amr ibn Aws said: “They are those who do not wrong others when they themselves are wronged.”

Allah Most High has said to the believers concerning their enemy:

“But if you are patient and mindful of Allah, their scheming will not harm you

---

## Page 16526

at all.”

Joseph, peace be upon him, spoke as follows after his brothers had done what they did to him, and he had remained patient and mindful of Allah until Allah granted him victory and they entered his presence while he held a position of honor:

“They said, ‘Are you really Joseph?’ He said, ‘I am Joseph, and this is my brother. Allah has indeed favored us. Whoever is mindful of Allah and patient—surely Allah does not let the reward of those who do good go to waste.’”

Whoever among these people or others fears Allah, acts truthfully and justly, does not transgress Allah’s limits, and patiently endures the harm and injustice inflicted by the other party will not be harmed by that party’s scheming. Rather, Allah will grant him victory over them. These trials arise from sins and wrongdoing, so each of the two groups must seek Allah’s forgiveness and repent to Him, for this removes punishment and brings down mercy. Allah Most High says:

“But Allah would not punish them while you are among them, nor would Allah punish them while they seek forgiveness.”

In a hadith, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever frequently seeks forgiveness, Allah will grant him relief from every worry, a way out of every hardship, and provision from where he does not expect.”

Allah Most High says:

“Alif, Lām, Rā. This is a Book whose verses have been perfected and then explained in detail, from One Who is All-Wise, All-Aware.”

“Worship none but Allah. Surely I am a warner and a bearer of glad tidings to you from Him.”

“And seek your Lord’s forgiveness, then repent to Him. He will grant you good enjoyment for an appointed term and give everyone of merit their due reward.”

---

## Page 16527

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

Two groups claiming to belong to the community of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, rally one another with the tribal calls of pre-Islamic ignorance, such as Asad, Hilāl, Tha‘labah, Harām, and others. There are grudges and blood feuds between them. Whenever the two groups come within sight of one another, the believers intervene to foster harmony and reconcile them.

Those aggressors say, “Allah has made seeking vengeance obligatory upon us through His words:

‘We prescribed for them therein: a life for a life’

—up to His words—

‘and wounds are subject to equal retaliation.’”

The believers then explain to them that this conduct leads to disbelief, involving as it does killing people and plundering wealth. They reply, “We have claims against them, and we will not leave until we have taken our revenge with our swords.” They then attack them, and whichever side prevails commits aggression, oversteps the bounds, and kills people. They spread corruption in the land.

Is it obligatory to fight and kill the aggressing group after enjoining them to do what is right? Or what must the ruler do about this aggressing group?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah. Fighting between these two groups is forbidden according to the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Indeed, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When two Muslims confront one another with their swords, both the killer and the one killed are in the Fire.”

He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, we understand this concerning the killer, but what about the one killed?”

He replied:

---

## Page 16528

“He intended to kill his companion.”

He, peace and blessings be upon him, also said:

“Do not become disbelievers after me, striking one another’s necks.”

And he, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Your blood and your wealth are inviolable among you, as sacred as this day of yours, in this city of yours, in this month of yours. Let those present convey this to those absent, for someone to whom it is conveyed may understand it better than someone who heard it directly.”

What is obligatory in such a case is what Allah and His Messenger have commanded. Allah says:

“If two groups of believers fight one another, make peace between them. But if one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing group until it returns to Allah’s command. If it returns, make peace between them with justice and act equitably. Indeed, Allah loves those who act equitably.”

“The believers are but brothers, so make peace between your brothers and fear Allah, so that you may receive mercy.”

It is therefore obligatory to reconcile these two groups, as Allah Most High has commanded. There are various ways to bring about reconciliation. One is to collect zakat funds and other contributions to be spent for this purpose. Whoever incurs a financial liability to reconcile people may receive zakat to cover the amount incurred, as stated by the jurists among the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said to Qabīsah ibn Mukhāriq:

“Asking for financial assistance is permissible only for three people: a man who has undertaken a financial liability, who may ask until he obtains enough to discharge it, then must stop; a man whose wealth has been swept away by a calamity, who may ask until he obtains enough to meet his living needs, then must stop; and a man afflicted by poverty, whose poverty is attested by three men of sound judgment from his people, who say, ‘So-and-so has been afflicted by poverty,’ so he may ask for assistance

---

## Page 16529

until he obtains enough to sustain himself and meet his living needs, then he must stop. Whatever is obtained by asking beyond these cases is unlawful gain for the one who takes it.”

Another way to bring about reconciliation is for one or both groups to waive some of their claims against the other concerning bloodshed and property:

“But whoever pardons and makes reconciliation, his reward is with Allah. Indeed, He does not love the wrongdoers.”

Another way to bring about reconciliation is to adjudicate between them justly. The lives and property each group has destroyed are assessed, and their respective claims are offset against one another:

“The free for the free, the slave for the slave, and the female for the female.”

If anything remains due to one group from the other, it should be sought fairly and paid graciously. If the number of those killed or the amount of property is unknown, what is unknown is treated as nonexistent.

If one group claims an additional amount from the other, either it has the other swear an oath denying that claim, or it produces evidence. Alternatively, if the other refuses to swear, judgment is rendered either by referring the oath back to the claimant or on the basis of the refusal to swear.

If one of the two groups transgresses by refusing to uphold the justice required of it and to comply with the command of Allah and His Messenger, and fights to maintain that refusal, or seeks to fight the other group and destroy lives and property, as has been their custom, then, if it cannot be restrained except by killing, it is to be fought until it returns to Allah’s command. If, however, it can be compelled to act justly without fighting,

---

## Page 16530

such as by punishing or imprisoning some of them, executing those among them whose execution is required, or taking similar measures—then that should be done, and there is no need for fighting.

As for the claim, “Allah has obliged us to seek vengeance,” this is a lie against Allah and His Messenger. Allah has not obliged anyone wronged by his fellow Muslim believer in matters of blood, property, or honour to exact his due. Indeed, whenever He mentions people’s rights in the Quran, He encourages pardon. He, Exalted is He, says:

“And wounds are subject to equal retaliation. But whoever waives it as charity, it is an expiation for him.”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“Then half of what you stipulated, unless they waive it or the one in whose hand is the marriage tie waives it.”

As for His saying, Exalted is He:

“And We prescribed for them therein: a life for a life, an eye for an eye, a nose for a nose, an ear for an ear, a tooth for a tooth, and equal retaliation for wounds. But whoever waives it as charity, it is an expiation for him. And whoever does not judge by what Allah has revealed—it is they who are the wrongdoers.”

Although this was prescribed for the Children of Israel—and even if the ruling for us is the same as theirs regarding laws that have not been abrogated—what is intended here is equality among believers with respect to their lives, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Muslims’ lives are equal in worth, and they stand united against all others.”

“A life for a life” applies even if the killer is an obeyed chief from a noble tribe and the victim an ordinary tradesman, a newcomer. The same applies whether one is old and the other young, one rich and the other poor, one Arab and the other non-Arab, or one a Hāshimite and the other a Qurashī. This refutes the practice followed by

---

## Page 16531

the people of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance: when a prominent member of a tribe was killed, they would kill several people in his place from another tribe, other than the killer’s own tribe. Yet when a powerless member of a tribe was killed, they would not execute his killer if he was an obeyed chief. Allah abolished this practice through His words:

“And We prescribed for them therein: a life for a life.”

What was prescribed for them was justice—namely, a life for a life—since injustice is forbidden. Whether to exact one’s right, however, is left to the person entitled to it. This is like His saying:

“And whoever is killed unjustly, We have given his heir authority, but let him not exceed the limits in taking life.”

That is, he must not kill anyone other than the victim’s killer. If, however, one of the two parties seeks the judgment of Allah and His Messenger, while the other says, “We shall take what is due to us with our own hands now,” this is among the gravest sins, warranting punishment of this unjust, wicked killer. If they refuse to submit to the judgment of Allah and His Messenger and have the strength to resist, the ruler must fight them. If they lack such strength, he must identify those who refuse to submit to the judgment of Allah and His Messenger and compel them to abide by justice.

As for their saying, “We have claims against them dating back many years,” they should be told: “We shall adjudicate between you concerning both old and recent claims, for the judgment of Allah and His Messenger covers all of this.”

Anyone who kills someone after reconciliation, or after a covenant and binding agreement have been concluded, deserves execution. Indeed, a group of scholars held that he must be executed as a prescribed punishment, and that the victim’s heirs may not pardon him. The majority, however, held that his execution is a matter of legal retaliation, and that the choice rests with the victim’s heirs.

---

## Page 16532

If the aggressor is a group, they deserve punishment. If their conduct cannot be stopped except by fighting them, they are to be fought. If it can be stopped by lesser measures, they are to receive a punishment that prevents them from oppression, aggression, and breaking covenants and solemn agreements. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“On the Day of Resurrection, a banner will be raised at the backside of every betrayer, in proportion to his betrayal, and it will be said: ‘This is the betrayal of so-and-so.’”

Allah Most High has said:

“But whoever is granted any pardon by his brother, then the claim should be pursued fairly and payment made to him graciously. This is a concession and a mercy from your Lord. But whoever transgresses after that will suffer a painful punishment.”

A group of scholars said that the transgressor is someone who kills after granting a pardon; such a person must be executed. Others said that he should instead be punished in a manner that prevents him from transgressing. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“About people who neither pray nor fast, while those among them who fast do not pray. Their wealth is unlawful, and they take people’s property, yet they are generous to their neighbors and the weak. They are not known to follow any particular school, and they are Muslims?”

He answered:

“Praise be to Allah. If these people are under the authority of the rulers, those rulers must command them to establish prayer, and they must be punished for neglecting it. The same applies to fasting, even if they acknowledge the obligation of the five daily prayers, fasting Ramadan, and the prescribed zakah. Otherwise, whoever does not acknowledge these obligations is a disbeliever. If they acknowledge the obligation of prayer but refuse to perform it, they are to be punished until

---

## Page 16533

they perform it. According to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad, any sane adult who does not pray must be executed. The prescribed punishments must likewise be enforced upon them. If they are a group possessing armed strength and resisting enforcement, they must be fought until they commit to fulfilling the manifest obligations established by mass transmission, such as prayer, fasting, and zakah, and abstaining from prohibited acts such as unlawful sexual intercourse, usury, highway robbery, and the like.

Whoever does not acknowledge the obligation of prayer and zakah is a disbeliever who must be called upon to repent. If he repents, well and good; otherwise, he is to be executed. Whoever does not believe in Allah, His Messenger, the Last Day, Paradise, and Hell is a disbeliever whose disbelief is greater than that of Jews and Christians. Disobedience to parents is among the major sins that incur punishment in Hell.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“About people living in the frontier regions who raid the Armenians and others, acquire wealth, and spend it on wine and unlawful sexual intercourse: are they martyrs if they are killed?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If they raid only belligerent disbelievers, then deeds are judged by intentions. They said:

“O Messenger of Allah, a man fights out of bravery, fights out of partisan zeal, or fights to show off: which of these is in Allah’s cause?”

He replied:

“Whoever fights so that the word of Allah may be supreme is fighting in Allah’s cause.”

If any of them seeks only to acquire wealth,

---

## Page 16534

and spend it on sinful acts, then such people are evildoers who deserve the threatened punishment. If their purpose is that the word of Allah should be supreme and that religion should be devoted to Allah, then they are fighting in His cause. However, if they commit major sins, they have both good deeds and evil deeds. But if they raid the Muslims living there, then they spread corruption in the land and wage war against Allah and His Messenger, deserving severe punishment in this world and the Hereafter. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“About a soldier serving under a commander: the sultan went out hunting and ordered that some Arabs be plundered and killed. He went up the mountain and found thirty men, who fled. The commander said, ‘Ride after them!’ They turned back to fight their pursuers, and the soldier struck one of them, who died. Is he liable for anything or not?”

He answered:
