---

## Page 16664

and other Jews, I know of no disagreement reported from Ahmad concerning the lawfulness of the animals they slaughtered, nor from the Companions, the Successors, or others among the early generations. Their disagreement concerned Banū Taghlib alone. Some scholars of Ahmad’s school, however, held that two views were reported from him concerning these other groups as well, just as with Banū Taghlib. The majority, including Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, held their slaughtered animals to be lawful. I know of no precedent among the early generations for the other view.

These same scholars of Ahmad’s school further stated that, where either parent was not of the People of the Book but was instead a Magian, neither their slaughtered animals nor marriage to their women was lawful. This is also al-Shāfi‘ī’s position when the father was a Magian. As for the mother, he held two views. If both parents were Magians, the animals slaughtered by their child were unlawful according to al-Shāfi‘ī and those scholars of Ahmad’s school who agreed with him. This view has also been attributed to Mālik. I strongly suspect, however, that this attribution to Mālik is mistaken, for I have not found it in the books of his followers.

This ruling is an extension of the position extrapolated from Ahmad concerning the other Arab Jews and Christians. That position, in turn, rests on one of the two views reported from him concerning the Christians of Banū Taghlib—the view preferred by these scholars.

If, however, one confines the two reported views to Banū Taghlib, excluding the other Arabs, or holds that the disagreement encompasses them all and proceeds on the view that the animals slaughtered by Banū Taghlib and marriage to their women are lawful, as the majority maintain, then lineage has no bearing under this reported view. Indeed, even if both parents were Magians or idolaters, but their child belonged to the People of the Book, that child would unquestionably be subject to the rulings applicable to the People of the Book according to this position. The jurists of the schools of Ahmad and Abū Hanīfah, among others, explicitly stated this.

---

## Page 16665

Those among Ahmad’s followers and others who suppose that prohibiting marriage to a woman whose parents are both Magians, or one of whose parents is a Magian, is the only position in his school are unquestionably mistaken, for they have not understood the origin of the disagreement over this issue. Hence some of them contradict themselves: they allow those who embraced the religion of the People of the Book after its abrogation and alteration to remain in that religion upon payment of the jizya, yet categorically prohibit marriage to Arab Christian women and to women whose parents were not both of the People of the Book. A group of Ahmad’s followers took this approach, but it is contradictory.

Although al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and a group of his followers held this position, he retracted it in al-Jāmi‘ al-Kabīr, his last book. There he discussed whether it is permissible to marry the women of former idolaters who embraced the religion of the People of the Book—such as the Romans and certain Arab tribes, namely Tanūkh, Bahrā’, and some of Banū Taghlib—and to eat the animals they slaughter. He stated that Ahmad’s expressly reported position was that there was no objection to marrying Christian women of Banū Taghlib, and that the other reported position was extrapolated from the two views reported from him concerning the animals they slaughtered. He preferred the view that anyone who adopts their religion is subject to the same rulings as they are, whether the conversion occurred before or after the advent of our revealed law, and whether the person adopted the religion of those who had altered it or a religion that had not been altered. Marriage to their women and eating the animals they slaughter are therefore permissible.

If this applies to those among the Arabs and Romans whose parents were both polytheists, then it applies with even greater force to those with only one polytheist parent. This is Ahmad’s expressly stated position, for he explicitly stated that anyone who embraces their religion after its abrogation and alteration—as someone might embrace their religion in our own time—is permitted to remain in it upon payment of the jizya.

His followers stated that once we permit them to remain upon payment of the jizya, the animals they slaughter and marriage to their women become lawful. This is also the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and others.

---

## Page 16666

The disagreement over this issue stems from the dispute I mentioned between ʿAlī and other Companions concerning Banū Taghlib. Al-Shāfiʿī—and Ahmad, according to one of the two reports from him—… (1), whereas the majority permitted eating the animals they slaughtered and marrying their women. This is the other position reported from Ahmad.

Those who disapproved of eating the animals slaughtered by Banū Taghlib then differed over the basis of ʿAlī’s position. Some supposed that ʿAlī prohibited eating the animals they slaughtered and marrying their women only because he did not know whether their forefathers had embraced the religion of the People of the Book before its abrogation and alteration. On this basis, they held that membership among the People of the Book is determined by ancestry, not by the individual’s own religious affiliation. Thus, if we are uncertain whether someone’s ancestors belonged to the People of the Book, we exercise caution: we spare his life upon payment of the jizya as a precaution, and likewise prohibit eating the animals he slaughters and marrying the women of his community as a precaution. This is the reasoning of al-Shāfiʿī and those of Ahmad’s followers who agreed with him.

Others said instead that ʿAlī disapproved of eating the animals slaughtered by Banū Taghlib only because they did not observe the obligations and prohibitions of the religion of the People of the Book. Rather, they adopted from it only permission to indulge in forbidden things. Hence his statement: “They have retained nothing of the religion of the People of the Book except drinking wine.” This understanding of ʿAlī’s statement is expressly reported from Ahmad and others, and it is correct.

In short, the claim that the People of the Book mentioned in the Quran are those whose ancestors embraced that religion before its abrogation and alteration is a weak position. The claim that this was what ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, may Allah be pleased with him, intended is likewise weak. Rather, the correct and certain view is that being

> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 246):
> “The missing wording is ‘they disapproved of them’ or ‘they prohibited them,’ or something similar. This refers to eating animals slaughtered by Banū Taghlib and marrying their women.”

---

## Page 16667

a member of the People of the Book or not is a status determined independently of ancestry. Everyone who follows the religion of the People of the Book belongs to them, whether or not his father or grandfather embraced their religion, and whether that conversion occurred before or after its abrogation and alteration. This is the position of the majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfa and Mālik, and it is explicitly stated by Ahmad, although there is a well-known disagreement among his followers over this matter.

This position is established from the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, and I know of no disagreement among them concerning it. Al-Tahāwī mentioned that this was an early consensus and invoked it in this matter against those who would not allow a person to remain in their religion if he embraced it after its abrogation and alteration. Thus, if someone in our own time embraces the religion of the People of the Book, the animals he slaughters may be eaten and the women of his community may be married. This exposes the error of those among them who contradict themselves.

Those who hold this position—which is the majority position—say that anyone who himself embraced their religion, or whose parents or grandfather embraced it, after its abrogation and alteration is allowed to remain in it upon payment of the jizya, whether that conversion took place in our own time or earlier.

Those who hold the other position say: Whenever we know that he embraced it only after its abrogation and alteration, the jizya is not accepted from him. This is the view of some of Ahmad’s followers, along with the followers of al-Shāfiʿī. The correct position, however, is that of the majority. There are several grounds supporting it:

First: It is established that a number of the children of the Ansār embraced Judaism shortly before the Prophet’s mission, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. As Ibn ʿAbbās said, a woman might be a miqlāt—a miqlāt being a woman whose children do not survive, one who experiences frequent qalat. Qalat means death and destruction. Similarly, the terms midhkār and mi'nāth are used of a woman who frequently gives birth

---

## Page 16668

to boys and girls, respectively; and al-summā (1) denotes a woman whose children frequently die. Ibn ʿAbbās said: A woman would vow that, if two of her children survived, she would make one of them a Jew, because the Jews possessed knowledge and scripture, whereas the Arabs were polytheists and idolaters. When Allah sent Muhammad, a number of the Ansār’s children had embraced Judaism. Their fathers sought to compel them to embrace Islam, so Allah Most High revealed:

“There is no compulsion in religion. The right course has become clear from the wrong”—to the end of the verse.

Thus, it is established that these people embraced Judaism while their fathers were still alive. Clearly, they themselves entered Judaism before Islam and after the mission of the Messiah—that is, after its abrogation and alteration. Nevertheless, Allah, Mighty and Majestic, forbade compelling these people, who had embraced Judaism after its abrogation and alteration, to embrace Islam, and allowed them to remain in their religion upon payment of the jizya. This explicitly establishes the permissibility of granting a covenant of protection to someone who personally embraces the religion of the People of the Book after its abrogation and alteration. It is therefore clear that this position, rather than the other, is correct.

Once it is established that such a person may be granted a covenant of protection, it follows that what matters is his own religious affiliation, not his ancestry, and that the animals he slaughters and his food are lawful by the agreement of the Muslims. Those who prohibit this do so only on the grounds that people of this category are not among the People of the Book and therefore are not included. Once the explicit text of the Sunnah establishes that they are among the People of the Book, they are included within the scope of the scriptural address without dispute.

Second: A number of the Jews living in Medina and its surroundings were Arabs who had embraced Judaism. Nevertheless, concerning the permissibility of eating their food, marrying their women, and allowing them to remain under a covenant of protection, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made no distinction between those whose parents had embraced Judaism after the mission of Jesus, peace be upon him, and those who had embraced it before that; nor did he distinguish those whose ancestry was uncertain. Rather, he ruled

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 16669

in all their cases according to a single, general ruling.

It is therefore clear that distinguishing between groups—allowing one group to remain upon payment of the jizya, but not another, and permitting the animals slaughtered by some of those allowed to remain while prohibiting those slaughtered by others—has no basis in the authentically established Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Authentic, widely transmitted reports establish that Medina’s inhabitants included many Jews, both Arabs and non-Arabs, including Arabs from Banū Kināna, Himyar, and other tribes.

Thus, when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sent him to Yemen, he said:

“You are going to a people of the Book.”

He instructed him to take from every male who had reached puberty one dinar or its equivalent in Ma‘āfirī cloth. He made no distinction between those whose fathers had embraced that religion before its abrogation and those whose fathers had embraced it afterward. Likewise, he allowed the delegation from Najrān and other Christians, among whom were many Arabs, to remain upon payment of the jizya. The same applied to all other Arab Jews and Christians: neither the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any of his caliphs or Companions distinguished between them. Rather, they accepted the jizya from them and permitted eating the animals they slaughtered and marrying their women. Likewise, they made no distinction between different groups of Byzantine Christians or others. Anyone who reflects upon the Prophet’s life will necessarily recognize all this and realize that such distinctions are an innovated position with no basis in the Sharia.

Third: A man’s being a Muslim, a Jew, a Christian, or bearing any similar religious designation is a ruling concerning him personally, based on his belief, will, speech, and actions. He does not acquire such a designation merely because it applies to his forefathers. A minor, however, shares his parents’ status in the rulings of this world because he is not independent. Once he reaches puberty and professes Islam or unbelief, his status is determined by his own religious affiliation, by the agreement of the Muslims. Thus, if his parents were

---

## Page 16670

Jews or Christians and he embraced Islam, he would be counted among the Muslims by the agreement of the Muslims. If his parents were Muslims and he disbelieved, he would be an unbeliever by the agreement of the Muslims. If his unbelief constitutes apostasy, he is not permitted to remain in it, for he is an apostate on account of his forefathers.

Every ruling attached to religious designations—Islam, faith, unbelief, hypocrisy, apostasy, adopting Judaism, or adopting Christianity—applies only to someone who possesses the characteristics that warrant that designation. A person’s being one of the polytheists or the People of the Book falls under this principle. Whoever is himself a polytheist is subject to the rulings governing polytheists, even if his parents are not polytheists. Whoever is a Muslim, though his parents are polytheists, is subject to the rulings governing Muslims, not those governing polytheists. Likewise, if he is a Jew or a Christian while his forefathers were polytheists, he is subject to the rulings governing Jews and Christians. But to apply the rulings governing polytheists to him, despite his being a Jew or a Christian, because his forefathers were polytheists before the abrogation and alteration, would contravene established principles.

Fourth: Allah Most High says:

“Those who disbelieved among the People of the Book and the polytheists were not…”

He also says:

“And say to those who were given the Scripture and to the unlettered, ‘Have you submitted yourselves to Allah?’ If they submit, they are rightly guided.”

These and similar passages address the people living at that time and speak about them. The Scripture meant here is the scripture in their possession, which had undergone whatever abrogation and alteration had occurred. What is meant is not those who adhered to it before its abrogation and alteration, for those people were not unbelievers. Nor were they among those addressed by the laws of the Quran, or those addressed in the Quran with the words:

“O People of the Book!”

They had died before the Quran was revealed. Since this is so, everyone who follows this scripture now held by

---

## Page 16671

the People of the Book belongs to the People of the Book. They are unbelievers who adhere to an altered and abrogated scripture, and they will remain eternally in the Fire of Hell, as will all other categories of unbelievers. Nevertheless, Allah Most High has legislated that they be allowed to remain upon payment of the jizyah tax, and has made their food lawful and permitted marriage to their women.

Fifth:

Those among the People of the Book who disbelieve in the Quran are unbelievers, even if their forefathers were believers. Their punishment in the Hereafter is no lighter than that of someone whose father was not one of the People of the Book. Indeed, noble ancestry is more likely to aggravate their unbelief than to mitigate it. Someone whose father was a Muslim and who then apostatizes is guilty of graver unbelief than someone who himself embraced Islam and then apostatized.

For this reason, scholars differed concerning someone born upon the fitrah (natural disposition) who apostatizes and then returns to Islam: is his repentance accepted? There are two opinions, both reported from Ahmad.

Accordingly, someone whose father belonged to the People of the Book before the abrogation and alteration, but who, when Allah sent Jesus and Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—disbelieved in them both and in what they brought from Allah, and followed the altered and abrogated scripture, is guilty of one of the gravest forms of unbelief. His unbelief is no lighter than that of someone who personally entered this altered religion. Nor does his lineage alone give him any special standing with Allah or His Messenger. His forefathers’ religion does not benefit him when he differs from them, for his forefathers were Muslims at that time.

Allah’s religion is Islam in every age. Whoever believes in Allah’s Books and Messengers in any age is a Muslim, and whoever disbelieves in any of Allah’s Books or Messengers is not a Muslim, whatever age he lives in.

---

## Page 16672

If the descendants of the Children of Israel, when they disbelieve, have no advantage over other unbelievers who likewise follow the altered and abrogated religion, it follows that distinguishing between the two groups is invalid: honoring one by allowing them to remain upon payment of the jizyah tax, permitting their slaughtered animals to be eaten and their women to be married, while denying this to the other. It is also clear that [therein] (1) lies a distinction contrary to the fundamentals of Islam, and that, were the distinction reversed, it would be more fitting. For this reason, Allah reproaches the Children of Israel for rejecting Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—more severely than He reproaches others among the People of the Book. For He Most High bestowed immense religious and worldly blessings upon their ancestors, yet they were ungrateful for His blessings, rejected His Messengers, altered His Book, and changed His religion:

“Humiliation has been laid upon them wherever they are found, except under a covenant from Allah and a covenant from people. They have incurred wrath from Allah, and destitution has been laid upon them. That is because they used to disbelieve in the signs of Allah and kill the prophets without right. That is because they disobeyed and habitually transgressed.”

Thus, despite the nobility of their forefathers and the truth of their ancestors’ religion, they are among the worst unbelievers in Allah’s sight, and His wrath against them is greater than against others. Their unbelief contains arrogance, envy, obstinate defiance, hardness of heart, concealment of knowledge, distortion of Scripture, alteration of its text, and other things not found in the unbelief of those others.

How, then, can these abominable, impure people, who are among the creatures most hateful to Allah, be accorded an advantage over their fellow unbelievers, when their unbelief is either equal to that of their fellow unbelievers or more severe? For no one can say that the unbelief of those who entered this religion is more severe than theirs, when both groups profess the same religion based on this existing scripture.

> **Footnotes:**

> Usāmah ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the Comprehensive Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 16673

The Sixth Argument

To make religious honor depend on lineage alone is to judge as the people of pre-Islamic ignorance did; the Rāfidah and other ignorant people like them have followed their example. For Allah Most High says:

“O mankind, indeed We have created you from male and female and made you peoples and tribes that you may know one another. Indeed, the most noble of you in the sight of Allah is the most righteous of you.”

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“An Arab has no superiority over a non-Arab, nor a non-Arab over an Arab, nor a black person over a white person, nor a white person over a black person, except through piety. All people descend from Adam, and Adam was created from dust.”

Thus, there is not a single verse in Allah’s Book in which He praises or condemns anyone for their lineage. Rather, He praises people for faith and piety and condemns them for disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience. It is also established in al-Sahīh that he—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“There are four practices of pre-Islamic ignorance that my community will not abandon: boasting of ancestral distinction, disparaging people’s lineage, wailing over the dead, and seeking rain through the stars.”

He thus counted boasting of ancestral distinction among the practices of pre-Islamic ignorance. If a Muslim has no grounds to boast over another Muslim because his forefathers possessed noble standing, how can an unbeliever among the People of the Book have grounds to boast over another unbeliever among the People of the Book because his forefathers were believers?

If, when two groups profess the same religion, neither has any religious superiority over the other on account of lineage, then it follows that Jews and Christians whose forefathers were believers adhering to the original Scripture before its abrogation and alteration have no superiority over those whose fathers entered that religion after its abrogation and alteration. When their religion is the same, the religious ruling concerning them is likewise the same.

---

## Page 16674

The Sharia has tied certain rulings to lineage—such as the caliphate belonging to Quraysh, the Prophet’s close relatives being entitled to the fifth, and the family of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—being forbidden to receive charity, and similar rulings—only because noble lineage provides grounds for expecting those who possess it to be better than others. As the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“People are like mines of gold and silver. The best of them in the days of pre-Islamic ignorance are the best of them in Islam, provided they gain understanding of the religion.”

A ruling is tied to such a presumption when [the underlying reality] (*) is hidden or diffuse. But when a man’s religious condition, upon which rulings depend, becomes apparent, and its nature and degree are known, religious rulings are not determined by his lineage. Thus, Abū Lahab had no advantage over others: once his disbelief was known, he was more deserving of blame than others. Likewise, any of the Prophet’s wives who commits a flagrant sin is assigned a double punishment, just as any of them who devoutly obeys Allah and His Messenger is assigned a double reward.

When people of noble lineage do wrong, their wrongdoing is graver than that of others, and their punishment is more severe. Thus, if the disbelief of those among the Children of Israel who disbelieve is not worse than that of others, and their punishment not more severe, then at the very least they are equal. Hence, no scholar has said that anyone among Quraysh or the Arabs who disbelieves and acts wickedly will have his punishment reduced in this world or the Hereafter. Rather, their punishment is either more severe than that of others, according to the better-known of the two views, or harsher, according to the other view. For someone whom Allah has honored with His blessings and raised in standing, but who responds to His rights with disobedience and to His blessings with disbelief, is more deserving of punishment than someone upon whom He has not bestowed the same blessings.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 272): “Perhaps the intended reading is al-hikma (wisdom).”

---

## Page 16675

“The Seventh Argument”

It may be said: When the Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—conquered Syria, Iraq, Egypt, Khurāsān, and other lands, they ate the animals slaughtered by their inhabitants without distinguishing between one group and another. No Companion is known to have distinguished between them on the basis of ancestry. They differed only concerning Banū Taghlib, owing to a circumstance particular to them. Likewise, ‘Umar levied zakat on them at double the rate and made their poll tax different from that of others. He did not extend this treatment to all other Arabs, but only to those in a comparable position.

“The Eighth Argument”

It may be said: This view necessarily entails that the food of most of the People of the Book is unlawful for us, since we do not know the ancestry of many of them, nor do we know whether their ancestors before the advent of Islam were Jews or Christians prior to abrogation and alteration. Yet it is well known that the permissibility of eating their slaughtered animals and marrying their women is established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus. Since this view necessarily entails overturning what is established by the Quran, the Sunnah, and consensus, it is known to be invalid.

“The Ninth Argument”

It may be said: Muslims in every age and land have always eaten the animals slaughtered by them. Whoever condemns this therefore opposes the consensus of the Muslims.

All these arguments demonstrate that the view permitting this is stronger and is what the evidence entails. However, to maintain that, in an issue such as this or in similar matters open to juristic reasoning, someone who adopts one of the two positions may censure someone holding the other without proof or evidence is contrary to the consensus of the Muslims.

---

## Page 16676

Indeed, Muslims have differed over cheese made by Magians and polytheists. Someone who considers either view stronger may not censure someone holding the other view except on the basis of evidence from Islamic law. Likewise, they have differed over animals slaughtered without Allah’s name being mentioned; animals slaughtered by the People of the Book over which they invoke a name other than Allah’s; the fat covering the stomach and intestines and that surrounding the kidneys; and their slaughter of claw-bearing animals, such as camels, ducks, and other creatures Allah has forbidden them. They have also differed over having a person from the People of the Book slaughter sacrificial animals, and other such questions.

Each position has been held by a group of renowned scholars. Someone who adopts one position by following its proponent’s authority may not censure someone who adopts the other by following its proponent’s authority. However, if either has evidence from Islamic law, one must submit to such evidence once it becomes clear. No one may prefer one position over another without evidence, or show partisan attachment to one position or scholar over another without proof. Rather, someone who follows a scholar’s authority must remain within the bounds of that adherence: he may neither give preference to a position, nor discredit it, nor declare it correct or mistaken.

Anyone who has knowledge and an explanation to offer should be heard. What becomes clear to be true should be accepted, what becomes clear to be false should be rejected, and judgment should be suspended where neither has become clear. Allah Most High has made people differ in their intellectual capacities just as He has made them differ in their physical capacities. This question and others like it involve depths and subtleties of jurisprudence understood only by those familiar with the scholars’ various positions and their grounds. Someone who knows only one scholar’s position and evidence, but not another scholar’s position and evidence, is among the laypeople who follow scholarly authority, not among the scholars qualified to weigh positions and expose their flaws.

May Allah Most High guide us and our brothers to what He loves and is pleased with. Success comes only through Allah, and Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16677

He also said—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

Slaughter performed by either a woman or a man is valid. A woman may slaughter even while menstruating, for menstruation does not make her hand impure. Slaughter performed by a woman is valid by the consensus of the Muslims. A woman once slaughtered a sheep, and the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed that it be eaten.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

If an animal, such as a buffalo or another animal, is slaughtered while in water and dies in the water, may it be eaten?

He answered:

If the wound is not fatal in itself and the animal’s head is submerged in the water, it is not lawful to eat it, because both a prohibiting factor and a permitting factor bear on its ruling. This is as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to ʿAdī ibn Hātim:

“If other dogs join your dog, do not eat the game, for you mentioned Allah’s name over your dog alone, not over the others.”

If its body is in the water but its head remains above water, that causes no harm. If, however, the wound is fatal in itself, there is a well-known scholarly disagreement concerning it.

---

## Page 16678

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling on an animal that was slaughtered and bled profusely but did not move?

He answered:

If the blood that flows from it is the blood that ordinarily flows from a living animal when slaughtered, then it is lawful to eat it according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions. Allah Most High knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

If “a strangled animal and others in the same category” reach a condition beyond which they cannot survive, does prescribed slaughter still make them lawful to eat? And what about an animal that falls into a well or river and cannot be slaughtered?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. There is a well-known scholarly disagreement concerning this issue. The strongest opinion is that if the animal moves when slaughtered and its blood flows, it may be eaten. This is what is reported from the Companions and is supported by the Quran and Sunnah. Allah Most High says:

“and animals that have been strangled”—through to His words—“except those you slaughter.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Eat whatever has had its blood made to flow and Allah’s name mentioned over it.”

---

## Page 16679

As for an animal that falls into a well or the like and whose throat cannot be reached, it should be wounded wherever possible—for example, by stabbing it in the thigh—as is done with game that cannot be captured. According to the majority of scholars, this makes it lawful to eat, unless another cause contributes to its death. For example, if its head is submerged in water, so that it dies from both the wound and drowning, it is not lawful to eat. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Regarding “sheep and cattle” and similar animals that are at the point of death: if someone comes upon them, may he slaughter any of them while certain that they are alive at the time of slaughter, even though some animals do not move a single limb when slaughtered? Does movement indicate the presence of life, and its absence indicate the absence of life, or not?

Most people ascertain that an animal is alive when they slaughter it and drain its blood, yet, because it does not move, they say, “It is dead,” and throw it away. Does thin, red blood flowing at the time of slaughter indicate that the animal has stable life, while a small amount of black, congealed blood is the blood of death, or not?

And what did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mean when he said:

“Eat whatever has had its blood made to flow and Allah’s name mentioned over it”?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Allah Most High says:

“Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, the flesh of swine, animals dedicated to other than Allah, animals killed by strangling, those killed by a violent blow, those killed by a fall

---

## Page 16680

and those killed by goring, and those partly eaten by a wild beast, except those you slaughter before they die.”

His words, Most High, “except those you slaughter before they die,” refer back to the animals mentioned earlier: those strangled, struck a fatal blow, killed by a fall, gored, or partly eaten by a wild beast. This is the view of the majority of scholars, including al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Abū Hanīfah, and others. Thus, any such animal reached and slaughtered before it dies is lawful to eat. However, the scholars differed over which of these animals may be slaughtered.

Some said that an animal certain to die cannot be rendered lawful by slaughter. This is Mālik’s view and one opinion reported from Ahmad.

Others said that an animal that can survive most of a day may be slaughtered.

Others said that an animal possessing stable life may be slaughtered. This is the view of some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad.

Among these scholars, some defined stable life as life exceeding the mere movements of a slaughtered animal. Others defined it as life that may outlast that of a slaughtered animal.

The correct view is that if the animal is alive when slaughtered, it is lawful to eat. The movements of a slaughtered animal are not the criterion here, for such movements have no fixed measure: some persist for a long time and are vigorous. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Eat whatever has had its blood made to flow and Allah’s name mentioned over it.”

Whenever blood flows as it does from an animal slaughtered while alive, the animal is lawful to eat. People distinguish between the blood of an animal that was alive and that of one that was dead, for the blood of a dead animal congeals and turns black. This is why Allah prohibited carrion: its fluids are retained within it. Thus, if blood flows from the animal as it does from an animal slaughtered while alive, it is lawful to eat, even if one is certain

---

## Page 16681

that it will die. What matters is slaughtering an animal while it still has life in it; it is alive even if one is certain that it will die an hour later. Thus, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, knew that he would die, yet he was still alive, and his will, prayers, and undertakings remained valid.

Several Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, ruled that if an animal swishes its tail, blinks its eye, or kicks its leg after slaughter, it is lawful to eat. They did not stipulate that its movements beforehand must exceed those of a slaughtered animal. The Companions said this because movement is evidence of life, but such evidence does not necessarily hold in reverse: the absence of these movements does not mean that the animal is dead. Rather, it may be alive even without displaying any such movement.

A person may be asleep and have his throat cut without struggling. Likewise, an unconscious person may have his throat cut without struggling. An animal, too, may be alive when slaughtered yet not struggle because it is too weak to move, despite being alive. However, the flow of blood of the kind that comes only from an animal slaughtered alive—not the blood of a dead animal—is evidence of life. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16682

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

“Mentioning Allah’s name over an animal at slaughter” is prescribed. However, some hold that it is recommended, as is al-Shāfi‘ī’s position. Others hold that it is obligatory, but that the requirement is waived in cases of forgetfulness. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and Ahmad according to the well-known report from him. Still others hold that it is obligatory without exception: an animal slaughtered without it may not be eaten, whether it was omitted deliberately or forgetfully. This is the other position reported from Ahmad, adopted by Abū al-Khattāb and others, and it was held by several of the early generations.

This last position is the strongest, for the Quran and Sunnah make permissibility conditional upon mentioning Allah’s name in several places, as in His words:

“So eat what they catch for you, and mention Allah’s name over it.”

And His words:

“So eat of that over which Allah’s name has been mentioned.”

“Why should you not eat of that over which Allah’s name has been mentioned?”

“And do not eat of that over which Allah’s name has not been mentioned.”

The two Sahīh collections record that he said:

“If the blood is made to flow and Allah’s name is mentioned over the animal, then eat of it.”

An authentic hadith collection also records that he said to ‘Adī:

“If you release your trained dog, mentioning Allah’s name, and it kills the game, then eat. But if other dogs join your dog, do not eat, for you mentioned Allah’s name over your dog alone, not over the others.”

---

## Page 16683

It is also recorded in an authentic hadith collection that the jinn asked him for provisions for themselves and their animals. He said:

“Every bone over which Allah’s name has been mentioned is yours, with the fullest covering of flesh, and every dropping is fodder for your animals.”

The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“So do not cleanse yourselves with either of them after relieving yourselves, for they are provisions for your brethren among the jinn.”

Thus, he (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) permitted the believing jinn only that over which Allah’s name had been mentioned. How much more, then, does this apply to human beings? However, if someone finds meat from an animal slaughtered by another person, he may eat it, mentioning Allah’s name over it, because people’s actions are presumed valid and sound. As recorded in an authentic hadith collection:

“Some people said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, people who have only recently embraced Islam bring us meat, and we do not know whether they mentioned Allah’s name over it or not.’ He replied, ‘Mention Allah’s name yourselves and eat.’”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Concerning a slaughtered animal over which it is known with certainty that Allah’s name was not mentioned: is it permissible to eat it? Does it render utensils ritually impure?”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. Mentioning Allah’s name over it is obligatory according to the Quran and Sunnah, and this is the position of the majority of scholars. However, if a person does not know whether the slaughterer mentioned Allah’s name or not, he may eat it. If he knows with certainty that the slaughterer did not mention Allah’s name, he must not eat it. The same applies to the Eid sacrifice.”

---

## Page 16684

Chapter: Oaths and Vows

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

“The Fifth Principle”
Concerning
“Oaths and Vows”

Allah Most High said:

“O Prophet, why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah has already prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths. Allah is your Protector, and He is the All-Knowing, the All-Wise.”

He Most High also said:

“Do not use your oaths by Allah as an excuse to refrain from doing good, fearing Him, and making peace between people. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

He Most High also said:

“Allah will not hold you accountable for your unintentional oaths, but He will hold you accountable for what your hearts intend. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing.”

“For those who swear to abstain from their wives, there is a waiting period of four months. If they return, then Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“But if they resolve upon divorce, then Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

He Most High also said:

“O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not love transgressors.”

“Eat of the lawful, good things Allah has provided for you, and fear Allah, in whom you believe.”

“Allah will not hold you accountable for your unintentional oaths, but He will hold you accountable for the oaths you solemnly swear. The expiation is to feed ten needy people with the average food you feed your own families, or to clothe them, or to free a slave

---

## Page 16685

; whoever cannot afford this must fast for three days. That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn them. And guard your oaths.”

These verses contain important principles, but explaining them requires some preliminary points that are extremely useful in this subject and others.

“The First Preliminary Point”

An oath comprises two clauses: a clause by which one swears and a clause stating what one swears to. Questions concerning oaths relate either to the ruling on what one swears by or to the ruling on what one swears to.

As for what one swears by, the oaths sworn by Muslims that may entail legal consequences fall into six categories, with no seventh:

First: an oath by Allah, and equivalent expressions involving a commitment to disbelief should the stated condition be fulfilled, such as saying, “I am a Jew or a Christian if I do such-and-such”—subject to the disagreement among the jurists concerning this.

Second: an oath by means of a vow, known as a “vow of obstinacy and anger,” such as saying, “The Hajj shall be binding upon me: I will not do such-and-such”; “If I do such-and-such, the Hajj shall be binding upon me”; or “My wealth shall be given in charity if I do such-and-such,” and similar expressions.

Third: an oath invoking divorce.

Fourth: an oath invoking the freeing of a slave.

Fifth: an oath invoking prohibition, such as saying, “Prohibition shall be binding upon me: I will not do such-and-such.”

Sixth: zihār, such as saying, “You are to me like my mother’s back if I do such-and-such.”

These comprise all the forms of oath sworn by Muslims that entail legal consequences.

---

## Page 16686

As for “swearing by created things”—such as the Kaaba, a shaykh’s grave, the sultan’s favour, a sword, or the standing of any created being—I know of no disagreement among the scholars that such oaths are disapproved of and forbidden, and that breaking them entails neither liability for oath-breaking nor expiation. Is swearing by them unlawful, or merely discouraged without being unlawful? There are two opinions in the school of Ahmad and among other scholars; the more correct is that it is unlawful.

For this reason, our scholars, such as al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā and others, said that if someone says, “The oaths of the Muslims shall be binding upon me if I do such-and-such,” he is bound by the requirements applicable to oaths by Allah, vows, divorce, manumission and zihār. They did not mention oaths invoking prohibition, because, according to Ahmad and his followers, an oath invoking prohibition constitutes zihār. Since both entail the same consequence in their view, prohibition was included under zihār.

Vows, however, were not included under oaths by Allah, even though one may offer expiation for an oath invoking a vow. This is because, when an oath invoking a vow—known as a “vow of obstinacy and anger”—is broken, one has the choice between offering expiation and fulfilling the vow, whereas breaking an oath by Allah requires expiation alone. Since their consequences differ, they treated them as two distinct kinds of oath. However, if they adopt the other position reported from Ahmad—that an oath invoking a vow requires expiation alone—then an oath invoking a vow is included under an oath by Allah Most High.

As for their disagreement, and that of other scholars, over whether wording of this kind constitutes a binding oath, I shall discuss it, Allah Most High willing. My purpose here is only to enumerate the kinds of oath sworn by Muslims.

As for oaths of allegiance, they said that the first to introduce them was al-Hajjāj ibn Yūsuf al-Thaqafī. The established practice had been for people to pledge allegiance to the caliphs as the Companions had pledged allegiance to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace

---

## Page 16687

—concluding the pledge as they would a contract of sale, marriage, or the like. Alternatively, they would state the conditions upon which they pledged allegiance, then say, “We pledge allegiance to you on these terms,” as the Ansār pledged allegiance to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on the night of al-‘Aqaba.

Among the oppressive practices introduced by al-Hajjāj was requiring people to confirm their allegiance to ‘Abd al-Malik ibn Marwān through oaths invoking divorce, manumission, an oath by Allah, and the giving of their wealth in charity. These four oaths constituted the original, innovated oaths of allegiance. Later, deputies acting on behalf of rulers—caliphs, kings, and others—introduced many additional oaths, with practices varying among them. Whoever introduced this bears the sin of the evil resulting from these oaths.

Second Premise

These oaths are sworn either using an oath formula or a conditional formula. An oath cannot fall outside these two forms.

The first includes statements such as: “By Allah, I shall not do such-and-such”; “I bind myself by divorce to do such-and-such”; “Prohibition shall be binding upon me: I shall not do such-and-such”; or “The pilgrimage shall be binding upon me: I shall not do it.”

The second includes statements such as: “If I do such-and-such, then I am a Jew, a Christian, or free of Islam”; “If I do such-and-such, then my wife is divorced”; “If I do such-and-such, then my wife is forbidden to me,” or “she is to me like my mother’s back”; or “If I do such-and-such, then the pilgrimage is binding upon me,” or “my wealth is to be given in charity.”

For this reason, the jurists devoted two chapters to questions concerning oaths. One is the “Chapter on Making Divorce Conditional,” in which they discuss oaths expressed in conditional form, using such expressions as “if,” “whenever,” “when,” and expressions like

---

## Page 16688

these, although oath formulas may also be included implicitly and incidentally. The second chapter is the “Chapter on Oaths in General,” covering matters common to oaths by Allah and oaths invoking divorce, manumission, and other things. In it, they discuss oaths expressed through an oath formula, although conditional formulas may also be included implicitly and incidentally. The questions addressed in each chapter overlap with those of the other, since their meanings frequently—or usually—coincide.

Thus, some jurists, such as Abū al-Khattāb and others, placed the “Chapter on Oaths in General” immediately after the “Chapter on Making Divorce Conditional” in the Book of Divorce. Others, such as al-Khiraqī, Judge Abū Ya‘lā, and others, included the “Chapter on Oaths in General” only in the Book of Oaths, because it is more closely connected with that subject. A comparable example is the “Chapter on the Prescribed Punishment for Slander”: some place it alongside the “Chapter on Mutual Imprecation” because the two are connected, while others defer it to the Book of Prescribed Punishments, to which it more specifically belongs.

Once it is clear that an oath has two forms—an oath formula and a conditional formula—what comes first in the oath formula comes last in the conditional formula, and what comes last in the conditional formula comes first in the oath formula. A condition expressed affirmatively in the conditional formula is expressed negatively in the oath formula. For example, if someone says, “Divorce is binding upon me: I shall not do such-and-such,” he has sworn an oath invoking divorce that he will not do it. Divorce comes first and is expressed affirmatively; the action comes last and is negated.

If, instead, he swears using a conditional formula, saying, “If I do such-and-such, then my wife is divorced,” he places the action first, expressing it affirmatively, and divorce last, expressing it negatively—just as, in the oath formula, he placed the ruling first and the action last. This principle resolves a number of questions concerning oaths.

---

## Page 16689

As for the “conditional formula,” it is fundamentally a verbal sentence, since conditional particles are normally followed only by a verb. The “oath formula,” however, may be verbal, as in “I swear by Allah,” or ta-llāhi (“By Allah”), or wa-llāhi (“By Allah”), and similar expressions. It may also be nominal, as in “By Allah’s life, I shall certainly do it,” and “What is lawful is forbidden to me: I shall certainly do it.”

This classification is not confined to oaths between a servant and Allah; it also applies to contracts between people. Sometimes these take a conditional form, consisting of a condition and its consequence, as when someone offers a reward: “Whoever returns my runaway slave shall receive such-and-such,” or declares in a competition: “Whoever wins shall receive such-and-such.” At other times, they take an unconditional form: either a declarative formula, such as “I have sold” and “I have given in marriage,” or a request, such as “Sell to me” and “Grant me a divorce in return for compensation.”

The third preliminary point—which reveals the underlying principle governing questions concerning oaths and similar matters—is that the conditional formula, also called the “condition-and-consequence formula,” falls into six types. The person swearing may intend only the fulfilment of the condition, only the occurrence of the consequence, or both. Alternatively, he may intend neither to occur, but seek only the non-fulfilment of the condition, only the non-occurrence of the consequence, or the absence of both.

The first type is exemplified by many forms of divorce in return for compensation, contracts of manumission by payment, devotional vows, reward undertakings, and similar arrangements. A man might say to his wife, “If you give me a thousand, you are divorced,” or “If you give me a thousand, I have granted you a divorce in return for compensation.” Or he might say to his slave, “If you pay a thousand, you are free”; or say, “If you return my runaway slave, you shall receive a thousand”; or say, “If Allah heals my sick loved one, or

---

## Page 16690

my property elsewhere remains safe, I undertake to free such-and-such slaves and give such-and-such in charity.” The person making this conditional undertaking may seek only to receive the money, have the slave returned, and ensure the soundness of the manumission and the safety of the property. He undertakes the consequence only as something given in exchange, like a seller whose sole purpose is to receive the price and who undertakes to hand over the item sold in return. This type thus resembles the exchange involved in sale and hire contracts.

The same applies when he makes divorce a punishment for her, saying, for example, “If you strike my mother, you are divorced,” or “If you leave the house, you are divorced.” In divorce in return for compensation, he gives her a divorce in exchange for money, because she wants the divorce; here, he repays her disobedience with divorce.

The second type is exemplified by a man saying to his wife, “When you become free of menstruation, you are divorced”; or to his slave, “When I die, you are free,” or “When the year is complete, you are free”; or saying, “then my property is to be given in charity.” These and similar conditional declarations merely specify a time. This type is comparable to an immediately effective declaration: in both cases, divorce or manumission is intended, but here it is simply deferred until the specified time. It resembles deferring payment of a debt, or postponing divorce from one time to another for some purpose served by the delay—not in exchange for something, nor to reinforce a request or an assertion.

For this reason, jurists of our school and others have said: suppose a man swears that he will not swear an oath, saying, for example, “By Allah, I will not swear an oath on your divorce,” or “If I swear an oath on your divorce, my slave is free,” or “you are divorced.” If he then says, “If you enter,” or “If you do not enter,” or something similar intended to encourage or prevent an action, he has sworn an oath. If, however, it is merely a conditional declaration, such as “When the sun rises, you are divorced,” or “If the sun rises,” they disagree. The followers of al-Shāfiʿī say that he has not sworn an oath, whereas the followers of Abū Hanīfa and al-Qādī in al-Jāmiʿ say that he has.

---

## Page 16691

As for the “third” type, in which he intends both the condition and its consequence to occur, an example is a man whose wife has mistreated him to the point that he wants to divorce her and recover money from her as compensation. He says, “If you release me from paying your dowry or your maintenance, you are divorced,” intending both outcomes.

As for the “fourth” type, he intends that the condition should not occur, but, if it does, he does not dislike the consequence; rather, he either wants it or is indifferent to it. An example is his saying to his wife, “If you commit adultery, you are divorced,” or “If you strike my mother, you are divorced.” Similar conditional declarations are intended to prevent the condition from occurring, while also intending the consequence should it occur. Thus, if she commits adultery or strikes his mother, he must part with her because she is not suitable for him. This combines the meaning of an oath with that of specifying a time: he has sought to prevent her from committing the act, while intending divorce to take effect when it occurs, just as he intends it to take effect upon receiving compensation from her, when she becomes free of menstruation, or when the new moon appears.

As for the “fifth” type, he intends that the consequence should not occur and makes it dependent on the condition to prevent it from occurring, although he has no interest in preventing the condition itself. This is uncommon, as when someone says, “If you hit the mark a hundred times, I will give you such-and-such.”

As for the “sixth” type, he intends that neither the condition nor its consequence should occur, and links the consequence to the condition only to prevent both from occurring. This is exemplified by vows made in obstinacy and anger.

---

## Page 16692

and by oaths invoking divorce or emancipation to urge or prevent an action, or to affirm or deny the truth of a statement. For example, someone is told, “Give charity,” and replies that if he gives charity, he must fast for such-and-such a period, or his wife is divorced, or his slaves are free. Or he says, “If I do not do such-and-such, I am bound by such-and-such a vow, or my wife is divorced, or my slave is free.”

Alternatively, he swears concerning the conduct of someone he wishes to restrain—such as his slave, relative, or friend, whom he urges to obey him—saying, “If you do this,” or, “If you do not do this, I must do such-and-such, or my wife is divorced, or my slave is free,” and similar statements. This is a vow made in obstinacy and anger.

This and similar oaths invoking divorce or emancipation differ in meaning from “vows of devotion and drawing near to Allah” and comparable arrangements such as “divorce in return for compensation” and “contractual emancipation.” Someone who says, “If Allah keeps me or my property safe from such-and-such, or if Allah grants me such-and-such, I must give charity, fast, or perform Hajj,” intends the condition—the gain or deliverance—to occur, and intends to thank Allah for it by fulfilling what he has vowed to Him. Likewise, a husband agreeing to a compensated divorce or a master entering an emancipation contract intends to receive compensation and to grant divorce or emancipation in exchange.

By contrast, in a vow made in obstinacy and anger, someone is told, “Do such-and-such,” but refuses and then says, “If I do it, I must perform Hajj or fast.” Here, his intention is that the condition should not occur. Because of his strong determination not to act, he binds himself, should he do it, to these things that are burdensome to him, so that their becoming binding upon him if he acts will deter him from doing so.

Likewise, when he says, “If I do it, my wife is divorced, or my slaves are free,” his sole intention is to refrain, and he undertakes, should he act, something extremely burdensome to him

---

## Page 16693

—namely, separation from his wife and loss of his property. His purpose is neither to draw near to Allah through emancipation or charity nor to part from his wife. For this reason, scholars call this a “vow made in obstinacy and anger,” an expression derived from the Prophet’s saying, recorded in both Sahīh collections, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“For one of you to persist in his oath concerning his family is more sinful for him before Allah than to offer the expiation Allah has prescribed for him.”

In its wording, this vow takes the same form as a vow of devotion, yet its meaning is profoundly different. This is the source of the “misconception” that arose among a group of scholars, which we shall discuss in this chapter—if Allah Most High wills. It also reveals the understanding of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, who considered the meanings of expressions rather than their outward forms.

Now that these types of conditional statements have been established, you know that some convey the meaning of an oath expressed through an oath formula, while others do not. Whenever a condition is intended to urge an action, prevent it, affirm a statement, or deny it, the intention is that neither the condition nor its consequence should occur. Such is the case with a vow made in obstinacy, or an oath invoking divorce out of obstinacy and anger.

“The first principle” is that Allah Most High has clarified, through the Book, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus, the ruling concerning one who swears by Allah, Glorified and Exalted be He. Allah Most High says:

“But He will hold you accountable for what your hearts have intended.”

He also says:

“Allah has prescribed for you a release from your oaths.”

And Allah Most High says:

“But He will hold you accountable for the oaths you deliberately make. The expiation for this is to feed ten needy people

---

## Page 16694

with the average food you feed your families, or to clothe them, or to free a slave. Whoever cannot afford this must fast for three days. That is the expiation for your oaths when you swear them. Guard your oaths. Thus Allah makes His signs clear to you, so that you may be grateful.”

As for the Sunnah, the two Sahīh collections report from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Samura that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him:

“O ‘Abd al-Rahmān, do not ask for authority. If you are given it after asking for it, you will be left to bear it on your own; but if you are given it without asking, you will be helped to discharge it. If you swear an oath and then see that another course is better, do what is better and expiate your oath.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus explained to him the ruling concerning a trust, represented here by authority, and the ruling concerning a pledge, represented here by an oath.

In the early days of Islam, before expiation was prescribed, they had no release from their oaths. Hence ‘Ā’isha said: “Abū Bakr would never break an oath until Allah revealed the expiation for oaths.” This is because an oath sworn by Allah is a binding commitment made by invoking Allah, so it must be fulfilled—even more strictly than other binding agreements. Saying “I swear by Allah,” “I take an oath by Allah,” or something similar amounts to saying, “I bind myself by Allah.” This is why these expressions take the preposition bā’, which conveys attachment and is used to express binding and joining. Thus, the undertaking sworn to is bound by invoking Allah, just as one hand is clasped with the other when concluding an agreement. For this reason, Allah calls it a binding commitment when He says:

“But He will hold you accountable for the oaths you deliberately make.”

Since a person has bound his oath by invoking Allah, breaking it would constitute a breach of Allah’s covenant and pledge, were it not for the release Allah has prescribed. This is why breaking it is called hinth (oath-breaking). The original meaning of hinth is sin: breaking an oath would give rise to sin were it not for the expiation that erases it. It is expiation alone that prevents oath-breaking from entailing sin.

---

## Page 16695

The concession permitting expiation for an oath after it has been sworn is paralleled by the concession permitting expiation for zihār, which had counted as divorce in the pre-Islamic period and the early days of Islam. Likewise, īlā’ counted as divorce among them. This accords with the principle that an oath must be fulfilled: if īlā’ required fulfilment of its undertaking to abstain from intercourse, intercourse would become forbidden. An absolute prohibition of intercourse necessarily entails the dissolution of the marital bond—that is, divorce. Likewise, if the prohibition imposed by zihār were binding, it would entail the dissolution of the marital bond, for a woman cannot remain a wife while being absolutely forbidden to her husband.

Hence Allah, Glorified be He, says:

“O Prophet, why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah has already ordained for you release from your oaths.”

Tahilla (“release”) is a verbal noun from hallaltu al-shay’a, uhilluhu (“I released something; I release it”), with the forms tahlīl and tahilla, just as karramtuhu (“I honored him”) has the verbal nouns takrīm and takrima. This verbal noun also names the means of release itself, namely the expiation. If the verbal-noun sense is intended, the meaning is that Allah has prescribed for you the releasing of an oath: its unbinding, which is the opposite of binding it.

For this reason, some scholars of our school and others, such as Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz, cited this verse as evidence for offering expiation before breaking an oath. Release cannot occur after the oath has been broken, since breaking it itself dissolves the oath. It constitutes release only when the expiation is offered before the breach, so that the oath is dissolved. After the breach, it is simply expiation, because it expiates the grounds for sin arising from breaking Allah’s covenant.

Once it is clear, then, that Allah has lifted from this community the obligation to fulfil an oath, which the oath itself entails, by prescribing expiation as a substitute for fulfilment—among the burdens He has lifted from them, as indicated by His words:

“And he relieves them of their burden”

---

## Page 16696

—acts fall into “three” categories: obedience, disobedience, and permissible conduct. If someone swears to do something permissible or to refrain from it, expiation is prescribed by consensus.

The same applies if the oath concerns doing something disliked or abandoning something recommended. This is what is mentioned in Allah’s words:

“Do not make your oaths by Allah an excuse for not doing good, being mindful of Him, and making peace between people.”

If, however, the oath concerns performing an obligation or refraining from something forbidden, then fulfilling it is impermissible by agreement; rather, expiation is obligatory according to the great majority of scholars. Before expiation was prescribed, someone who swore such an oath was not permitted to fulfil it, nor was there any expiation that could relieve him of the consequences of breaking it. Rather, whether he fulfilled it or not, he would be guilty of a sin for which there was no expiation. This is comparable to vowing to commit a sin, according to those who hold that no expiation is prescribed for such a vow. The same applies if the oath concerns performing an act of obedience that is not obligatory.

Section

As for an oath expressed as a vow, known as a “vow of obstinacy and anger,” an example is saying, “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj,” or “my wealth shall be given in charity,” or “I must fast,” intending thereby to deter oneself from that action. Another example is saying, “If I do not do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj,” or something similar.

Most scholars—including those of Mecca, Medina, Basra, and Kufa—hold that expiation for an oath suffices. This is the position of the hadith jurists, such as al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Ishāq, Abū ‘Ubayd, and others. It is also one of the two positions reported from Abū Hanīfa, and is the later report from him.

---

## Page 16697

These scholars then differed among themselves. Most held that a person may choose between fulfilling his vow and offering expiation for an oath. This is al-Shāfi‘ī’s position and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

Others held that expiation alone is required, just as it is required for an oath sworn by Allah. This is the other position reported from Ahmad and the view of some of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers.

Mālik, Abū Hanīfa according to the other report from him, and a group of scholars held that this vow must be fulfilled. It is related that al-Shāfi‘ī was asked about this issue in Egypt and ruled that expiation was required. The questioner said, “Abū ‘Abd Allāh, is this your own view?” He replied, “It is the view of someone better than me: ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh.”

It is also related that when ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Qāsim’s son broke an oath of this kind, he instructed him to offer expiation for an oath, following the position of al-Layth ibn Sa‘d. He added, “If you do it again, I shall give you the ruling according to Mālik’s position”—namely, that the vow must be fulfilled.

For this reason, Mālik’s followers apply the rules governing vows to questions concerning this kind of oath. They rely on the general texts enjoining the fulfilment of vows, including the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him.”

They also reason that this is a valid legal undertaking made contingent upon a condition, so it becomes binding when that condition is met, as with other legal rulings.

The first view, however, is correct. Besides the evidence from the Quran and Sunnah that we shall mention, Allah willing, it is supported by the evidence upon which Imam Ahmad and others relied. Abū Bakr al-Athram said in his Masā’il (Questions):

“I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh being asked about a man who declared that his wealth was dedicated to the Kaaba. He replied, ‘Expiation for an oath,’ citing the hadith of ‘Ā’isha as evidence.”

He also said:

“I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh being asked about a man who swears an oath committing himself to walk to the House of Allah, give his property in charity, or undertake something similar. He replied:

---

## Page 16698

‘If he breaks it, expiation is due. However, I do not urge him to break it so long as he has not done so.’ He was asked, ‘Would you do that?’ Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked, ‘If he breaks it, should he offer expiation?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’ He was asked, ‘Is it not the expiation for an oath?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’”

He said:

“I also heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh discussing the report of Laylā bint al-‘Ajmā’, who swore such-and-such oaths, including that every slave she owned would be free, and was advised to offer expiation for an oath. He cited as evidence the report of Ibn ‘Umar and Ibn ‘Abbās, who gave a ruling concerning someone who had sworn to free a slave woman and had made other oaths. He said, ‘As for the slave woman, she is set free.’”

Al-Athram said: Al-Fadl ibn Dukayn narrated to us, from Hasan, from Ibn Abī Najīh, from ‘Atā’, from ‘Ā’isha, who said:

“Whoever says, ‘My wealth belongs to the Kaaba’s patrimony, all my wealth is dedicated as an offering, and all my wealth is for the poor,’ should offer expiation for his oath.”

He also said: ‘Ārim ibn al-Fadl narrated to us, from Mu‘tamir ibn Sulaymān, who said: My father said: Bakr ibn ‘Abd Allāh narrated to us: Abū Rāfi‘ informed me, saying:

“My mistress, Laylā bint al-‘Ajmā’, declared that every slave she owned would be free, all her wealth would be dedicated as an offering, and she would be a Jew and a Christian, saying, ‘If you do not divorce your wife or separate from her.’

“So I went to Zaynab bint Umm Salama. Whenever a woman learned in religion was mentioned in Medina, Zaynab was mentioned. I went to her, and she accompanied me to my mistress. She said, ‘Hārūt and Mārūt are in this house!’

“She replied, ‘Zaynab, may Allah make me your ransom! She has declared that every slave she owns is free, all her wealth is dedicated as an offering, and she is a Jew and a Christian.’

“Zaynab said, ‘A Jew and a Christian! Do not come between the man and

---

## Page 16699

his wife.’ Then I went to Hafsa, the Mother of the Believers. She sent for my mistress, who came to her and said, ‘Mother of the Believers, may Allah make me your ransom! She has declared that every slave she owns is free, all her wealth is dedicated as an offering, and she is a Jew and a Christian.’

“Hafsa replied, ‘A Jew and a Christian! Do not come between the man and his wife.’

“Then I went to ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, who accompanied me to her. He stood at the door, greeted her, and said, ‘Are you made of stone? Or are you made of iron? What are you made of? Zaynab has given you a ruling, and the Mother of the Believers has given you a ruling, yet you have not accepted her ruling!’

“She said, ‘Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān, may Allah make me your ransom! She has declared that every slave she owns is free, all her wealth is dedicated as an offering, and she is a Jew and a Christian.’

“He replied, ‘A Jew and a Christian! Offer expiation for your oath, and do not come between the man and his wife.’”

Al-Athram said: ‘Abd Allāh ibn Rajā’ narrated to us: ‘Imrān informed us, from Qatāda, from Zurāra ibn Abī Awfā:

A woman asked Ibn ‘Abbās about a woman who had pledged to dedicate her cloak as an offering if she wore it. Ibn ‘Abbās asked, “Did she say this in anger or when she was content?”

They replied, “In anger.”

He said, “Allah, Blessed and Exalted, is not to be approached through anger. Let her offer expiation for her oath.”

He also said: Ibn al-Tabbā‘ narrated to me: Abū Bakr ibn ‘Ayyāsh narrated to us, from al-‘Alā’ ibn al-Musayyib, from Ya‘lā ibn al-Nu‘mān and ‘Ikrima, from Ibn ‘Abbās:

He was asked about a man who had dedicated his wealth to the poor. He replied, “Keep your wealth, spend it on your dependants, pay your debts with it, and offer expiation for your oath.”

---

## Page 16700

Al-Athram also narrated from Ahmad: ‘Abd al-Razzāq narrated to us: Ibn Jurayj narrated to us:

‘Atā’ was asked about a man who said, “I pledge a thousand sacrificial camels.” He replied, “It is an oath.” He was asked about a man who said, “I pledge a thousand pilgrimages.” He replied, “It is an oath.” He was asked about a man who said, “My wealth is dedicated as an offering.” He replied, “It is an oath.” He was asked about a man who said, “My wealth is dedicated to the poor.” He replied, “It is an oath.”

Ahmad said: ‘Abd al-Razzāq narrated to us: Ma‘mar informed us, from Qatāda, from al-Hasan and Jābir ibn Zayd, concerning a man who says, “If I do not do such-and-such, then I am in a state of consecration for Hajj.” They both said, “Only someone who intends to perform Hajj enters the state of consecration. This is an oath for which he must offer expiation.”

Ahmad also said: ‘Abd al-Razzāq narrated to us: Ma‘mar informed us, from Ibn Tāwūs, from his father, who said, “It is an oath for which he must offer expiation.”

Harb al-Kirmānī said: Al-Musayyib ibn Wādih narrated to us: Yūsuf ibn Abī al-Safar narrated to us, from al-Awzā‘ī, from ‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, who said:

I asked Ibn ‘Abbās about a man who swears to walk to Allah’s Sacred House. He replied, “Walking is binding only upon someone who intends it. As for someone who swears in anger, he must offer expiation for an oath.”

Moreover, what counts in speech is its meaning, not its wording. The person taking such an oath does not intend an act of devotion to Allah; rather, he intends to urge an action or prevent it. This is what an oath means: the person swearing intends to urge an action or prevent it. Now, if expiation suffices when he binds himself to that action by invoking Allah Most High, would it not suffice all the more when he makes the obligation of an act of worship, or the prohibition of something permissible, conditional upon that action?

For when he invokes Allah in connection with that action and then breaks his oath, the consequence is that he has undermined his faith in Allah by failing to honor his covenant. But when he makes the obligation or prohibition of an action conditional upon it, the consequence of breaking his oath is merely the omission of an obligation or the commission of something forbidden. It is well known that a breach of oath that

---

## Page 16701

impairs monotheism is graver than one that results in an act of disobedience. If Allah has prescribed expiation to rectify and repair the harm that breaking an oath causes to monotheism and the like, then it is all the more fitting and appropriate that He should prescribe it to rectify the harm that breaking an oath causes to obedience.

We also say that the legal effect of an oath formula is the same as that of a conditional formula. A vow is a kind of oath, and every vow is an oath. Thus, a person’s statement, “I owe it to Allah to do this,” is equivalent to saying, “I swear by Allah that I will certainly do this.” Both statements entail binding oneself to perform the act by invoking Allah. The evidence for this is the Prophet’s statement (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“A vow is an oath.”

Thus, saying, “If I do such-and-such, I am obliged to perform Hajj for Allah,” is equivalent to saying, “If I do such-and-such, then, by Allah, I will certainly perform Hajj.” A consistent application of this principle entails that, if someone swears to perform a righteous deed, he must perform it and may not discharge his obligation through expiation, since his oath to perform it constitutes a vow to do so.

Likewise, a consistent application of this principle entails that, if someone vows to commit a sin or perform a permissible act, he has sworn to do it, just as though he had said, “By Allah, I will certainly do such-and-such.” If he swears by Allah to commit a sin or perform a permissible act, expiation for an oath becomes obligatory upon him; the same therefore applies if he says, “I owe it to Allah to do such-and-such.” Some jurists, both of our school and of others, distinguish between these two cases.

Section

As for oaths involving “divorce and manumission” in contention or anger—where the intention is to urge an action, prevent it, affirm a statement, or deny it—an example is saying, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will certainly do

---

## Page 16702

such-and-such, or not do such-and-such”; or, “If I do such-and-such, my slaves shall be free,” or, “If I do not do it, my slaves shall be free.”

Those earlier jurists who held that vows made in contention or anger must be fulfilled also hold that divorce and manumission take effect in this case. As for the majority, who held that expiation suffices for vows made in contention or anger, they differed on this question. No statement concerning oaths invoking divorce has reached me from any of the Companions; statements on this matter have reached us only from the Successors and those after them, since such oaths were a later development, unknown in the Companions’ time. Statements concerning oaths invoking manumission have, however, reached us, as we shall mention, Allah willing.

The Successors and those after them thus differed concerning oaths invoking divorce and manumission. Some distinguished these from oaths involving vows, saying that divorce and manumission take effect when the oath is broken, and that expiation does not suffice, unlike an oath involving a vow. This is what ʿAwf reported from al-Hasan. It is also the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad—as explicitly transmitted from him—Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Abū ʿUbayd, and others.

Harb al-Kirmānī reported from Muʿtamir ibn Sulaymān, from ʿAwf, that al-Hasan said:

“Every oath, however weighty—even one pledging Hajj and ʿumrah, or dedicating one’s wealth to the poor—is simply an oath, provided it does not involve divorcing a woman who was his wife when he swore, or freeing a male slave whom he owned when he swore.”

Ismāʿīl ibn Saʿīd said:

“I asked Ahmad ibn Hanbal about a man who says to his son, ‘If I speak to you, my wife is divorced and my slave is free.’

“He replied, ‘This does not have the legal standing of an oath; it is binding upon him whether he is angry or content.’”

Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd said:

“Breaking the oath makes the divorce and manumission binding upon him.”

Abū Khaythama held the same view. It was stated by

---

## Page 16703

Ismāʿīl:

“Ahmad ibn Hanbal also informed us, saying: ‘ʿAbd al-Razzāq narrated to us, from Maʿmar, from Ismāʿīl ibn Umayyah, from ʿUthmān ibn Abī Hāzim, that a woman swore that her wealth would be devoted to Allah’s cause or to the poor, and her slave woman would be free, if she did not do such-and-such. She then asked Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās, and they said: “As for the slave woman, she becomes free. As for her declaration concerning her wealth, she is to pay zakah on it.”’”

Abū Ishāq al-Jūzajānī said:

“Divorce and manumission do not have the legal standing of oaths in this matter. If they were treated as oaths, expiation would be obligatory upon anyone who swore by them and then broke his oath. Yet people are agreed that no expiation is due for them.”

I say: Abū Ishāq reported what had reached him of the scholarly positions on this matter. Most of those issuing fatwas at that time among the scholars of Medina and Iraq—the followers of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik—ruled that vows made in contention or anger had to be fulfilled, rather than expiated, even though most of the Successors held that expiation was due.

Indeed, when al-Shāfiʿī ruled in Egypt that expiation was due, his position was unfamiliar among his Mālikī associates. The questioner said to him, “Abū ʿAbd Allāh, is this your own view?” He replied, “It is the view of someone better than me: ʿAtāʾ ibn Abī Rabāh.”
