Thus, when the hadith jurists—such as al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, Ishāq, Abū ʿUbayd, Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd, Ibn Abī Shaybah, ʿAlī ibn al-Madīnī, and others—ruled that expiation was due for oaths involving vows, while some distinguished these from divorce and manumission for reasons we shall mention, the result was that anyone acquainted with the views of both groups

---

## Page 16704

was unaware of any disagreement regarding divorce and manumission. Yet, Allah Most High willing, we shall mention the disagreement reported from the Companions, the Successors, and those who came after them.

Imam Ahmad offered two reasons for setting aside what we have related from the Companions concerning expiation for oaths involving manumission. First, Sulaymān al-Taymī alone reported it. Second, it conflicted with what he narrated from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās: that manumission takes effect without expiation.

I have not found any renowned scholar who received as many reports from the Companions on this issue as Ahmad did. Al-Marwadhī said:

“Abū ʿAbd Allāh said: ‘If someone says, “Every slave I own is free,” his slaves become free when he breaks his oath, because there is no expiation for divorce or manumission.’

“He also said: ‘In the hadith of Laylā bint al-ʿAjmāʾ, related by Abū Rāfiʿ—that she consulted Ibn ʿUmar, Hafsah, and Zaynab, mentioned manumission, and was instructed by them to offer expiation—only al-Taymī includes the wording, “Every slave she owns is free.” The others did not mention manumission.’”

He said:

“I asked Abū ʿAbd Allāh about the hadith of Abū Rāfiʿ, the account concerning his wife, and how she consulted Ibn ʿUmar and Hafsah, who instructed her to offer expiation for an oath. I asked, ‘Does it involve walking on foot?’

“He replied, ‘Yes. My view is that expiation for an oath is due in this case.’

“Abū ʿAbd Allāh also said, ‘Only al-Taymī includes the wording, “Every slave.”’

“I asked, ‘Then if someone swears an oath involving the manumission of his slave and breaks it?’

“He replied, ‘The slave becomes free. This is what is reported from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās: they said that the slave woman becomes free.’

“Then he said, ‘We have heard this only from ʿAbd al-Razzāq, from Maʿmar.’

“I asked, ‘What is its chain of transmission?’

“He replied, ‘Maʿmar, from Ismāʿīl, from ʿUthmān ibn Abī Hāzim, from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās.’

“He also said, ‘Ismāʿīl ibn Umayyah and Ayyūb ibn Mūsā are both Meccans.’”

---

## Page 16705

He thus distinguished oaths involving divorce and manumission from oaths involving vows, on the grounds that neither divorce nor manumission can be discharged through expiation. He followed what had reached him on this matter from Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās, setting it against the reports concerning expiation from Ibn ʿUmar, Hafsah, and Zaynab, especially since al-Taymī alone transmitted this additional wording.

Sālih ibn Ahmad said:

“My father said, ‘What if someone says, “My slave woman is free if I do not do such-and-such”?’

“He said, ‘Ibn ʿUmar and Ibn ʿAbbās said that she becomes free. But if someone says, “All my wealth is for the poor,” without including his slave woman, expiation is due. These two cases are not alike. Do you not see that Ibn ʿUmar distinguished between them? Neither manumission nor divorce can be discharged through expiation.’”

The followers of Abū Hanīfah say that if a man says, “My wealth is for the poor,” he gives it in charity to the poor; and if he says, “My wealth is charity for so-and-so.”

They distinguished between saying, “If I do such-and-such, my wealth is charity,” or, “I must perform Hajj,” and saying, “My wife is divorced,” or, “My slave is free.” In the former case, the legal consequence of the statement is that charity or Hajj becomes obligatory, not that the charity has actually been given or the Hajj performed. Thus, when the condition makes this obligatory, expiation serves as a substitute for that obligation, just as it serves as a substitute for other obligations. In the early days of Islam, it served as a substitute for obligatory fasting, and it remains a substitute for fasting for those unable to fast. Likewise, it serves as a substitute for fasting that remained due from a deceased person. When an obligation rests upon a person, it is possible to give him the choice between fulfilling it and performing something else in its place.

As for manumission and divorce, the legal consequence of the statement is that they actually take effect. Once the condition is fulfilled, manumission or divorce takes effect; and once either has taken effect, it cannot subsequently be undone, since neither admits of rescission. This differs from saying, “If I do such-and-such, I undertake before Allah to free a slave,” for he

---

## Page 16706

has not made manumission itself conditional here; rather, he has made the obligation to manumit conditional. He is therefore given the choice between carrying out the manumission he has made binding upon himself and offering expiation in its place.

Thus, if he says, “When I die, my slave is free,” the slave becomes free upon his death without any further act of manumission. According to the majority, he cannot revoke this arrangement for manumission upon death, although one opinion of al-Shāfiʿī and one report from Ahmad allow it. Whether he may sell the slave is the subject of the well-known disagreement.

But if he makes a bequest for the slave’s manumission, saying, “When I die, set him free,” he may revoke it, as with other bequests. He may also sell the slave in this case, even if selling a slave designated for manumission upon death is not permitted.

Abū ʿAbd Allāh Ibrāhīm ibn Muhammad ibn Muhammad ibn ʿArafah mentioned in his history that, when al-Mahdī saw that his household unanimously favored appointing his son as his successor, he resolved to remove ʿĪsā from the succession and called upon them to pledge allegiance to Mūsā. ʿĪsā refused to relinquish his position, claiming that he was bound by oaths that would strip him of his property and divorce his wives. Al-Mahdī summoned Ibn ʿUlāthah, Muslim ibn Khālid, and a group of jurists for him. They gave him a legal ruling that would release him from his oath, and he received a large sum of money—whose amount the historian mentions—in compensation for what his oath would entail. The matter continued until he was removed and allegiance was pledged to al-Mahdī and to Mūsā al-Hādī as his successor.

As for “Abū Thawr,” he held that, when manumission is made conditional as a form of oath, expiation for an oath suffices, as with a vow made in contention and anger. This was based on the previously cited report concerning Laylā bint al-ʿAjmāʾ, to whom ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, Hafsah, Mother of the Believers, and Zaynab, the stepdaughter of the Messenger of Allah (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), gave a ruling concerning her statement:

“If I do not separate you from your wife, every slave I own is free.”

This account is among those relied upon as evidence by the jurists concerning the question of “a vow

---

## Page 16707

made in contention and anger.” However, Ahmad and Abū ʿUbayd held back from applying this to manumission because of the distinction I have mentioned. Ahmad also objected to this. As for divorce, no transmitted report concerning it had reached Abū Thawr, so he withheld judgment, although he considered it analogous to manumission. He feared, however, that he might be opposing the consensus.

The “correct view” is that disagreement encompasses all these cases—divorce and otherwise—for reasons we shall mention. Even if no specific dissenting view concerning divorce itself had been transmitted, the ruling of those Companions who prescribed expiation for an oath involving manumission would, by implication, indicate the ruling for an oath involving divorce. For if expiation suffices for a vow of manumission—which is an act of devotion—when it takes the form of an oath, then an oath involving divorce, which is not an act of devotion, must either be discharged by expiation or entail no obligation at all, according to those who hold that a vow involving something other than obedience entails no obligation.

Thus, his statement, “If I do such-and-such, you are divorced,” would be equivalent to saying, “Then I am obliged to divorce you,” just as, according to those Companions and those who agreed with them, his statement, “Then my slaves are free,” was equivalent to saying, “Then I am obliged to set them free.”

Nevertheless, to this very day, no statement from any of the Companions concerning oaths involving divorce has reached me. This—and Allah knows best—is because such oaths had not yet arisen in their time. People only introduced them during the time of the Successors and those who came after them, and the Successors and later generations differed over them.

Thus, “one of the two views” is that divorce takes effect through such an oath, as explained above. The “second view” is that divorce does not necessarily take effect.

ʿAbd al-Razzāq related from Ibn Jurayj, from Ibn Tāwūs, from his father, that he

---

## Page 16708

used to say:
“An oath involving divorce has no effect.”
I asked, “Did he regard it as an oath?”
He replied, “I do not know.”

Ibn Tāwūs thus reported that his father did not regard it as effecting divorce, while leaving undecided whether it constituted an oath requiring expiation, since it falls under vows concerning matters that are not acts of devotion. Whether such a declaration constitutes an oath is a well-known point of disagreement.

This is also the view of the Zāhirīs, such as Dāwūd and Abū Muhammad ibn Hazm, though their position rests on the view that neither conditional divorce nor conditional manumission takes effect. They differed concerning deferred divorce and manumission. This rests on the principle mentioned earlier: contracts are valid only when a text or consensus establishes their obligation or permissibility. Their position rests on “three premises” that are disputed:

“The first” is that the presumption regarding contracts is prohibition.

“The second” is that what is analogous in meaning to cases covered by the texts is not thereby rendered permissible.

“The third” is that deferred and conditional divorce do not fall within the general scope of the texts.

As for the previously mentioned reasoning—that this resembles a vow made in contention and anger—this follows by analogy from the position of those who permit expiation for vows made in contention and anger and distinguish devotional vows from vows made in anger. That distinction requires a corresponding distinction between a conditional declaration intended to take effect when its condition is fulfilled and a conditional declaration used as an oath, which is intended not to take effect—unless the aforementioned distinction is valid between making the actual occurrence conditional and making the obligation conditional. We shall discuss this.

We have already explained that this view can be derived from Ahmad’s principles in several ways mentioned earlier. It is likewise a necessary implication of the position of anyone who holds that a vow made in contention and anger entails an expiation

---

## Page 16709

as is the apparent position of al-Shāfiʿī’s school, one of the two positions reported from Abū Hanīfah—which most of his later followers adopted—and one of the two positions reported from Ibn al-Qāsim—which many later Mālikīs adopted. Treating oaths involving vows and oaths involving manumission alike is the sound approach. Hence, this was among the strongest arguments of those who maintained that an oath involving a vow must be fulfilled: they drew an analogy with oaths involving divorce and manumission. Some Mālikīs believed there was consensus on this.

Furthermore, if someone swears using an oath formula, saying, for example, “My slaves are free—I shall certainly do it,” or “My wives are divorced—I shall certainly do it,” this is equivalent to saying, “My wealth is pledged to charity—I shall certainly do it,” or “I am bound to perform Hajj—I shall certainly do it.”

What makes this equivalence clear is that al-Shāfiʿī based his position on conditional divorce solely on the compensation paid for khulʿ (divorce in return for compensation). He stated this in al-Buwaytī, “a work from his Egyptian period” containing some of his finest discussions. Jurists call divorce made conditional upon a particular circumstance “divorce subject to a qualification,” and they call that condition a “qualification.” They say, for example, “If the qualification is fulfilled during a period of irrevocable separation,” or “If the qualification is not fulfilled,” and similar expressions.

There are two explanations for this terminology. “The first” is that this divorce is qualified in some respect, rather than being entirely unqualified. If a man says, “You are divorced at the beginning of the year,” or “when you become free of menstruation,” he has qualified the divorce by specifying a particular time, since an adverbial expression qualifies that to which it applies. Likewise, if he says, “If you give me a thousand, you are divorced,” he has qualified it by specifying the compensation to be paid for it.

---

## Page 16710

And the “second” explanation is that the grammarians of Kufa call prepositions and similar particles “particles of qualification.” Since this divorce is made conditional through particles that may be called “particles of qualification,” it is termed “divorce subject to a qualification,” as when a man says, “You are divorced in return for a thousand.” The “first explanation” is the fundamental one, for this explanation ultimately comes back to it: grammarians call prepositions “particles of qualification” only because a preposition and its object together qualify, in meaning, that to which they relate.

Thus, if al-Shāfiʿī and others based their treatment of qualified divorce solely on divorce in return for compensation, and drew an analogy between it and every divorce subject to a qualification, then this becomes … (1)

Similarly, a vow made conditional upon something is mentioned in the words of the Most High:

“And among them are those who pledged to Allah, ‘If He gives us of His bounty, we will surely give in charity and be among the righteous.’”

It is well known that a conditional vow is a vow subject to a qualification. Yet they distinguished between a vow whose condition one wishes to be fulfilled and a vow whose condition one wishes not to be fulfilled, the latter serving as an oath. Accordingly, a distinction should be drawn between a divorce whose qualification one wishes to be fulfilled—such as divorce in return for compensation, where the compensation is what is sought—and a divorce invoked in an oath, where neither the divorce nor its condition is intended to occur. For an analogy with what is found in the Book and the Sunnah may be drawn only where there is a relevant resemblance. The distinction between a qualification whose fulfilment is desired and one invoked in an oath whose fulfilment is not desired is well established, exactly as the distinction between the two is made in the case of vows.

The evidence for this position is the Book, the Sunnah, transmitted reports, and reasoning.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 16711

As for the Book, Allah, Glorified is He, says:

“O Prophet, why do you prohibit what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. Allah has already ordained for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths. Allah is your Protector, and He is the All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

The evidence lies in Allah’s words: “Allah has already ordained for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.” This is a general text establishing that Allah has prescribed a means of release from every oath Muslims swear. He, Glorified is He, addresses the community in the plural after first addressing the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, in the singular, knowing that the community swears many different kinds of oaths. If even one oath were posited for which there was no means of release, that would contradict the verse. How could that be, when not a single case has been excluded from this general text by either a scriptural text or consensus? Indeed, its generality extends to its underlying rationale as well as its wording: an oath is a bond that bars the legally accountable person from acting, so prescribing a means of release from this bond is fitting because of the relief and latitude it affords. This consideration applies even more strongly to oaths invoking manumission or divorce than to other oaths involving vows made in obstinacy and anger.

For if a man swears by divorce that he will kill someone, sever his ties of kinship, or withhold something obligatory upon him, such as returning something entrusted to him, then by invoking divorce in his oath he makes it a greater obstacle to doing good, being mindful of Allah, and reconciling people than invoking Allah in his oath would be. If he then fulfils his oath, he incurs worldly and religious harm that Muslims unanimously agree it is forbidden to bring upon oneself. If, instead, he divorces his wife, divorce too entails obvious religious and worldly harm.

As for religious harm, the entire Muslim community agrees that divorce is disapproved of when relations between the spouses are sound:

---

## Page 16712

whether as something merely discouraged or as something forbidden. How much more serious, then, when the spouses are deeply attached to one another, with children and a shared life between them, so that their divorce would cause immense religious harm. The worldly harm is likewise immense, as experience attests: if either spouse were given the choice between losing their wealth and homeland or divorce, they would choose to part with their wealth and homeland rather than divorce. Indeed, Allah has mentioned separation from one’s homeland alongside being killed.

For this reason, according to one of the two reports from him, Imam Ahmad, following ʿAtā’, said that if a woman enters the state of consecration for Hajj and her husband then swears by divorce that she will not perform Hajj, she is considered prevented from completing the pilgrimage and may leave the state of consecration. This is because the harm she would suffer exceeds, or is comparable to, the harm caused by being prevented by an enemy.

This is clear when a man says, “If you do such-and-such, I shall be obliged to divorce you or free my slaves.” By agreement, this falls under vows made in obstinacy and anger, just as if he had said, “By Allah, I shall divorce you or free my slaves.” Those who distinguish between the two cases have relied solely on the distinction between manumission taking effect and its becoming obligatory. We shall discuss this, if Allah Most High wills.

Furthermore, Allah says:

“Why do you prohibit what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

This implies that whenever someone prohibits what Allah has made lawful, Allah is forgiving and merciful toward that person, and that there is no cause requiring that prohibition to remain in force. For His word “Why?” means “For what reason?” It is a question conveying negation and disapproval. The meaning is: there is no reason for you to prohibit what Allah has made lawful for you, and Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.

Thus, if someone who swears, by invoking a vow, manumission, or divorce, that he will not do something were afforded no concession, there would then be a cause requiring something lawful to become prohibited, and the grounds for forgiveness and mercy toward that person would no longer hold.

---

## Page 16713

Furthermore, Allah, glorified and exalted is He, says:

“O you who believe, do not prohibit the good things Allah has made lawful for you,”

through to His words:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn. And guard your oaths.”

The argument drawn from this passage is like that drawn from the first, and stronger still. For He says:

“Do not prohibit the good things Allah has made lawful for you.”

This applies generally to prohibiting them through oaths invoking divorce or anything else. He then explains the way out of this by saying:

“Allah will not hold you accountable for your thoughtless oaths, but He will hold you accountable for the oaths you have bound yourselves by. Its expiation, then…”

That is, the expiation for your making oaths binding, or contracting them. This is general in scope. He then says:

“That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn.”

This is general, just as His words “And guard your oaths” are general.

What further clarifies its “general scope” is that they have included oaths invoking divorce within the general meaning of the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ may act if he wishes and refrain if he wishes.”

Thus, they included within it oaths invoking divorce, manumission, and vows, as well as oaths sworn by Allah. Mālik, Ahmad, and others excluded an immediate pronouncement of divorce, in agreement with Ibn ‘Abbās, only because actually pronouncing divorce is not swearing an oath. A binding oath must include both something by which one swears and a matter sworn to, whether expressed through an oath formula, a conditional formula, or something equivalent, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

This argument points to the principles adopted by al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and those who agree with them concerning “vows made in obstinacy and anger.” They cited this verse as evidence for expiation in such cases, treating His words “release from your oaths” and “the expiation for your oaths” as encompassing both oaths sworn by Allah and oaths invoking vows. It is well known that the wording encompasses vows made in obstinacy and anger equally, whether they concern Hajj, manumission, or similar acts.

---

## Page 16714

If it is objected:

“The verse refers only to an oath sworn by Allah, for this is what is understood when an oath is mentioned without qualification. Moreover, the definiteness conveyed by the definite article and the possessive construction in His words ‘the oaths you have bound yourselves by’ and ‘release from your oaths’ may refer to the kind of oath familiar to them, namely, an oath sworn by Allah. In that case, the wording encompasses only what was familiar to them. Oaths invoking divorce and the like were not familiar to them. Even if the wording is general, we have been taught that it does not include unlawful oaths, such as oaths sworn by created beings. Thus, it does not include oaths invoking divorce and the like, since these are not lawful oaths, as indicated by his statement:

‘Whoever swears an oath, let him swear by Allah or remain silent.’”

This is the objection raised by those who maintain that an unlawful oath entails neither expiation nor breach.

The response is that the term “oath” encompasses all these forms, as shown by the Prophet’s usage, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and that of the Companions and scholars. For example, he said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “A vow is an oath.” The Companions also told someone who had sworn an oath committing himself to a sacrificial offering or manumission, “Expiate your oath.” This is likewise what the Companions understood from the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as we shall mention. Furthermore, the scholars included these forms within his statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ may act if he wishes and refrain if he wishes.”

Its general scope in the verse is also indicated by the fact that He, glorified is He, said:

“Why do you prohibit what Allah has made lawful for you?”

Then He said:

“Allah has already prescribed for you release from your oaths.”

This implies that the very act of declaring something lawful forbidden is an oath, as Ibn ‘Abbās and others inferred from it.

The occasion of the verse’s revelation was either his declaring honey forbidden to himself or his declaring Māriyah

---

## Page 16715

the Coptic woman forbidden to himself. In either case, according to the apparent meaning of the verse, declaring something lawful forbidden is an oath, though not an oath sworn by Allah. For this reason, the majority of the Companions—including ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās, and others—ruled that declaring something lawful forbidden constitutes an oath subject to expiation: either a “major expiation,” as in zihār, or a “minor expiation,” as with an oath sworn by Allah. The early generations consistently referred to zihār and similar acts as oaths.

Furthermore, His words, “Why do you prohibit what Allah has made lawful for you?” may mean: Why do you prohibit it by expressly declaring it forbidden? Or: Why do you prohibit it by swearing an oath by Allah Most High or a similar oath? Or: Why do you prohibit it, in an unrestricted sense?

If the first or third meaning is intended, then it is established that declaring something forbidden without swearing by Allah constitutes an oath; thus, the wording has a general scope. If what is intended is prohibiting it by swearing by Allah, then Allah has described swearing by Him as prohibiting something lawful. It is well known that an oath sworn by Allah does not make the act itself unlawful under divine law. However, because it requires the person who swore it to refrain from the act, it prohibits the act for him conditionally, rather than making it unlawful in itself under divine law. Thus, every oath that requires him to refrain from an act prohibits that act for him and falls within the general scope of His words:

“Why do you prohibit what Allah has made lawful for you?”

Accordingly, His words, “Allah has already prescribed for you release from your oaths,” must encompass every oath that prohibits something lawful. This is the ruling governing that act, so it must cover all its forms. For declaring something lawful forbidden is what occasioned His words:

“Allah has already prescribed for you release from your oaths.”

When the circumstance occasioning a response is general, the response must also be general; otherwise, it would address only some cases despite the presence of a reason requiring its application to all. The same reasoning applies to the passage beginning with His words, Most High, “O you who believe, do not prohibit the good things Allah has made lawful for you,” and continuing through His words, “That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn.”

---

## Page 16716

Moreover, the Companions understood the verse in this general sense. Likewise, scholars generally understood it to encompass oaths sworn by Allah and other oaths.

We may also say: Very well, let us concede that the oath mentioned in the verse means an oath sworn by Allah Most High, and that no binding ruling results from any other oath. It is nevertheless well known that swearing by His attributes is like swearing by Him, as when someone says, “By Allah’s might,” “By Allah’s life,” or “By the Magnificent Quran.” Reports from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and the Companions establish the permissibility of swearing by these attributes and others like them. Furthermore, swearing by His attributes is like seeking refuge in them—even though refuge may be sought only in Allah—as in the Prophet’s words (peace and blessings be upon him):

“I seek refuge in Your Face.”

“I seek refuge in Allah’s perfect words.”

“I seek refuge in Your pleasure from Your wrath.”

There are other examples of this, and it is well established among the scholars.

That being so, an oath involving a vow, divorce, or the like is an oath by Allah’s attributes. When someone says, “If I do such-and-such, I must perform Hajj,” he has sworn by the imposition of Hajj upon himself as an obligation. Making Hajj obligatory upon him is one of Allah Most High’s rulings, and it is among His attributes. The same applies if he says, “Then I must free a slave.”

When he says, “Then my wife is divorced and my slave is free,” he has sworn by the termination of his ownership, which amounts to making it forbidden to him. Making something forbidden is among Allah’s attributes, just as making something obligatory is among His attributes. Allah has counted this among His signs in His words:

“Do not take Allah’s signs in jest.”

Thus He counted the issuing of such rulings concerning marriage, divorce, and divorce by redemption among His signs.

But when someone swears by making something obligatory or forbidden, he has made his oath a commitment to Allah, just as he makes a vow to Allah. For his statement, “I must perform Hajj and fast,” constitutes a pledge

---

## Page 16717

to Allah. But when he is swearing an oath, he does not intend to make a pledge to Allah; rather, he intends to swear by it. If he breaks his oath and does not fulfil it, he has abandoned what he pledged to Allah, just as, if he performs the act sworn about, he has abandoned what he pledged to Allah.

“This is clarified as follows:”

When someone swears by Allah, or by something other than Allah that he honours by swearing by it, he does so only to bind the matter sworn about to that by which he swears. His reverence for it in his heart means that, once he has bound something by it, he will not undo that bond. If he does undo what he has bound by it, his reverence for it in his heart has diminished, and he has severed the bond between himself and it.

As some have said:

“An oath is a binding commitment one imposes upon oneself in recognition of the right of one who has a right over him.”

Thus, a deliberately false oath is among the major sins that incur the Fire, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who exchange Allah’s covenant and their oaths for a small price will have no share in the Hereafter. Allah will neither speak to them nor look at them on the Day of Resurrection, nor will He purify them; and they will have a painful punishment.”

The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) also included it when enumerating the major sins. This is because, when someone deliberately binds something by Allah when it is not truly so bound, he impairs the bond between himself and his Lord. He is like someone who attributes to Allah something of which He is entirely free, or who disavows Allah. This differs from swearing about the future: in that case, he binds an action by Allah, intending to make that commitment out of reverence for Him. Allah, however, has permitted him to dissolve the commitment he has made, just as He permits him to leave certain obligations unfulfilled because of a need, or removes their obligatory character for him.

For this reason, most scholars have said that if someone says, “I am a Jew or a Christian if I do not do that,” this constitutes an oath equivalent to saying, “By Allah, I shall certainly do it,” because he has linked the non-performance

---

## Page 16718

of the act to his disbelief, which is his disavowal of Allah. He has thus linked the act to his faith in Allah, and this is the very essence of swearing by Allah. Linking an act to Allah’s rulings of obligation or prohibition is therefore of a lesser order than linking it to Allah Himself.

“This is clarified as follows:”

When someone binds an oath by Allah, he binds it by his faith in Allah: the reverence and honour for Allah within his heart, namely Allah’s grandeur and His highest description in the heavens and the earth. Likewise, when he glorifies Allah and remembers Him, he does so in proportion to the knowledge of Him and worship of Him within his heart. Thus, glorification is sometimes expressed in relation to Allah’s name, as in His words:

“And remember the name of your Lord morning and evening.”

Alongside this, He says:

“Remember Allah with much remembrance.”

Whenever a servant honours his Lord by glorifying His name, swearing by Him, or seeking refuge in Him, he glorifies Him through the highest description present in his heart: his knowledge of Him, worship of Him, reverence for His greatness, and love for Him—in knowledge and excellence, veneration and honour. Whether he is judged to have faith or disbelief depends solely on what his heart has acquired of these things, as Allah, glorified be He, says:

“Allah will not hold you accountable for what is unintentional in your oaths, but He will hold you accountable for what your hearts have earned.”

And elsewhere:

“But He will hold you accountable for the oaths you have deliberately made binding.”

Thus, if the Lawgiver were to consider what the wording of an oath entails—its being bound by oaths and linked to it—without regard to the intention of swearing, this would entail that, if he broke it by something other than his oaths, its reality would cease, as in the saying:

“The adulterer is not a believer while committing adultery.”

And likewise, if he swears an oath concerning that

---

## Page 16719

falsely, it is one of the major sins. If he uses it to acquire property protected by the Law, he will have no share in the Hereafter; Allah will neither speak to him on the Day of Resurrection nor purify him, and he will have a painful punishment. Yet the Lawgiver knows that someone who swears that he will or will not do something does not intend to dishonour the sanctity of Allah’s name or invoke it for the purpose pursued by someone swearing a deliberately false oath. He therefore prescribed expiation for him, allowing this bond to be dissolved. He waived expiation for an unintentional oath, because the person’s heart had formed no resolve involving an offence against his faith, so there is no need for expiation.

Once it becomes clear that the wording of an oath entails binding the act to that oath—which is his faith in Allah—then, if the act does not occur, his wording would entail the absence of his faith. This would be so were it not for the expiation Allah has prescribed. Similarly, when someone says, “If I do such-and-such, such-and-such becomes obligatory upon me,” doing the former would make the latter obligatory, were it not for the expiation Allah has prescribed.

This is clarified by the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever swears an oath invoking a religion other than Islam is as he has said.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded it in their Sahīh collections. Thus, a deliberately false oath expressed by someone saying that he is a Jew or a Christian if he has done such-and-such is treated like the deliberately false oath, “By Allah, I have not done such-and-such.” In both cases, he has severed his covenant with Allah, having made faith contingent upon something that is not the case, and disbelief upon something that is the case. This differs from an oath concerning the future.

Pursuing this reasoning consistently entails that, when a deliberately false oath involves a vow, divorce, or emancipation, the stipulated consequence takes effect and expiation does not undo it—just as disbelief results from such an oath according to one of the two scholarly views.

This also provides the answer to their statement: “What is meant is an oath sanctioned by the Law.”

---

## Page 16720

There are also His words, glorified and exalted is He:

“Do not make Allah, through your oaths, an obstacle to doing good, being pious, and making peace between people. Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

The early generations unanimously, or almost unanimously, understood this to mean that, when you swear by Allah, you must not make Him an obstacle to goodness, piety, and reconciliation between people. For example, a man might swear not to perform a recommended or obligatory good deed, or swear to do something disliked or forbidden, or something similar. Then, when told, “Do that,” or, “Do not do this,” he replies, “I have sworn by Allah!” He thus makes Allah an obstacle by invoking Him in his oath.

Now, Allah has forbidden His servants to make Him, through their oaths, an obstacle to goodness and piety. If swearing these other oaths falls within the general scope of swearing by Him, it must not constitute an obstacle either. This follows by reasoning from the greater to the lesser: if He, glorified is He, has forbidden us to make Him an obstacle, through our oaths, to goodness and piety, then we are all the more clearly forbidden to make anything else such an obstacle.

Once it is clear that we are forbidden to make anything, through our oaths, an obstacle to doing good, being pious, and making peace between people, it is evident that this is because goodness, piety, and reconciliation embody what Allah loves and commands. Thus, if a man swears an oath involving a vow, divorce, or emancipation that he will not do good, act piously, or bring about reconciliation, he faces two alternatives. If he keeps his oath, he has made these things an obstacle, through that oath, to doing good, being pious, and making peace between people. If he breaks it, divorce takes effect and he becomes obliged to perform what he vowed. Yet separation from his wife may take him further from goodness and piety than the conduct specified in his oath.

If he maintains his oath, he forsakes goodness and piety; and if he parts with his wife and wealth, he forsakes goodness and piety. These things have thus become an obstacle, through his oath, to doing good and being pious. There is no way out of this predicament except through expiation.

---

## Page 16721

This meaning is also established by the Sunnah.

In the two Sahīhs, Hammām narrates from Abū Hurayrah that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“For one of you to persist in an oath concerning his family is more sinful in Allah’s sight than to offer the expiation Allah has prescribed for him.”

Al-Bukhārī also narrated through ‘Ikrimah, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever stubbornly persists in an oath concerning his family incurs a greater sin.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus explained that stubbornly persisting in an oath concerning one’s family is graver than making expiation. Obstinacy means persisting in a dispute; hence a man who persists in a dispute is called obstinate. This is why scholars call this a “vow of obstinacy and anger”: a person persists obstinately until he binds himself by the vow, then persists in refusing to break it. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, thus made clear that stubbornly persisting in an oath is more sinful than making expiation. This applies generally to all oaths.

Furthermore, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Samurah:

“If you swear an oath and then see that another course is better, do what is better and make expiation for your oath.”

Both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded this in their Sahīhs. Another version in the two Sahīhs reads:

“Make expiation for your oath and do what is better.”

Muslim also narrated in his Sahīh, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should make expiation for his oath and do what is better.”

Another version reads:

“He should do what is better and make expiation for his oath.”

This is an indefinite expression

---

## Page 16722

in a conditional context, so it encompasses every instance of swearing an oath, whatever form that oath may take. If a person sees that a course other than the one to which he has sworn is better—whether he has sworn to refrain from something good and sees that doing it is better than leaving it, or has sworn to do something evil and sees that refraining from it is better than doing it—the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has commanded him to do what is better and make expiation for his oath.

The wording here, “concerning an oath,” is—and Allah knows best—an instance of using a verbal noun to denote the object of the action. The matter sworn to is called an “oath,” just as “creation” may denote what is created, “beating” what is beaten, and “sale” what is sold, and so forth.

Likewise, both al-Bukhārī and Muslim recorded in the two Sahīhs:

“Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī, in the account of himself and his companions, related that when they came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to ask him for mounts, he said: ‘By Allah, I will not provide you with mounts, and I have none to give you.’ Then he said: ‘By Allah, if Allah wills, whenever I swear an oath and then see that another course is better, I do what is better and release myself from the oath.’”

Another version in the two Sahīhs reads:

“I make expiation for my oath and do what is better.”

Muslim narrated in his Sahīh that ‘Adī ibn Hātim said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If one of you swears an oath and then sees that another course is better, he should make expiation for it and do what is better.”

Another version, also recorded by Muslim, reads:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should make expiation for it and do what is better.”

This Sunnah has also been narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through channels other than

---

## Page 16723

these, in reports from ‘Abdullāh ibn ‘Umar and ‘Awf ibn Mālik al-Jushamī. These mass-transmitted statements of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, establish that he commanded anyone who swore an oath and then saw that another course was better to make expiation for his oath and do what was better. He made no distinction between swearing by Allah and using a vow or the like as an oath.

Al-Nasā’ī narrated from Abū Mūsā that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no oath on earth that I swear, then see that another course is better, but that I take the better course.”

This explicitly shows that he intended to include every oath on earth. The Companions likewise understood his words to include using vows as oaths. Abū Dāwūd narrated in his Sunan: Muhammad ibn al-Minhāl narrated to us; Yazīd ibn Zuray‘ narrated to us; Khubayb al-Mu‘allim narrated to us, from ‘Amr ibn Shu‘ayb, from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib:

Two brothers from the Ansār jointly held an inheritance. One asked the other to divide it, whereupon the latter said, “If you ask me again to divide it, all my wealth shall be dedicated to the Kaaba.”

‘Umar said to him:

“The Kaaba has no need of your wealth. Make expiation for your oath and speak to your brother. I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

‘No oath or vow is binding upon you if it involves disobedience to the Lord, severing ties of kinship, or something one does not own.’”

Thus, the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, instructed this man—who had sworn an oath in conditional form and made a vow of obstinacy and anger—to make expiation for his oath rather than carry out what he had vowed. He cited as evidence what he had heard the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

“No oath or vow is binding upon you if it involves disobedience to the Lord, severing ties of kinship, or something one does not own.”

It was thus understood that whoever undertakes an oath or vow involving disobedience or severing ties of kinship is not obliged to fulfil that vow, but only to make expiation, as ‘Umar ruled for him. Were it not that this vow

---

## Page 16724

was regarded by him as an oath, he would not have told him, “Make expiation for your oath.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “No oath or vow is binding,” because an oath is intended to urge or restrain, whereas a vow is intended to draw closer to Allah. Neither is to be fulfilled when it involves disobedience or severing ties of kinship.

This hadith provides further evidence: the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “No oath or vow is binding if it involves disobedience to the Lord or severing ties of kinship,” encompasses everything called an oath or a vow. This applies whether the oath is sworn by Allah, involves making an otherwise non-obligatory act binding—such as charity, fasting, pilgrimage, or a sacrificial offering—or involves making something lawful forbidden, as with zihār (declaring one’s wife forbidden to oneself), divorce, or manumission.

The Prophet’s intent, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was either merely to prohibit the sworn act of disobedience or severance of kinship, or, in addition, to indicate that the obligations and prohibitions stipulated in the oath or vow are not binding upon the person. The latter is the apparent meaning, since ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb cited it as evidence. Had the hadith not indicated this, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, could not validly have cited it to support his answer to the questioner: that he should make expiation rather than spend his wealth on the covering of the Kaaba. Moreover, the Prophet’s wording, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, encompasses all of this.

Further evidence that oaths involving vows, divorce, and manumission fall within the terms “oath” and “swearing” in the words of Allah Most High and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the report of Ibn ‘Umar, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ is not held to have broken his oath.”

It was narrated by Ahmad, al-Nasā’ī, Ibn Mājah, and al-Tirmidhī, who said, “A good hadith.”

---

## Page 16725

and Abū Dāwūd, whose wording is: Ahmad ibn Hanbal narrated to us: Sufyān narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, who traced it back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ has made an exception.”

He also narrated it through ‘Abd al-Razzāq, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and makes an exception may retract it if he wishes or leave it if he wishes, without being held to have broken his oath.”

Abū Hurayrah reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ is not held to have broken his oath.”

It was narrated by Ahmad, al-Tirmidhī, and Ibn Mājah, whose wording is, “His exception holds,” and by al-Nasā’ī, whose wording is, “He has made an exception.”

Now, jurists generally included oaths involving vows, divorce, and manumission within this hadith, holding that making an exception by invoking Allah’s will is effective in such oaths. Indeed, many of Ahmad’s followers hold that its effectiveness in oaths involving divorce is undisputed within his school, and that disagreement concerns only cases expressed in conditional form. What most of them exclude is the actual pronouncement of divorce or manumission itself. The distinction between effecting either of these and swearing an oath involving them is clear. Allah willing, we shall discuss “The Rule of Making an Exception.”

Since they have included oaths involving these matters within his statement:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ is not held to have broken his oath,”

they likewise fall within his statement:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should do what is better and make expiation for his oath.”

The wording of both is the same, as is clear to anyone who reflects upon it. For the general scope of his statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ is not held to have broken his oath,”

is the same as that of his statement:

---

## Page 16726

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better should do what is better and make expiation for his oath.”

Since the wording of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning the ruling on making an exception is the same as his wording concerning the ruling on expiation, it necessarily follows that whenever making an exception is effective, expiation is also effective, and whenever expiation is effective, making an exception is also effective. Ahmad explicitly stated this in several places.

Whoever claims that, by saying:

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ is not held to have broken his oath,”

the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, intended all oaths that people swear—whether by Allah or involving vows, divorce, or manumission—but that, by saying:

“Whoever swears an oath and then sees that another course is better,”

he intended only oaths by Allah, or only oaths by Allah and those involving vows, holds a weak view. For the import of one wording was just as present in the mind of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as the import of the other, since both use the same wording. Moreover, the ruling in both is of the same kind: release from the oath, whether by making an exception or by expiation.

Having said this, know that the Muslim community has divided into three groups over whether divorce and manumission fall within the hadith concerning making an exception.

One group said that divorce and manumission themselves fall within it. Thus, even if someone says, “You are divorced, if Allah wills,” or, “You are free, if Allah wills,” this falls within the general scope of the hadith. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī, and others.

---

## Page 16727

Another group said that divorce and manumission do [not] (1) fall within it—neither their actual enactment nor oaths involving them, whether expressed in conditional form or in the form of an oath. This is the better-known of the two positions in Mālik’s school and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad.

The “third view” is that actually effecting divorce or manumission does not fall within it, whereas swearing an oath involving divorce or manumission does. This is the second position reported from Ahmad. Some scholars of his school said: If it is expressed in the form of an oath, it falls within the hadith, and saying “If Allah wills” is effective, according to the sole position reported from him on this point. If it is expressed in conditional form, however, two positions are reported from him.

This “third view” is the correct one, whose substance is transmitted from the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the majority of the Successors, such as Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib and al-Hasan. They did not recognize an exception in divorce, nor did they class divorce among oaths. Yet we have already mentioned that the Companions and the majority of the Successors regarded oaths involving charity, sacrificial offerings, manumission, and the like as oaths for which expiation may be made.

This is the meaning of Ahmad’s statement in several places: “As for making an exception in divorce and manumission, these two are not oaths.” He also said: “I do not recognize making an exception in divorce, because divorce and manumission are two pronouncements that take effect.”

He also said: “An exception applies only where expiation is possible, and neither divorce nor manumission can be expiated.”

What he said is clear, for actually effecting divorce or manumission is not an oath at all. Rather, it is comparable to waiving the right to legal retribution or releasing someone from a debt. Thus, if someone were to say, “By Allah, I will not swear an oath,” and then freed a slave he owned, divorced his wife, or released

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 228, footnote): “In al-Fatāwā, the reading is yadkhul (‘is included’), which clearly contradicts the context.”

---

## Page 16728

someone liable to him from a claim concerning bloodshed, property, or injury to his honor, he would not have broken his oath. I know of no one who disagrees with this. Thus, whoever includes actually effecting divorce or manumission within the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“Whoever swears an oath and says, ‘If Allah wills,’ will not have broken his oath,”

has made the general wording bear a meaning it cannot bear. Likewise, whoever excludes from this general wording someone’s saying, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will certainly do such-and-such—or will not do it—if Allah wills,” or, “If I do it, my wife is divorced, if Allah wills,” has excluded something that falls within it. For this is an oath involving divorce or manumission.

Here Ahmad should be followed in his statement: “Divorce and manumission are not oaths.” Swearing an oath involving them, however, is like swearing an oath involving charity, Hajj, and the like. This is self-evident in reason, customary usage, and Islamic law. Thus, if someone says, “By Allah, I will never swear an oath,” and then says, “If I do such-and-such, my wife is divorced,” he has broken his oath.

We have already mentioned that the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called this an oath. All the jurists likewise called it an oath, as did Muslims generally. It also has the substance of an oath. For when someone says, “I swear by Allah that I will certainly do it, if Allah wills,” the reference to Allah’s will, unless otherwise qualified, relates to the act sworn to. The meaning is: “I am swearing to do this act if Allah wills that it be done.” If the person does not do it, Allah has not willed it, and so he is not bound to do it. But if he intended the reference to relate to the swearing itself—meaning that the person taking the oath intended, “If Allah wills that I be swearing an oath”—the meaning of this would differ from making an exception in performative pronouncements such as divorce,

---

## Page 16729

and, according to the majority view, this does not avail him.

Likewise, when someone says, “Divorce shall be binding upon me: I will certainly do such-and-such, if Allah wills,” the reference to Allah’s will, unless otherwise qualified, relates to the act. The meaning is: “I will certainly do it if Allah wills that I do it.” Whenever he does not do it, Allah has not willed it, and so divorce is not binding upon him. This differs from his intending, by “Divorce shall be binding upon me, if Allah wills,” that divorce should bind him if Allah wills it to bind him. This is equivalent to saying, “You are divorced, if Allah wills.”

Ahmad’s statement, “The qualification ‘if Allah wills’ applies only where expiation is applicable, whereas divorce and manumission cannot be expiated,” is sound and eloquent. As explained above, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, set out the ruling on this qualification and the ruling on expiation in one and the same conditional formulation. No distinction should therefore be drawn between what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, joined together. Rather, the qualification concerns only that upon which the act has been made conditional. Legal effects such as divorce, manumission, and the like are not made conditional upon the will of Allah Most High once their legal causes exist, for they necessarily follow from their causes. Once those causes have been established, Allah has already willed their effects. Only events that Allah may or may not will—such as people’s actions and similar occurrences—are made conditional upon His will. Expiation was prescribed only for breaking an oath that may sometimes be fulfilled through compliance and at other times broken through noncompliance.

The obligation to offer expiation upon breaking an oath that may be fulfilled or broken corresponds to the removal of an oath’s binding force by making it conditional upon Allah’s will: an oath may or may not be made conditional in this way.

Thus, anyone who swears that he will do something and then does not do it has not broken his oath if he made it conditional upon Allah’s will. If he did not make it conditional upon Allah’s will, expiation becomes binding upon him. Accordingly, qualification by Allah’s will and expiation apply as alternatives to an oath when it is not fulfilled.

---

## Page 16730

Thus, this is a sound principle that rules out the unwarranted additions and omissions that have arisen in this matter. It accords with what the words of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, require.

It may then be said that Ahmad’s and others’ statement, “Divorce and manumission cannot be expiated,” is like their statement, “Neither admits the qualification ‘if Allah wills.’” This concerns actually effecting divorce or manumission. As for oaths involving them, expiation is not for divorce or manumission themselves, but for the oath involving them.

Likewise, when someone binds himself by oath to prayer, fasting, charity, pilgrimage, sacrificial offerings, or similar acts, in a vow made in contentiousness and anger, he does not offer expiation for the prayer, fasting, charity, pilgrimage, or sacrificial offerings themselves. Rather, he offers expiation for the oath involving them. Prayer itself does not admit expiation, nor do these other acts of worship admit expiation for someone capable of performing them.

Similarly, if someone says, “If I do such-and-such, I shall be obliged to free a slave,” expiation is binding upon him, without disagreement within the school of Ahmad and among those who agree with him concerning vows made in contentiousness and anger. This is not expiation for manumission itself, but for the oath involving it.

It therefore follows from this statement of Ahmad that, if he holds that oaths involving divorce and manumission may validly be qualified by ‘if Allah wills,’ then expiation for such oaths must also be valid. This is what the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, requires, as we have explained above.

As for the view that oaths involving divorce and manumission do not admit this qualification—as held in one of the two views within Ahmad’s school and in Mālik’s school—it is a less well-supported view. Here, however, we are discussing the matter only on the assumption that it is conceded. Allah willing, we shall discuss “the question of qualification by Allah’s will” separately.

---

## Page 16731

If Ahmad or another scholar says that oaths involving divorce and manumission admit no expiation because they do not admit qualification by ‘if Allah wills,’ this statement entails a conclusion concerning such qualification in oaths involving them.

As for those among Ahmad’s followers who distinguish between the two, saying, “Qualification by ‘if Allah wills’ is valid in oaths involving them, but expiation is not,” I know of no explicit statement from Ahmad making this distinction. They are, however, excused for understanding his words in this way, since they did not find two explicitly reported positions from him concerning expiation for oaths involving them, as they did concerning qualification by ‘if Allah wills’ in such oaths. Nevertheless, the permissibility of expiation necessarily follows from one of the two positions reported from him—the very position they defend.

Anyone other than the prophets may make a statement whose necessary implications he does not recognize. Were he to recognize them, he might accept them; or, if he did not, he might retract the statement from which they follow, or retain it while believing that those implications do not follow.

When jurists of our school and others derive rulings from the implications of a scholar’s statements and his analogical reasoning, either he has made no explicit statement affirming or denying the implication in question, or he has explicitly denied it. If he has explicitly denied it, he may or may not also have explicitly denied that it necessarily follows from his position.

If he has explicitly denied that implication, yet they derive from his position a ruling contrary to his express statement on that issue, this may occur, for example, when he gives two different rulings on two similar issues, or bases a ruling on a rationale that he contradicts elsewhere. Thus, Ahmad here explained the absence of expiation by the absence of qualification by ‘if Allah wills,’ although two positions are reported from him concerning that qualification.

This rests on the question of deriving a ruling on a matter concerning which he has expressed neither affirmation nor denial: may that ruling be called his position, or not? There is a well-known disagreement among the scholars of our school concerning this.

---

## Page 16732

Thus al-Athram, al-Khiraqī and others regard it as his position, whereas al-Khallāl, his associate and others do not. The precise assessment is that it follows by analogy from his position and is a necessary implication of it. It therefore does not have the same standing as a position explicitly reported from him, nor does it have the same standing as something that is not a necessary implication of his position. Rather, it occupies an intermediate position between the two. This applies where it is possible for that implication not to bind him.

Furthermore, Allah has legislated divorce, permitting it, commanding it, or making it binding when the person concerned pronounces it. The same applies to manumission and vows. These legal acts—vows, divorce and manumission—entail certain things becoming obligatory or forbidden for a person. Such obligations and prohibitions become binding upon him only if he intends them or intends their cause. If these words pass his lips unintentionally, nothing becomes binding upon him, by agreement. If he utters them under coercion, their legal effect does not bind him according to our school and the majority of scholars, as indicated by the Sunnah and reports from the Companions. His sole purpose is to ward off the harm threatened against him: he intends neither their legal effect nor to utter them of his own accord.

The same applies when someone swears an oath, saying, “If I do not do such-and-such, Hajj or divorce shall be binding upon me.” He does not intend to commit himself to Hajj or divorce, nor has he made a direct declaration imposing either. His purpose is merely to urge himself to perform that act or to prevent himself from doing it, just as the person under coercion seeks to ward off the harm threatened against him.

He then says, as a forceful means of urging or restraining himself, “If I do such-and-such, this shall be binding upon me,” or, “This shall be forbidden to me.” Because he is so averse to incurring that obligation or prohibition, he makes it conditional upon that act. His intention is to prevent both, not to bring about either the legal effect or its cause. Since he intends neither the legal effect nor its cause, but instead intends that the legal effect should not occur, it does not follow that this effect must become binding upon him.

---

## Page 16733

Furthermore, an oath of divorce is an innovation introduced into the Muslim community. No report has reached me that such oaths were sworn in the time of the earliest Companions. They have, however, been mentioned among the oaths of allegiance instituted by al-Hajjāj ibn Yūsuf, which include swearing by Allah and oaths involving giving one’s wealth in charity, divorce, and manumission. To this day, I have not encountered any statement by a Companion concerning oaths of divorce. What has reached us from them consists only of answers concerning oaths of manumission, as discussed earlier.

This “innovation” then became widespread throughout the Muslim community. When some came to believe that divorce inevitably takes effect through such oaths, this imposed shackles upon the community resembling those that had burdened the Children of Israel. It gave rise to “five kinds of legal stratagems and harmful practices” concerning oaths, until people made a mockery of Allah’s signs.

They swear oaths of divorce to refrain from things they must do, whether under religious law or by human nature, and to do things that are proper to do, whether under religious law or by human nature. They generally swear such oaths in a state of contention and anger. Yet separation from one’s wife entails harm to both religious and worldly life exceeding that of many of the shackles imposed upon the Jews. It has been said that Allah forbade a woman divorced three times to her former husband until she marries another husband precisely to prevent people from rushing into divorce, because of the harm it entails.

Thus, when they swear oaths of divorce concerning things that are obligatory or prohibited, yet need to do those things or refrain from them without separating from their wives, ingenuity devises for them “four kinds of legal stratagems,” adopted from the Kufans and others.

---

## Page 16734

“The First Stratagem”

This concerns the matter to which the oath relates: it is interpreted for them contrary to what they intended and contrary to what the wording signifies in ordinary usage and custom. This is what some writers on jurisprudence have described under the headings “The Chapter on Riddles” and “The Chapter on Stratagems Concerning Oaths.” Most of this consists of things whose impermissibility is necessarily known in the religion, and the oath-taker’s words cannot legitimately be construed in such ways. For this reason, the imams, such as Ahmad and others, sternly condemned those who employed stratagems concerning these oaths.

“The Second Stratagem”

When they cannot devise a stratagem concerning the wording of the oath, they devise one concerning the act to which it relates. They instruct the man to dissolve his marriage through khulʿ (marital dissolution in return for compensation), so that he may perform the act covered by the oath while the marriage is dissolved. This stratagem is more recent than the preceding one, and I think it arose around the third century AH. Most stratagems originated with certain Kufans, but the khulʿ stratagem does not work according to their principles. They hold that if he performs the act covered by the oath during the waiting period, divorce takes effect, because, in their view, a woman observing the waiting period following an irrevocable separation remains subject to divorce. Someone employing this stratagem would therefore have to wait until the waiting period ends, then perform the act covered by the oath. This causes him hardship because of the length of time involved. Some followers of al-Shāfiʿī thus began issuing fatwas permitting this stratagem.

Sometimes they combined it with one of his two views, which agrees with the better-known of the two positions reported from Ahmad: that khulʿ is an annulment, not a divorce. Thus, whenever the oath-taker wishes to break his oath, he dissolves his marriage through khulʿ, performs the act covered by the oath, and then remarries her. They may rule either that this reduces the number of divorces remaining to him or that it does not. This form of khulʿ, known as “khulʿ to circumvent oaths,” is exactly like a marriage contracted to make a thrice-divorced woman lawful again for her former husband. For the latter

---

## Page 16735

entered into a contract not for its own sake, but only to dissolve it, while the former effected an annulment not for its own sake, but only to reverse it. This is a feeble, newly devised stratagem. Abū ʿAbd Allāh Ibn Batta wrote a treatise refuting it, citing reports from the early generations, some of which I have mentioned elsewhere.

“The Third Stratagem”

When they cannot devise a stratagem concerning the act to which the oath relates, they devise one concerning the divorce invoked in the oath, seeking to invalidate it by scrutinizing its conditions. Some later followers of al-Shāfiʿī thus began examining how the marriage contract was concluded, hoping to discover something that would render it invalid, so that they could conclude that divorce does not take effect in an invalid marriage. According to one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two positions and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, a marriage contracted by a sinful guardian is invalid. Since sinfulness is prevalent among many people, this opinion finds a ready market as a stratagem for setting aside an oath of divorce. I have even seen someone write a treatise on this issue with the aim of devising a stratagem to avert divorce.

Yet you find that those who employ this stratagem examine how the marriage contract was concluded, and invoke the invalidity of a sinful guardian’s authority, only when divorce is pronounced—even though many, or most, scholars hold that divorce does, in principle, take effect in an invalid marriage. As for intercourse and sexual enjoyment, which Muslims unanimously agree are impermissible in an invalid marriage, they do not examine the matter then. Nor do they examine it in connection with inheritance or the other legal consequences of a valid marriage, but only when divorce takes effect.

This is a form of making a mockery of Allah’s verses and scheming against them. It is brought about solely by swearing oaths of divorce and the need to prevent divorce from taking effect.

---

## Page 16736

“The Fourth Stratagem”

This legal stratagem likewise seeks to invalidate the divorce invoked in the oath, but through the presence of an impediment rather than the absence of a condition.

Abū al-ʿAbbās ibn Surayj and a group after him believed that if a man says to his wife, “If my divorce takes effect against you, and if I divorce you, you are divorced three times beforehand,” no divorce can ever take effect against her thereafter. For if the immediate divorce takes effect, the conditional divorce must take effect; yet if the conditional divorce takes effect, it prevents the immediate divorce from taking effect. Its taking effect would thus entail its not taking effect, so it does not take effect.

The general body of Muslim jurists, however, from all schools, rejected this. Indeed, they regarded it as one of those errors that are self-evidently no part of Islam. For it is known as a self-evident teaching of the religion of Muhammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that divorce is legally sanctioned in every marriage, and that there is no marriage in which divorce is impossible.

Their error arose from regarding this formula as valid. They therefore said, “If the immediate divorce takes effect, the conditional divorce takes effect.” But this formula is not valid, for it entails a divorce taking effect after three preceding divorces, which is impossible under Islamic law. A formula entailing this is therefore invalid. Since it is invalid, the immediate divorce taking effect does not entail the conditional divorce taking effect: that consequence follows only if the conditional stipulation is valid.

They then differed: does the conditional divorce take effect to complete the total of three, or is the conditional stipulation void, so that only the immediate divorce takes effect? These are two positions within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others.

I do not know whether Ibn Surayj devised this formulation as a stratagem to prevent divorce from taking effect, or whether he proposed it simply by consistently applying an analogy he believed valid, and those after him then used it as a stratagem. But I have seen

---

## Page 16737

a work on this issue by one of the later scholars, written after the fifth century AH, whose purpose was to devise a stratagem to prevent divorce from taking effect.

For this reason, they formulated it as follows: “If my divorce takes effect against you, you are divorced three times beforehand.” For if he said, “If I divorce you, you are divorced three times beforehand,” this wording would not serve his stratagem, although both formulations involve the same circularity.

The reason is that if a man says to his wife, “If I divorce you, my slave is free,” or, “you are divorced,” he breaks this oath only by pronouncing an immediate divorce after taking it, or by subsequently making a divorce conditional upon an event that then occurs. Both an immediate divorce and a conditional divorce whose condition has been fulfilled constitute an act of divorcing. If, however, he had made her divorce conditional before taking this oath, and the condition was fulfilled afterward, the mere fulfilment of the condition and the resulting divorce would not constitute an act of divorcing. Such an act must proceed from the husband, whereas a divorce taking effect through a condition fulfilled by someone else is not an act on his part.

But if he says, “If my divorce takes effect against you,” this encompasses an immediate divorce, a divorce made conditional after this statement, and a divorce taking effect afterward through a condition stipulated before it.

They therefore framed the case using the wording, “If my divorce takes effect against you.” Thus, when a man swears an oath of divorce not to do something, they tell him: “Say, ‘If my divorce takes effect against you, you are divorced three times beforehand.’” He says this, and they then tell him: “Now do what you swore not to do, for no divorce will take effect in your case.”

This is the “Surayjian stratagem,” rejected by the general body of Muslims and known with certainty not to belong to the law with which Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is generally the practice of swearing oaths of divorce that has given it currency and driven many people to resort to it. Were it not for that, no one would adopt it, for only rarely would a rational person deliberately seek to close off the possibility of divorce for himself.

---

## Page 16738

“The Fifth Stratagem”

If divorce takes effect and no stratagem is possible concerning either the words or deeds covered by the oath, or the divorce invoked in it—whether by invalidating it or preventing it from taking effect—they resort to a stratagem to restore the marriage through a “tahlīl marriage”: a marriage arranged to make the woman lawful again for her former husband. The Sunnah and the consensus of the Companions, together with the evidence of the Quran and the corroboration of established legal principles, demonstrate that such a marriage is forbidden and invalid. Moreover, tahlīl marriages have given rise to evils whose extent Allah alone knows. We have drawn attention to some of them in the book *Iqāmat al-Dalīl ‘alā Butlān al-Tahlīl* (*Establishing the Proof of the Invalidity of Tahlīl Marriage*).

What most often drives people to resort to tahlīl marriage is swearing oaths of divorce. Otherwise, a man generally pronounces a threefold divorce only when he intends it. Someone who intends it does not experience the same regret and harm as someone whose need to break his oath forces the divorce upon him.

These, then, are the “five evils”: contriving ways to break oaths and strip them of their meaning and purpose; resorting to khulʿ (divorce by compensation) and then renewing the marriage; searching for grounds on which the marriage was invalid; contriving to prevent divorce from taking effect; and resorting to tahlīl marriage.

These practices involve scheming, deception, mockery of Allah’s revelations, and frivolity that repel rational people from the religion of Islam and give unbelievers grounds to attack it, as I have seen in some Christian writings and elsewhere. Every believer whose innate disposition is sound can plainly see that the religion of Islam is innocent of, and far above, these absurd contrivances, which resemble the stratagems of the Jews and the trickery of monks.

---

## Page 16739

What has most often led people into these practices, and prompted jurists so frequently both to denounce them and to devise them, is people’s swearing oaths of divorce and believing that divorce inevitably takes effect when such an oath is broken. Indeed, the Kufan jurists and others have elaborated a great many subsidiary rulings concerning oaths on this basis. Many of the weak subsidiary rulings developed by these jurists and others like them resemble the example that Shaykh Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī—may Allah have mercy on him—used to give: a man builds a fine house upon stones wrongfully taken from someone else. When his ownership of those foundation stones is challenged and another person’s right to them is established, his building collapses. For even well-crafted subsidiary rulings are of no benefit unless they rest upon sound foundations.

If, then, swearing oaths of divorce and believing that divorce necessarily takes effect when the oath is broken have brought about these tremendous evils—which have altered certain aspects of Islam among those who engage in these practices, so that they have come to resemble the People of the Book, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, foretold—even though the binding effect of divorce when invoked in an oath is found neither in Allah’s Book nor in His Messenger’s Sunnah; nor did the Companions of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, issue such a ruling—not a single one of them, so far as I know; nor did those who followed them in righteousness and the scholars after them agree upon it; nor does it accord with the foundations of Islamic law; and those who maintain it have no argument beyond an established practice founded upon an analogy reinforced by adherence to certain imams, scholars esteemed by the Muslim community—and, praise be to Allah, their stature is greater still than people suppose, but in cases of disagreement we have been commanded only to refer matters back to Allah and the Messenger, and they have been opposed by others no less distinguished than themselves, indeed their equals or superiors—then we have already cited reports from leading Companions

---

## Page 16740

such as ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, whose eminence, understanding of the religion, and piety are unanimously acknowledged; his sister Hafsah, Mother of the Believers; and Zaynab, the stepdaughter of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who was among the foremost female jurists of the Companions—to the effect that an oath invoking the emancipation of a slave may be expiated. This applies all the more to divorce.

We have also cited the view of Tāwūs, one of the most distinguished scholars among the Successors in knowledge, jurisprudence, and piety: he did not consider an oath of divorce to bring about divorce.

Thus, if divorce necessarily taking effect upon the breaking of such an oath leads to these evils, while its standing in Islamic law is as described, this is evidence that whatever leads to such corruption was not legislated by Allah or His Messenger. We have drawn attention to this concerning contracts for gardens undertaken by those who cultivate them, obtain their produce, and sell vegetables and the like.
