For when someone swears an oath of divorce that he will sever his ties of kinship, mistreat his father, kill a Muslim enemy whose life is inviolable, commit sexual immorality, drink wine, separate a man from his wife, or commit similar grave sins and shameful deeds, he faces “three alternatives.”

Either he carries out what he has sworn to do—something no Muslim would advocate, because of the harm it brings in this world and the Hereafter. Yet many people, even muftis, regard his having sworn an oath of divorce as grounds for treating his conduct more leniently and considering him excused.

---

## Page 16741

Or he resorts to some of the aforementioned stratagems, as devised by certain muftis. These plainly entail mockery of Allah’s revelations, attempts to deceive Him, scheming within His religion and plotting against Him, weakness of intellect and faith, transgression of His limits, violation of His prohibitions, and deviation concerning His revelations. Even if some of our fellow jurists consider certain such practices permissible, errors have entered into this matter whose harmfulness is evident to anyone who reflects on the true nature of the religion—even if those errors are forgiven when committed by a God-fearing scholar exercising independent judgment.

Or he neither resorts to a stratagem nor does what he swore to do, but instead divorces his wife, as a person who fears Allah does if he believes that the divorce takes effect. This entails harm to religion and worldly life that neither Allah nor His Messenger permits.

As for “harm to religion,” scholars agree that divorce is prohibited or discouraged when the husband’s conduct is sound. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Women who seek divorce in return for compensation and those who seek to break free of their marriages are hypocrites.”

He also said:

“Any woman who asks her husband for divorce without any hardship warranting it will be forbidden even the fragrance of Paradise.”

The scholars have differed over whether such divorce is forbidden or merely disliked, and two positions are reported from Ahmad. They have also commended Ahmad’s answer—may Allah be pleased with him—when he was asked about a man who swore, invoking divorce and a declaration of prohibition, that he would have intercourse with his wife while she was menstruating. He replied:

“He should divorce her and not have intercourse with her. Allah has permitted divorce and forbidden intercourse with a menstruating woman.”

Commending this answer is justified on either of two grounds: either on the basis of his view that divorce is not forbidden, or on the basis that its prohibition is less severe than the prohibition of such intercourse. Otherwise, if both are forbidden, he has merely left one forbidden act for another.

---

## Page 16742

As for “harm to worldly life,” it is too obvious to need describing. Treating divorce and whatever else is invoked in an oath as binding often causes harm of a kind that the Sharia has never imposed in such circumstances. A righteous woman may live with her righteous husband for many years, providing the companionship of which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“This world is an enjoyment, and its best enjoyment is a believing woman: when you look at her, she pleases you; when you instruct her, she obeys you; and when you are absent, she guards her chastity and your property.”

She is also the one whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed men to seek when the Emigrants asked him, “What wealth should we acquire?” He replied:

“A tongue that remembers Allah, a grateful heart, or a righteous wife who helps one of you maintain his faith.”

Al-Tirmidhī narrated this through Sālim ibn Abī al-Ja'd, from Thawbān. Through her come the affection and mercy that Allah Most High mentions in His Book as a blessing. The pain of separation may sometimes be harder for her to bear than death, harder than losing wealth, and harder than leaving her homeland—particularly if either spouse is attached to the other, or if they have children who will be neglected and suffer through the separation. This may then lead to severed ties among her relatives and the outbreak of hostility, once the blessing of marriage ties has been lost—the blessing Allah Most High mentions in His words:

“Then He made him kin by blood and marriage.”

Clearly, this is among the hardships covered by His words:

“He has placed no hardship upon you in religion.”

It is also among the difficulties negated by His words:

“Allah intends ease for you, not hardship.”

Furthermore, if the act to which an oath invoking divorce relates is an act of righteousness and kindness—such as giving charity, freeing slaves, imparting knowledge, maintaining ties of kinship, striving in Allah’s cause, or reconciling people

---

## Page 16743

and similar righteous deeds that Allah loves and is pleased with—then, because of the immense harm divorce would cause him, he has all the more reason not to perform such deeds. Indeed, the Sharia does not even instruct him to perform them, since the harm resulting from divorce may outweigh the good achieved through these deeds.

This is precisely the harm that Allah and His Messenger removed through His words, Most High:

“Do not make your oaths by Allah an obstacle [to doing good].”

And through the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“For one of you to persist in his oath concerning his family is more sinful for him in Allah’s sight than to make expiation.”

If it is said, “He himself has brought upon himself one of these three harms, so should he not refrain from swearing such oaths?”

The answer is: Our Sharia contains no sin which, once committed, leaves a person unable to escape it through repentance except at the cost of grave harm. Allah has not laid upon us a burden like that which He laid upon those before us. Suppose that, by swearing an oath invoking divorce, this man has committed a major sin and then repented of it. How could it accord with the principles of our Sharia that the consequences of that sin should remain binding upon him, with no way out?

This differs from the case of someone who pronounces divorce directly, rather than invoking it in an oath. He does so only because he intends divorce, whether out of dislike for his wife, anger with her, or something similar. Allah has allowed divorce three times. Thus, when a man pronounces divorce only by his own choice, and has three opportunities to do so, harm of this kind will rarely occur. This is unlike the first case, in which his intention was not divorce; rather, it was to

---

## Page 16744

do or refrain from doing the act to which his oath related. Yet the Sharia may then command him, or necessity compel him, to do it or refrain from it. Divorce would thus become binding upon him without his having chosen either it or its cause.

Furthermore, what Allah Most High sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to establish concerning oaths was to ease their burden through expiation, not to make them more burdensome by imposing obligations or prohibitions. During the pre-Islamic era, people regarded zihār—a declaration likening one’s wife to one’s mother—as divorce. They continued to do so in the early days of Islam, until Aws ibn al-Sāmit made such a declaration concerning his wife.

Moreover, an analogy may be drawn with vows made out of obstinacy or anger. The only difference between the two is what we have mentioned, and we shall explain, Allah willing, why that difference has no bearing on the ruling. Analogy based on excluding any legally relevant distinction is the soundest form of analogical reasoning, by agreement of the recognized scholars.

Thus, if a man says, “If I eat or drink, I shall be obliged to free my slave,” or “I shall be obliged to divorce my wife,” or “I shall be obliged to perform Hajj,” or “I shall be in a state of consecration for Hajj,” or “My wealth shall be given in charity,” or “I shall be obliged to give charity,” then, according to the majority of scholars, expiation for an oath suffices him, as we have already explained on the evidence of the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the Companions.

The same therefore applies if he says, “If I eat this or drink this, divorce shall be incumbent upon me,” or “Divorce shall be binding upon me,” or “My wife shall be divorced,” or “My slaves shall be free.” For his saying, “Divorce is incumbent upon me: I will not do such-and-such,” or “Divorce is binding upon me: I will not do such-and-such,” is equivalent to his saying, “Hajj is incumbent upon me: I will not do such-and-such,” or “Hajj is binding upon me: I will not do such-and-such.” Both are newly devised forms of oath, neither transmitted from the Arabs nor known to have been used by the Companions. Rather,

---

## Page 16745

those who came later fashioned oaths from these concepts and linked one clause to the other, in the manner of the oaths sworn by the Muslim Companions and by the Arabs.

There is no difference between the two except that saying, “If I do it, my wealth shall be given in charity,” entails an obligation to give charity upon performing the act, whereas saying, “then my wife shall be divorced,” entails the divorce itself taking effect. Thus, the wording entails that divorce takes effect merely upon fulfilment of the condition, even if he does not subsequently pronounce a divorce; it does not entail that the charitable disposition takes effect until he actually makes it.

The distinction relied upon by the jurists who differentiate between the two may be answered in two ways. First, by disputing the alleged distinguishing feature in some of the original cases used as bases for analogy and in some forms of the subsidiary cases used in the same way. Second, by showing that the distinction has no bearing on the ruling.

As for the first: if he says, “If I do such-and-such, my wealth shall be given in charity,” or “then I shall be in a state of consecration,” or “then my camel shall be a sacrificial offering,” what is made conditional is the actual dedication as charity, entry into consecration, and dedication as a sacrificial offering—not the obligation to undertake them. Likewise, in saying, “then my slave shall be free and my wife shall be divorced,” what is made conditional is divorce and manumission actually taking effect, not their becoming obligatory.

For this reason, the jurists of our school and others disagreed over whether, when someone says, “This is a sacrificial offering,” or “This is charity for Allah,” it passes out of his ownership. According to those who hold that it does, this is analogous to his wife and slave passing out of his ownership. The most that can be said in this regard is that property given in charity

---

## Page 16746

and sacrificial offerings may pass into other people’s ownership, unlike a wife or slave. This distinction, however, has no bearing on the ruling.

Likewise, if someone says, “Divorce is incumbent upon me—I shall certainly do such-and-such,” or, “Divorce binds me—I shall certainly do such-and-such,” this is like saying, “Hajj is incumbent upon me—I shall certainly do such-and-such.” Here, he has made the obligation to divorce, rather than divorce actually taking effect, the subject of his oath. It is as though he had said, “If I do such-and-such, I am obliged to divorce.” Thus, in some forms of oaths involving divorce, the oath is expressed in terms of an obligation, just as some forms of oaths involving vows are expressed in terms of something actually taking effect.

As for the second answer, we say: suppose that what is made conditional upon the act here is divorce and manumission actually taking effect, whereas what is made conditional there is the obligation to give charity, perform Hajj, fast, or present a sacrificial offering. Does fulfilment of the condition not entail that this obligation becomes binding? Yet expiation for an oath suffices for him. Likewise, upon fulfilment of the condition, this obligation does not become binding; rather, expiation for an oath suffices for him when the condition takes effect. If that obligation does not become binding upon fulfilment of the condition, then likewise the divorce or manumission does not actually take effect upon its fulfilment.

This is comparable to someone saying, “I am a Jew, a Christian, or an unbeliever if I do such-and-such.” What is made conditional here is actually entering unbelief upon fulfilment of the condition. Yet, by agreement, when the condition is fulfilled, he does not enter unbelief. Rather, he must either offer expiation for an oath or nothing is required of him.

If, however, he were to declare outright, “I am a Jew, a Christian, or an unbeliever,” he would thereby enter unbelief. This is comparable to his declaring outright, “My slave is free,” “My wife is divorced,” “This camel is a sacrificial offering,” “I am bound to present a sacrificial offering,” or, “I am bound to fast

---

## Page 16747

on Thursday.”

If he were to make unbelief conditional upon something whose occurrence he intends, saying, for example, “When the new crescent appears, I shall have renounced the religion of Islam,” he would have to be judged an unbeliever, although he does not commit the unbelief immediately, for assigning it a time indicates the corruption of his belief.

If it is said, “An oath involving a vow requires only expiation,” the answer is: the same applies to an oath involving manumission, and likewise to an oath involving divorce, just as if he had said, “Then I am obliged to divorce my wife.” Whoever holds that nothing is binding upon someone who says, “Then I am obliged to divorce my wife,” must, by the same reasoning, hold that nothing is binding upon him in an oath involving divorce. This is why Tāwūs hesitated over whether it constitutes an oath.

If it is said that he may choose between fulfilling it and offering expiation, then here too he may choose between divorce or manumission, on the one hand, and expiation, on the other. If he has intercourse with his wife, this constitutes choosing expiation. Likewise, in the case of zihār, he may choose between offering expiation and divorcing her; if he has intercourse with her, expiation becomes obligatory upon him. In the case of zihār, however, he may not have intercourse until he has offered expiation, because zihār is an objectionable and false utterance that has made her unlawful to him.

Here, however, his saying, “If I do this, she is divorced,” is equivalent to saying, “Then I am obliged to divorce her,” or, “By Allah, I shall certainly divorce her.” If he does not divorce her, nothing is required of him; if he divorces her, he must offer expiation for an oath.

The remaining question is: does expiation become obligatory immediately if he does not divorce her at that point, as when he says, “By Allah, I shall divorce her this very moment,” but does not do so? Or does it become obligatory only when he resolves to retain her as his wife? Or does it not become obligatory until he says or does something indicating his willingness to remain with her, as in the case of someone who is given a choice

---

## Page 16748

between parting with her and retaining her on grounds of a defect or the like, and as in the case of an emancipated woman married to his slave? Or does expiation not become obligatory at all until divorce is no longer possible?

The answer is that the ruling is the same as if he had said, “Then one-third of my wealth shall be given in charity or as a sacrificial offering,” or something similar. The view most consistent with analogy is that he may defer choosing between the two, unless he says or does something indicating his acceptance of one of them, as with other rights of choice.

Section

According to the well-known position in our school, a “vow prompted by obstinacy and anger” entails one of two things: either offering expiation or performing the conditionally stipulated act.

There is no doubt that statements such as, “If I do such-and-such, I shall be obliged to pray two units of prayer, give a thousand in charity, perform Hajj, or fast for a month,” entail an obligation when the specified act occurs. He therefore has a choice between undertaking this obligation and becoming obliged to offer expiation. If he does not undertake the conditionally stipulated obligation, expiation becomes obligatory. Thus, one of the two obligations is binding upon him, each becoming binding if the other is not undertaken, as with an obligation that allows a choice between alternatives.

The same applies if he says, “If I do such-and-such, I shall be obliged to free this slave, divorce this woman, give charity, or make a sacrificial offering.” This establishes that the slave is due to be freed, the property given in charity, and the sacrificial camel offered in sacrifice.

If he were instead to make this unconditional, saying, “This property is charity, this camel is a sacrificial offering, and I am obliged to free this slave,” would it thereby pass out of his ownership, or would its transfer out of his ownership merely become obligatory? There is disagreement on this.

---

## Page 16749

and it resembles his saying, “This is an endowment.”

If, however, he says, “This slave is free,” or “This woman is divorced,” this constitutes a relinquishment of rights, like saying, “So-and-so is discharged from liability for such-and-such, for the blood of so-and-so, or for slandering me.” For relinquishing claims concerning bloodshed, property, and honour belongs to the same category as relinquishing proprietary rights, as in marital rights and ownership of slaves.

If he says, “If I do this, divorce shall be binding upon me,” “Emancipation shall be binding upon me,” “My wife shall be divorced,” or “My slaves shall be free,” and we hold that this entails one of two alternatives, then he has the choice between that consequence taking effect and expiation becoming obligatory. This is like his saying, “Then this property shall be charity,” or “This camel shall be a sacrificial offering.”

A comparable case is his saying, “When the sun rises, either my slaves shall be free or my wives shall be divorced,” if we hold that the choice rests with him. His choosing either alternative is equivalent to his choosing between the stipulated consequence taking effect and expiation becoming obligatory. Another example is when a man embraces Islam while married to more than four women, or to two sisters, and chooses one of the sisters.

In these cases, where separation is one of the two binding alternatives—whether separation from a specified individual or separation as such—no new declaration of divorce is needed. Yet divorce does not become the binding alternative without something that makes it so, as in the comparable cases mentioned. Then, if he chooses divorce, does it take effect from the time of his choice or from the time he broke his oath? The ruling is to be derived by analogy with comparable cases.

Thus, if, in cases involving a vow prompted by obstinacy and anger, he says, “I have chosen

---

## Page 16750

expiation,” or “I have chosen to perform what I vowed,” is his choice settled by his words, or only by his action?

If the choice is between two obligations, it is settled by a verbal declaration, as in the choice between making a declaration and divorce or emancipation. If it is between two actions, it is settled only by action, as with the choice between the various forms of expiation. If it is between an action and a legal consequence—as when he says, “If I do such-and-such, my slave shall be free,” “My wife shall be divorced,” “My blood may be shed with impunity,” “My property shall be charity,” or “My camel shall be a sacrificial offering”—the legal consequence is settled by a verbal declaration, whereas the action is settled only by its performance. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

A Chapter of Great Importance

An oath that urges a person to do something or restrains him from it, such as saying, “I shall certainly do it,” or “I shall not do it,” contains both a directive and an assertion. The same applies to promises and threats. This differs from a purely declarative oath, such as his saying:

“By Him in Whose hand is my soul, the son of Mary will surely descend among you as a just judge and an equitable leader.”

Or: “By Allah, the caravan will surely arrive.” This is purely a statement about something that will happen, just as a similar statement may be made about the past.

It also differs from a pure request, such as saying to someone else, “Do this,” or “By Allah, do this,” and similar expressions, when the speaker is merely making a request and does not know whether the other person will obey or disobey him. For this reason, adding an exception is not appropriate in this kind of request, nor is any expiation due, since there is no breach.

---

## Page 16751

for it is a pure request reinforced by invoking Allah, as when he says: “I ask you by Allah to do this,” or “I ask you by Allah not to do this.” But if the person singled out by the request or prohibition is someone whom the speaker considers very likely to comply—such as his slave, wife, or child—that person is like the speaker himself: the oath contains both a request and an assertion. Since that person ordinarily obeys him, this is comparable to his own compliance with his intentions. In either case, he requests the act and couples that request with an assertion that it will occur.

Thus, his saying, “I shall certainly stand up tomorrow,” entails two things: first, “I intend to stand up tomorrow”; and second, “Standing up will occur tomorrow.” This differs from a purely declarative oath, which means, “It will occur,” and from an oath that is purely a request, which means, “I want you, and ask you, to stand up.” A breach of oath arises not from failure to fulfil the request—as explained above regarding a pure request—but from events contradicting the assertion, just as with a purely declarative statement about the future.

The exception makes the act conditional upon Allah’s will, so the meaning becomes: “This will certainly occur if Allah wills.” If Allah does not will it, the speaker has made no assertion that it will occur; hence there is no contradiction and no breach of oath. For this reason, the exception is valid. An example of a purely declarative statement is his saying:

“I shall certainly go in to ninety women tonight, and each woman shall surely bear a horseman who fights in the cause of Allah.”

Giving birth is not an act within his power to perform. Likewise, you may say: “By Allah, Zayd will certainly come, if Allah wills.”

Thus, a person who says, “I shall certainly do such-and-such, if Allah wills,” may have one of three intentions:

---

## Page 16752

At times, his purpose is to make his intention conditional, meaning: “If Allah wills, I now intend and seek it; otherwise, I do not.” In this case, he cannot properly be said to intend it, and this alone does not remove the obligation of expiation. This is like a man saying to his wife, “You are divorced if you wish,” and her replying, “I wish it if you wish.” Since an act of willing cannot validly be made conditional, the same applies here. Whenever he says this, his intention has not been established. He is like someone who, when asked for something, says: “I shall give it to you, if Allah wills.” He has made no promise.

If he intends this meaning in an oath, that is valid, but it does not remove the obligation of expiation. Failure to fulfil the request is not what makes expiation obligatory; rather, it is the contradiction of the assertion. Even an unconditional assertion unaccompanied by any request would entail expiation. Thus, the most this achieves is to remove the element of request and urging from the oath.

Second, his purpose may be to make the assertion conditional. The meaning is: “My standing up will occur if Allah wills,” or, “Your standing up will occur if Allah wills.” Thus: “I assert that it will occur if Allah wills its occurrence; if He does not will it, I make no assertion that it will occur.”

If he makes no such assertion, there is no contradiction and hence no breach of oath, even if his present intention to do it is firm. This is the meaning that removes the obligation of expiation. It is as though he said: “I am uncertain whether it will occur, so I do not assert its occurrence unconditionally. I assert only that it will occur under this condition.”

This is like saying: “I shall certainly stand up if Zayd arrives,” or, “if you give me a hundred,” and similar expressions. It is a promise or a threat contingent upon a condition, even though the person making the promise or threat presently intends to carry it out.

For this reason, we have said that his saying, “I shall certainly fast tomorrow in Ramadan, if Allah wills,” does not impair his intention, because the condition qualifies the assertion, not the intention. Some jurists, however,

---

## Page 16753

say: “This impairs his intention.” They hold that if he intends the qualification to apply to his request and intention, it avails him with regard to expiation, or that the obligation of expiation is removed only on this condition. An opinion comes to mind here, but I cannot recall it with certainty.

Third: His purpose may be to make neither of them conditional, for he is firm in his intention and certain that the event will occur. This also applies to a purely factual assertion, such as saying: “The son of Mary shall certainly descend, the Dajjāl (False Messiah) shall certainly emerge, and the Hour shall certainly come.”

These are oaths of a kind that Allah commanded His Messenger to swear, as in His words:

“They ask you, ‘Is it true?’ Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord.’”

This concerns the past and present. He also says:

“Those who disbelieve say, ‘The Hour will never come upon us.’ Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord, it will certainly come upon you.’”

And He says:

“Those who disbelieve claim that they will never be resurrected. Say, ‘Yes, by my Lord, you will certainly be resurrected.’”

He thus commanded him to swear that the Hour would come and that people would be raised from their graves—both being independent events brought about by another. This is like the Prophet’s statement to ʿUmar, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “I shall certainly come to it and circumambulate it.”

Here, when someone says, “If Allah wills,” his purpose may not be to make the assertion conditional, but rather to affirm it, as in His words:

“You will certainly enter the Sacred Mosque, if Allah wills.”

This is a valid statement, since no event occurs except by Allah’s will. It is like saying: “It will certainly happen if the causes of its occurrence come together.” People know that if Allah wills it, and if the causes of its occurrence come together, it will happen. If he is not informing them of that, his words convey nothing useful.

---

## Page 16754

If he intends this meaning, does it remove the obligation of expiation? Considering his intention and the certainty of his assertion, his oath has been contradicted. Considering his wording, however, and the fact that he categorically asserted something conditional rather than unconditional, it has not been contradicted, even if his belief was mistaken. This resembles someone who swears concerning a person, believing the matter to be as he swore, only for the contrary to become apparent. Since his statement about the past reflected his belief, he has not broken his oath, unlike someone who deliberately lies. Likewise, the person under discussion has not presumptuously sworn about what Allah will do.

It may, however, be said: He ought to have remained uncertain. Since he presumptuously swore about what Allah would do and invoked the divine will as confirmation, intending thereby to reinforce the certainty of his assertion, mentioning it only increased the presumption of his oath rather than making it conditional.

In response, it may be argued: His certainty pertains to his belief, not his words. In his words, he did not presumptuously swear about what Allah would do; rather, he stated that this would happen if Allah willed, adding: “I firmly believe that it will happen.”

Is expiation required because my statement fails to correspond to reality, or because my belief fails to correspond to reality? It is required only because the statement fails to correspond to reality. For if I said, “I believe this will happen, and I am firm in my belief,” I would not have broken an oath if it did not happen. What my words mean is that I am certain this will happen, and I tell you that it will happen if Allah wills. Thus, I have made my statement to you conditional, not my belief. Otherwise, saying “if Allah wills” would serve no purpose: if I meant that I was certain it would happen if Allah willed, my certainty would not be unconditional.

Likewise, if I meant that both my belief and my statement were conditional upon Allah’s will, that would be the first case. Rather, the meaning is that my belief in its occurrence is firm, while my statement to you is conditional upon His will. I have made it conditional upon His will because no one should make statements about future events without qualifying them by Allah’s will. This requires further consideration.

---

## Page 16755

This distinction clarifies the view of those who say, “If, by the qualification, he intends the meaning of His words”:

“Never say of anything, ‘I will do that tomorrow,’ except, ‘If Allah wills.’”

For a person is commanded not to say, “I will certainly do it tomorrow,” without saying, “If Allah wills.”

The foregoing discussion makes clear that the qualification which removes the obligation of expiation makes conditional only the oath’s assertive meaning, whether that meaning stands alone or is combined with another. It does not make conditional its meaning as a demand, whether that meaning stands alone or is combined with another. Failing to fulfil a demand does not entail expiation; rather, expiation is entailed when an assertion proves contrary to fact. This is because the obligation is removed only when invoking Allah’s will makes the statement conditional, and only something that has not yet occurred can be made conditional, unlike something that has already occurred.

Hence we know that this qualification does not nullify performative declarations at all, whether pronouncements of divorce or otherwise, just as it does not remove what a demand entails. From this it should be inferred that these formulas are governed predominantly by the rules of performative declarations, since conditional qualification is inapplicable to them. In all such cases, the qualification expresses affirmation, not conditionality, as when someone says, “This happened by Allah’s will,” or, “It happened through Allah’s power.”

The issue of “qualification in oaths” is to be understood on this basis. If it concerns one’s ultimate outcome, it retains its usual sense, because applying the designation implies entitlement to Paradise, as Ibn Masʿūd stated. Muʿādh’s companion disagreed with him on this, adopting a valid interpretation. Omitting the qualification is permissible, although including it is better

---

## Page 16756

than omitting it. This is the meaning of Ahmad’s statement concerning . . . (1) Some of our companions consider it obligatory, whereas the Murji’ah prohibit it. Others may consider omitting it preferable.

Thus, it is variously classified as obligatory, recommended, disallowed—whether prohibited or disliked—prescribed by the Sunnah, or equally permissible either way. What we have explained concerning oaths also clarifies the question of promises and threats: whether they may be abrogated or left unfulfilled. Whoever regards them as assertions says that abrogation would entail falsehood. Another holds that they are assertions incorporating the meaning of a demand.

When someone says, “If you do this, I will strike you,” his words imply, “I now intend to strike you if you do it, and I am informing you of that.” This is therefore not a mere assertion. Abrogation would apply to the demand it contains, giving that demand precedence over the assertion, just as, in the matter of invoking Allah’s will and expiation, the assertion takes precedence over the demand. When speech contains two meanings, one may predominate according to the accompanying considerations.

This is why a distinction is made between leaving a promise unfulfilled and leaving a threat unfulfilled. When a promisor’s words entail requiring himself to fulfil what he has promised in return for something, this is equivalent to undertaking to provide consideration under a contract. It is an obligation he owes another, and he may not annul it. By contrast, the words of someone issuing a threat entail demanding the threatened harm by way of requital. This is equivalent to requiring consideration from another when he himself has rendered what he owes. Whatever another owes him, he may insist upon it or choose not to do so.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

> Perhaps the intended report is his statement concerning someone who makes this qualification: “If he says that faith consists of speech and deeds and increases and decreases, [his qualification is] out of fear and caution, not out of doubt as they claim. He makes the qualification only with regard to deeds.”

---

## Page 16757

Thus, saying, “I have sold you this for a thousand,” carries the sense of promising the thousand upon receipt of the item sold, and of demanding the item sold upon payment of the thousand. Insisting on the threatened punishment has no stronger claim than insisting on any other rights owed to one in return. Exacting one’s rights from people involves some measure of pain and is therefore not entirely free of a punitive element, even if it is not called punishment. The point is simply to draw an analogy between the two regarding what is owed to the speaker and what he himself owes.

Although promises and threats contain an assertion, they also contain a demand. This places them on the same footing as a performative declaration which, though worded as an assertion about the past, brings something into effect in the present. Likewise, although promises and threats are worded as assertions about the future, they establish an intention and a demand. Where there is a promise, fulfilling it is obligatory, and breaking it is therefore called lying, as He said concerning those who declared:

“We will certainly go out with you, and will never obey anyone against you.”

“Allah bears witness that they are indeed liars.”

Where there is a threat, however, carrying it out is not obligatory, since it includes the meaning of declaring that the punishment is deserved.

Accordingly, a threat may be abrogated, as the early generations mentioned concerning His words:

“Whether you reveal what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it.”

A promise, however, may not be abrogated once entitlement has been established, because it is the consequence of the conditional undertaking. Before the action is performed, there is a case for permitting its abrogation, just as revocable, nonbinding conditional undertakings—such as offers of a reward for completing a task and similar arrangements—may be rescinded. If someone says, “Whoever returns my runaway slave will receive a dirham,” he may rescind this before the work is undertaken.

Rescission is analogous to abrogation. The former rescinds performative declarations, namely contracts entailing a commitment of will concerning what is due to oneself or owed by oneself; the latter likewise rescinds a demand. And just as rescission is understood as the removal of a ruling consisting of a demand or permission,

---

## Page 16758

so rescission removes the ruling consisting of intention or permission. The same applies to promises and threats: what is removed is the ruling consisting of the intention to give or to grant permission. All this arises because some speech combines the meanings of demand and assertion: oaths, vows, promises, threats, and contracts. It is over the rulings governing this composite “third category” that people have differed. For this reason, some have divided speech into assertions and performative declarations, making the latter broader than demands, since a performative declaration establishes either a demand or permission. There is nothing beyond demand and permission, for one calls upon either oneself or another for something to occur or not to occur.

It may also be said that permission entails a demand, since one requires of oneself that the person granted permission be enabled to act. Likewise, undertaking an obligation entails a demand, since one imposes upon oneself a right whose holder may demand its fulfilment as an obligation, whereas in the case of permission one makes it permissible for him. The two thus amount to the same thing. And Allah knows best.

The matter therefore comes down to a demand, an assertion, or a combination of the two. And Allah knows best. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

Section

The principle we have set out clarifies the question of qualifying a declaration of zihār with “if Allah wills.” This concerns a man’s saying, “You are forbidden to me,” or, “You are to me like my mother’s back.”

Ahmad said: “This qualification is valid in such a declaration, since expiation becomes due if he breaks it by returning to her.”

Ahmad’s principle is that wherever expiation is prescribed, an oath is prescribed as well; otherwise, it is not.

---

## Page 16759

And a group of his companions, including Ibn Battah, al-ʿUkbarī, and Ibn ʿAqīl, said:

“Such a qualification is not valid here, because this is a performative declaration, like pronouncing divorce or emancipation. It does not consist of two clauses, as an oath does; rather, it is a single clause, like other performative declarations. Thus, saying, ‘You are forbidden to me,’ is like saying, ‘You are divorced.’ There is no future action here to be made conditional upon Allah’s will, as there is in saying, ‘I shall certainly go out.’”

At first glance, this view appears stronger because of the similarity in form. Ahmad’s view, however, displays greater juristic understanding and penetrates more deeply into the meaning. The issue here—and Allah knows best—is comparable to the differing approaches to a vow made in contention or anger: some classify it with a vow of devotion because their verbal forms are identical; others classify it as an oath because it resembles an oath in its substantive feature—the matter sworn to; and others apply the rulings of both to it because it combines both meanings. For one half of it resembles an oath in meaning, while the other half resembles a vow.

Likewise, with all other expressions to which legal rulings are attached, one person may consider their form, another their meaning, and another both together. For example, in saying, “I shall certainly do it,” the form is that of an assertion, whereas the meaning may be an assertion, a demand, or a combination of the two.

Thus, saying, “You are to me like my mother’s back,” was, in the pre-Islamic period of ignorance, purely a performative declaration establishing prohibition. Since prohibition could not take effect without divorce, they regarded it as divorce in accordance with the apparent import of its wording, because divorce entails prohibition. They thus took the consequence as evidence of that which entails it. Allah invalidated this, because it is a reprehensible statement and a falsehood: what is lawful cannot be like what is permanently forbidden. Nor did He make it a divorce, even if divorce was intended by it, because divorce cannot take effect unless the invalid meaning is first established, namely, the resemblance

---

## Page 16760

that is forbidden. It thus becomes like saying, “You are a Jew or a Christian,” intending divorce, since this could take effect only after establishing disbelief in her, which he has no right to attribute to her. The same applies to saying, “You are a she-donkey or a she-camel,” or, “You are to me like a she-donkey and a she-camel.”

Hence, most of the Companions said that the statement, “You are forbidden to me,” is likewise an oath, not a divorce. Some explicitly described it as a graver oath, like zihār (likening one’s wife to one’s mother’s back). This is Ahmad’s position. Thus, saying, “You are to me like my mother’s back,” amounts to saying, “I shall certainly not approach you,” because establishing her resemblance to his mother entails both abstaining from intercourse with her and dissolving the marriage contract. The Lawgiver denied effect to the dissolution of the contract, because that rests with the Lawgiver, not with him: contracts and their dissolution are established by Allah and take effect only with the Lawgiver’s permission. He did, however, give effect to the husband’s undertaking to abstain, since having intercourse or refraining from it is his own act, in which he has a choice.

Since this amounts to saying, “I ought not to have intercourse with you,” it has the meaning of an oath. Yet He made it a graver oath, unlike an oath sworn by Allah. Swearing the latter is lawful, so a person commits no sin in making it, whereas this oath is a reprehensible statement and a falsehood. Moreover, this oath must be abandoned, and expiation serves as compensation for that. Accordingly, an oath sworn by Allah does not render the act forbidden pending expiation, whereas this oath makes breaking it forbidden until expiation has been offered. He may therefore not break it until he has secured release from it, and the greater expiation is required for it.

Its consisting of a single clause does not prevent its inclusion under the designation of an oath. The wording of a vow likewise constitutes an oath while consisting of a single clause. What matters is whether it contains a pledge.

---

## Page 16761

And Allah has called every declaration of prohibition an “oath,” in His words, “Why do you forbid what Allah has made lawful for you?” through to His words, “Allah has prescribed for you release from your oaths.” Likewise, the Companions called a vow made in obstinacy and anger an “oath,” although it is a conditional sentence, considering its meaning.

This is further clarified by the fact that, if zihār were purely a declaration establishing a legal state, its legal effect would necessarily follow, and no expiation would apply. Expiation does not undo a contract or its dissolution; rather, it removes the sin of violating the commitment undertaken. Thus, since neither an oath nor a declaration of zihār requires expiation unless a violation occurs, it is clear that zihār is an oath.

Al-Shāfiʿī holds that the declaration of zihār requires termination of the marriage contract. Thus, if the husband retains his wife for long enough to have been able to terminate it, expiation becomes obligatory. Ahmad and the majority, however, hold that the declaration requires abstention from intercourse by making it forbidden. Expiation removes this prohibition, so intercourse is not permissible before it has been removed.

Ahmad likewise holds that a husband’s statement, “You are forbidden to me,” requires abstention from intercourse on the grounds that it is forbidden. Those who distinguish between the two, however, argue that the wording of zihār could not be given its apparent meaning, since under Islam she does not become like his mother. Its effect was therefore restricted to part of that meaning—abstention from intercourse—rather than termination of the marriage contract, as was their practice in pre-Islamic times.

---

## Page 16762

And the wording “forbidden” can be given its legal effect.

Ahmad, however, might argue that the wording “forbidden” cannot be given its full effect: the woman herself can never become intrinsically forbidden, while an incidental prohibition cannot be established without something analogous. Such a prohibition is not what an unrestricted declaration of prohibition conveys; rather, it is a qualified prohibition. Thus, only part of the wording’s implication is applied: prohibiting the act, namely intercourse. Moreover, when prohibition is attributed to a person, what is intended is the act involving that person. It is therefore as though he [said] (1): “Intercourse with you is forbidden.”

This amounts to saying, “By Allah, I will not have intercourse with you.” Just as an oath to abstain from marital intercourse does not constitute divorce, even if divorce is intended, neither does a declaration of prohibition. An oath of abstention belongs to the category of oaths sworn by Allah, while zihār belongs to the category of oaths made by declaring something forbidden.

In examining this matter, one could reasonably say: we interpret it as imposing the lowest degree of prohibition, since the wording is unrestricted, and nothing beyond that is established without grounds. Similarly, saying, “You are divorced,” effects only one divorce; and, in the comparison involved in zihār, prohibition alone is taken as sufficient. As for when he intends divorce, the response may be: “Even if he intends divorce through zihār.”

Section

Connected with this is the case of someone who swears an oath by zihār or by declaring something forbidden, to secure some benefit or prevent something. For example, he says, “If you do this, you are to me like my mother’s back,” or, “You are forbidden to me”; or he says, “A declaration of prohibition—or zihār—shall bind me: I will not do it,” or, “I will certainly do it.”

In this case, our fellow jurists hold that zihār takes effect if he breaks his oath, just as divorce and emancipation take effect through such an oath. Hence, when discussing the oaths sworn by Muslims, they included zihār among them.

> Usāmah ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the Comprehensive Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 16763

I used to give this fatwa in reliance on the opinions of others and on their argument that this is a ruling made contingent upon a condition, as when a man says, “If you do this, you are forbidden to me,” as a punishment for her for doing it. Later, however, I ruled that he owes expiation for an oath if his intention is to refrain from the act, not to bring about the prohibition. This accords with what we have said concerning a “vow prompted by obstinacy and anger,” and concerning a man’s declaration that he is a Jew or a Christian if he does such-and-such, or that he deems wine and carrion lawful if he does such-and-such.

For just as his intention in those cases is not for the stipulated consequence to take effect when the condition is fulfilled, but merely to refrain from an act, so too when he says that what is lawful is forbidden to him if he does such-and-such. His purpose is not to make lawful things forbidden upon performing the act, but to refrain from it. He mentions hypothetically undertaking that consequence to reinforce his resolve to abstain, just as he hypothetically undertakes to become a Jew or a Christian. Similarly, an oath sworn by Allah carries the meaning: “If I do this, I will have violated the sanctity of oaths sworn by Allah,” or, “I will have diminished the reverence due to Allah,” or, “I will have treated the sanctity of Allah lightly if I do it.”

What all oaths entail, according to their wording, is fulfillment; and whenever someone breaks his oath, he has violated his oaths and has become a Jew or a Christian. Likewise, the wording of a vow prompted by obstinacy and anger entails the obligation to fulfill it, for a ruling made contingent upon a condition becomes binding when that condition is fulfilled. Someone who swears by something to perform an act has committed himself to that act and bound it to the object of reverence by which he swears. Whenever he fails to perform it, he has violated that sanctity.

---

## Page 16764

His statement, “I swear by Allah,” or “by such-and-such,” means: “I bind it to Him and attach it to Him.” Hence a constant companion is called a halīf, as ‘Uthmān was called “the constant companion of the prayer niche”; and its underlying cause never fails to produce its effect. Thus it has been said that the particle bā (by) attaches the matter sworn to to the one by whom the oath is sworn. The lām introducing the sworn declaration supplies a second emphasis, as though he were saying: “I attach and bind to Allah the substance of my declaration: I shall certainly do it.”

This is why offering expiation before breaking an oath is called “release”: it dissolves the bond established through the one by whom the oath was sworn, just as rescinding a sale dissolves the contractual bond between seller and buyer. The Lawgiver has therefore classed oaths among contracts that may be dissolved by providing this substitute, rather than those that are unconditionally binding. Since the bond connects the matter sworn to with the one by whom the oath is sworn—Allah, glorified be He—He has permitted His servant to dissolve this bond, contracted “for Me and by Me,” through expiation, which is an act of worship and a means of drawing near to Him.

The servant thus has the choice between fulfilling his undertaking and dissolving it through the prescribed substitute, since it was he who bound the matter sworn to to Allah, glorified be He. Similarly, in the early days of Islam, they were given the choice between observing the fasting He had prescribed and forgoing it through expiation. Likewise, someone who performs ‘umrah during the months of Hajj and wishes to perform Hajj that same year has the choice between undertaking a separate journey for Hajj and dispensing with that journey by making the sacrificial offering for combining ‘umrah and Hajj. He thus has the choice of completing his Hajj through either the journey or the offering.

This is why we have said that it is not a compensatory sacrifice: one is not given a choice concerning the act that necessitates such a sacrifice, such as omitting obligatory duties. Rather, it is an obligatory offering, as though he were given the choice between purely physical worship

---

## Page 16765

or worship combining physical and financial elements, namely the sacrificial offering.

But it may be objected: “If it is obligatory, one may not eat from it, unlike a voluntary offering.” We reply: There is also disagreement concerning an offering pledged by a vow. As for an offering that becomes obligatory as a specifically designated animal, there is agreement that one may eat from it. For slaughtering for Allah as an offering to His House is the greater of the two objectives. Hence the scholars disagreed over whether its meat must be distributed within the Sacred Precinct. We ourselves require this for a dedicated offering, but not for a ritual sacrifice, thereby giving effect to its designation as an offering: something presented to the Kaaba.

If, then, it becomes clear that the grounds requiring fulfilment remain in place, and that the Lawgiver has merely allowed expiation as a concession—which may itself become obligatory or recommended, as with a person compelled by necessity to eat carrion—then this same rationale applies to a “vow made in obstinacy or anger” and similar cases. It also applies to a man’s saying, “If you do such-and-such, you are forbidden to me.”

This differs from a case in which he intends the prohibition actually to take effect as a punishment for her, such as when he says to her or to her mother: “If you do such-and-such, you are forbidden to me.” Here, his intention is for the prohibition to take effect, just as his intention in a “devotional vow” is for an obligation to take effect, and his intention in a “divorce in return for compensation” is to receive the compensation, and so forth.

This distinction is sound according to our principles. Just as we distinguish between different intentions when someone undertakes a conditional obligation, we should likewise distinguish between them when someone undertakes a conditional prohibition. If he breaks his oath, we should give him the choice between abiding by the prohibition and expiating his oath, just as we give him that choice in the case of a vow.

---

## Page 16766

If we then extend this principle to divorce and manumission—as may be inferred from our principles, and in keeping with reports from the Companions that manumission was included under a “vow made in obstinacy,” and reports from Tāwūs and others that they regarded an oath of divorce as having no effect, although the narrator was uncertain whether Tāwūs counted it as an oath—this is a sound approach. Indeed, it is the stronger view, if Allah wills. There is no power and no strength except through Allah.

If, however, we distinguish divorce and manumission from declarations of marital prohibition and zihār, that too is a sound approach. In the former case, he makes the actual taking effect of divorce or manumission conditional—something that is not contingent upon an act of will. In the latter, he makes an oath conditional, as though saying: “If I do this, I shall be bound by an oath of marital prohibition or an oath of zihār,” or: “If I do this, I shall become one who has pronounced zihār and declared his wife forbidden to him.” When he becomes someone who has pronounced zihār and declared his wife forbidden to him, this does not itself bring anything into effect; it merely establishes a prohibition removable by expiation. Thus, it becomes like saying: “If I do such-and-such, I shall be bound to perform Hajj,” or: “I shall be a pilgrim,” or: “I shall be in a state of ihrām (ritual consecration).” This, however, requires further consideration and should be carefully examined.

---

## Page 16767

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Concerning a man who swore that he had not committed a particular sin since reaching the age of understanding, although he had committed that sin when he was about twenty years old. In his heart, he intended that he had not committed it since reaching puberty.

Here, consideration must be given to what he meant by saying, “Since reaching the age of understanding.” If he meant since reaching puberty, then he has undoubtedly kept his oath and has not broken it.

If, however, he meant that he had not committed it since acquiring discernment—and a ten-year-old possesses discernment—then, if he knew he was lying, his oath is a deliberately false oath. This is one of the major sins, and he must repent to Allah for it.

If it belongs to a category of oaths subject to expiation, there are two views concerning it. The majority of scholars say that it is too grave to be expiated; it can only be erased through sincere repentance. This is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad according to the better-known of the two reports from him.

The “second view” is that expiation is required. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and of Ahmad according to the second report from him. An oath sworn by Allah is subject to expiation by scholarly consensus.

As for oaths involving vows, zihār, declarations of marital prohibition, divorce, manumission, or disbelief, examples include saying: “If I do such-and-such, I shall be bound to perform Hajj,” or “My wealth shall be given in charity,” or “I shall be bound by marital prohibition,” or “Divorce

---

## Page 16768

shall be binding upon me—I shall certainly do such-and-such,” or “If I have done such-and-such, my slaves are free,” or “If I have done such-and-such, then I am a Jew or a Christian.”

The scholars hold “three views” on this question. One view is that if he breaks the oath, he must repent. Another is that nothing is required of him. A third is that he must offer expiation for an oath. This is the strongest view, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

If someone uses one of these forms to swear a deliberately false oath, then those who require expiation for a deliberately false oath and consider these forms of oath subject to expiation require expiation in this case.

As for those who say that a deliberately false oath is too grave to be expiated, they hold “two views.”

The “first” is that he incurs whatever he has pledged, whether a vow, divorce, manumission, or disbelief—even if it is held that these consequences do not follow from a forgivable oath, namely an oath concerning the future. This is the position of a group of the followers of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad. They cite as evidence the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Whoever swears falsely by a religion other than Islam is as he has said.”

They argue that this oath does not take binding effect; rather, its breach coincides with pardon, so no expiation is due for it. Moreover, he has pledged what he pledged while knowing that he was lying. He must therefore be held to it as punishment for his lying and as a deterrent to anyone who swears a false oath. This differs from a binding oath, whose maker is obeying Allah, not disobeying Him.

---

## Page 16769

The “second view,” held by the majority, is that he does not incur what he has pledged, whether disbelief or anything else, just as he does not incur it through an oath concerning the future. In both cases, his sole intention was to swear an oath. If he was lying, he did not intend to become a disbeliever or to incur what he had pledged, whether a vow, divorce, manumission, or anything else—just as he did not intend to incur these consequences if he broke an oath concerning the future.

Rather, the true meaning and purpose of his words in both cases is to swear an oath. Whatever distinction is drawn in one case between disbelief, a vow, divorce, or manumission themselves and swearing an oath invoking them must also be drawn in the other. In both cases, however, he has committed a major sin by swearing a deliberately false oath. He must therefore repent to Allah of it, just as he repents of other major sins. When he repents of the sin, he becomes like one who has no sin. No disbelief, vow, divorce, or manumission results from it; he has merely sworn an oath invoking these things. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16770

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

If someone swears an oath pledging to walk to Mecca, must he walk, or perform Hajj riding and offer compensation, or offer expiation for an oath?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

Rather, expiation for an oath suffices, according to the vast majority of Muslim scholars among the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness. These include ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, his son ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, Hafsah bint ʿUmar, Zaynab, the stepdaughter of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and others. May Allah be pleased with them.

This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, and the later view transmitted from Abū Hanīfah. Ibn al-Qāsim also gave this ruling to his son when he broke such an oath. The Quran and Sunnah support this view, as has been explained at length elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16771

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whomever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whomever He leads astray, none can guide. We testify that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, with no partner, and we testify that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and his family and grant them abundant peace.

Allah Most High said:

“Do not make your oaths by Allah an excuse for not doing good, being mindful of Him, and making peace between people. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

“Allah will not hold you accountable for unintentional oaths, but He will hold you accountable for what your hearts have intended. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing.”

“For those who swear to abstain from their wives, there is a waiting period of four months. If they return, then Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. But if they resolve on divorce, then Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

And He Most High said:

“O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not love the transgressors.”

“Eat of what Allah has provided for you, lawful and good, and be mindful of Allah, in whom you believe.”

“Allah will not hold you accountable for unintentional oaths

---

## Page 16772

, but He will hold you accountable for oaths you have solemnly made. The expiation is to feed ten needy people with food of the average standard you feed your families, or to clothe them, or to free a slave. Whoever cannot afford this must fast for three days. That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn them. And guard your oaths. Thus Allah makes His revelations clear to you, so that you may be grateful.”

Allah mentions the word “oaths” in four places: in His words, “Allah will not hold you accountable for unintentional oaths”; His words, “for oaths you have solemnly made”; His words, “That is the expiation for your oaths when you have sworn them”; and His words, “And guard your oaths.”

And He Most High said:

“O Prophet, why do you forbid yourself what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah has already prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths. Allah is your Protector, and He is the All-Knowing, the All-Wise.”

This question expresses disapproval and implies a prohibition. Allah does not ask questions to acquire understanding or knowledge, for He knows all things. Rather, specialists in Arabic call this a “question expressing disapproval.” Such a question conveys rejection of what the sentence expresses: either a denial, if its content is declarative, or a prohibition, if its content is non-declarative. Speech is either declarative or non-declarative. Examples include His words, “May Allah pardon you! Why did you give them permission?” and His words, “Why do you say what you do not do?” and similar passages.

Allah Most High thus forbade His Prophet to prohibit what is lawful, just as He forbade the believers to do so. He also stated that He had prescribed for them a way to release themselves from their oaths, just as He mentioned expiation for an oath after forbidding the prohibition of lawful things in Sūrat al-Mā’idah.

His words, “Allah has already prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths,” refer to what He mentioned

---

## Page 16773

in Sūrat al-Mā’idah.

The occasion for the revelation of Sūrat al-Tahrīm was that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade himself something lawful: either his slave woman Māriyah the Copt, or honey, or both. Likewise, the verse in Sūrat al-Mā’idah concerned a group of Muslims who had forbidden themselves wholesome things, either in pursuit of celibacy and monastic asceticism—as ‘Uthmān ibn Maz‘ūn and those Companions who agreed with him had resolved to do, until the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade them—or for other reasons. Allah explained to them that He had provided a way out for anyone of this community who forbade himself something lawful, and that an oath involving such a prohibition could be discharged through the expiation Allah had prescribed.

They were unlike those before them, who, when they forbade themselves something, found it made forbidden to them, with no possibility of offering expiation. Allah Most High said:

“All food was lawful for the Children of Israel, except what Israel had forbidden himself before the Torah was revealed.”

Accordingly, it has been said that when they swore to do something, they were bound to do it and could not offer expiation instead. Thus ‘Ā’ishah said: “Abū Bakr al-Siddīq would not break an oath until Allah revealed the expiation for oaths.” This is also why Allah commanded Job to do what would release him from his oath, since no expiation was available to them.

An oath concerning something may serve to urge and oblige, or to prevent and prohibit. Likewise, the covenant of Allah and His Messenger, and His ruling concerning His creation, fall into these two categories. Thus, in the pre-Islamic era and the early days of Islam, “zihār” constituted divorce, until Allah revealed an expiation for it. Similarly, “īlā’” constituted divorce until Allah revealed His ruling concerning it. This was because zihār is a form of prohibition, so it entailed the dissolution of the marital bond, since a wife cannot be permanently forbidden to her husband. In their understanding, “īlā’” entailed the prohibition of intercourse, which was incompatible with marriage.

---

## Page 16774

Allah has mentioned the term “oath” in several places in His Book. He, Most High, says:

“Detain them both after the prayer and, if you have doubts, let them swear by Allah: ‘We will not exchange this oath for any price, even for a near relative, nor will we conceal Allah’s testimony; otherwise, we would surely be among the sinful.’”

Then, further on:

“Then let two others take their place—the two nearest of kin among those entitled to a claim—and let them swear by Allah: ‘Our testimony is truer than theirs, and we have not transgressed; otherwise, we would surely be among the wrongdoers.’”

“That makes it more likely that they will give testimony properly, or fear that other oaths will be sworn to refute their own.”

In Sūrat Barā’ah, when discussing treaties with the polytheists, He, Most High, says:

“Then fight the leaders of disbelief, for they have no regard for oaths, so that they may desist.”

“Will you not fight a people who broke their oaths and plotted to expel the Messenger, and who attacked you first?”

He, Most High, also says:

“Fulfil the covenant of Allah when you make a covenant, and do not break oaths after confirming them, having made Allah your guarantor. Indeed, Allah knows what you do.”

“Do not be like the woman who unravelled her yarn after spinning it firmly, using your oaths to deceive one another because one community is greater than another. Allah only tests you through this.”

He, Most High, says:

“They swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that, if a sign came to them, they would surely believe in it.”

He, Most High, says:

“They swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that Allah will not resurrect those who die.”

He, Most High, says:

“They swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that, if you commanded them, they would surely go forth. Say: ‘Do not swear; proper obedience is what is required.’”

The lexicographers state—and these are al-Jawharī’s words—“Yamīn means an oath. Its plurals are aymun and aymān.” He then says: “It was so named because, when people pledged themselves to one another by oath, each would grasp the other’s right hand.”

---

## Page 16775

Section

The term “oath” in the Book of Allah, as well as in the language of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who were the Quran’s first addressees, encompasses whatever one swears to by Allah, regardless of the wording used or which of His names is invoked. The same applies to swearing by His attributes, such as His might and . . ., and His rulings, such as prohibition and obligation, for prohibition and obligation are among His rulings.

When someone says, “I swear by Allah that it shall indeed be so,” he has committed himself to that action, making it obligatory or forbidden for himself, and has bound his oath by Allah. He has thus tied the binding force of the action to Allah so that it cannot be undone or broken. In itself, his oath therefore entails either that the action is binding upon him or that his faith in Allah, by whom he bound the oath, is nullified. Since the latter is not an option for him, the former necessarily follows.

However, in our religious law, the Lawgiver has not granted a person unrestricted authority to declare things forbidden or obligatory for himself. Rather, He has prescribed a means of release from his oath and an expiation that removes the liability for sin arising from breaking it, when breaking the oath and offering expiation is better than keeping it.

The jurists have differed over whether an oath entails an obligation or prohibition that is lifted by expiation; whether it entails neither; or whether it would entail these were it not for an impediment established by the Sacred Law that prevents this consequence. These are three views. The third is the soundest, as we shall point out, if Allah Most High wills.

---

## Page 16776

Our purpose is to cite statements of the Companions that clarify the meaning of an oath in the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and their own usage. In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, it is reported:

Muhammad ibn al-Minhāl narrated to us: Yazīd ibn Zuray‘ narrated to us: Habīb al-Mu‘allim narrated to us, from ‘Amr ibn Shu‘ayb, from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyib, that two brothers from the Ansār jointly held an inheritance. One asked the other to divide it, and the latter said, “If you ask me again to divide it, all my wealth shall be devoted to the door of the Kaaba.” ‘Umar said to him, “The Kaaba has no need of your wealth. Offer expiation for your oath and speak to your brother. I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—say: ‘You are bound by neither an oath nor a vow involving disobedience to the Lord, severing ties of kinship, or what you do not own.’”

This man used a conditional formulation, consisting of a condition and its consequence. He made the obligation to spend his wealth on the door of the Kaaba conditional upon his brother asking him to divide the inheritance. Such a formulation may be intended as a devotional vow, as when someone says, “If Allah heals the sick person whose illness concerns me and safeguards my property that is elsewhere, then a third of my wealth shall be given in charity.” It may also be intended as an oath-vow, called a “vow of obstinacy and anger,” as intended by the man who made this conditional declaration.

The formulation is conditional in both cases, but the meaning and intention differ. In the first case, the person desires the fulfilment of the condition, which is a blessing from Allah, such as the recovery of the sick or the preservation of wealth. He also intends to undertake an act of obedience to Allah in gratitude for His blessing and to draw closer to Him.

In the other type, his aim is to restrain himself or someone else from an action, or to urge its performance, and he swears an oath. The obligation deters him because he is unwilling to incur it and because he dislikes and hates it, just as he shuns disbelief, hates it, and finds it abhorrent. Thus he says, “If I do it, then he is

---

## Page 16777

a Jew or a Christian.” His intention is not to become a disbeliever. Rather, his intense hatred of disbelief leads him to invoke it in swearing that he will not act, intending to rule out the antecedent by ruling out its necessary consequence. He intends to exclude disbelief, the necessary consequence, and thereby aims to exclude the act as well.

Likewise, when he swears by Allah, it is because of Allah’s greatness in his heart that he binds his oath to Him, making what he swears to do a necessary consequence of his faith in Allah. The existence of the antecedent—faith in Allah—thus entails the existence of its necessary consequence: the obligation to perform the act he has sworn to perform. Similarly, if he swears not to do something, he makes his abstention from it a necessary consequence of his faith in Allah. This is what it means to bind an oath. His intention is not to relinquish his faith; rather, he intends that neither his faith nor the abstention he has bound to it should cease.

Thus, ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb called this an “oath.” To establish that the man was not obliged to perform the act made conditional upon the stipulated event, he cited the Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“You are bound by neither an oath nor a vow involving disobedience to the Lord, severing ties of kinship, or what you do not own.”

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—mentioned both oaths and vows, just as Allah mentioned both in His Book. An oath is intended to urge or restrain action, or to affirm or deny the truth of a report. A vow, however, is intended to draw one closer to Allah. This is why He, glorified be He, has made the fulfilment of vows obligatory: the person making a vow has undertaken an act of obedience to Allah, obliging himself to do something Allah loves and is pleased with, intending thereby to draw closer to Him.

Similarly, the Lawgiver has required anyone who begins Hajj or ‘umrah to complete it for Allah, for He says:

---

## Page 16778

“And complete Hajj and ‘umrah for Allah,”

even if the person undertaking them does so voluntarily. Scholars have differed over whether other acts of worship must likewise be completed. Allah, glorified be He, has not made fulfilling an oath obligatory, because the person swearing it intends to urge or restrain action, not to draw closer to Allah Most High.

A vow, however, is generally expressed conditionally, making an obligation contingent upon something else, as when someone says, “If Allah cures my sick relative, I shall be obliged to free a slave.” An oath, on the other hand, generally takes the form of swearing, as when someone says, “By Allah, I shall certainly do such-and-such.” Swearing and a conditional undertaking may also be combined, as in His words:

“Among them are those who made a covenant with Allah: ‘If He gives us of His bounty, we will surely give in charity and be among the righteous.’”

“But when He gave them of His bounty, they withheld it and turned away in aversion.”

“So He caused hypocrisy to persist in their hearts until the Day they meet Him, because they broke their promise to Allah and because they used to lie.”

For this reason, jurists discuss one of these two forms under the heading “Chapter on Making Matters Conditional,” covering such matters as conditional divorce, manumission, vows, and the like. They discuss the other under “Chapter on General Rules of Oaths,” covering matters common to oaths by Allah, divorce, manumission, zihār, declarations of unlawfulness, and the like. The questions addressed in these two chapters overlap. Thus, some jurists discuss the general rules of oaths together with conditional declarations, while others discuss them in the “Chapter on Oaths.”

What is negated in one form is affirmed in the other, and what comes first in one comes last in the other. Thus, if someone says, “If I do such-and-such, my wealth is unlawful to me,” or “my slave is free,” or “my wife is divorced,” or “my wealth is to be given in charity,” or “I am bound to perform such-and-such a number of Hajj pilgrimages,” or “to fast for a month,” or the like,

---

## Page 16779

this is equivalent to saying, “Divorce shall be binding upon him—he will not do such-and-such,” or “Manumission or prohibition shall be binding upon him,” or “Walking to Mecca shall be binding upon him—he will not do such-and-such,” and similar expressions. In the conditional form, he affirms the act and places it first, putting its consequence last. When he places the act last and negates it, putting the consequence first, his intention regarding what he swears by is that it should not occur and that he should not violate its sanctity.

Likewise, if he says, “If I do such-and-such, then I am a disbeliever, a Jew, or a Christian,” this is like saying, “By Allah,” for such is its nature. Thus, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and his Companions considered the meaning of the expression and the speaker’s intention, whether it took a conditional form or the form of an oath. If his purpose was to lessen or prevent something, they treated it as an oath, even when expressed conditionally. If his purpose was to draw closer to Allah, they regarded him as making a vow, even when he used the form of an oath.

For this reason, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, treated someone making a vow as someone swearing an oath, since he commits himself to an act through a conditional expression. If the act vowed was something Allah had commanded, he instructed him to perform it; otherwise, he required him to make expiation for an oath. Likewise, he instructed someone who had sworn an oath to make expiation when he saw that another course was better than what he had sworn to do. In both cases, consideration was given to the intended objective.

If the intended act is something Allah loves and is pleased with, the person is instructed to perform it. This is the vow that must be fulfilled, even if expressed in the form of an oath. If another course is more beloved and pleasing to Allah, he is instructed to follow that more beloved and pleasing course, even if his undertaking took the form of a vow, and he is instructed to make expiation for an oath.

All of this serves to ensure obedience to Allah and His Messenger, and that all religion is devoted to Allah alone. Any oath, vow, contract, or condition that entails something contrary to the command of Allah and His Messenger is not binding. Rather, the command of Allah and His Messenger must take precedence over all of these.

---

## Page 16780

Thus, whatever people intend to do or refrain from doing, if it is something Allah and His Messenger have commanded, Allah commands it and commands helping others to carry it out. If it is something Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, Allah forbids it and forbids helping others to carry it out. If it is permissible, a good intention makes it an act of obedience, a bad intention makes it a sin, and the absence of either makes it neither. Accordingly, with oaths, vows, conditions, and contracts, Islamic law invariably invalidates whatever conflicts with the command of Allah and His Messenger. However, if a person has linked those matters to his faith in Allah, expiation is prescribed to efface the consequences of this undertaking. Otherwise, it would entail sin if he broke his oath; hence the term hinth (oath-breaking). Allah Most High says:

“And do not make Allah, through your oaths, an obstacle to doing good, being mindful of Him, and making peace between people.”

Reports from the Companions, the Successors, and others have been transmitted in such abundance as to establish that this verse means that none of you should swear not to do good, fear Allah, or maintain ties of kinship, and then, when instructed to do so, say, “I have sworn by Allah,” thereby making an oath by Allah an obstacle to obeying Allah and His Messenger. Since He, glorified be He, has forbidden making Allah—that is, swearing by Allah—an obstacle to obeying Him, it is all the more fitting that anything else should be forbidden from becoming an obstacle to obeying Allah. All oaths recognized by Islamic law that entail expiation ultimately amount to swearing by Allah, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.
