Our purpose here, however, is simply to mention some reports. Abū Bakr al-Athram said in his Sunan:

I heard Abū Abd Allāh Ahmad ibn Hanbal being asked about a man who had declared that his wealth was pledged to the Kaaba.

---

## Page 16781

He replied, “The expiation for an oath is due,” citing the report from Ā’isha as evidence.

He continued: I also heard Abū Abd Allāh ask about a man who swears to walk to the House of Allah, give his property in charity, or undertake similar oaths. He said, “If he breaks his oath, the expiation for an oath is due. However, I do not urge him to break it so long as he has not already done so.” He was told, “He will not do so.”

Abū Abd Allāh was asked, “Then, if he breaks his oath, does he offer expiation?”

“Yes,” he replied.

He was asked, “Is it not the expiation for an oath?”

“Yes,” he replied.

Al-Athram said: Al-Fadl ibn Dukayn narrated to us, from Hasan, from Ibn Abī Najīh, from Atā’, that Ā’isha said: “Whoever says, ‘My wealth is part of the Kaaba’s inheritance, all my wealth is an offering, and all my wealth is for the poor,’ should offer expiation for his oath.”

Al-Athram also said: Ārim ibn al-Fadl narrated to us: Mu‘tamir ibn Sulaymān narrated to us, saying: My father said: Bakr ibn Abd Allāh narrated to us: Abū Rāfi‘ informed me, saying:

My mistress, Laylā bint al-Ajmā’, declared that every slave she owned would be freed, all her wealth would be an offering, and she would be a Jew and a Christian, “if you do not divorce your wife or separate yourself from her.”

He said: I went to Zaynab, the daughter of Umm Salama. Whenever a woman learned in religion was mentioned in Medina, Zaynab was mentioned. I went to her, and she accompanied me to my mistress. Zaynab said, “Hārūt and Mārūt are in this house!”

She replied, “O Zaynab, may Allah make me your ransom!”

She had declared: “Every slave she owns is free, all her wealth is an offering, and she is a Jew and a Christian.”

Zaynab said, “A Jew and a Christian! Leave the man and his wife alone.”

I then went to Hafsa, the Mother of the Believers. She sent for my mistress, who came to her and said, “O Mother of the Believers, may Allah make me

---

## Page 16782

your ransom!” She had declared that every slave she owned was free, all her wealth was an offering, and she was a Jew and a Christian.

Hafsa said, “A Jew and a Christian! Leave the man and his wife alone.”

He continued: I then went to Abd Allāh ibn Umar, who accompanied me to her. He stood at the door and greeted her. She said, “Baynan anta wa-baynan abūka.”

He said, “Are you made of stone? Are you made of iron? What are you? Zaynab gave you a ruling, and the Mother of the Believers gave you a ruling, yet you did not accept their rulings!”

She said, “O Abū Abd al-Rahmān, may Allah make me your ransom!” She had declared that every slave she owned was free, all her wealth was an offering, and she was a Jew and a Christian.

He said, “A Jew and a Christian! Offer expiation for your oath, and leave the man and his wife alone.”

Abd al-Razzāq also related this in his Musannaf, from al-Taymī, from Mu‘tamir ibn Sulaymān, from his father, from Bakr ibn Abd Allāh al-Muzanī, who said: Abū Rāfi‘ informed me, saying:

My mistress, Laylā bint al-Ajmā’, said to me that every slave she owned was free, all her wealth was an offering, and she was a Jew and a Christian, “if you do not divorce your wife.”

He continued: Zaynab bint Umm Salama came to us. Whenever a woman learned in religion was mentioned, Zaynab was mentioned. I told her about this, and she said, “Leave the man and his wife alone, and offer expiation for your oath.”

He continued: Hafsa, the wife of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came to us. I said, “O Mother of the Believers, may Allah make me your ransom!” My mistress told her about her oath, and Hafsa said, “Offer expiation for your oath.”

He continued: We went to Abd Allāh ibn Umar and said, “O Abū Abd al-Rahmān!” She told him about her oath, and he said, “Offer expiation for your oath, and leave the man and his wife alone.”

---

## Page 16783

Ibn Abd al-Barr said:

“The phrase ‘and every slave she owned was free’ appears in the versions of this report narrated by Sulaymān al-Taymī and Ash‘ath al-Humrānī from Bakr al-Muzanī. Ash‘ath’s version mentions Ibn Abbās, Abū Hurayra, Ibn Umar, Hafsa, Ā’isha, and Umm Salama; but the reference is actually to Zaynab bint Umm Salama.”

Al-Athram said: Abd Allāh ibn Rajā’ narrated to us: Imrān informed us, from Qatāda, from Zurāra ibn Abī Awfā, that a woman asked Ibn Abbās about a woman who had bound herself to give her cloak as an offering if she wore it.

Ibn Abbās asked, “Was she angry or content when she said it?”

They replied, “Angry.”

He said, “Allah, Blessed and Exalted, is not approached through anger. Let her offer expiation for her oath.”

I say: Ibn Abbās inquired about the vow to determine whether she intended to draw near to Allah through what she had vowed—as someone might say, ‘If my wealth remains safe, I will give it in charity’—or whether she intended to swear that she would not wear it, in which case she would owe expiation for an oath. He therefore asked, “Was she angry or content when she said it?” When they replied, “Angry,” he knew that she was swearing an oath, not making a vow. This is why the jurists call this “a vow of obstinacy and anger.” It is an oath, even though it takes the form of a conditional statement.

Al-Athram said: Ibn al-Tabbā‘ narrated to me: Abū Bakr ibn Ayyāsh narrated to us, from al-Alā’ ibn al-Musayyib, from Ya‘lā ibn al-Nu‘mān, from Ikrima, from Ibn Abbās, who was asked about a man who had dedicated his wealth to the poor. He said, “Keep your wealth and spend it on

---

## Page 16784

your dependants, pay off your debts with it, and offer expiation for your oath.”

Harb al-Kirmānī said in his Masā’il: Al-Musayyib ibn Wādih narrated to us: Yūsuf ibn Abī al-Safar narrated to us, from al-Awzā‘ī, from Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, who said: I asked Ibn Abbās about a man who swears an oath binding himself to walk to Allah’s Sacred House.

He replied, “Walking is obligatory only for someone who intended it. As for someone who swore in anger, he must offer expiation for an oath.”

Al-Athram said: Abū Bakr ibn Abī al-Aswad narrated to us: Mu‘tamir narrated to us, from his father, from Ibn Umar and al-Hasan, who said:

“If it was a vow of gratitude, he must fulfil his vow. A vow involving disobedience or made in anger is an oath.”

Al-Athram said: Abū Abd Allāh Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Hanbal narrated to us: Abd al-Razzāq narrated to us: Ibn Jurayj narrated to us, saying: Atā’ was asked about a man who said, “I bind myself to offer a thousand sacrificial animals.”

He replied, “It is an oath.”

He was asked about a man who said, “I bind myself to perform a thousand pilgrimages.” He replied, “It is an oath.”

He was asked about a man who said, “My wealth is dedicated as an offering.” He replied, “It is an oath.”

He was asked about a man who said, “My wealth is for the poor.” He replied, “It is an oath.”

Ahmad said: Abd al-Razzāq narrated to us: Ma‘mar informed us, from Qatāda, from al-Hasan and Jābir ibn Zayd, concerning a man who says, “If I do not do such-and-such, I am in a state of consecration for Hajj.” The reply was:

“Only someone who intends Hajj is bound by consecration. This is an oath for which he must offer expiation.”

Ahmad said: Abd al-Razzāq narrated to us: Ma‘mar informed us, from Ibn Tāwūs, from his father, who said, “It is an oath for which he must offer expiation.”

Al-Athram said: Abū Abd Allāh narrated to us: Wakī‘ narrated to us, from Sufyān, from Layth, from al-Minhāl, from Abū Wā’il, concerning a man who declared that he was in a state of consecration for Hajj. He said, “It is an oath.”

He also said: Abū Abd Allāh narrated to us: Muhammad ibn Yazīd al-Wāsitī narrated to us, from Ayyūb—meaning Abū al-Alā’—from

---

## Page 16785

Qatāda and Mansūr, from al-Hasan, concerning a man who declared that if he entered so-and-so’s house, he would be bound to walk to Allah’s House.

He said, “He must offer expiation for an oath.” He added, “But if he vows to walk, he must walk. If he cannot manage the walk, he should ride and offer a sacrificial animal.”

Abū Abd Allāh said: Yaʿqūb ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd narrated to us: Āsim ibn Muhammad narrated to us, from his brother Umar ibn Muhammad, who said:

A man came to seek a ruling from al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad ibn Abī Bakr. He said, “Abū Muhammad, what is your view concerning a man who has bound himself to walk to Allah’s House?”

Al-Qāsim asked, “Did he make it a vow?”

He replied, “No.”

“Or did he undertake it for Allah’s sake?”

He replied, “No.”

He said, “Then let him offer expiation for his oath.”

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Ibn Asākir related the report recorded by Hanbal, who said: I heard Abū Abd Allāh say, “It is said that Marwān ibn al-Hakam was versed in judicial rulings and followed the rulings of Umar.” He also related what Abū Zurʿa al-Dimashqī had reported:

“People differ regarding these two men: Muhammad ibn al-Walīd al-Zubaydī and Shuʿayb ibn Abī Hamza. Al-Hakam ibn Nāfiʿ informed me that he had seen both of them—al-Zubaydī and Shuʿayb ibn Abī Hamza—and I observed that he held al-Zubaydī in greater esteem. Both were companions of al-Zuhrī at al-Rusāfa, serving under Hishām ibn Abd al-Malik: Muhammad ibn al-Walīd al-Zubaydī was in charge of the treasury, and Shuʿayb ibn Abī Hamza was in charge of Hishām’s expenditure.”

It is narrated from Baqiyya, who said: Al-Awzāʿī asked us, “What has become of Muhammad ibn al-Walīd al-Zubaydī?”

I replied, “He has taken charge of the treasury.”

He said, “Indeed, to Allah we belong, and to Him we shall return.”

---

## Page 16786

He also cited what al-Dhuhlī recorded among the reports of al-Zuhrī: Saʿīd ibn Kathīr ibn ʿUfayr narrated to us: Abd Allāh ibn Wahb informed us, from Yūnus, from Ibn Shihāb, who said:

Qabīsa ibn Dhuʾayb informed me that a woman vowed to slaughter her son at the Kaaba if she did a certain thing. She then did that thing and came to Medina to seek a ruling concerning her vow. She approached Abd Allāh ibn Umar, who told her, “I know of nothing Allah has commanded concerning vows except that they be fulfilled.”

The woman asked, “Then should I slaughter my son?”

Abd Allāh ibn Umar replied, “Allah has forbidden you to kill yourselves.” Ibn Umar said nothing further to her.

She then approached Abd Allāh ibn Abbās and sought his ruling on the matter. He said, “Allah has commanded that vows be fulfilled and has forbidden you to kill yourselves. Abd al-Muttalib ibn Hāshim once vowed that, if he came to have ten sons, he would slaughter one of them. When he had ten, he drew lots among them to determine whom he would slaughter. The lot fell upon Abd Allāh ibn Abd al-Muttalib, who was the person dearest to him. Abd al-Muttalib said, ‘O Allah, is it to be him or a hundred camels?’ Then, in those pre-Islamic times, he drew lots between him and a hundred camels, and the lot fell upon slaughtering a hundred camels.”

Ibn Abbās told the woman, “My view, therefore, is that you should slaughter a hundred camels in place of your son.”

The report reached Marwān ibn al-Hakam, who was then governor of Medina. He said, “I do not think Ibn Umar and Ibn Abbās have given the correct ruling. ‘No vow to disobey Allah is binding.’ Ask Allah’s forgiveness, repent to Him, and do as much good as you can. As for slaughtering your son, Allah has forbidden you to do that.”

He said: The people were pleased with this and admired Marwān’s statement. They considered his ruling correct, and people continued thereafter to rule that no vow to disobey Allah is binding.

---

## Page 16787

I say: Ibn Umar’s practice regarding vows to commit a sin was to withhold judgment, directing neither their fulfillment nor their abandonment. Thus, when asked about someone who had vowed to fast on the day of Eid, he said:

“Allah has commanded the fulfillment of vows, and the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, has forbidden fasting on this day.”

This was because he saw a conflict between two proofs: the command and the prohibition. It had not become clear to him that the command to fulfill vows is restricted to acts of obedience to Allah. Hence Mālik transmitted in his Muwatta’ the hadith subsequently recorded by al-Bukhārī, from Ā’isha, that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Moreover, the Quran contains no unrestricted command to fulfill vows explicitly using the word “vow.” His words, “They fulfill their vows,” are a statement of fact and praise, while His words, “Let them fulfill their vows,” concern a specific case. Allah has, however, commanded the fulfillment of covenants and contracts, and vows fall within that category. This, and Allah knows best, is what the two men meant when they said, “Allah has commanded the fulfillment of vows.”

Such is the position of someone who regards covenants and contracts as requiring fulfillment unconditionally, without considering their subject matter. This often happens to some scrupulously pious people, as it did to Ibn Umar: they refrain from breaking many covenants and contracts that contravene the Sharia, yet their scrupulousness also makes them refrain from contravening the Sharia, leaving them bewildered.

As for Ibn Abbās, two opinions are reported from him on this issue. The first is the view just mentioned. The second is that the person must slaughter a ram. This is also one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad, and the view of Abū Hanīfa and others. This is what accords with the Sharia, rather than appealing to the story of Abd al-Muttalib, for the practices of the people of pre-Islamic ignorance cannot be cited as proof at all unless Islam has endorsed them.

---

## Page 16788

But Ibn Abbās cited it as evidence because Islam had endorsed blood money, which is compensation for a life. He therefore held that this compensation could take the place of the life itself as a ransom. He then prescribed a ram as the ransom, following the account of Abraham, which is more fitting.

The other report from Ahmad holds that he must offer the expiation for an oath, as with other vows to commit a sin. Marwān’s ruling that nothing is required of him is also the view of al-Shāfiʿī, of Ahmad according to one report, and of everyone who holds that a vow to commit a sin entails no obligation.

On its face, this vow appears to have been an oath-like vow. Yet the established position of Ibn Umar and Ibn Abbās is that such a vow is an oath for which expiation must be offered. It thus becomes clear that this was a devotional vow, like the vow of Abd al-Muttalib. Mālik and other scholars of Medina, however, do not distinguish between the two categories, so they related the account according to their own understanding of its meaning.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said:

Section

I have written in “The Principle of Covenants and Contracts”—the principle concerning religious covenants being included among the general principles, and that concerning worldly contracts among the jurisprudential principles—and also in “The Book of Vows,” that when something is obligatory under the Sharia, and a person vows to perform it, makes a covenant with Allah to do so, pledges allegiance to the Messenger or the imam on that basis, or joins a group in mutually swearing to do so, then these covenants

---

## Page 16789

and solemn pledges impose upon him a second obligation, distinct from the obligation established by the original command alone. The act is thus obligatory on two grounds, so that whoever neglects it deserves both the punishment due to someone who breaks covenants and solemn pledges and the punishment due to someone who disobeys Allah and His Messenger. This is the correct analysis. Some scholars of our school maintain that when someone vows to perform an obligatory act, its status after the vow remains as it was before, unlike a vow to perform a recommended act. This is incorrect. If a vow makes a recommended act obligatory, then it has even greater reason to impose an obligation to perform an already obligatory act. This is not a matter of bringing about what already exists; rather, these are two different kinds of obligation, each with its own distinct ruling. An example is a grandmother who is both the mother of one’s maternal grandmother and the mother of one’s paternal grandmother: she has two grounds of entitlement, each of which entitles her to one-sixth.

The same applies to those scholars of our school who say that conditions inherent in a contract cannot validly be stipulated, or that they become invalid (1). Some scholars of al-Shāfiʿī’s school have even said that if someone says, “I give her to you in marriage on the terms Allah has commanded: retaining her honorably or releasing her with kindness,” the marriage is invalid because he has stipulated divorce within it. This is profoundly unsound. The obligations arising from contracts become binding precisely because the contracting parties impose them upon themselves. A contract stated without qualification has an understood meaning; when the parties conclude it without qualification, they thereby bind themselves to what it is understood to entail. What a contract entails becomes obligatory through the contract itself, just as what a vow entails becomes obligatory through the vow. The Lawgiver did not impose it as an obligation in the first instance; rather, He commanded the fulfillment of contracts, just as He commanded the fulfillment of vows.

Thus, when a contract entails a known obligation, whether understood from its unqualified wording or established by custom, and the contracting parties expressly impose that obligation through specific wording, this amounts to joining the specific to the general. Thus the contracting party

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 16790

has imposed it twice, or has imposed a specific obligation that dispenses with the general obligation.

There are comparable examples in the Quran, such as His saying:

“and His angels and His messengers, and Gabriel and Michael”;

and His saying:

“And when We took from the prophets their covenant, and from you, and from Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus son of Mary”;

and His saying:

“Guard the prayers, and the middle prayer in particular”;

and His saying:

“Tell your wives, your daughters, and the believing women”;

and His saying:

“He commands justice, good conduct, and giving to relatives.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man instructed his employee to pledge an item as security with a particular person, but the employee pledged it with someone else, and the pledged item was lost. Its owner swore that unless the employee brought it back to him, he would not employ him, believing that it had not been lost. He then discovered that it had been lost. Does he break his oath if he employs him?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If, when he swore, he believed that the pledged item itself still existed and had not been lost, and he swore so that it would be brought to him, then he does not break his oath in these circumstances. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man swore an oath concerning his son that he would not enter the house until he gave him the cloak he had taken. He then discovered that he had taken nothing. Does he break his oath if he enters, or not?

---

## Page 16791

He answered:

If he enters his house, he does not break his oath, provided the circumstances are as described. This is because what was sworn to is intrinsically impossible—just as, according to the sounder of the two opinions, a person does not break his oath if he swears to drink the water in a particular vessel when it contains no water. Moreover, he swore only because he believed that his son had taken it, and the contrary became apparent. There is disagreement concerning cases of this kind as well, but the correct view is that the oath is not broken. Thus, on either of these two grounds, he does not break his oath.

The well-known case concerns someone who swears to something believing it to be as he has sworn, only for the contrary to become apparent. This involves ignorance of the very subject of the oath, whereas that involves ignorance of an attribute of its subject. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man’s mother swore that he must not reconcile with his wife, and that if he did, she would not speak to him again. What does the pure Sharia require regarding his situation, his wife’s welfare, and his mother’s situation?

He answered:

If he reconciles with his wife as Allah and His Messenger have commanded, his mother should speak to him and expiate her oath. Expiation for an oath consists of either freeing a slave, feeding ten poor people—giving each poor person two ritls of bread—or clothing ten poor people, giving each one a garment. The bread should be accompanied by something eaten with it, such as bananas, cheese, meat, or other foods. The man who swore the oath or his wife may offer the expiation on her behalf with her permission.

---

## Page 16792

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—also said:

The expiation for an oath is that mentioned in Sūrat al-Māʾidah. Allah Most High says:

“Its expiation is to feed ten poor people from the average of what you feed your own families, or to clothe them, or to free a slave. Whoever cannot afford this must fast for three days.”

Anyone who has the means must therefore offer expiation through one of these three options. If he lacks the means, he must fast for three days. If he chooses to feed ten poor people, he may do so.

“The amount of food to be given” depends on a fundamental question: is the amount prescribed by the Sharia or determined by customary practice? Scholars hold two opinions on this.

Some maintain that it is “prescribed by the Sharia,” but they differ over the amount.

Some say that each poor person should be given one sāʿ of dates, one sāʿ of barley, or half a sāʿ of wheat. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and a group of scholars.

Others say that each should be given half a sāʿ in the case of dates and barley, or a quarter of a sāʿ—which is one mudd—in the case of wheat. This is the view of Ahmad and a group of scholars.

Still others say that one mudd of any of these foods suffices in every case. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī and a group of scholars.

“The second opinion” is that the amount is determined by customary practice, not prescribed by the Sharia. Thus, the poor in each locality should be given food of the average quantity and kind that people there feed their own families. This is the meaning of Mālik’s statement. It was stated by Ismāʿīl

---

## Page 16793

ibn Ishāq:

Mālik held that one mudd sufficed in Medina as expiation for an oath. Mālik said: “As for other lands, their diet differs from ours, so I consider that they should offer expiation according to the average standard of their own diet.” This is based on Allah Most High’s words:

“From the average of what you feed your own families, or to clothe them.”

This is also the position of Dāwūd and his followers without qualification. It is the view transmitted from most of the Companions and Successors. Thus, they would describe the average as bread with milk, bread with clarified butter, or bread with dates, and the higher standard as bread with meat.

Elsewhere, we have presented the reports from them at length and explained that this is the correct view, supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and sound reasoning. It also follows by analogy from Ahmad’s school and its principles. His underlying principle is that whatever the Lawgiver has not quantified is referred to customary practice. The Lawgiver has not quantified this, so it should be referred to customary practice, particularly in light of His words, Most High:

“From the average of what you feed your own families.”

Ahmad does not prescribe fixed amounts of food for a wife, a child, or a slave. Nor, according to the apparent position of his school, does he prescribe a fixed wage for a worker hired in return for his food and clothing. He likewise sets no fixed amount for the hospitality he regards as obligatory, with no differing position reported on this point. Nor, according to the apparent position of his school, does he set a fixed amount for the hospitality stipulated as due to Muslims from non-Muslims under Muslim protection. This is so even though that obligation arises from a stipulation; how, then, would he prescribe a fixed amount of food for an obligation imposed by the Sharia?

Indeed, according to the more evident of the two positions transmitted from him, he does not prescribe a fixed amount for the jizyah (poll tax), nor does he do so for the land tax. More generally, he does not prescribe fixed amounts for obligatory food provisions, whether the obligation arises from the Sharia or from a stipulation, nor for other obligatory provisions. It is therefore all the more appropriate that no fixed amount be prescribed for the food provided as expiation.

---

## Page 16794

And “there are three categories”: whatever is defined by the Sharia or by language is referred to its respective definition; whatever is defined by neither is referred to customary practice. Thus, Ahmad does not prescribe particular wording for contracts. Rather, his underlying principle in these matters resembles Mālik’s. Likewise, reasoning from the principles of his school entails that the obligatory amount of wheat for zakāt al-fitr should be half a sā'. His own statements also indicate this, as has been explained elsewhere, although the well-known position transmitted from him fixes the amount at one sā', as with dates and barley.

The scholars have differed over whether an accompaniment to bread is obligatory or recommended, holding two views. The correct view is that if a person feeds his family bread with an accompaniment, he should likewise provide the needy with an accompaniment. If he feeds his family bread without an accompaniment, he may not give the needy better food than his family; rather, he should feed them according to the average standard of what he feeds his family.

Accordingly, in some lands, the average amount eaten by their inhabitants is one mudd of wheat, as is reported concerning the people of Medina. When made into bread, this yields approximately two Iraqi ratls, equivalent to five and five-sevenths Damascene ounces. If he allocates part of it to an accompaniment, as has been reported from the early generations, the bread amounts to approximately four ounces. This is insufficient for most inhabitants of other towns.

For this reason, the majority of scholars have said that outside Medina one should provide more than this: either two mudds or one and a half mudds, according to how much the people eat. Thus, the bread provided may amount to half a Damascene ratl, two-thirds of a ratl, one ratl, or more—either with an accompaniment, according to how much they customarily eat at a given time (1); for people’s customary diet varies with low prices

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 16795

and high prices, prosperity and hardship, and also with winter, summer, and other factors. If the amount required by Abū Hanīfa is calculated in terms of bread, it comes to one and one-third Damascene ratls, for he requires half a sā', reckoning a sā' as eight ratls. As for dates and barley, he requires one sā', or eight ratls. This is six times the amount required by al-Shāfi'ī and three times that required by Ahmad ibn Hanbal. The preferred view is to refer this matter to people’s customary practice and habits. Thus, in one locality the amount required by Abū Hanīfa may suffice, in another that required by Ahmad, and in another an amount between the two, according to local custom, in keeping with the words of the Most High:

“From the average of that which you feed your families.”

If someone gathers ten needy people and serves them an evening meal of bread with an accompaniment, according to the average standard of what he feeds his family, this suffices according to most of the early generations. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, Ahmad—according to one of the two reports from him—and others. It is the better-supported of the two views, for Allah Most High commanded feeding; He did not require a transfer of ownership, and this is feeding in the true sense.

Those who require “transfer of ownership” advance two arguments. “The first” is that the amount of food required is prescribed by the Sharia, and when the recipients eat, it cannot be known whether each consumes the amount to which he is entitled. “The second” is that transferring ownership enables the recipient to dispose of the food in ways unavailable to him when he is merely given a meal.

The answer to the first is that we do not concede that the amount is prescribed by the Sharia. Even supposing that it is so prescribed, the discussion concerns

---

## Page 16796

only a case in which he gives each of them both a midday and an evening meal until they are satisfied. In that case, each has received his rightful amount and more.

As for disposing of it however one wishes, Allah Most High did not require that. It is in the case of zakat that He required a transfer of ownership, because He mentioned its recipients with the preposition li- (“for”), saying:

“Alms are only for the poor and the needy.”

Thus, where Allah describes the use of funds with the preposition fī (“in”), as in His words, “for freeing slaves” and “in the cause of Allah,” the correct view is that a transfer of ownership is not required. Rather, zakat funds may be used to free a slave, even though this does not transfer ownership of those funds to the person freed. They may also be used to purchase weapons to support the cause of Allah, and for other such purposes. Hence some scholars have said that providing a meal is preferable to transferring ownership: the recipient may sell what you give him rather than eat it, or may even hoard it. When food is actually served, however, the Lawgiver’s purpose is certainly fulfilled.

At most, one might argue that transferring ownership may also be termed it‘ām (feeding), as in the statement:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, granted the grandmother a one-sixth share.”

And in the hadith:

“Whatever allotment Allah grants a prophet belongs to whoever assumes authority after him.”

The reply, however, is that the term undoubtedly includes feeding in its ordinary sense, with even greater justification. Moreover, that usage occurs only when the giver is mentioned, as when one says, “He granted him such-and-such.” When the expression is used without qualification, as in “Feed these needy people,” nothing is understood from it except actually feeding them. Transferring ownership of food was termed “feeding” because they would eat what they received, and feeding was the intended purpose. But when the intended use is something other than eating, this is not called “feeding” without qualification.

---

## Page 16797

He also said—may Allah sanctify his soul:

As for vows, they are of two kinds: those involving obedience and those involving disobedience. Whoever vows to pray, fast, or give charity must fulfil his vow. But if he vows something that is not an act of obedience—such as pledging oil, wax, funds, or anything else to certain graves, shrines, or similar places—this is a vow involving disobedience. In some respects, it resembles making vows to idols such as al-Lāt, al-‘Uzzā, and Manāt, the third and last. By scholarly agreement, such a vow must not be fulfilled. Some scholars, however, require the expiation prescribed for breaking an oath, including Imam Ahmad and others. Others require nothing; this is the view of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī.

If a person instead devotes what he pledged to a lawful act of devotion—for example, using the oil to light mosques, which are the houses of Allah, and giving the funds to righteous poor people—this is a righteous deed that Allah will accept from him. Nevertheless, making a vow in the first place is disliked, for it is authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, “forbade making vows and said, ‘It brings no good; it merely extracts something from the miser.’”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16798

Chapter: Adjudication

Abū al-ʿAbbās—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

“A Useful, Comprehensive Observation”

The purpose of adjudication is to ensure that people receive their due and to put an end to disputes. Ensuring that people receive their due secures a benefit, while ending disputes removes a harm. The aim, therefore, is to secure that benefit and remove this harm. Ensuring that people receive their due is part of the justice by which the heavens and the earth are upheld. Ending disputes falls under “the prevention of injustice and harm.”

Each involves preserving what already exists and warding off what has not yet arisen. When rights reach those entitled to them, existing rights are preserved and their purpose is achieved. As for disputes, existing disputes are ended and those that have not yet arisen are prevented. When reconciliation is achieved, the dispute ends, thereby fulfilling one of the two objectives. As for “rights,” either they have been secured through the reconciliation, or the person entitled to them has willingly relinquished them, which is permissible.

When claims are adjudicated through a ruling, testimony, or the like, settling them may involve impugning judges and witnesses, or similar measures. These are among the harms that should only be resorted to out of necessity, as with litigation itself; for adjudication may give rise to difficulties between the litigants and others.

---

## Page 16799

“There are thus four possibilities”:

First, resolution through reconciliation. This is the ideal, for it achieves all three objectives in full. Second, resolution through a judgment that leaves bitterness. This ensures that the right reaches its holder and ends the dispute, but does not restore harmony between the parties.

Third, reconciliation whereby someone relinquishes part of what he claims as his right. This too achieves reconciliation and ends the dispute. Although the objective of ensuring that rights reach their holders is not achieved, voluntary relinquishment takes its place. This shows that settling a case through reconciliation is better than resolving it through a judgment that leaves bitterness. Both end the dispute, but reconciliation has the added merit of restoring harmony, although one party relinquishes his right. The other approach ensures that the rightful claimant receives his due, but leaves resentment behind. The former benefit is more complete, especially if the right is established only outwardly, while the underlying reality may be otherwise.

As for neither resolution nor reconciliation, this is unacceptable, for it brings about the harm of leaving matters unadjudicated.

If a right is already in its holder’s possession—as with an endowment or the like—and it is feared that, unless safeguarded by evidence, a stipulation may be forgotten, the right denied, and its holder fail to receive it, or something similar, then hearing the claim and testimony without an opposing party safeguards the right against denial by a hypothetical adversary. This is one of the two purposes of adjudication; hence such claims and testimony are heard.

Those jurists who hold that they should not be heard—as do groups among the Hanafīs, Shāfi'īs, and Hanbalīs—consider the sole purpose of adjudication to be the settlement of disputes: where there is no dispute, there is no adjudication. Accordingly, evidence is heard only in the presence of a defendant, so that a dispute is manifest.

Those who permit the appointment of a nominal adversary thereby introduce the harm of a dispute and then put an end to it. Those who hold that such evidence may be heard preserve an existing right without introducing a harm that was not there. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16800

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

On matters in which Allah has authorized a judge to adjudicate, and matters in which He has given no created being authority to judge. In the latter, judgment binding upon all creation belongs to Allah Most High and His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him. No judge may impose a ruling in such matters upon another person, even an ordinary member of the public.

These include universal matters which Allah has commanded all people to believe in and act upon, and which He has clarified in His Book and the Sunnah of His Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him—whether the Muslim community has reached consensus on them or differed over them. If a dispute concerning these matters arises between judges and individual Muslims—whether scholars, soldiers, ordinary people, or others—a judge has no authority to rule against someone who disagrees with him, compel him to adopt his position, or prevent him from expressing the other view, let alone harm or punish him.

For example, a judge or someone else may dispute the meaning of His words:

“Or have touched women.”

Does this mean sexual intercourse, as Ibn ‘Abbās and others explained it? They held that touching a woman does not invalidate ablution, whether with desire or without it. Or does it mean contact with the skin, either with desire or without qualification? The former of these last two views is reported from Ibn ‘Umar, while the third view was held by some scholars. Thus, scholars hold “three views” on this question.

---

## Page 16801

The first view is the stronger one: touching women does not invalidate ablution at all. Muslims have always touched their wives, yet no one has ever reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed Muslims to perform ablution because of this. Nor is it reported from the Companions during his lifetime that he performed ablution for this reason; nor has it ever been reported of him that he did so. Rather, the Sunan collections report:

“He would kiss one of his wives without performing ablution afterward.”

The authenticity of this hadith is disputed. There is, however, no disagreement that no report states that he performed ablution because of touching a woman.

Likewise, Muslims have differed over performing ablution after bleeding caused by venesection, cupping, wounds, or nosebleeds, and after “vomiting.” There are two well-known views on this. It has been reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, performed ablution because of these things, as did many of the Companions. Yet it has never been established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, made ablution obligatory because of them. Indeed, his Companions would go on military expeditions and pray without performing ablution on that account.

For this reason, a group of scholars held that ablution because of these things is recommended, not obligatory. They likewise held that ablution after “touching the penis” or “touching a woman with desire” is recommended, not obligatory. They also said that “ablution after laughing aloud” and “after eating food cooked by fire” is recommended, not obligatory. Whoever performs ablution has done well, and whoever does not incurs no blame. This is the strongest of the views.

The purpose here is not to discuss these issues, but to use them as examples.

---

## Page 16802

Likewise, they disagreed over many questions of inheritance, such as those concerning the grandfather and the “shared-inheritance” case; over many questions of divorce, oaths of abstention from marital relations, and other matters; and over many questions of worship relating to prayer, fasting, and Hajj. They also disagreed over visiting graves: some disapproved of it without qualification, some permitted it, and some recommended it when conducted in the prescribed manner. This last view is held by most of them.

They disagreed over “greeting the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him”: should one greet him in the mosque while facing the direction of prayer, or while facing the chamber? After offering the greeting, should one remain standing and supplicate for him, or not?

They also disagreed over which of the two mosques is superior: the Sacred Mosque or the Mosque of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. They agreed that both are superior to al-Aqsā Mosque. They also agreed that travelling to a place to worship there is not recommended except in the case of the three mosques. They agreed that anyone who vows to perform Hajj or ‘umrah must fulfil that vow. The four imams and the majority of scholars agreed that anyone who vows to travel somewhere other than the three mosques is not obliged to fulfil that vow. They disagreed, however, over the case of someone who vows to travel to the two mosques, as well as other matters too lengthy to enumerate.

They also disagreed over certain interpretations of the verses and over certain hadiths: were they established as having come from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, or not?

---

## Page 16803

Thus, in these general matters, no judge, whoever he may be—even if he is one of the Companions—may impose his own opinion by judicial ruling upon someone who disagrees with him, saying: “I have obliged him neither to act nor to issue fatwas except according to the view that accords with my school.” Rather, judgment in these questions belongs to Allah and His Messenger. The judge is but one of the Muslims: if he possesses knowledge, he should present it; and if his opponent possesses knowledge, he too should present it. If the truth becomes clear and the ruling of Allah and His Messenger is known, everyone must follow that ruling. If it remains unclear, each is allowed to retain his position—the proponent of one view retaining his position, and the proponent of the other retaining his. Neither may seek to stop the other except through knowledge, proof, and explanation, presenting whatever knowledge he possesses.

As for “physical force and coercion,” he may issue a binding ruling only in a particular case brought before him. For example, someone dies and his heirs dispute the division of his estate: if they bring their dispute before him, he divides it among them. If he rules according to one of the two scholarly opinions, he binds the litigant to his judgment. The litigant is not entitled to say: “I will not accept this unless he rules according to the other opinion.”

Likewise, if two people bring before him a dispute concerning a claim made by one against the other, he adjudicates between them as Allah and His Messenger have commanded, and obliges the party against whom judgment is given to comply with his ruling. That party is not entitled to say: “You have ruled against me according to an opinion I do not accept.” For the judge must exercise independent judgment, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When a judge exercises independent judgment and reaches the correct conclusion, he has two rewards; and when he exercises independent judgment and errs, he has one reward.”

Allah may single out certain prophets and scholars

---

## Page 16804

and judges with knowledge not granted to others, as the Most High says:

“And David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field in which some people’s sheep had grazed by night; We were witness to their judgment.”

“We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave sound judgment and knowledge.”

Judges must judge only with justice. “Justice” is what Allah has revealed, as the Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners and, when you judge between people, to judge with justice. Excellent indeed is what Allah instructs you to do. Surely Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing.”

Then the Most High says:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and best in outcome.”

Thus Allah has made obedience to those in authority obligatory alongside obedience to the Messenger. He has also obliged the Muslim community, whenever they disagree, to refer their dispute to Allah and His Messenger—to the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger. For Allah, glorified and exalted is He, is the Judge who judges between His servants, and judgment belongs to Him alone. Allah revealed the Scriptures and sent the messengers to judge between them. Whoever obeys the Messenger is among Allah’s pious allies and will enjoy happiness in this world and the Hereafter. Whoever disobeys the Messenger is among those destined for misery and punishment.

The Most High says:

“Mankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning their disagreements. None differed over it except those who had been given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of envy

---

## Page 16805

among themselves. Then Allah guided those who believed, by His permission, to the truth concerning which they differed. And Allah guides whom He wills to a straight path.”

In Sahīh Muslim, Ā’ishah reports that when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, rose to pray at night, he would say:

“O Allah, Lord of Gabriel, Michael, and Isrāfīl, Originator of the heavens and the earth, Knower of the unseen and the seen! You judge between Your servants concerning their disagreements. Guide me, by Your permission, to the truth concerning which I differ; indeed, You guide whom You will to a straight path.”

The Most High says:

“Those who were given the Scripture did not differ until knowledge had come to them, out of envy among themselves.”

Thus He, glorified and exalted is He, makes clear that He guided them and made the truth clear to them. Yet some of them wrong others despite knowing the truth, following their own desires and disobeying Allah’s command. Such a person knows the truth but deviates from it, as the Most High says:

“Relate to them the story of the one to whom We gave Our signs, but who cast them off. Satan then pursued him, and he became one of those who went astray.”

“Had We willed, We would have elevated him through them, but he clung to the earth and followed his own desires. His likeness is that of a dog: if you drive it away, it pants, and if you leave it alone, it pants. Such is the likeness of those who reject Our signs. So recount these stories, that they may reflect.”

He, glorified and exalted is He, has thus made clear that He sent the messengers and sent down with them the Scripture to judge between people concerning their disagreements. The Most High says:

“Whatever you disagree about, its judgment rests with Allah. Such is Allah, my Lord; in Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

Joseph said:

“O my two fellow prisoners! Are many different lords better, or Allah, the One, the All-Subduing?”

“You worship besides Him nothing but names that you and your forefathers have named, for which Allah has sent down no

---

## Page 16806

authority. Judgment belongs to Allah alone. He has commanded that you worship none but Him. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

Judgment thus belongs to Allah alone, and His messengers convey His message. Their judgment is His judgment, their command is His command, and obedience to them is obedience to Him. Whatever the Messenger rules, commands them to do, or prescribes as religion, all created beings must follow and obey, for that is Allah’s judgment concerning His creation.

The Messenger conveys Allah’s message. The Most High says:

“We sent no messenger except to be obeyed by Allah’s permission. If, when they wronged themselves, they had come to you and sought Allah’s forgiveness, and the Messenger had sought forgiveness for them, they would have found Allah Accepting of repentance, Merciful.”

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge concerning their disputes, then find within themselves no discomfort over what you have decided and submit fully.”

All created beings must therefore accept as their judge the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the Seal of the Prophets, the finest of the messengers, and the most honoured of creation before Allah. No one may depart from his judgment in anything, whether a scholar, a king, a shaykh, or anyone else.

Had Moses, Jesus, or any other messenger lived to witness his coming, they would have been obliged to follow him, as the Most High says:

“And when Allah took the covenant of the prophets: ‘Whatever Scripture and wisdom I give you, should a messenger then come to you confirming what is with you, you must believe in him and support him.’ He said, ‘Do you affirm this and accept My binding covenant?’ They said, ‘We affirm it.’ He said, ‘Then bear witness, and I am with you among the witnesses.’”

It is reported from several of the early generations—ʿAlī, Ibn ʿAbbās, and others—that they said:

“Never

---

## Page 16807

did Allah send a prophet from the time of Noah onward without taking a covenant from him that, if Muhammad were sent while he was alive, he would certainly believe in him and support him. He also commanded him to take a covenant from his people that, if Muhammad were sent while they were alive, they would certainly believe in him and support him.”

He, glorified be He, took a covenant from each earlier prophet to affirm the truth of whoever came after him, and from each later prophet to affirm the truth of whoever came before him. Thus, the prophets did not differ; rather, their religion is one, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“We prophets have one religion.”

The Most High says:

“O messengers, eat of the good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do. And indeed this religion of yours is one religion, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

That is, your faith is one faith, as in their words:

“Indeed, we found our forefathers following a religion”—that is, a faith.

The Most High also says:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: establish the religion and do not become divided over it. What you call the polytheists to is grievous to them.”

The religion of the prophets is therefore one: the religion of Islam. They were all Muslims and believers, as Allah has made clear in several places in the Quran. Some of their prescribed laws, however, differ. He may prescribe something at one time for a wise purpose, then prescribe something else at another time for a wise purpose, just as, in the early days of Islam, He prescribed

---

## Page 16808

prayer facing Jerusalem. He then abrogated that ruling and commanded prayer facing the Kaaba. Thus, the prescribed law changed while the religion remained one. Facing the Levant at that time was part of the religion of Islam, as was observing the Sabbath for Moses. Once the earlier ruling was abrogated, however, the religion of Islam lay in the superseding ruling: praying towards the Kaaba. Whoever adheres to the abrogated ruling rather than the superseding one is neither following the religion of Islam nor following any of the prophets. Whoever alters the prophets’ law and invents a law of his own produces a false law that may not be followed, as Allah says:

“Or do they have partners who have ordained for them in religion what Allah has not authorized?”

This is why the Jews and Christians disbelieved: they adhered to a law that had been altered and abrogated. Allah has obliged all creation to believe in all His Books and messengers. Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the last of the messengers, so all creation must follow him and the religion he prescribed, namely what he brought of the Book and Sunnah. What the Book and Sunnah teach is the law that all creation must follow; no one may depart from it. It is the law that those who wage jihad fight to uphold: the Book and Sunnah.

The swords of the Muslims support this law—the Book and Sunnah—as Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded us to strike with this—meaning the sword—whoever departs from this—meaning the written copy of the Quran.”

The Most High says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance so that people may uphold justice. And We sent down

---

## Page 16809

iron, in which there is great might and benefits for people, and so that Allah may know those who support Him and His messengers without seeing Him. Indeed, Allah is All-Powerful, Almighty.”

He, glorified and exalted, thus makes clear that He sent down the Book, justice, and the means by which justice is known, so that people may uphold justice; and He sent down iron. Whoever departs from the Book and the balance is fought with iron. The Book and justice are inseparable. The Book explains the prescribed law; thus, the law is justice, and justice is the law. Whoever judges justly has judged according to the law. Yet many people attribute their statements to the law when they are no part of it. They make such statements out of ignorance, error, or deliberate fabrication. This is the altered law whose proponents deserve punishment, not the revealed law that Gabriel brought from Allah to the last of the messengers. That revealed law is entirely just: it contains neither injustice nor ignorance. The Most High says:

“And if you judge, judge between them with justice. Indeed, Allah loves those who act justly.”

The Most High also says:

“And judge between them by what Allah has revealed.”

What Allah has revealed is justice, and justice is what Allah has revealed. The Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners and, when you judge between people, to judge with justice.”

The Most High also says:

“Indeed, We have sent down to you the Book in truth so that you may judge between people by what Allah has shown you.”

What Allah has shown him in His Book is justice.

Many Muslim scholars endowed with knowledge and religious devotion—from among the Companions, the Successors, and the other imams of the Muslims, such as the four imams and others—may express opinions based on their independent scholarly judgment; with regard to these opinions, it is permissible

---

## Page 16810

to adopt them, and no Muslim is obliged to adhere to any statement except that of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This, then, is law involving interpretation and independent scholarly judgment. It may, in fact, accord with the revealed law, in which case the one who holds it receives two rewards; or it may not accord with it. Yet Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. If a servant fears Allah as much as he can, Allah rewards him for that and forgives his error. No one is entitled to condemn, disparage, or punish someone in this position. However, once the truth is known to contradict his opinion, it is not permissible to abandon the truth with which Allah sent His Messenger for the opinion of any created being.

That truth is the law revealed by Allah—the Quran and the Sunnah—and it is the religion of Allah and His Messenger, so that Allah’s word may be supreme and all worship may be devoted to Allah. They do not wage jihad to uphold the opinion of a scholar, a shaykh, or someone exercising interpretation. Rather, they wage jihad so that Allah alone may be worshipped and worship may be devoted to Him. Thus, it is reported in al-Musnad from Ibn ‘Umar that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I was sent with the sword ahead of the Hour, so that Allah alone may be worshipped, without any partner. My provision has been placed beneath the shade of my spear, and humiliation and abasement have been imposed upon whoever opposes my command. Whoever imitates a people is one of them.”

The Most High says:

“Fight them until there is no more fitnah and all worship is devoted to Allah.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī reported:

“It was asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, a man fights out of bravery, out of partisan zeal, or for show. Which of these is in Allah’s cause?’ He replied, ‘Whoever fights so that Allah’s word may be supreme is in Allah’s cause.’”

---

## Page 16811

Thus, the purpose of jihad is that none should be worshipped except Allah: no one should invoke, pray to, prostrate before, or fast for anyone other than Him; perform ‘umrah or Hajj except to His House; offer sacrifices or make vows except to Him; swear except by Him; rely upon anyone but Him; fear anyone but Him; or be mindful of anyone but Him. He alone brings good and averts harm; He alone guides His creation, grants them victory, provides for them, frees them from need, and forgives their sins. The Most High says:

“Allah has said, ‘Do not take two gods. He is but One God, so fear Me alone.’

“To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth, and to Him alone is worship perpetually due. Will you then fear anyone other than Allah?

“Whatever blessing you have is from Allah. Then, when hardship touches you, it is to Him that you cry for help.

“Then, when He removes the hardship from you, some of you associate partners with their Lord,

“showing ingratitude for what We have given them. So enjoy yourselves; you will soon know.”

Allah Most High has forbidden every form of associating partners with Him and setting up any rival to Him. None besides Him may be invoked—not the angels, the prophets, the righteous, the sun, the moon, the stars, idols, or anything else. Indeed, He has made clear that whoever takes the angels and prophets as lords is a disbeliever. The Most High says:

“It is not for a human being to whom Allah has given the Scripture, judgment, and prophethood to then say to people, ‘Be my servants rather than Allah’s.’ Rather, he would say, ‘Be godly scholars because of your teaching of the Scripture and your study of it.’

“Nor would he command you to take the angels and prophets as lords. Would he command you to disbelieve after you have become Muslims?”

And the Most High says:

---

## Page 16812

“Say, ‘Call upon those you claim to be gods besides Him. They have no power to remove your hardship or shift it elsewhere.’

“Those whom they invoke themselves seek a means of drawing near to their Lord, seeking to be nearest to Him. They hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. Indeed, your Lord’s punishment is to be feared.”

Allah, glorified and exalted is He, censures those who invoke the angels, the prophets, and other righteous people. He explains that those whom they invoke have no power to remove their hardship or shift it elsewhere. They themselves draw near to Allah through the means of approach—namely, righteous deeds—and hope for His mercy and fear His punishment. How, then, can people invoke created beings and forsake the Creator? The Most High says:

“Do those who disbelieve think they can take My servants as protectors besides Me? We have prepared Hell as a welcome for the disbelievers.”

He, glorified and exalted is He, knows His servants’ circumstances and is merciful to them, as related in an authentic hadith about the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“He saw a woman among the captives who, upon seeing a child, pressed him to her belly. He said, ‘Do you think this woman would cast her child into the Fire?’ They replied, ‘No, O Messenger of Allah.’ He said, ‘Allah is more merciful to His servants than this woman is to her child.’”

He, glorified is He, is All-Hearing and Near. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘If I go astray, I do so only to my own detriment; but if I am guided, it is through what my Lord reveals to me. Indeed, He is All-Hearing and Near.’”

He, Most High, is Merciful and Loving. “Affection” means kindness and love. He loves His believing servants and places affection for them in people’s hearts, as the Most High says:

“Indeed, those who believe and do righteous deeds—the Most Merciful will grant them affection.”

Ibn ‘Abbās and others said: He loves them and makes His servants love them.

---

## Page 16813

Hearing one person does not distract Him, glorified is He, from hearing another. Requests do not confuse Him, nor does He grow weary of persistent supplication. Rather, He loves those who call upon Him and implore Him, and He detests those who do not call upon Him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah becomes angry with whoever does not ask Him.”

The Most High says:

“Your Lord says, ‘Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell, humiliated.’”

And He, the Most High, exalted is He, says:

“When My servants ask you about Me, I am indeed near. I answer the supplicant’s call when he calls upon Me. So let them respond to Me and believe in Me, so that they may be rightly guided.”

Some of the Companions said, “O Messenger of Allah, is our Lord near, so that we may address Him quietly, or distant, so that we should call out to Him?” Allah then revealed this verse.

He, glorified and exalted is He, is unlike created beings, to whom requests are conveyed through barriers. Rather, in an authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah says, ‘I have divided the prayer into two halves between Myself and My servant: half is for Me and half is for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.’ When the servant says, ‘All praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds,’ Allah says, ‘My servant has praised Me.’ When he says, ‘The Most Merciful, the Ever-Merciful,’ Allah says, ‘My servant has extolled Me.’ When he says, ‘Master of the Day of Judgment,’ Allah says, ‘My servant has glorified Me.’

“When he says, ‘You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help,’ Allah says, ‘This verse is divided into two halves between Myself and My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.’ When he says, ‘Guide us to the straight path—the path of those You have blessed, not those who have incurred Your anger, nor those who have gone astray,’ He says, ‘These verses are for My servant, and My servant shall have what he asks for.’”

---

## Page 16814

He, glorified is He, will Himself speak to His servants on the Day of Resurrection, as related in the authentic collection from ʿAdī ibn Hātim, who reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is not one of you but that his Lord, mighty and majestic, will speak to him, with neither a doorkeeper nor an interpreter between them. He will look to his right and see only what he has sent ahead, and look to his left and see only what he has sent ahead. He will look before him, and the Fire will confront him. So whoever among you can protect himself from the Fire, even with half a date, should do so. If he cannot find that, then with a kind word.”

He, glorified is He, is near to those who call upon Him and draws near to those who worship and obey Him. As related in an authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah, the Most High, says: ‘I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me. If he remembers Me within himself, I remember him within Myself. If he remembers Me in a gathering, I remember him in a gathering better than it. If he draws near to Me by a handspan, I draw near to him by a cubit. If he draws near to Me by a cubit, I draw near to him by an armspan. If he comes to Me walking, I come to him running.’”

Allah, glorified is He, bestows kindness, bounty and generosity upon His servants, not because He has any need of them. As the Most High says:

“And say, ‘All praise is for Allah, Who has never taken a son, nor has He any partner in His dominion, nor does He need a protector out of weakness.’ And proclaim His greatness.”

He alone calls His servants to account, and He is the One who recompenses them for their deeds:

“So whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.”

He is the One who provides for them, grants them well-being, helps them and guides them; no one other than Him does this. The Most High says:

“Who is there to serve as an army for you, helping you against the Most Merciful? There is nothing

---

## Page 16815

but delusion for the disbelievers.”

“Or who will provide for you if He withholds His provision? Yet they persist in insolence and aversion.”

The Most High also says:

“Say, ‘Who guards you by night and day from the Most Merciful?’ Yet they turn away from the remembrance of their Lord.”

The more correct of the two interpretations of this verse is that it means: Who is there to guard you in Allah’s stead? Who wards off the harms you fear from humans and jinn?

The Messenger is the intermediary and emissary between them and Allah, mighty and majestic. He conveys to them Allah’s commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings, and what He has made lawful and forbidden. Thus, the lawful is what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful; the forbidden is what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden; and the religion is what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed. No one may depart from anything prescribed by the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is the revealed law that those in authority must require people to observe, that those engaged in jihad must fight to uphold, and that every individual must follow and support.

The revealed law binding upon all creation does not mean “a judge’s ruling,” even if the judge is the best person of his time. Rather, the ruling of a knowledgeable, just judge binds particular people who have brought a particular case before him; it does not bind all creation. Nor is any Muslim scholar obliged to follow a judge’s opinion in any matter, great or small, if he knows what Allah and His Messenger have commanded. Indeed, no individual among the general public is obliged to follow the judge’s opinion in any matter. Rather, he may seek a fatwa from anyone he is permitted to consult, even if that person is not a judge. Whenever a scholar abandons what he knows from

---

## Page 16816

the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger and follows a judge’s ruling that contradicts the ruling of Allah and His Messenger, he becomes an apostate and disbeliever, deserving punishment in this world and the Hereafter. The Most High says:

“Alif Lām Mīm Sād.”

“A Book sent down to you—so let there be no distress in your heart because of it—that you may warn thereby, and as a reminder to the believers.”

“Follow what has been sent down to you from your Lord, and do not follow other protectors besides Him. How little you heed the reminder!”

Even if he were beaten, imprisoned, and subjected to all kinds of harm to make him abandon what he knows of the law of Allah and His Messenger, which he is obliged to follow, he would still deserve Allah’s punishment if he followed someone else’s ruling. Rather, he must remain patient, even if he suffers harm for Allah’s sake. This is Allah’s established way with the prophets and their followers. Allah Most High says:

“Alif Lām Mīm.”

“Do people think they will be left to say, ‘We believe,’ without being tested?”

“We certainly tested those before them. Allah will surely know those who are truthful, and He will surely know the liars.”

The Most High also says:

“We will surely test you until We know those among you who strive and those who remain steadfast, and We will put your deeds to the test.”

The Most High also says:

“Or do you think you will enter Paradise without undergoing what befell those who passed away before you? They were afflicted with hardship and suffering, and were so shaken that even the Messenger and those who believed with him said, ‘When will Allah’s help come?’ Indeed, Allah’s help is near.”

This applies when a judge has ruled on a matter open to scholarly judgment over which the Companions and Successors differed, adopting the opinion of some of them, while others know of a Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that conflicts with his ruling. Such a person must follow

---

## Page 16817

what he knows of the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, enjoin it, issue fatwas in accordance with it, and call others to it, rather than defer to the judge. All of this is agreed upon by the Muslims.

If a Muslim, whether a scholar or otherwise, abandons what he knows of the command of Allah and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in favor of someone else’s opinion, he deserves punishment. The Most High says:

“So let those who oppose his command beware, lest a trial befall them or a painful punishment afflict them.”

If, however, that judge was unaware of this text—as was the case with many of the Companions, the Successors, the four imams, and others who expressed opinions on matters through independent scholarly judgment when a Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, contradicted their conclusions—then such people are excused, because they exercised independent scholarly judgment, and:

“Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

But whoever knows the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, may not turn away from it to anything else. The Most High says:

“It is not for a believing man or woman, when Allah and His Messenger have decided a matter, to have any choice in their affair. Whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger has clearly gone astray.”

Whoever follows that with which Allah sent His Messenger will be guided and granted Allah’s support in this world and the Hereafter, as the Most High says:

“We will surely support Our messengers and those who believe, both in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.”

The Most High also says:

“Our word has already gone forth to Our servants, the messengers:

They will surely be given victory,

And Our forces will surely prevail.”

When a calamity befalls a servant, it is because of his sin, not because he follows the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Rather, it is through following the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that

---

## Page 16818

he receives mercy and support, while it is because of his sins that he is punished and forsaken. The Most High says:

“Whatever calamity befalls you is because of what your own hands have earned, and He pardons much.”

Thus, when the Muslims were defeated at Uhud, despite being with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the enemy gained the upper hand over them, Allah explained that this was because of their sins. The Most High says:

“Those of you who turned away on the day the two armies met—it was Satan who caused them to slip because of some of what they had earned. But Allah has pardoned them. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Forbearing.”

The Most High also says:

“When a calamity befell you, although you had inflicted twice as much, did you say, ‘How has this happened?’ Say, ‘It is from yourselves.’”

He, glorified be He, explained the wisdom behind testing them. The Most High says:

“Similar situations have passed before you, so travel through the land and see how those who denied the truth met their end.”

“This is a clear explanation for mankind, and guidance and an admonition for the God-fearing.”

“Do not lose heart or grieve, for you will have the upper hand, if you are believers.”

“If you have suffered a wound, the other people have suffered a similar wound. We alternate such days among people, so that Allah may make evident those who believe and take martyrs from among you. And Allah does not love the wrongdoers.”

“And so that Allah may purify those who believe and destroy the disbelievers.”

The Most High also says:

“Whatever good befalls you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.”

Allah has decreed it, and He has decreed everything. Yet whatever well-being, victory, or provision a servant receives comes from Allah’s favor and kindness toward him. All good is from Allah; the servant has nothing of his own. Rather, he is utterly dependent and has no power

---

## Page 16819

to benefit or harm himself, or to bring about his own death, life, or resurrection.

Whatever calamity befalls him is because of his sins. Through these calamities, Allah Most High expiates the believers’ sins and rewards them for patiently bearing them. He forgives those who seek His forgiveness and accepts the repentance of those who repent. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No fatigue, illness, anxiety, distress, sorrow, or harm afflicts a believer—even the prick of a thorn—without Allah expiating some of his sins through it.”

When Allah Most High revealed His words, “Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it,” Abu Bakr said, “O Messenger of Allah, this is a crushing blow! Which of us has not done evil?”

He said, “O Abu Bakr, do you not grow weary? Do you not grieve? Do you not suffer hardship? That is how you are recompensed.”

Allah has related to us in the Quran the accounts of the prophets and the harm they and their believing followers suffered for His sake. Then He granted them victory and made the final outcome theirs. He related this to us so that we might learn from it. The Most High says:

“In their stories there is certainly a lesson for people of understanding. This is not a fabricated account, but a confirmation of what came before it, an explanation of all things, and guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

Thus, the divine law that every Muslim must follow, and that those in authority must uphold and wage jihad for, is the Quran and the Sunnah. As for a judge’s ruling, that is called a judicial decision; it is not the divine law that Allah has required all creation to obey. Rather, a learned and just judge is sometimes right and sometimes wrong. If a judge were to rule in someone’s favor contrary to

---

## Page 16820

the actual truth, it would not be lawful for that person to take what he was awarded, even if the judge were the foremost of all mankind, first and last. Thus, in the two Sahīh collections, Umm Salama reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“You bring your disputes before me, and some of you may present their case more eloquently than others, so I rule in their favor according to what I hear. If I award anyone something that rightfully belongs to his brother, he must not take it, for I am only allotting him a piece of the Fire.”

Here is the foremost of judges, rulers, and kings saying: “If I award someone something he knows he is not entitled to, he must not take it.” The Muslims unanimously agree that a judge’s ruling concerning ownership as such has no effect on the underlying reality. Thus, if he awarded Zayd property belonging to ʿAmr, exercising independent legal judgment and earnestly seeking the truth, it would still not be lawful for Zayd to take it.

As for “contracts and their dissolution”—such as a ruling establishing a marriage, a divorce, a sale, or the rescission of a sale—there is a well-known disagreement. The majority hold that these rulings likewise have no effect on the underlying reality. This is a well-known issue. All this concerns a learned and just judge who has ruled on a worldly matter.

“Judges are of three kinds,” as reported in the Sunan collections from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Judges are of three kinds: two are in the Fire and one is in Paradise. A man who knows the truth and judges accordingly is in Paradise. A man who judges between people without knowledge is in the Fire. A man who knows the truth but rules contrary to it is in the Fire.”

Even when a judge who belongs among the people of Paradise awards someone something that the recipient knows is not rightfully his, it is unlawful for him to take it, according to the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the consensus of the Muslims. What, then, if he rules on matters of religion over which he has no authority to adjudicate? In such matters, he is merely one Muslim among others: if he has knowledge, he may speak; otherwise, he must remain silent;

---

## Page 16821

as when he rules that travel to places other than the three mosques is prescribed and recommended, that whoever undertakes it is rewarded, and that anyone who says it is not recommended is to be harmed and punished or imprisoned.

Such a ruling is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims. No one who knows the religion of Islam may follow it, nor may any ruler enforce it. Whoever enforces and supports such a ruling is subject to the same judgment as others like him: if the proof with which Allah sent His Messenger has been established against him and he contravenes it, he deserves punishment. Likewise, if he imposes such a ruling out of ignorance and obliges people to follow something of which he has no knowledge, he deserves punishment. If, however, he errs while exercising independent legal judgment, he is pardoned.

Allah has required Muslim rulers to follow the revealed law, namely the Quran and Sunnah. When Muslims disagree over a religious question, even if the person disputing it is merely an individual student of knowledge, the rulers may not compel him to follow a judge’s ruling. Rather, they must explain the truth to him, just as someone with knowledge explains the truth to someone who is ignorant. If the truth with which Allah sent His Messenger becomes clear and manifest to him, and he stubbornly opposes it thereafter, he deserves punishment.

As for someone who says, “What I have stated is my own view or the view of a group of Muslim scholars, and I have stated it on the basis of independent legal judgment or in reliance on scholarly authority,” the Muslims agree that he may not be punished, even if he has committed an error contrary to the Quran and Sunnah. Were such a person to be punished, all Muslims would be punished, for every one of them holds some mistaken views reached through independent legal judgment or reliance on scholarly authority. If Allah punished everyone who erred, He would punish all of creation. Rather, Allah Most High has said in the Quran:

“The Messenger has believed in what was sent down to him from his Lord, as have the believers. Each has believed

---

## Page 16822

in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers. ‘We make no distinction between any of His messengers.’ And they say, ‘We hear and obey. Grant us Your forgiveness, our Lord, and to You is the final return.’”
