“Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. It will have the good it has earned and bear the evil it has earned. Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord, do not lay upon us a burden like that which You laid upon those before us. Our Lord, do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our Protector, so grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”

It is established in the Sahīh, on the authority of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), that Allah answered this supplication. When the believers said, “Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake,” Allah said, “I have done so.” The same applies to the rest of the supplication. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) also said, “Allah has pardoned my community for mistakes, forgetfulness, and what they are compelled to do.”

Thus, when a mufti, a soldier, or a layperson expresses a view according to their best judgment—whether through independent legal reasoning or reliance on scholarly authority—intending to follow the Messenger to the best of their knowledge, they do not deserve punishment, by the consensus of the Muslims, even if their error is unanimously recognized as such.

If they say, “We have spoken the truth,” and support their position with evidence from the revealed law, no judge may compel them to accept his view merely on his own authority, nor rule that his view, rather than theirs, is the truth. Rather, the Quran and Sunnah must judge between him and them. The truth with which Allah sent His Messenger must not be concealed; rather, it must be brought to light. If it becomes clear, everyone must return to it. If it does not, each side should leave the other alone. This is the case with questions disputed by adherents of the different schools of law. No one says that a follower of one school must follow someone else’s school merely because the latter is a judge, for the situation may be reversed: the other person may become a judge

---

## Page 16823

and rule that his own view is correct. Yet it is impossible for both of two opposing views to be binding upon all Muslims. This is unlike what the Messenger (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) brought, for it is from Allah: truth, guidance, and clarification, containing no error whatsoever, no inconsistency, and no contradiction. Allah Most High says:

“Do they not reflect upon the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have found in it much contradiction.”

Those in authority must prevent them from wronging one another. If some transgress against others, they must stop that aggression. They are already obliged to prevent oppression of protected non-Muslim subjects and to ensure that no Jew or Christian in their lands who observes the stipulated conditions is compelled to abandon his religion—even though it is known that his religion entails punishment. How, then, can those in authority permit groups of Muslims to transgress against one another and impose rulings on one another according to their own views and schools of law? This is among the things that bring about the upheaval and collapse of states, for people cannot fare well under such conditions.

All this applies when the judges have ruled on a matter subject to independent legal reasoning and recognized disagreement. But if the position on which they have ruled was never held by any of the leading Muslim scholars, is not the position of the imams whose followers they claim to be, was never expressed by any of the Companions or Successors, and has no support in any verse of Allah’s Book or in the Sunnah of His Messenger (may Allah bless him and grant him peace)—indeed, their position contradicts the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the imams—how, despite all this, can it be lawful to compel Muslim scholars

---

## Page 16824

to follow this view and subject them to this ruling, which contradicts the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus? How can it be declared that the view supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and the statements of the early generations must neither be expressed nor made the basis of a fatwa? Instead, those who issue fatwas according to it, those who voice it, and others besides are punished and harmed. Muslims suffer harm to their persons, families, and property because they have followed what they know of the religion of Islam, even if others remain unaware of it. Yet they excuse those who are unaware of it, neither compel them to follow them nor transgress against them.

How, then, can those who do not know the truth be supported, while judgment is passed in ignorance and injustice, and those who possess knowledge of the Messenger’s law be compelled, for this reason, to abandon what they know of his law—may Allah bless him and grant him peace? There is no doubt that this is a grave matter before Allah Most High, His angels, His prophets, and His servants. Allah is not heedless of such things. Nor is the right at issue here the personal right of any created being. Those who have followed what they know of the Messenger’s law—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—have wronged no one in life, property, or honour, and no one has any claim against them.

Rather, they have said: “We follow what we know of the religion of Islam and what the Quran and the Sunnah have brought concerning Allah’s oneness and the worship of Him without any partner. We worship Allah alone, and we worship Him through what His Messenger commanded and prescribed as religion. Whatever Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—calls us to and commands us to do, we obey him in it. Whatever the Messenger has established as religion, a means of drawing near to Allah, obedience, a good deed, righteous action, and goodness, we hear and obey Allah and His Messenger concerning it. We believe it to be a means of drawing near to Allah and an act of obedience; we practise it, love those who act upon it, and call others to it. And whatever we have been forbidden

---

## Page 16825

by the Messenger, we refrain from, even if others believe it to be a means of drawing near to Allah. Our duty is to obey the Messenger, not to obey anyone who opposes him, even if that person is acting on an interpretation.”

It is well known that the People of the Book and the adherents of religious innovations engage in many forms of worship which they regard as means of drawing near to Allah and acts of obedience, although Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—has forbidden them. If someone says, “I obey the Messenger and do not engage in these forms of worship; rather, I forbid what Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—has forbidden,” how can it be permissible to oppose him? Indeed, even if he were mistaken despite exercising independent scholarly judgment, he would not deserve punishment, by the consensus of the Muslims.

Nor is he obliged to follow anyone’s ruling, by the consensus of the Muslims. A judge has no authority to rule that Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—commanded a particular thing; that a particular deed is, or is not, an act of obedience or a means of drawing near to Allah; or that travelling to mosques and graves, including the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is or is not prescribed. Judges have no authority in such matters beyond that shared by other Muslims. Rather, discussion of these matters is open to the entire community of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and whoever possesses knowledge may speak on the basis of that knowledge.

No one has the right to pass judgment against a scholar, by the consensus of the Muslims. Rather, his error must be explained to him. If it is demonstrated to him, through scriptural evidence that must be accepted, that he has erred, and his error becomes apparent to people, yet he does not retract it but persists in publicly expressing what contradicts the Quran and the Sunnah and calling others to it, then he must be restrained from doing so and punished if he refuses to desist. But if this has not been demonstrated to him by evidence

---

## Page 16826

from the revealed law, then, by the consensus of the Muslims, it is not permissible to punish him, prevent him from expressing that view, or rule that he must not express it, when he says, “This is what the Quran and Sunnah indicate, as such-and-such Muslim scholars have also stated.” If such a person exercises independent scholarly judgment and errs, he may be judged only according to the Quran and Sunnah. His opponent, however, speaks without knowledge, and the ruling he has issued has not been stated by any Muslim scholar. Even if the leading Muslim scholars expressed a view like that of the judges, they could compel people to accept it only on the basis of evidence from the revealed law, not merely on the strength of their own ruling.

For the one whom Allah has obliged people to follow and obey is the Messenger, whose judgment, command, law, and religion they must follow. He is Allah’s proof against His creation. Through him Allah distinguished between truth and falsehood, guidance and misguidance, right conduct and error, and the path to Paradise and the path to Hell. Through him Allah guided His creation. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, We have revealed to you as We revealed to Noah and the prophets after him. And We revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, the Tribes, Jesus, Job, Jonah, Aaron, and Solomon; and We gave David the Psalms.”

“And messengers We have already told you about, and messengers We have not told you about. And Allah spoke directly to Moses.”

“Messengers bringing good tidings and warnings, so that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers.”

An authentic report relates that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“No one loves to accept an excuse more than Allah; for that reason He sent the messengers as bearers of good tidings and warnings.”

Thus, the proof against creation is established through the messengers. What the Messenger brought is the revealed law that all people must accept, and everyone must refer their disputes to the Quran and Sunnah.

---

## Page 16827

For this reason, one of the fundamental principles of the Sunnah and the community is that none of those who assumed authority after the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—such as the Rightly Guided Caliphs and others, need possess knowledge unknown to everyone else. Rather, Muslims share in knowing the religion prescribed by the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When some possess knowledge of hadith that others do not, they convey it to them.

For this reason, the caliphs would ask the Companions concerning certain matters, “Do you have any knowledge from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—about this?” Once the Messenger’s Sunnah became clear to them, they ruled accordingly. Thus, Abū Bakr al-Siddīq asked them about a grandmother’s inheritance when she came to him. He said, “There is no share prescribed for you in Allah’s Book, and I know of nothing prescribed for you in the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah. But let me ask the people.” He asked them, and Muhammad ibn Maslama and others informed him that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had awarded her one-sixth.

Likewise, “When ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb asked them about an unborn child who had been killed, one of the Companions stood and informed him that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had ruled that compensation should be a male or female slave.” That is, whoever killed an unborn child was liable to give its heirs a male or female slave in compensation. So ʿUmar ruled accordingly. They stated that the slave’s value should equal one-tenth of the blood money payable for the child’s mother.

The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said concerning ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb:

“Among the nations before you there were people who were inspired. If there is anyone like that in my nation, it is ʿUmar.”

It is also reported that “truth was firmly established on his tongue and in his heart.” And he said:

“Had I not been sent among you, ʿUmar would have been sent among you.”

Despite this, he would not bind anyone to his own opinion or rule on public matters; rather, he would consult the Companions and reconsider. At times he would express a view, only to be corrected

---

## Page 16828

by a woman, whose view he would then accept. For example, he wanted to set a limit on bridal gifts, so that they would not exceed those given to the Prophet’s wives—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said, “Whoever gives more, the excess will be placed in the public treasury.” Muslims would pay the bridal gift before consummating the marriage; they deferred it only rarely.

A woman said, “Commander of the Faithful, why do you deny us something Allah has granted us in His Book?”

He asked, “Where?” She replied, “In His words, Most High:

‘If you wish to replace one wife with another, and have given one of them a great sum, do not take anything back from it.’”

So ʿUmar accepted her view and said, “A woman was right, and a man was wrong.”

In disputed matters, such as questions of inheritance and divorce, he would hold one view, ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib another, ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd another, and Zayd ibn Thābit another. He did not compel anyone to adopt his position; rather, each of them would issue fatwas according to his own view, even though ʿUmar—may Allah be pleased with him—was the leader of the entire Muslim community, the most knowledgeable and devout among them, and the best of them. How, then, could any judge be better than ʿUmar?

This applies when he has ruled on a matter open to independent scholarly judgment. What, then, if his position has never been held by any of the imams of the Muslims—not the four imams, nor the Companions and Successors before them—but is held only by him and others like him who have no knowledge of the Book, the Sunnah, or the views of the early generations and the imams?

They merely judge according to the customs in which they were brought up, like those who said:

“We found our forefathers following a way, and we follow in their footsteps.”

And just as judgments are passed by

---

## Page 16829

the Bedouins according to their inherited precedents, which are customs, just as the Tatars judge according to the “Yāsiq,” which they customarily follow. As for people of faith, Islam, knowledge, and religious devotion, they judge only according to the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger, as Allah Most High says:

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge in their disputes, then find within themselves no resistance to your judgment and submit completely.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Is it the judgment of the age of ignorance they seek? And who is better than Allah in judgment for people who are certain?”

Allah, glorified be He, did not deem the judgment of one person sufficient between spouses when discord is feared between them, since it is not known which of them is at fault and there is no evidence to establish this. Rather, He commanded the appointment of two arbitrators who must not be open to suspicion of bias: one from the husband’s family and one from the wife’s family. As Allah Most High says:

“If you fear a breach between them, appoint an arbitrator from his family and an arbitrator from hers. If they both desire reconciliation”—meaning the two arbitrators—“Allah will bring harmony between them”—meaning the spouses.

If the arbitrators consider it best to keep the spouses together, they do so; and if they consider it best to separate them, they do so. If the wife is at fault, the separation is in exchange for compensation she pays, making it a khulʿ divorce. If the husband is at fault, they are separated without his consent.

Most scholars hold that these two are arbitrators, as Allah has called them, and that they issue their judgment without being appointed as agents by the spouses. This is the position of Mālik, and one of the two views reported from each of al-Shāfiʿī and Imam Ahmad. Another view holds that they are agents, as maintained by Abū Hanīfah and in the other view within the two schools.

---

## Page 16830

Thus, in this case, when it was unclear who was in the right, Allah did not entrust judgment to one person, even though this concerned a particular dispute between two spouses. Even if a single judge were to rule between the spouses on a clear matter, his ruling would not be enforced, by agreement of the Muslims. How, then, could this be permitted in matters of religion and worship that concern all Muslims and have become unclear to many people?

By the consensus of the Muslims, judgment in such matters belongs to Allah and His Messenger alone. Whoever has knowledge of what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought should explain and clarify it to the Muslims. Once Muslims know the law of their Prophet, they will not depart from it. If, however, each group says, “We possess knowledge from the Messenger,” and there is no clear point on which they agree concerning their dispute, then one of the two groups receives two rewards and the other receives one reward. As Allah Most High says:

“And David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which some people’s sheep had strayed by night; We were witness to their judgment.”

“We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave wisdom and knowledge.”

As for “the ruler,” if he knows what the Book and the Sunnah teach, he must judge between people accordingly. If he does not know, but can learn what each side says until he recognizes the truth, he must judge according to it. If neither course is possible for him, he must leave the Muslims as they are, each worshipping Allah according to his own scholarly judgment. He has no right to compel anyone to accept another person’s opinion, even if he is a judge.

---

## Page 16831

And when rulers depart from this, they judge by other than what Allah has revealed, and strife breaks out among them. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No people judge by other than what Allah has revealed without strife breaking out among them.”

This is among the greatest causes of changes in rule, as has happened time and again in our age and in earlier ages. Whoever Allah wills to grant happiness, He enables him to learn from what has befallen others, so that he follows the path of those whom Allah has supported and granted victory, and avoids the path of those whom Allah has forsaken and humiliated. For Allah says in His Book:

“Allah will surely support those who support Him. Indeed, Allah is Powerful and Almighty.”

“Those who, if We establish them in the land, establish prayer, give zakah, enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong. And to Allah belongs the outcome of all matters.”

Allah has thus promised to support those who support Him. Supporting Him means supporting His Book, His religion and His Messenger—not supporting those who judge by other than what Allah has revealed and speak without knowledge. For if a judge is devout but judges without knowledge, he is among the people of the Fire. If he is knowledgeable but judges contrary to the truth he knows, he is among the people of the Fire. If he judges with neither justice nor knowledge, he is all the more deserving of being among the people of the Fire. This applies when he judges a particular case concerning an individual.

But if he issues a general ruling concerning the Muslims’ religion, declaring truth to be falsehood and falsehood to be truth, the Sunnah to be innovation and innovation to be the Sunnah, right to be wrong and wrong to be right, forbidding what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and commanding what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden—then this is another matter entirely. Judgment in it belongs to the Lord of all worlds, the God of the messengers, the Master of the Day of Judgment, to whom

“belongs all praise in this world and the Hereafter. His is the judgment, and to Him you will be returned,”

“who sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religion. And sufficient is Allah as a Witness.”

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless Muhammad, his family and his Companions, and grant them peace.

---

## Page 16832

Shaykh al-Islām, may Allah have mercy on him, also said: (*)

The cases adjudicated by those in authority—whether they are called judges or governors, or whether some have at times been called wulāt al-ahdāth or officials charged with redressing grievances, or other customary, conventional titles—are all subject to the judgment of Allah, Blessed and Exalted, which encompasses all creation.

Everyone entrusted with the affairs of the Muslim community, or who adjudicates between two people, must judge with justice and equity, according to the Book of Allah and the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is the law revealed by Allah. Allah Most High says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance so that people may uphold justice. And We sent down iron, with its great might and benefits for people.”

He Most High also says:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners and, when you judge between people, to judge with justice.”

He Most High also says:

“Indeed, We have sent down to you the Book in truth so that you may judge between people by what Allah has shown you.”

He Most High also says:

“So judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires.”

Cases fall into two categories: those involving an accusation and those that do not. A case involving an accusation is one in which the claimant alleges that the defendant committed an act forbidden to him that warrants punishment, such as killing or highway robbery

> “I have seen a question and answer on this matter by Shaykh al-Islām Ibn Taymiyya, may Allah have mercy on him and be pleased with him:

> He answered: ‘The cases adjudicated by those in authority, whether they are called judges or governors…’”
