> 3 – 35/400: “They supposed that revealed law alone could not [sustain] the governance of the world.” The word in square brackets is missing from al-Fatāwā but appears in al-Turuq, p. 104.
> 
> 4 – 35/405: The Shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, mentioned three opinions concerning the extent of ta‘zīr (discretionary punishment), beginning with:
> “The first, and best, is the view of a group of the followers of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others: for each offense, discretionary punishment must fall short of the prescribed punishment for that offense, even if it exceeds a punishment prescribed for another offense…”
> 
> In al-Turuq, p. 107, Ibn al-Qayyim, may Allah have mercy on him, mentioned four opinions. The first—the fourth view, which is absent from al-Fatāwā—is:
> “It is determined by the public interest and is proportionate to the offense; the ruler exercises his judgment in determining it.”
> 
> The point is that, after mentioning these opinions, he said in both al-Fatāwā and al-Turuq:
> “According to the first opinion, may such punishment extend to execution, as in the execution of a Muslim spy…?”
> 
> In al-Fatāwā, this refers to the position that “for each offense, discretionary punishment must fall short of the prescribed punishment for that offense.”
> 
> In al-Turuq, it refers to the position that “it is determined by the public interest.”
> 
> The more likely conclusion is that the text in al-Turuq al-Hukmiyya is correct and that an omission has occurred in al-Fatāwā—and Allah knows best. His words, “According to the first opinion, may such punishment extend to execution?” indicate that the discretionary punishment in question is not subject to a specified limit. By contrast, the first opinion mentioned in al-Fatāwā stipulates that, for each offense, discretionary punishment must fall short of its prescribed punishment. This question therefore does not arise under that position; it arises only under the position that makes the punishment dependent on the public interest. Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 16833

or theft, or other forms of unlawful aggression, such as acts committed secretly in ways that ordinarily make it impossible, in most cases, to establish proof of them. A case not involving an accusation is a claim concerning a contract—such as a sale, loan, pledge, or guarantee—or a claim that does not arise from an unlawful act, such as an established debt arising from a purchase price, loan, bridal due, compensation for harm caused unintentionally, or the like.

Either category may involve a claim concerning a prescribed punishment that is exclusively a right of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, as with drinking intoxicants and unlawful sexual intercourse. It may concern an exclusively human right, as with financial claims, or it may involve both, as with theft and highway robbery. In these two “categories,” unless the claimant presents proof recognized by Islamic law, the defendant’s statement is accepted upon his taking an oath. This is based on what Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“If people were granted what they claimed merely on the basis of their claims, some would lay claim to other men’s lives and property. Rather, the oath is incumbent upon the defendant.”

Another narration from Ibn ‘Abbās in the two Sahīh collections states:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ruled that the oath was incumbent upon the defendant.”

This hadith explicitly states that no one is to be granted anything merely on the basis of his claim. It also explicitly states that, in a claim seeking an award, the defendant is initially required to take an oath. It does not state that claims warranting punishment require nothing more than an oath from the defendant. Rather, it is established that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “

---

## Page 16834

to the Ansār when they complained to him about their slain kinsman, ‘Abdullāh ibn Sahl, who had been killed at Khaybar. His brother ‘Abd al-Rahmān and his paternal cousins Huwayyisa and Muhayyisa came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Muhayyisa had been with him at Khaybar. He said:

‘Will you swear fifty oaths and thereby establish your right over your kinsman’s killer?’

They replied, ‘How can we swear when we neither witnessed nor saw what happened?’

He said, ‘Then the Jews can clear themselves of your accusation by swearing fifty oaths.’

They replied, ‘How can we accept the oaths of people who are unbelievers?’”

This was reported by all the compilers of the Sahīh and Sunan collections, including al-Bukhārī, Muslim, Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, and al-Nasā’ī. In one narration in the two Sahīh collections, he said:

“Fifty of you shall swear oaths against a man from among them, and he will be handed over to you, bound.”

It is also established in Sahīh Muslim, on the authority of Ibn ‘Abbās, that:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ruled on the basis of one witness and an oath.”

Al-Tirmidhī and Ibn Mājah reported this from Jābir, while Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, and Ibn Mājah reported it from Abū Hurayrah. It has also been narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through many other routes. These hadiths are the most authentic and widely known reports narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on this subject. Ibn ‘Abbās himself narrates from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that “he ruled on the basis of an oath together with a witness”; the Prophet gave one ruling in some claims and the other ruling in others.

As for the hadith commonly quoted by jurists:

“Proof is incumbent upon the claimant, and an oath upon the one who denies the claim,”

this too has been narrated. However, its chain of transmission does not match the others in authenticity and renown, nor was it reported by most compilers of the well-known Sunan collections. None of the scholars of the Muslim community applied it without qualification except a group of jurists from Kufa, such as Abū Hanīfah and others, for they

---

## Page 16835

hold that the oath must always be taken by the party denying the claim. Even in the oath procedure for homicide cases, they require the defendant to swear; they do not rule on the basis of one witness and an oath, nor do they refer the oath back to the claimant when the defendant refuses to swear. They cite the general wording of this hadith as evidence.

As for the other scholars of the Muslim community—from Medina, Mecca, and Syria, as well as the jurists versed in hadith and others, such as Ibn Jurayh, Mālik, al-Layth ibn Sa‘d, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, and others—they sometimes require the claimant to swear and sometimes the defendant, in accordance with the established practices of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The principle held by most of them is that the oath is prescribed for the party whose case is stronger.

In their usage, “proof” denotes whatever makes the truth clear. They differ over the detailed applications of this principle: proof may consist of circumstantial evidence together with the oaths prescribed in homicide cases; it may consist of one witness and an oath; or it may consist of evidence other than witnesses, such as a description identifying lost property.

They have answered that hadith in several ways: by judging it weak; by arguing that its wording is general, whereas the hadiths they cite concern specific cases; or by maintaining that their hadiths are more authentic, more numerous, and better known, and thus deserve precedence when the reports conflict. It is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, required proof from the claimant and an oath from the party denying the claim in certain disputes that did not involve claims based on suspicion;

---

## Page 16836

such as the report recorded in the two authentic hadith collections from al-Ash‘ath ibn Qays, who said:

“I had a dispute with a man over a well, and we brought our case before the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said: ‘Produce your two witnesses; otherwise, he must swear an oath.’ I said: ‘Then he will swear without a care!’ He replied: ‘Whoever swears a false oath to deprive a Muslim of his property will meet Allah while He is angry with him.’”

In another version, he said: “Produce your evidence that the well is yours; otherwise, he must swear an oath.”

It is also narrated from Wā’il ibn Hujr, who said:

“A man from Hadramawt and a man from Kindah came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The man from Hadramawt said: ‘O Messenger of Allah, this man has seized land that belonged to my father.’ The man from Kindah said: ‘It is my land, in my possession, and I cultivate it. He has no right to it.’

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, asked the man from Hadramawt: ‘Do you have any proof?’ He replied: ‘No.’ He said: ‘Then you are entitled to require his oath.’ The man said: ‘O Messenger of Allah, he is wicked. He does not care what he swears to and has no scruples about anything.’ He replied: ‘You are entitled to nothing more from him.’

“When the man turned away, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘If he swears an oath concerning property in order to appropriate it wrongfully, he will surely meet Allah while He turns away from him.’”

Reported by Muslim and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it authentic.

This authentic hadith shows that he required nothing of the defendant beyond an oath, even though the claimant had mentioned his wickedness. He said: “You are entitled to nothing more from him.” Likewise, in the first hadith, al-Ash‘ath’s opponent was a Jew, as stated in the two authentic collections. Nevertheless, he required nothing of him beyond an oath. And in the hadith concerning the oath procedure in homicide cases, when the Ansār said:

---

## Page 16837

“How can we accept the oaths of unbelievers?” he did not object to their saying this. Thus, it is clear that claims differ in this respect. I know of no disagreement concerning this category: namely, that the defendant’s word is accepted under oath if the claimant produces no legally valid proof—that is, evidence.

Evidence constituting legally valid proof may consist of two upright male witnesses, or one man and two women. It may consist of four witnesses, or, according to some scholars among the followers of Ahmad and some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī, three witnesses. The latter concerns a claim of insolvency by someone known to have possessed wealth. For it is reported in Sahīh Muslim, from Qabīsah ibn Mukhāriq al-Hilālī, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Begging is lawful only for three kinds of men: a man who has assumed a financial liability, for whom begging is lawful until he obtains enough to discharge it, after which he must refrain; a man whose property has been swept away by a calamity, for whom begging is lawful until he obtains enough to sustain himself; and a man afflicted by poverty—once three men of sound judgment from his people come forward and say, ‘So-and-so has indeed been afflicted by poverty,’ begging becomes lawful for him until he obtains enough to sustain himself. Any begging beyond these cases, O Qabīsah, is unlawful gain, which its recipient consumes unlawfully.”

Another reason is that wealth is a hidden matter, giving rise to a strong suspicion that assets are being concealed.

At times, proof consists of one witness together with the claimant’s oath, according to the majority of Muslim jurists among the scholars of the Hijāz and the jurists of hadith.

At times, proof consists of women’s testimony: either one woman, according to Abū Hanīfah and the best-known view reported from Ahmad; two women, according to Mālik and one view reported from Ahmad; or four women, according to al-Shāfi‘ī. At other times, proof takes some other form.

---

## Page 16838

At other times, proof consists of circumstantial indications, incriminating circumstances, and suspicion, together with fifty oaths sworn by the claimant. This is the procedure known as qasāmah, in which the claimant’s oaths are taken first according to most jurists of the Hijāz and scholars of hadith. It differs from other procedures in requiring fifty oaths, just as the oaths of mutual imprecation are distinguished by consisting of four testimonies sworn by Allah, since each oath takes the place of a witness. According to Mālik and Ahmad, qasāmah establishes liability to retaliatory execution, whereas according to al-Shāfi‘ī it establishes liability only to blood money. The jurists of opinion administer the oaths only to the defendant, as mentioned earlier, yet they still require him to pay blood money despite his having sworn them. There are well-known details concerning this, which are not our purpose to discuss here.

Our purpose is simply to draw attention to the principal rulings governing legal claims, for this is a subject of great importance, and the need for it is both pressing and widespread. Neglect by some authorities and transgression by others have led to ignorance of the truth and injustice towards people. The term “religious law” has consequently ceased to correspond to its original meaning. Rather, in these times it is used in three senses.

The first is revealed law: the Quran and the Sunnah. Following it is obligatory, and whoever departs from it must be put to death. It encompasses the fundamentals of religion and its subsidiary rulings, the administration of rulers and those entrusted with public funds, the judgments of judges, the leadership exercised by shaykhs, and other matters. No one, whether among earlier or later generations, may depart from obedience to Allah and His Messenger.

The second is the law as interpreted: matters over which the Muslim community differs and exercises independent juristic reasoning. Whoever adopts a position in a matter legitimately open to such reasoning is allowed to maintain it; others are not all required to agree with him except on the basis of incontrovertible proof from the Quran and the Sunnah.

---

## Page 16839

And the third is altered law: what is established through false testimony, judgments rendered in ignorance and injustice rather than justice and truth—thus judging by other than what Allah has revealed—or instructions to make a false acknowledgment that deprives someone of a right. An example is instructing a sick person to make an unfounded acknowledgment in favour of one heir, thereby invalidating the rights of the remaining heirs. Giving such instructions and bearing witness to such an acknowledgment are forbidden. However, a judge who is unaware of the true circumstances incurs no sin if he rules according to what appears to him to be right. The foremost of judges, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“You bring your disputes before me, and some of you may present their case more eloquently than others. I judge only according to what I hear. If I award anyone something that rightfully belongs to his brother, he must not take it, for I am only allotting him a piece of the Fire.”

The other category of legal claims consists of “claims involving criminal accusations”: allegations of crimes and forbidden acts, such as killing, highway robbery, theft, and aggression against others through beating or other means. In such cases, defendants fall into three categories. The accused may be someone who is not the sort of person to commit the alleged offence; a wicked person of that sort; or someone whose character is unknown to the judge. If he is upright, there is agreement that he may not be punished.

They differed, however, over punishing the person who accuses him. An example is when stolen property is found in the possession of an upright man, and the possessor says, “I bought it in the market; I do not know

---

## Page 16840

who sold it.” There is agreement that he is not to be punished.

Mālik’s followers and others said: “The rightful owner swears that it is his property and has not passed out of his ownership, and then takes it back.” They hold that the defendant is not required to swear an oath. They then differed over whether the accuser should be punished.

Mālik and Ashhab said: “The claimant is not to be disciplined unless he intended to harm, discredit and insult the defendant, in which case he is to be disciplined.”

Asbagh said: “He is to be disciplined whether or not he intended to harm him.” Likewise, scholars generally hold that defendants are not required to swear oaths concerning the prescribed punishments that are Allah’s rights. Once the rightful owner has recovered his property, no claim remains against those possessing it except one concerning the prescribed punishment, and no oath is required.

The second category

The accused may be someone whose character is unknown, who is known neither for righteousness nor for wickedness. According to the generality of Muslim scholars, such a person is detained until his circumstances become clear. The explicit position of most leading scholars is that both the judge and the governor may detain him. This was expressly stated by Mālik and his followers; it is also the explicit position of Imam Ahmad and the most discerning scholars among his followers, and was mentioned by the followers of Abū Hanīfah.

Imam Ahmad said: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, detained someone on suspicion.” Ahmad said: “That is until his circumstances become clear to the judge.” This is based on the report narrated by Abū Dāwūd in his Sunan, al-Khallāl and others, from Bahz ibn Hakīm, from his father, from his grandfather:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, detained someone on suspicion.”

Al-Khallāl also narrated from Abū Hurayrah:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, detained someone on suspicion for a day and a night.”

The principles agreed upon by the leading scholars support this. They agree that when a claimant requests the appearance of a defendant whose attendance must be compelled, the judge must bring him to the session of

---

## Page 16841

the court until he settles the dispute between them. He must summon him from within the prescribed distance for such a summons, which some hold to be one barīd (postal stage)—a distance that cannot be travelled there and back in one day—as maintained by some followers of al-Shāfiʿī and by Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. Others hold that the distance permitting the shortening of prayers is four postal stages, or two days’ travel at a moderate pace, as Ahmad maintains according to one of the two reports from him. The judge may then be too occupied to settle the dispute immediately, or may have earlier cases before him. The defendant thus remains detained and restrained from the time he is summoned until the dispute between him and his opponent is settled. This is detention without suspicion; detention on suspicion is therefore all the more warranted.

“Detention under Islamic law” does not mean confinement in a cramped place. Rather, it means restraining a person and preventing him from moving about freely, whether in a house or a mosque, or by placing him in the custody of his opponent or his opponent’s representative. This is why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called such a person a captive. Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Mājah narrated from al-Hirmās ibn Habīb, from his father, who said:

“I brought a debtor of mine to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He told me, ‘Stay with him.’ Then he said, ‘O brother of Banū Tamīm, what do you intend to do with your captive?’”

In Ibn Mājah’s version:

“Then he passed by me towards the end of the day and said, ‘What has become of your captive, O brother of Banū Tamīm?’”

This was detention in the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. During the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and Abū Bakr, there was no prison set aside for confining people. However, when the subjects spread over a wider territory during the time of ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, he purchased a house in Mecca, made it a prison and detained people there. Indeed,

---

## Page 16842

the scholars among Ahmad’s followers and others differed over whether the ruler should establish a prison, holding two views. Those who held that he should not establish a prison said that he should restrain the person in some location or appoint a guard over him—a practice known as “guarded custody.”

Accordingly, since attendance at the judge’s court entails restraint and restriction akin to imprisonment and detention, the scholars differed: should the defendant be summoned merely on the basis of a claim? Or, if appearing would demean him, should he not be summoned until some basis for the claimant’s case becomes apparent? There are two views, both reported from Ahmad. The second is Mālik’s view, while the first is that of Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī.

Some scholars held that detention on suspicion falls within the authority of the governor responsible for military affairs, not the judge. This view was mentioned by a number of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers, such as Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Zubayrī, Chief Judge al-Māwardī and others, as well as by some of Ahmad’s followers who wrote on judicial conduct, and others.

They also differed over the duration of detention on suspicion: is it fixed, or is it left to the ruler’s judgment? These are two views mentioned by Judge Abū Ya‘lā, Judge al-Māwardī and others. It was said that it is fixed at one month; this is the view of Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Zubayrī. It was also said that it has no fixed duration; this is the view preferred by al-Māwardī.

---

## Page 16843

The third category is where the accused is known for wrongdoing: for example, someone accused of theft who was previously known for it, someone accused of highway robbery who was known for it, or someone accused of murder; or where any of these persons is known for conduct that gives grounds for such an accusation. If detaining someone whose character is unknown is permissible, then detaining someone known for wrongdoing is all the more justified.

I know of none of the authoritative imams of the Muslims who held that, in all such cases, the defendant should swear an oath and then be released by any of the authorities, without detention or any other measure. No imam holds this position without qualification. Whoever claims that this, in its unrestricted and universal application, is the religious law has committed a gross error, contrary to the explicit teachings of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the consensus of the Muslim community.

Such gross errors have emboldened rulers to contravene the religious law. They imagined that the religious law alone could not provide for the governance of society and the interests of the Muslim community, and consequently transgressed Allah’s limits in these matters. Both groups’ ignorance of the true nature of the religious law has led people to abandon it in favour of various innovations in governance.

This category includes cases involving qasāmah—the procedure of fifty oaths in homicide cases. Its rulings are well known and need not be mentioned here. As for accusations of theft, highway robbery and similar offences, detention in such cases has already been discussed.

As for questioning through beating and similar means, scholars differed: is it authorized for both the judge and the governor? Or for the governor but not the judge? Or is beating authorized for either of them? There are three views.

---

## Page 16844

The first view is that both the judge and the governor may administer beatings in such cases. This is the position of a group of scholars among Mālik’s followers and others, including Ashhab, the judge of Egypt. Ashhab said: “He is to be questioned through imprisonment and disciplinary punishment, and flogged with his body bared.”

The second view is that he should not be beaten, but detained, as discussed above. This is the position of Asbagh, one of Mālik’s followers, and of many Hanafī, Shāfiʿī and other scholars. However, they regard the detention of such an accused person as more stringent than that of someone whose character is unknown. They therefore differed over whether he should be detained until he dies. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and a group of Mālik’s followers, including Mutarrif, Ibn al-Mājishūn and others, held that he should be detained until he dies.

Likewise, it is reported from Imam Ahmad that someone who does not desist from his religious innovation should be detained until he dies. Mālik, however, said: “He should not be detained until he dies.”

The third view is that the governor may beat him, but not the judge. This view was mentioned by a group of Shāfiʿī’s and Ahmad’s followers, including Judge Abū al-Hasan al-Māwardī, Judge Abū Yaʿlā and others. They discussed it at length in their works on the ordinances of government. They said that military authority is the mainstay for punishing crimes and preventing corruption in the land, and that this cannot be accomplished without punishing suspects known for criminal conduct. Judicial authority, by contrast, can achieve its purpose without this.

This view thus affirms that such measures are permissible under the religious law. Each officeholder, however, is instructed to carry out what has been entrusted to him. Thus, just as the official responsible for alms does not have the same powers of collection and disbursement as the official responsible for

---

## Page 16845

land tax, although both are forms of revenue sanctioned by the religious law. Likewise, the military governor and the judge each perform the duties required by their lawful office, while observing justice and the principles of the Sharia.

As for punishing someone known to possess what is rightfully due to another, but who has denied or withheld it, the scholars agree on this. I know of no disagreement that anyone who owes a debt or must return a specific item of property, and is able to do so but refuses, is to be punished until he fulfils that obligation. They have explicitly stated that he may be punished by beating. The Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, Hanbalīs and others have mentioned this, citing the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Delay in payment by someone able to pay makes it lawful to censure and punish him.”

This was narrated by the compilers of the Sunan, including Abū Dāwūd, al-Nasā’ī and Ibn Mājah. It is also established in the two Sahīh collections that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Delay in payment by one who can afford to pay is wrongdoing.”

And a wrongdoer deserves punishment.

The scholars agree that discretionary punishment is prescribed for every sin for which there is no prescribed penalty. Sin is of two kinds: neglecting an obligation or committing a prohibited act.

If someone neglects obligations despite being able to fulfil them—such as repaying debts; returning entrusted property to its rightful owners, including property held under agency arrangements, deposits, orphans’ property, endowments and government funds; or returning usurped property and redressing wrongs—he is to be punished until he fulfils them.

The same applies to someone obliged to produce a person so that a right owed by that person may be exacted. For example, a man may commit highway robbery and flee to someone with power, who then prevents the prescribed penalties from being carried out upon him and the claims against him from being satisfied. This is forbidden by consensus. Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from

---

## Page 16846

‘Alī, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has cursed anyone who commits an offence or shelters an offender.”

Abū Dāwūd narrated in his Sunan from Ibn ‘Umar that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever knowingly argues in support of falsehood remains under Allah’s displeasure until he desists. Whoever intercedes to prevent a prescribed penalty ordained by Allah from being carried out has opposed Allah’s command. Whoever attributes to a Muslim something of which he is innocent will be confined in Radghat al-Khabāl until he extricates himself from what he has said.”

Thus, whenever there is an obligation to produce a person or property, anyone who refuses to fulfil that duty deserves punishment until he does so. But bringing a person before someone who will wrong him, or delivering property to someone who will take it without right, is neither obligatory nor permissible. Assisting wrongdoing is itself wrongdoing. Allah Most High says:

“Cooperate in righteousness and piety, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression.”

He also says:

“O you who believe, when you converse privately, do not converse in sin, aggression and disobedience to the Messenger, but converse in righteousness and piety.”

As for “cases of uncertainty” involving wrongdoing on both sides—for example, when officials entrusted with government funds take what they are not entitled to, while the person seeking to recover those funds would also spend them wrongfully—no one is obliged to help the wrongdoer who has the power to retain them, nor should anyone help the wrongdoer seeking to take possession of them. Rather, if some rightful claim tips the balance in favor of one side, one should support that right. If both are wrongdoers and the funds cannot be delivered to anyone entitled to them,

---

## Page 16847

one should deal justly between the two wrongdoers in this matter, for justice is commanded in all matters as far as possible. Justice here includes not enabling either of them to commit aggression against the other; rather, one should do whatever comes closest to justice.

The scholars differed over whether a confession made under interrogation by imprisonment or beating may be acted upon. Some held that it should be accepted if its truth becomes apparent—for example, if the person produces the very property that was stolen. Even if he retracts his confession after the beating, his retraction is not accepted; rather, he is held to his confession. This is Ashhab’s position concerning both the judge and the governor, and it is the position mentioned by the two judges, al-Māwardī and Abū Ya‘lā, concerning the governor.

Others held that a further confession corresponding to the number of blows is required, and that if he retracts his confession, he is not held to it. This is the position of Ibn al-Qāsim and many Shāfi‘ī and Hanbalī scholars, as well as others.

As for “the extent of beating,” if someone is beaten for failing to fulfil an obligation—for example, being beaten until he fulfils it—then no fixed overall limit applies. Rather, he is beaten on one day; if he fulfils the obligation, that ends the matter, otherwise he is beaten on another day. However, on each occasion the beating must not exceed the maximum discretionary punishment, according to those who prescribe an upper limit for it. The scholars have expressed several views concerning “the maximum discretionary punishment” imposed for committing prohibited acts:

---

## Page 16848

The first—and best—view, held by some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad and others, is that discretionary punishment for any offence must fall short of the prescribed punishment for that offence, even if it exceeds the prescribed punishment for another offence. Thus, unlawful intimate contact and theft from a place other than secure keeping may be punished with a beating exceeding the prescribed punishment for falsely accusing someone of unchastity, but not with stoning or amputation.

The second view is that discretionary punishment must fall short of the lowest prescribed punishment: either forty or eighty lashes. This is the view of many followers of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and Abū Hanīfah.

The third view is that discretionary punishment must not exceed ten lashes. This is one of the positions within the school of Ahmad and others.

According to the first view, may discretionary punishment extend to execution, as in the execution of a Muslim spy? There are two views on this.

The first is that it may extend to execution. Thus, a Muslim spy may be executed if the aim is to serve the public interest. This is the view of Mālik and some followers of Ahmad, such as Ibn ‘Aqīl. Some followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad have expressed a similar position concerning the execution of those who promote religious innovations and those whose corruption cannot be stopped except by execution. Likewise, Mālik’s school holds that those who promote religious innovations, such as the Qadariyyah and similar groups, are to be executed.

The second view is that the spy is not to be executed. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī and Judge Abū Ya‘lā among the followers of Ahmad.

---

## Page 16849

And Ahmad’s explicitly reported position is to withhold judgment on the matter. Among those who permit execution as a discretionary punishment for “grave sins” are the followers of Abū Hanīfah, in certain cases where they describe execution as a measure of governance. Examples include executing someone who repeatedly commits sodomy or repeatedly kills with a heavy implement. They permit his execution as a measure of governance and discretionary punishment, although Abū Hanīfah neither requires nor even permits execution for someone who has committed the act only once.

His two leading disciples, however, join the other imams in differing with him: they hold that retaliatory execution is obligatory for such killing, and that the perpetrator of sodomy is subject either to execution in all cases, whether he is muhsan (possessing the requisite marital status) or not—as held by Mālik, Ahmad in the better-known of the two positions reported from him, and al-Shāfi‘ī in one of his two opinions—or to the same prescribed punishment as someone who commits unlawful sexual intercourse. The latter is the position of Abū Hanīfah’s two disciples, al-Shāfi‘ī in the better-known of his two opinions, and Ahmad in one of the two positions reported from him.

What is transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Rightly Guided Caliphs accords with the first view. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ordered that a man whose wife had permitted him to have intercourse with her slave woman be given one hundred lashes. Abū Bakr and ‘Umar gave one hundred lashes to a man found in bed with a woman. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb also gave one hundred lashes to a man who had forged his seal and thereby taken money from the public treasury; he then gave him another hundred lashes on each of the second and third days. This is not the place to discuss the various forms of discretionary punishment at length, for they have many branches.

As for beating an accused person, when it is known that the property is in his possession but he has concealed and denied it, in order to make him disclose its whereabouts, there is no doubt concerning this. He is beaten to compel him to fulfil his obligation to disclose its whereabouts, just as he is beaten

---

## Page 16850

to pay what he owes when he is able to do so. In this connection, there is the hadith of Ibn ‘Umar in al-Sahīh:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made peace with the people of Khaybar on condition that they surrender their gold and silver, he questioned Zayd ibn Sa‘yah, the uncle of Huyayy ibn Akhtab, saying: ‘Where is Huyayy ibn Akhtab’s treasure?’ He replied: ‘O Muhammad, the wars have consumed it.’ He then said to al-Zubayr: ‘Take charge of this man.’ Al-Zubayr subjected him to some torture, whereupon he directed them to it in a ruined building. It consisted of jewellery in a bull’s hide.”

This establishes a basis for beating an accused person who is known to have neglected an obligation or committed a prohibited act. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who has undertaken to adjudicate among a group of pellet shooters and says, “This is the law of pellet shooting,” while also serving as administrator over a school and jurists: does his administering this system of adjudication and the law he mentions invalidate the uprightness required for his administrative office? Must the Muslim judge before whom his uprightness has been established remove him from that office upon hearing that he administers the law of pellet shooting, which neither Allah nor His Messenger has prescribed?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. No one may judge between any of Allah’s creatures—whether Muslims, unbelievers, young men, pellet shooters, soldiers, the poor, or anyone else—except according to the judgment of Allah and His Messenger. Whoever seeks anything else falls under

---

## Page 16851

the words of Allah Most High:

“Is it the judgment of the age of ignorance they seek? And who is better than Allah in judgment for people who have certainty?”

And His words:

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge in their disputes, then find within themselves no objection to what you have decided and submit completely.”

Muslims must therefore refer every dispute among themselves to the judgment of Allah and His Messenger. Whoever knowingly judges according to the rulings and law of pellet shooting, or anything else that contradicts the law of Allah and His Messenger and the judgment of Allah and His Messenger, is of the same kind as the Tatars who give precedence to the judgment of the “Yāsiq” over the judgment of Allah and His Messenger. Whoever deliberately does so has compromised his uprightness and religious integrity and must be barred from overseeing the endowment. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16852

Chapter on Testimony

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked about narration:

“Is everyone whose narration is accepted also accepted as a witness?”

He replied:

As for the question, “Is everyone whose narration is accepted also accepted as a witness?” this is disputed. Scholars agree that a slave’s narration is accepted, but they disagree over the admissibility of his testimony. According to Alī, Anas, and Shurayh, his testimony is admissible; this is also the position of Ahmad and others. According to Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, and al-Shāfi‘ī, his testimony is not admissible.

A woman’s narration is accepted without restriction, whereas her testimony is accepted in principle. This is because testimony concerns a particular individual, and its ruling does not extend to the witness. By contrast, the ruling conveyed through narration has wider application: the narrator relates a ruling that applies equally to himself and to others. For this reason, narration does not require multiple transmitters, whereas testimony requires multiple witnesses. This is one of the distinctions scholars have drawn between the two.

---

## Page 16853

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A debtor drew up a written declaration of his insolvency, and witnesses testified that he was unable to repay the debt he owed, without its amount being specified. Is this sufficient? If the witness specifies the amount, must he add, “nor any part of it”? If he does say this, does that include three dirhams, or one and a half dirhams?

He replied:

As for testimony to insolvency, if the witnesses testify that he is unable to repay the debt he owes, and they know its amount, their testimony is valid. However, this does not rule out his ability to repay part of it. Such testimony is also valid even if they do not know the amount, provided they testify that he is unable to repay anything at all. Yet knowledge of this is generally unattainable.

However, if the debt arose from a transaction involving an exchange—such as the price owed for a sale or repayment of a loan—and he had known assets, then, if the witnesses testify that his assets are gone, he is treated like someone not known to possess any assets. In such a case, his word is accepted, supported by his oath that he is insolvent and unable to repay the amount to which his oath relates. If he claims to be unable to repay any amount, small or large, he swears to that, and the intended purpose is thereby achieved. If he claims that he possesses only such-and-such an amount, he swears to that.

According to one of the two positions in the school of Ahmad and others, the witnesses testifying to his insolvency must number three if he had assets, on account of the transmitted report concerning this, unlike when the testimony establishes

---

## Page 16854

that his assets were lost through an evident cause. The hadith in question is that of Qabīsa ibn Mukhāriq al-Hilālī, which Muslim narrated in his Sahīh from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), who said:

“Begging is lawful only for three kinds of people: a man whose wealth has been swept away by a calamity, for whom begging becomes lawful until he obtains enough to sustain him—or, he said, enough to meet his needs—after which he must stop; a man afflicted by poverty, such that three men of sound judgment from his people stand up and say, ‘So-and-so has indeed been afflicted by poverty,’ for whom begging becomes lawful until he obtains enough to sustain him—or, he said, enough to meet his needs—after which he must stop; and a man who has assumed a financial liability, for whom begging becomes lawful until he obtains enough to discharge it, after which he must stop. Any begging beyond these cases, O Qabīsa, is unlawful gain, which the one who takes it consumes unlawfully.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who, while sound in mind and body, called witnesses to his declaration: “This person is my heir; no one else shall inherit from me”? Is this permissible, and who inherits after his death?

He replied:

This testimony is not accepted. Rather, if that person is an heir under Islamic law, he inherits from him whether the declarant wishes it or not. If he is not an heir under Islamic law, he does not inherit. No one may transgress Allah’s limits or alter Allah’s religion. Even if he did so unwillingly, he would be a flagrant sinner guilty of a major sin, as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“Whoever deprives someone of an inheritance, Allah will deprive him of his inheritance in Paradise.”

---

## Page 16855

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Is the testimony of a wet nurse accepted or not?

The Answer

He replied:

If the witness is upright, the testimony is accepted in this matter. There is, however, disagreement over requiring the witness to swear an oath. It has been reported from Ibn ‘Abbās—may Allah be pleased with them both—that she must swear an oath; if she is lying, a year will not pass before both her breasts turn white.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Is the testimony of a co-wife accepted?

He replied:

A co-wife’s testimony is not accepted in matters that would invalidate her fellow wife’s marriage, whether on grounds of breastfeeding or otherwise. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning testimony against a sinner or an innovator: may it be based on widespread reports and common knowledge, or must it be based on direct hearing and observation? If widespread reports suffice in this matter, who has held this view among

---

## Page 16856

the imams? What is the basis for treating it as authoritative evidence? May someone who calls others to religious innovation and argues in its favour be shielded from exposure, or is there a stronger obligation to testify against him so that people may beware of him? What defines the religious innovation by which a person is counted among the adherents of deviant beliefs?

He replied:

Matters that impugn the integrity and religious commitment of a witness or anyone else may be testified to when the person testifying knows of them through widespread reports. This constitutes a legally valid challenge to that person’s integrity, as groups of Mālikī, Shāfiʿī, Hanbalī and other jurists have explicitly stated in their larger and smaller works. They expressly state that, when a person’s integrity is challenged on disqualifying grounds, the challenger may base his criticism on what he has heard from that person or witnessed, and what has become widely reported.

I know of no disagreement among people concerning this. All Muslims in our time testify to the integrity and religious commitment of such people as ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, al-Hasan al-Basrī and others like them, on grounds known to them only through widespread reports. Likewise, they testify to the injustice and religious innovation of such people as al-Hajjāj ibn Yūsuf, al-Mukhtār ibn Abī ʿUbayd, ʿAmr ibn ʿUbayd, Ghaylān al-Qadarī, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sabaʾ the Rāfidī, and others like them, on grounds known to them only through widespread reports.

An authentic report from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—relates:

A funeral procession passed by him, and those present spoke well of the deceased. He said, “It is assured.” Another funeral procession passed by him, and they spoke ill of the deceased. He said, “It is assured; it is assured.”

They asked, “Messenger of Allah, what do you mean by saying, ‘It is assured; it is assured’?”

He replied, “You spoke well of the first deceased person, so I said that Paradise was assured for that person. You spoke ill of the other deceased person, so I said that the Fire was assured for that person. You are Allah’s witnesses on earth.”

This applies when the purpose is to declare someone a sinner in order to disqualify him as a witness and from exercising authority.

---

## Page 16857

But if the aim is to warn against him and guard against his harm, less evidence than that suffices. As ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said, “Judge people by their close companions.” When ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—was informed that young men were gathering around a certain man, he forbade keeping company with him. Thus, if a man associates with evildoers in the course of his affairs, people should be warned against him.

“One who calls others to religious innovation” deserves punishment by the consensus of the Muslims. His punishment may sometimes be execution and sometimes a lesser penalty, just as the early Muslims executed Jahm ibn Safwān, al-Jaʿd ibn Dirham, Ghaylān al-Qadarī and others. Even supposing that he does not deserve punishment, or that punishing him is not possible, his innovation must nevertheless be exposed and people warned against it. This is part of enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong, which Allah and His Messenger have commanded.

The “religious innovation” by which a person is counted among the adherents of deviant beliefs is one whose opposition to the Quran and Sunnah is well known among scholars versed in the Sunnah. Examples include the innovations of the Khawārij, the Rāfidah, the Qadariyyah and the Murjiʾah. For ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, Yūsuf ibn Asbāt and others said:

“The seventy-two sects stem from four principal groups: the Khawārij, the Rāfidah, the Qadariyyah and the Murjiʾah.”

Ibn al-Mubārak was asked, “What about the Jahmiyyah?”

He replied, “The Jahmiyyah are not part of the community of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

The “Jahmiyyah” are those who deny the divine attributes. They say that the Quran is created, that Allah will not be seen in the Hereafter, that Muhammad was not taken up to Allah, and that Allah has neither knowledge, nor power, nor life, and other such things, as maintained by the Muʿtazilah, the philosophers and their followers.

---

## Page 16858

ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī said:

“There are two groups, so beware of them: the Jahmiyyah and the Rāfidah.”

These two groups are the worst of the adherents of religious innovation. Through them came the esoteric Qarmatians, such as the Nusayriyyah and the Ismāʿīliyyah, and with them the adherents of divine union became connected, for they are of the same kind as the Pharaonic sect. In these times, the “Rāfidah” combine their Rāfidī beliefs with the doctrines of the Jahmiyyah and the Qadariyyah, having added the doctrine of the Muʿtazilah to their own. They may then go further, adopting the doctrine of the Ismāʿīliyyah and similar adherents of heresy and divine union. Allah and His Messenger know best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Concerning witnesses who gave testimony warranting a prescribed punishment, but when he appeared, they said, ‘We were mistaken,’ and retracted their testimony: is their retraction accepted?”

He replied:

“Yes. If a witness retracts his testimony before judgment is passed on its basis, no judgment is to be based upon it. If he knows that he was mistaken, he must retract it, and this does not impugn his religious commitment or uprightness. Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 16859

Chapter: Partition

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Two men jointly own a house. One requests its partition, but his co-owner refuses. May he be compelled to agree to partition?”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. If the house can be partitioned without detriment, such that its sale value is not diminished, the refusing party is compelled to accept partition. Otherwise, the party seeking partition is entitled to request its sale; the refusing party may be compelled to sell, and the proceeds are divided between them.

“Compulsory partition as described is the position of the four imams. Compulsory sale as described is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Imam Ahmad.”

---

## Page 16860

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man owns ten out of twenty-four shares in an orchard jointly owned with a mentally impaired person whose management of his property has been restricted by the judge. The orchard is amenable to partition. May the judge carry out the partition on his behalf? Must money from it also be spent on its upkeep?”

He replied:

“If it is amenable to partition and the co-owner requests partition, the judge must grant his request, even if the other co-owner is competent to manage his affairs. How much more so, then, if he is under legal restriction?

“If it is not amenable to partition other than compulsory partition, the judge may carry out the partition on behalf of the person under legal restriction if he considers it to be in his interest.

“If the co-owner requests either partition or upkeep, the judge may grant either of these requests.”

---

## Page 16861

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Three partners own a mill. One of them owns a sixth and is poor, with nothing to live on except the rental income from his share. The others refuse to pay him anything except the income of one day in every six. He has asked them to give him his proportionate share each day to help meet his living expenses, but they have refused, using their superior wealth and influence against him. What must be done in this case?”

He replied:

“All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

“If a partner asks that they rent out the property and divide the rent in proportion to their respective shares, or that they allocate turns among themselves to share its use, the other partners must agree to one of these two arrangements.

“If they agree to allocate turns, but wish to lengthen the interval between his turns while he wishes to shorten it, his request must be granted rather than theirs. Allocating use by time entails delaying some partners’ entitlements relative to others, so the sooner each can receive his entitlement, the better. The basic principle is that all partners must receive their entitlements; any delay is permitted only out of necessity. Thus, the shorter the delay, the better.

“This is especially so when the delay means that a partner cannot receive his entitlement without suffering harm—for example, having to provide animals for a single day’s use while bearing their upkeep throughout the week. He is not obliged to agree to an arrangement that harms him when an equitable arrangement can be made among them without harm. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 16862

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“May meat be divided without weighing it? And may figs, grapes, pomegranates, melons and cucumbers be divided by number?”

He replied:

“As for dividing meat according to its value, the correct view is that this is permissible, because division separates the respective shares; according to the correct view, it is not a sale. This is how the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to divide the produce with the people of Khaybar, by estimation. ‘Abd Allāh ibn Rawāhah would estimate the dates on the palm trees and divide them between the Muslims and the Jews, whereas selling fresh dates by estimation is not permissible. Likewise, the Muslims used to slaughter camels and divide them among themselves without weighing them. They did this during the lifetime of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

“The same applies to everything in this category. Figs and grapes may be divided without measuring by volume or weighing, and pomegranates may be divided by number, as may melons and cucumbers. This is the correct view concerning all items counted individually: they may be divided according to their value. Such division is not a sale, but expertise must be taken into account when balancing the portions.

“The point is that the shares may be apportioned equitably [by] (1) whatever means are available: measuring by volume or weighing where possible, and otherwise by estimation and valuation. This is not like a sale, for division is permissible for all kinds of property, and dates may be divided before they begin to ripen. And Allah knows best.”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Protecting Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā from Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> —Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’, book formatter for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 16863

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked:

“May meat be divided without weighing it? And may figs, grapes, pomegranates, melons and cucumbers be divided by number?”

He replied:

“Fresh produce, such as fresh dates, grapes and the like, may be divided. The purpose of division is to achieve fairness. If the shares are not balanced by measuring by volume or weighing, estimation and valuation serve instead, including for commodities subject to the rules of usury.

“The jurists agree that fruit may be purchased with wheat or barley when the exchange is immediate. They disagree only over whether it may be sold on deferred terms. The majority permit this. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, the well-known position reported from Ahmad, and al-Shāfiʿī’s earlier position.

“This depends on the effective cause of the prohibition of usury: is it similarity and staple nourishment, and food being similar in edibility? Those who hold that it is similarity and staple nourishment, and those who hold that it is similarity combined with edibility, permit this. Those who hold that it is edibility alone do not permit it. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 16864

Chapter: Acknowledgment

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

“A man acknowledged that the entire shop known as his residence, together with all the tangible property it contained, was an endowment dedicated to Allah Most High for a mosque and its appurtenances.

“The witnesses were unable to weigh those items before the founder of the endowment died. Some of the witnesses do not know whether the items covered by that acknowledgment are the very items now present. May such a witness give this testimony in reliance on the declarant’s acknowledgment and on what has become widely known through those two upright witnesses?”

He replied:

“The witness testifies to what he heard the declarant say. An acknowledgment is valid whether its subject matter is known or unknown, distinguishable or indistinguishable. If further testimony establishes precisely what was included in the wording, that is admissible, and their testimony is acted upon.

“For example, someone might acknowledge, ‘I owe so-and-so, son of so-and-so, such-and-such,’ or, ‘My house known as such-and-such, or defined by such-and-such boundaries, belongs to so-and-so.’ Two witnesses might then testify that this particular person or property is the one named, described, or defined by those boundaries. This is permissible by agreement of the imams.

“They differed only concerning the person providing the identification: does one suffice, or are two required? There are two well-known scholarly opinions on this, both transmitted from Imam Ahmad. The ‘second’ view is that of al-Shāfiʿī and others. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 16865

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Two people released one another from liability and had witnesses attest to their acknowledgment that neither had any right to demand payment from, or bring a claim against, the other concerning a dinar or a dirham, or anything less or more. One of them, however, owed the other a specified sum in dirhams recorded in a legally valid document. The creditor excepted this debt when granting the release: he did not discharge the debtor from the obligation recorded in that document, nor did he mention it in the mutual release.

“When the creditor demanded payment on the strength of the document, the other party said, ‘Did we not release one another from liability?’ He replied, ‘I released you from everything except the debt recorded in this document.’ May his legal claim based on that document be heard?”

He replied:

“If he claims that he did not release the debtor from that obligation, that the debtor knows he did not release him from it, and requests the debtor’s oath that the creditor had not released him from it, he is entitled to do so.”

---

## Page 16866

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man died, leaving a paternal cousin and a wife whom he had divorced during his final illness. The wife’s agent seized hold of the deceased’s paternal cousin and demanded her share of the inheritance. The cousin acknowledged that she was an heir and that he had taken possession of his own share of the inheritance, knowing that the matter was disputed among scholars. He also released the wife, her father, and her agent from all claims.

“Afterwards, he brought witnesses before a Shāfiʿī judge, who testified that the deceased had divorced her during his final illness. The judge ruled accordingly, saying, ‘In my view, she does not inherit.’ The cousin then demanded the return of what had been taken from him. May this testimony be admitted despite his acknowledgment that she was an heir and his release of them from claims concerning what they had received? If he claims that he was ignorant of what he had acknowledged, is his claim of ignorance to be accepted?”

He replied:

“Given the acknowledgment, payment, and release mentioned, together with his knowledge of the scholarly disagreement, he may not bring a claim contradicting his acknowledgment and release, nor may a judgment be issued in his favour on that basis. As for claiming ignorance of this while knowing of the scholarly disagreement, that is a lie. And Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A woman was married to a soldier and had two children by him, a boy and a girl. The boy died, and the husband divorced her. She took the daughter

---

## Page 16867

into her care more than eight years ago. He has now fallen seriously ill, summoned witnesses, and recorded two thousand dirhams as owed to his wife. Her sister is divorced, and he recorded the dowries as owed to both of them, although she had released him from hers and has been in Syria since he divorced her. He also recorded five hundred as owed to their mother. He has withheld what is due to me and deprived the daughter he had by me of her right to inherit. Ever since she had children, he has not treated them equally in any worldly matter, and he has given his income to her?”

He replied:

“His acknowledgment of a debt to his wife is invalid, especially if he makes it a bequest, for a bequest to an heir is not binding without the other heirs’ approval, by the consensus of the Muslims. Likewise, his acknowledgment of a debt to an heir is invalid according to the majority of scholars, particularly where there are grounds for suspicion. It is invalid according to the schools of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, Imam Ahmad, and others.

“Likewise, his acknowledgment of a debt from which the woman entitled to it has released him is invalid. If she released him from her dowry and he subsequently acknowledged owing it to her, that acknowledgment is invalid, because it is known to be false. Even if he instead sought to grant her ownership of that amount, the majority likewise do not permit such a grant to be constituted as a debt owed by him.

“He has no right to deprive his daughter of her share of the inheritance, nor may he withhold from the divorced woman what he owes her. A hadith states:

‘Whoever cuts off someone’s inheritance, Allah will cut off his inheritance in Paradise.’

“In the Sunan collections, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

‘A man may spend sixty years obeying Allah, then act unjustly in his will, so that his life ends in evil and he enters the Fire. And a man may spend sixty years disobeying Allah, then act justly in his will, so that his life ends in goodness and he enters Paradise.’

He then recited the words of the Most High:

---

## Page 16868

“These are the limits set by Allah. Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, He will admit them to Gardens beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein forever. That is the great triumph.”

“And whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger and transgresses His limits, He will admit him to a Fire, abiding therein forever, and he will have a humiliating punishment.”

And Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man was in partnership with another person. His partner gained the upper hand, seized and humiliated him, and drew up a document against him stating that the sheep belonged to him alone, rather than to the partnership?”

He replied:

“If he wrongfully coerced him into making the acknowledgment, that acknowledgment is invalid, and having witnesses attest to it does not benefit him. Rather, the unjust aggressor who subjected this wronged man to coercion must be punished. The wronged man must be helped, and the property restored to its rightful owner. If he produces evidence that he was coerced into this, his evidence must be heard. And Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A woman died, leaving five children: four full siblings—one son and three daughters—and another son who was their maternal half-brother. She also left a husband by whom she had no children. During the illness that led to her death, she acknowledged that she owed the four children who were full siblings one thousand dirhams, intending thereby to deprive her other son and her husband of their inheritance.”

---

## Page 16869

He replied:

If she was lying in this acknowledgment, then, by the consensus of the Muslims, she was disobeying Allah and His Messenger. Indeed, she was committing a major sin subject to the threat of punishment, for injustice in making a will is a major sin:

“Whoever deprives someone of their inheritance, Allah will deprive him of his inheritance in Paradise.”

Allah Most High has said:

“These are the limits set by Allah. Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger, He will admit them to Gardens beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein forever. That is the great triumph.”

“And whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger and transgresses His limits, He will admit him to a Fire, abiding therein forever, and he will have a humiliating punishment.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A servant may spend sixty years obeying Allah, then act unjustly in his will, thus meeting an evil end and entering the Fire. And a servant may spend sixty years disobeying Allah, then be granted a good end, acting justly in his will and entering Paradise.” He then recited this verse: “These are the limits set by Allah.”

Anyone who helps her in this falsehood and injustice shares in her wrongdoing, whether a scribe, an adviser, or anyone else. All such people are cooperating in sin and aggression. Any witness who prompts her to make the false acknowledgment is a transgressor whose testimony is inadmissible.

If, however, she was telling the truth, then she was doing good in this and obeying Allah and His Messenger. The same applies to anyone who helps her in that for the sake of Allah Most High.

As for the legal ruling based on outward evidence, most scholars—including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad, and others—do not accept such an acknowledgment, because the grounds for suspicion are evident. Moreover, the heirs’ rights attach to the deceased’s property upon the onset of the final illness, so the owner becomes restricted in disposing of it insofar as their rights are concerned. By consensus, the owner has no right to make a gratuitous transfer to any one of them.

---

## Page 16870

And some scholars, such as al-Shāfiʿī, accept the acknowledgment, on the basis of thinking well of a Muslim and presuming that, at the point of death, he would neither lie nor act unjustly.

Those who know the truth of this case and similar cases must cooperate in righteousness and piety, not in sin and aggression. A case like this should be investigated. If evidence contrary to this acknowledgment is found, it should be acted upon; if evidence of its falsity emerges, the acknowledgment should be invalidated. Indications of truthfulness include knowing that the father of these four possessed wealth comparable to the amount acknowledged. Indications of falsehood include evidence showing that she intends to deprive her son and husband of their inheritance. If evidence supporting either side emerges, that side should be given greater weight. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man died, leaving two sons and a daughter. The two sons compensated the woman for her share of her father’s estate, and she gave her brothers a legally valid discharge, before upright witnesses, concerning what remained in their possession. This occurred more than sixty years ago. She continued to reside in the same district as them and made no claim against them throughout that time.

When her brothers died and she ascertained that the witnesses had also died, she denied what had been attested against her. She brought a claim against her brothers’ heir, asserting that her share of her father’s estate had remained in her brothers’ possession. The judge upheld her claim, but evidence of her discharge was then duly established against her. Does this overturn what the judge established in her favor?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. If legally valid evidence establishes her acknowledgment

---

## Page 16871

of having received payment and granted a legally valid discharge, her heirs’ claim is invalid. Even if they produce evidence and establish their claim before the judge, the evidence of her acknowledgment of receipt and discharge takes precedence, because it provides additional knowledge. This is unless she claims that she made the acknowledgment under coercion or out of embarrassment, or that she acknowledged receipt before actually receiving anything and what she acknowledged never occurred. She may then require the defendant to swear that the acknowledgment’s apparent meaning reflected the actual circumstances, or that they have no knowledge of that acknowledgment.

If the witnesses to the discharge have died and their handwriting is recognizable, those familiar with their handwriting may testify to it, and scholars who consider such evidence admissible may rule on that basis. Moreover, her claim to her entitlement after this lengthy period, without any obstacle preventing her from bringing it earlier, is inadmissible according to one of the two scholarly opinions found in Mālik’s school and elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

A man has two daughters, one married and the other unmarried. He had drawn up a written acknowledgment of three thousand dirhams for the married daughter and seven thousand dirhams for the unmarried daughter. The married daughter died, leaving a son and a husband, who have demanded the amount recorded in the document from her father.

Do they inherit that amount, and may they demand it from the son? The father maintains that, at the time, he had no male child and wrote this document for fear that others would gain control over his property, while the son is alive.

---

## Page 16872

He replied:

If he acknowledged owing money to each of the two daughters, when he had previously owed neither of them anything, that acknowledgment gave her no claim against him; it was false and invalid. Even if he subsequently pledged a gift to her as a debt owed by him, it would not be binding. Rather, preferential treatment among one’s children is prohibited, and he should treat them equitably, by the consensus of the Muslims, even if the aforementioned acknowledgment had already been made. Equitable treatment among them is obligatory according to the sounder of the scholars’ two opinions. The woman’s heirs are not entitled to any of that money.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man freed a slave woman and then married her. Subsequently, while of sound mind, legally competent, and in good health, he granted her ownership of property, declaring that everything contained in the home where they lived—copperware, textiles, chests, jewelry, furnishings, and anything else apart from his own clothing, riding animals, and equipment for his horses—belonged to his aforementioned wife. He had no right to any of it or any share in it; all of it was in her possession and at her disposal, with no control over it remaining to him. He then acknowledged this in her favor and drew up a legally valid deed of acknowledgment in these terms. Is an itemized description required, or not?

Our shaykh and master, Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-ʿAbbās, replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he acknowledged that everything in his house belonged to his wife except the weapons, riding animals, and equipment for the horses, this was a valid acknowledgment whose terms must be given effect, without disagreement. If the basis for this acknowledgment was that the property had been transferred to his wife through a legally valid and binding conveyance, then the acknowledgment was valid both in substance and in its outward legal effect. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16873

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man acknowledged in a written deed that he owed another man a sum in dirhams. Some time later, the person in whose favor the acknowledgment had been made came to the witnesses to the deed and said: “The acknowledgment he made is invalid. My only claim against him is for gold belonging to my daughter.” Does this render the acknowledgment void? May a witness testify to the contents of the deed after hearing the creditor say this?

He replied:

The witness testifies to what he heard from the man who made the acknowledgment; nothing more is required of him, whether the creditor confirms or contradicts it.

However, if the creditor makes such a statement, and his words are explained in a way that is ordinarily plausible—for example, by his saying, “My claim against him was for gold, and we agreed that he would acknowledge silver in its place”—then, if the man who made the acknowledgment confirms his account, effect is to be given accordingly. If he denies it, he is to swear an oath denying what the creditor has alleged. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 16874

He—may Allah sanctify his soul and be pleased with him—was also asked:

A craftsman worked for a master craftsman for several years. Upon leaving his service, he said to him, “Settle accounts with me.” The master then beat him, drew up a deed against him, and threatened him with the authorities. Does the master have any rightful claim under that document?

He replied:

If the master drew up a deed against him and he acknowledged its contents under wrongful coercion, his acknowledgment is invalid, and he may not be compelled to fulfill what it contains. His master must settle accounts with him. And Allah knows best.

May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them peace.

> End of Volume Thirty-Five.
